On the structure of noncollapsed Ricci flow limit spaces
Hanbing Fang, and, Yu Li

Abstract
We establish a weak compactness theorem for the moduli space of closed Ricci flows with uniformly bounded entropy, each equipped with a natural spacetime distance, under pointed Gromov–Hausdorff convergence. Furthermore, we develop a structure theory for the corresponding Ricci flow limit spaces, showing that the regular part, where convergence is smooth, admits the structure of a Ricci flow spacetime, while the singular set has codimension at least four.



1
Introduction

A Ricci flow solution
(g(t))_{t\in I}
on a closed Riemannian manifold
M^{n}
is given by the evolution equation:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}g(t)=-2\mathrm{Ric}(g(t))

for any
t\in I
, where
I
is a closed time interval. Ricci flow was introduced by Hamilton in his pioneering 1982 paper
[
Ham82
]
, where he used it to prove that a closed 3-manifold with positive Ricci curvature evolves under Ricci flow to a manifold with constant curvature. This result was a major breakthrough in the use of geometric evolution equations to study the topology of manifolds. In the early 2000s, building on Hamilton’s program, Perelman introduced several new ideas that revolutionized the understanding of Ricci flow and finally resolved the Poincaré Conjecture and the more general Geometrization Conjecture
[
Per02
,
Per03a
,
Per03b
]
.

Compactness theory plays a central role in the analysis of geometric flows, particularly in Ricci flow, where understanding the behavior of sequences of solutions is essential to studying singularity formation, convergence, and geometric limits. The classical compactness theorem for the Ricci flow, established by Hamilton
[
Ham95
]
, asserts that a sequence of Ricci flows with uniform curvature bounds and non-collapsing conditions admits a subsequence converging in the Cheeger–Gromov sense. Another example is the compactness of
\kappa
-solutions to the Ricci flow, which are introduced by Perelman as local models for singularities after appropriate blow-up procedures. In three dimensions, Perelman used this compactness result to essentially classify all 3-dimensional
\kappa
-solutions, leading to a detailed understanding of singularity models and enabling the implementation of Ricci flow with surgery.

In general dimensions, the weak compactness theory of Ricci flows has been developed under the additional assumption of a uniform scalar curvature bound; see, for instance,
[
CW12
,
TZ16
,
CW17
,
CW20
,
Bam18
]
. In the case of Kähler Ricci flow on Fano manifolds, this scalar curvature bound is automatic due to Perelman’s crucial estimate
[
ST18
]
. These weak compactness theories focus on the convergence of the time-slices of Ricci flows in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense. A key observation under the scalar curvature bound is that the distance functions at different time-slices are mutually comparable; see
[
CW20
, Lemma 4.21]
and
[
BZ17
, Theorem 1.1]
. Consequently, the weak compactness theory implies that the time-slices converge in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense to a singular metric space, whose singular set has codimension at least 4. Moreover, in
[
CW20
]
(see also
[
Bam18
]
), the authors further established the convergence of Ricci flows as
spacetimes
—a perspective that already appeared in Perelman’s work.

The convergence theory of Ricci flows can be viewed as a natural generalization of the convergence theory for Einstein manifolds, developed by Cheeger, Colding, Naber and others; see
[
CC97
,
CN13
,
CN15
]
. However, in the case of general Ricci flows without any curvature assumptions, the lack of distance comparability prevents one from establishing Gromov–Hausdorff convergence for individual time-slices.

In a series of seminal works
[
Bam20a
,
Bam23
,
Bam20b
]
, Bamler introduced a number of innovative ideas to develop the theory of
\mathbb{F}
-convergence. Within this framework, Bamler proved that for almost every time, the time-slices of a Ricci flow converge in the Gromov-
W_{1}
-Wasserstein distance (see Definition
2.3
), when equipped with a conjugate heat kernel measure. Moreover, he established that Ricci flows
\mathbb{F}
-converge to a limit known as a metric flow (see Definition
4.1
), and that the family of time-slices in this limiting metric flow is almost continuous in the
GW_{1}
-sense. A metric flow can be regarded as a weak notion of Ricci flow; see also alternative formulations in
[
HN18
,
CH24
]
. In dimension three, an example of a metric flow is a branch of a weak Ricci flow, as established in
[
KL17
]
, in which each time-slice remains connected.

In general, a limiting metric flow may carry limited geometric information due to potential collapsing phenomena. However, when a uniform bound on the Nash entropy at the base point is imposed, Bamler showed in
[
Bam20b
]
that the limiting metric flow exhibits favorable geometric properties. Notably, the limit space admits a regular-singular decomposition: the regular part forms a Ricci flow spacetime (see Definition
4.16
), while the singular part has codimension at least 4, defined with respect to coverings by
P^{*}
-balls (see Definition
3.14
). Moreover, several key results originally established in the context of Einstein manifolds—such as the stratification of the singular set, volume estimates for the quantitative singular strata, and integral curvature radius bounds from
[
CN13
,
CN15
]
—continue to hold in the setting of Ricci flow.

In this paper, we consider the moduli space
\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
of closed Ricci flows defined as follows:

Definition 1.1

(Moduli space)
.

For fixed constants
T\in(0,+\infty]
, and
Y>0
, the moduli space
\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
consists of all
n
-dimensional closed Ricci flows
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}
satisfying

(i)

g(t)
is defined on
\mathbb{I}^{++}:=[-T,0]
.

(ii)

For any spacetime point
x^{*}\in M\times\mathbb{I}^{++}
,

\displaystyle\inf_{\tau>0}\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}(\tau)\geq-Y,

where the infimum is taken over all
\tau>0
for which the Nash entropy
\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}(\tau)
is well-defined.

It is clear that any closed Ricci flow defined on a closed time interval of length
T
can, via a time translation, be assumed to be defined on
\mathbb{I}^{++}
. The definition of the Nash entropy
\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}
based at a spacetime point
x^{*}
is given in Definition
2.7
. Condition (ii) is equivalent to a uniform non-collapsing condition. By Perelman’s celebrated monotonicity formula, any closed Ricci flow
\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}
satisfying

\displaystyle\inf_{\tau\in(0,2T]}\boldsymbol{\mu}(g(-T),\tau)\geq-Y

automatically satisfies condition (ii).

For any Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
, the absence of curvature bounds makes it difficult to define a natural distance between spacetime points. Nevertheless, a key result—proved in
[
MT10
, Theorem 2]
and
[
CRT12
, Theorem 3.1]
(see also
[
Bam20a
, Lemma 2.7]
)—states that

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{t}(\nu_{x^{*};t},\nu_{y^{*};t})\quad\text{and}\quad d_{W_{2}}^{t}(\nu_{x^{*};t},\nu_{y^{*};t})

are nondecreasing in
t
for any spacetime points
x^{*},y^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
, where
d^{t}_{W_{p}}
denotes the
W_{p}
-Wasserstein distance with respect to the metric
g(t)
(see Definition
2.1
), and
\nu_{z^{*};t}
denotes the conjugate heat kernel measure based at
z^{*}
(see Definition
2.6
). In fact, this monotonicity is equivalent to the notion of a super Ricci flow
[
MT10
]
, and is closely related to weak formulations of super Ricci flows; see
[
Stu18
,
KS18
]
.

For
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}
, we can use the monotonicity to define a spacetime distance by restricting on the slightly smaller interval
\mathbb{I}^{+}:=[-(1-\sigma)T,0]
, where
\sigma
is a small parameter in
(0,1/100]
. Specifically, we have the following definition:

Definition 1.2
.

For any
x^{*}=(x,t),y^{*}=(y,s)\in M\times\mathbb{I}^{+}
with
s\leq t
, we define

\displaystyle d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*}):=\inf_{r\in[\sqrt{t-s},\sqrt{t+(1-\sigma)T})}\left\{r\mid d_{W_{1}}^{t-r^{2}}(\nu_{x^{*};t-r^{2}},\nu_{y^{*};t-r^{2}})\leq\epsilon_{0}r\right\}.

(1.1)

If no such
r
exists, we define
d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*}):=\epsilon_{0}^{-1}d_{W_{1}}^{-(1-\sigma)T}(\nu_{x^{*};-(1-\sigma)T},\nu_{y^{*};-(1-\sigma)T})
.

Here,
\epsilon_{0}\in(0,1]
is called the spacetime distance constant, depending only on
n
,
Y
and
\sigma
(see Definition
3.3
). In practice, one can fix
\sigma=1/100
so that
\epsilon_{0}
depends only on
n
and
Y
.

Definition
1.2
ensures that the natural time-function
\mathfrak{t}
, defined as the projection of a spacetime point onto its time component, is 2-Hölder continuous, that is,

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-\mathfrak{t}(y^{*})|\leq d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*})^{2}\quad\text{for all}\quad x^{*},y^{*}\in M\times\mathbb{I}^{+}.

It can be shown—see Lemma
3.7
—that
d^{*}
is indeed a distance function. Moreover, the topology induced by
d^{*}
coincides with the standard topology on
M\times\mathbb{I}^{+}
(see Corollary
3.11
). In addition, the metric balls
B^{*}
, defined via
d^{*}
, are comparable to the parabolic balls
P^{*}
introduced by Bamler (see Proposition
3.15
). Furthermore, if the scalar curvature is locally bounded,
B^{*}
is comparable to the standard parabolic balls; see Proposition
3.10
.

There is some flexibility in choosing the parameter
\epsilon_{0}
in Definition
1.2
. Also, one may define a similar spacetime distance using the monotonicity of
d_{W_{2}}^{t}
, as in (
1.1
). Nonetheless, all such spacetime distances are equivalent in the sense that they are bi-Lipschitz to one another; see Appendix
B
for details.

Our first main result is the following pointed Gromov–Hausdorff convergence for a sequence of Ricci flows in
\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
, with respect to the
d^{*}
-distance, when restricted to the smaller time interval
\mathbb{I}:=[-(1-2\sigma)T,0]
.

Theorem 1.3

(Weak compactness)
.

Given any sequence
\mathcal{X}^{i}=\{M_{i}^{n},(g_{i}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
with base points
p_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}

(
when
T=+\infty
, we additionally assume
\limsup_{i\to\infty}\mathfrak{t}_{i}(p_{i}^{*})>-\infty
)
, by taking a subsequence if necessary, we obtain the pointed Gromov–Hausdorff convergence

\displaystyle(M_{i}\times\mathbb{I},d^{*}_{i},p_{i}^{*},\mathfrak{t}_{i})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathrm{pGH}\hskip 8.19447pt}(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t}),

where
d^{*}_{i}
denotes the restriction of the
d^{*}
-distance on
M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
, and
\mathfrak{t}_{i}
is the standard time-function on
M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
. The limit space
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
is a complete, separable, locally compact metric space coupled with a
2
-Hölder continuous time-function
\mathfrak{t}:Z\to\mathbb{I}
.

In Propositions
3.17
and
3.18
, we establish uniform lower and upper volume bounds for spacetime balls
B^{*}(x^{*},r)
. Once these bounds are in place, Theorem
1.3
follows from a standard ball-packing argument, analogous to the convergence theory for sequences of Riemannian manifolds with uniform Ricci curvature lower bounds. The full proof is given in Theorem
3.23
.

The limit space
(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t})
is referred to as a
noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space
over
\mathbb{I}
. A natural question arises: what is the relationship between the space
Z
and the
\mathbb{F}
-limits obtained from the sequence
\mathcal{X}^{i}
?

To investigate this, consider a sequence of points
z_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
converging to
z\in Z
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense. By the theory of
\mathbb{F}
-convergence (see Section
4
), there exists a correspondence
\mathfrak{C}
such that

(\mathcal{X}^{i},(\nu_{z_{i}^{*};t})_{t\in[-T,\mathfrak{t}_{i}(z_{i}^{*})]})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathbb{F},\mathfrak{C}\hskip 8.19447pt}(\mathcal{X}^{z},(\nu_{z;t})_{t\in[-T,\mathfrak{t}(z)]}),

(1.2)

where the metric flow
\mathcal{X}^{z}
is future continuous for all
t\in[-T,\mathfrak{t}(z)]
, except possibly at
t=-(1-\sigma)T
, at which we require that the convergence (
1.2
) is uniform. The metric flow
\mathcal{X}^{z}
is referred to as the metric flow associated with
z
. On
\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
, one can define a spacetime function
d_{z}^{*}
as Definition
1.2
(see Definition
4.22
). In general,
d_{z}^{*}
is only a pseudo-distance on
\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
. However, by passing to the corresponding quotient space
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}}
, one obtains an isometric embedding into the limit space
Z
(see Theorem
4.27
for the proof).

Theorem 1.4
.

For any
z\in Z
, there exists an isometric embedding

\displaystyle\iota_{z}:(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}},d^{*}_{z})\longrightarrow(Z,d_{Z})

such that
\iota_{z}(z)=z
and
\mathfrak{t}\circ\iota_{z}=\mathfrak{t}^{z}
, where
\mathfrak{t}^{z}
is the time-function on
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}}
. Moreover, for any
y_{i}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}^{i}_{\mathbb{I}}
and
y_{\infty}\in\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}
,
y_{i}^{*}
converge to
y_{\infty}
within
\mathfrak{C}
if and only if
y_{i}^{*}\to\iota_{z}(\tilde{y}_{\infty})
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense, where
\tilde{y}_{\infty}
is the quotient image of
y_{\infty}
from
\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}
to
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}}
.

The space
Z
contains a regular part
\mathcal{R}
, whose restriction on
\mathbb{I}^{-}
is a dense open subset of
Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
(see Corollary
5.7
) and carries the structure of a Ricci flow spacetime
(\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{t},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}},g^{Z})
. On this regular part, the convergence described in Theorem
1.3
is smooth, in the following sense.

Theorem 1.5

(Smooth convergence)
.

There exists an increasing sequence
U_{1}\subset U_{2}\subset\ldots\subset\mathcal{R}
of open subsets with
\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}U_{i}=\mathcal{R}
, open subsets
V_{i}\subset M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
, time-preserving diffeomorphisms
\phi_{i}:U_{i}\to V_{i}
and a sequence
\epsilon_{i}\to 0
such that the following holds:

(a)

We have

\displaystyle\|\phi_{i}^{*}g^{i}-g^{Z}\|_{C^{[\epsilon_{i}^{-1}]}(U_{i})}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon_{i},

\displaystyle\|\phi_{i}^{*}\partial_{\mathfrak{t}_{i}}-\partial_{\mathfrak{t}}\|_{C^{[\epsilon_{i}^{-1}]}(U_{i})}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon_{i},

where
g^{i}
is the spacetime metric induced by
g_{i}(t)
, and
\partial_{\mathfrak{t}_{i}}
is the standard time vector field induced by
\mathfrak{t}_{i}
.

(b)

Let
y\in\mathcal{R}
and
y_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
. Then
y_{i}^{*}\to y
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense if and only if
y_{i}^{*}\in V_{i}
for large
i
and
\phi_{i}^{-1}(y_{i}^{*})\to y
in
\mathcal{R}
.

(c)

For
U_{i}^{(2)}=\{(x,y)\in U_{i}\times U_{i}\mid\mathfrak{t}(x)>\mathfrak{t}(y)+\epsilon_{i}\}
,
V_{i}^{(2)}=\{(x^{*},y^{*})\in V_{i}\times V_{i}\mid\mathfrak{t}_{i}(x^{*})>\mathfrak{t}_{i}(y^{*})+\epsilon_{i}\}
and
\phi_{i}^{(2)}:=(\phi_{i},\phi_{i}):U_{i}^{(2)}\to V_{i}^{(2)}
, we have

\displaystyle\|(\phi_{i}^{(2)})^{*}K^{i}-K_{Z}\|_{C^{[\epsilon_{i}^{-1}]}(U_{i}^{(2)})}\leq\epsilon_{i},

where
K^{i}
and
K_{Z}
denote the heat kernels on
(M_{i}\times\mathbb{I},g_{i}(t))
and
(\mathcal{R},g^{Z})
, respectively.

(d)

If
z_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
converge to
z\in Z
in Gromov–Hausdorff sense, then

\displaystyle K^{i}(z_{i}^{*};\phi_{i}(\cdot))\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}}K_{Z}(z;\cdot)\quad\text{on}\quad\mathcal{R}_{(-\infty,\mathfrak{t}(z))}.

(e)

For each
t\in\mathbb{I}
, there are at most countable connected components of the time-slice
\mathcal{R}_{t}
.

The full proof of Theorem
1.5
can be found in Theorems
5.2
,
5.20
and Proposition
5.32
.

The proof of Theorem
1.5
is similar to the smooth convergence for the
\mathbb{F}
-limit (see
[
Bam23
, Section 9]
). Roughly speaking, the approach involves constructing a product domain
U_{z}
for each
z\in\mathcal{R}
, such that
U_{z}
is realized by a Ricci flow spacetime satisfying the required properties. These local pieces are then glued together using a standard patching procedure. Special care must be taken in the case where
\mathfrak{t}(z)=0
.

The associated metric flow
\mathcal{X}^{z}
also contains a regular part
\mathcal{R}^{z}
, which admits the structure of a Ricci flow spacetime
(\mathcal{R}^{z},\mathfrak{t}^{z},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{z}},g^{z})
; see Theorem
4.17
. It can be shown—see Proposition
5.6
—that the isometric embedding
\iota_{z}
from Theorem
1.4
is, in fact, an isometric embedding of Ricci flow spacetimes. As a result, the regular part
\mathcal{R}
can be viewed as a gluing of all pieces
\iota_{z}(\mathcal{R}^{z})
.

In general, the regular part
\mathcal{R}
may not be connected in the spacetime. We provide a sufficient condition (see Corollary
5.28
) under which two points in
\mathcal{R}
lie in the same connected component. In particular,
\mathcal{R}
is connected if
T=+\infty
. We emphasize that this stands in sharp contrast to the regular part of an
\mathbb{F}
-limit, which is connected for each time-slice. For example, as illustrated in Figure
1
, the slice
\mathcal{R}_{t_{3}}
consists of two components, namely
\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{t_{3}})
and
\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{t_{3}})
.

For each
z\in Z
, we can assign a conjugate heat kernel measure
\nu_{z;s}
based at
z
for
s\leq\mathfrak{t}(z)
, which is a probability measure on
\mathcal{R}_{s}
. All these probability measures together satisfy the reproduction formula (see (
5.5
)). With the help of conjugate heat kernel measures, we can define a distance
d_{t}^{Z}
at the time-slice
Z_{t}
for any
t\in\mathbb{I}^{-}:=(-(1-2\sigma)T,0]
.

Definition 1.6
.

For each
t\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, we define the distance at the time-slice
Z_{t}
by

\displaystyle d^{Z}_{t}(x,y):=\lim_{s\nearrow t}d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{R}_{s}}(\nu_{x;s},\nu_{y;s})\in[0,\infty]

for any
x,y\in Z_{t}
, where
d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{R}_{s}}
denotes the
W_{1}
-Wasserstein distance on
(\mathcal{R}_{s},g^{Z}_{s})
.

It can be proved that the limit in Definition
1.6
must exist, since
d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{R}_{s}}
is nondecreasing (see Lemma
5.29
).

Theorem 1.7
.

For the distance
d^{Z}_{t}
defined in Definition
1.6
, the following properties hold.

(a)

For any
t\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
,
(Z_{t},d^{Z}_{t})
is a complete extended metric space.

(b)

\left(Z,\mathfrak{t},(d^{Z}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{-}},(\nu_{z;s})_{s\in\mathbb{I}^{-},s\leq\mathfrak{t}(z)}\right)
is an
H_{n}
-concentrated extended metric flow over
\mathbb{I}^{-}
, in the sense of Definition
6.15
.

(c)

For any
w\in\mathcal{R}_{t}
, there exists a small constant
r>0
such that for any
x,y\in B_{g^{Z}_{t}}(w,r)
,

\displaystyle d^{Z}_{t}(x,y)=d_{g^{Z}_{t}}(x,y).

(d)

For all but countably many times
t\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, we have on each connected component of
\mathcal{R}_{t}
,

\displaystyle d^{Z}_{t}=d_{g^{Z}_{t}}.

(e)

For any
x,y\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
with
t_{0}=\mathfrak{t}(x)\geq\mathfrak{t}(y)
, if
r=d_{Z}(x,y)
satisfies
t_{0}-r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{t\nearrow t_{0}-r^{2}}d_{W_{1}}^{Z_{t}}(\nu_{x;t},\nu_{y;t})\leq\epsilon_{0}r\leq\lim_{t\searrow t_{0}-r^{2}}d_{W_{1}}^{Z_{t}}(\nu_{x;t},\nu_{y;t}),

where
d_{W_{1}}^{Z_{t}}
denotes the
W_{1}
-Wasserstein distance on
(Z_{t},d^{Z}_{t})
(see Definition
6.9
).

The proof of Theorem
1.7
can be found in Lemma
6.3
, Propositions
6.6
,
6.8
,
6.11
, Theorem
6.16
, Propositions
6.20
and
6.23
.

In general, any conjugate heat kernel measure
\nu_{z;s}
has full measure on a single connected component of
\mathcal{R}_{s}
. Parts (c) and (d) of Theorem
1.7
show that, locally, the distance function
d^{Z}_{t}
agrees with the Riemannian distance
d_{g^{Z}_{t}}
induced by the metric
g^{Z}_{t}
, and for almost every
t\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, the two coincide on each connected component of
\mathcal{R}_{t}
. However, one should not expect this agreement to hold globally on all of
\mathcal{R}_{t}
, as it is possible for
d^{Z}_{t}(x,y)
to be finite even when
x
and
y
lie in different components of
\mathcal{R}_{t}
(see Figure
1
at
t_{3}
). Part (e) of Theorem
1.7
further clarifies the relationship between the spacetime distance
d_{Z}
and the time-slice distance
d^{Z}_{t}
, in alignment with Definition
1.2
.

Definition 1.8

(Tangent flow)
.

For any
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
, a
tangent flow

(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
at
z
is a pointed Gromov–Hausdorff limit of
(Z,r_{j}^{-1}d_{Z},z,r_{j}^{-2}(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}(z)))
for a sequence
r_{j}\searrow 0
.

It can be shown (see Section
7
) that any tangent flow is a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space. We now introduce a broader class of Ricci flow limit spaces, called
Ricci shrinker spaces
, which encompass all tangent flows. Roughly speaking, a Ricci shrinker space
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space with
\mathbb{R}_{-}\subset\mathrm{image}(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
such that the base point
z^{\prime}
has constant Nash entropy (see Definition
7.16
).

For Ricci shrinker spaces, we have the following:

Theorem 1.9

(Characterization of Ricci shrinker spaces)
.

Let
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
be a Ricci shrinker space so that its regular part is given by a Ricci flow spacetime
(\mathcal{R}^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}},g^{Z^{\prime}}_{t})
. Then the following statements hold.

(a)

On
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
, the following equation holds:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}(g^{Z^{\prime}})+\nabla^{2}f_{z^{\prime}}=\frac{g^{Z^{\prime}}}{2|\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}|},

where
f_{z^{\prime}}
is the potential function at
z^{\prime}
.

(b)

For any
t<0
, the slice
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}
is connected. Moreover, the distance
d^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}
, when restricted on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}
, coincides with the Riemannian distance induced by the metric
g^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}
.

(c)

Z^{\prime}_{(0,\infty)}=\emptyset
if
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is
collapsed
(see Definition
7.18
).

(d)

The space
Z^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
is
self-similar
in the following sense: there exists a flow
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}
on
Z^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
such that, when restricted to
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
, it is generated by
\tau(\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}-\nabla f_{z^{\prime}})
, with
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{0}=\mathrm{id}
. Moreover, for any
x,y\in Z^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle d_{Z^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(x),\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(y))=e^{-\frac{s}{2}}d_{Z^{\prime}}(x,y).

In addition, for any
x,y\in Z^{\prime}_{t}
with
t<0
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
, the time-slice distance satisfies

\displaystyle d^{Z^{\prime}}_{e^{-s}t}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(x),\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(y))=e^{-\frac{s}{2}}d^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}(x,y).

(e)

For any
t<0
,
Z^{\prime}_{t}\setminus\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}
has Minkowski dimension at most
n-4
with respect to
d^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}
.

The proof of Theorem
1.9
can be found in Proposition
7.9
, Corollary
7.10
, Theorem
7.25
and Proposition
7.30
.

We will show (see Section
7
) that the metric flow
\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}
associated with
z^{\prime}
is a metric soliton in the sense of Definition
4.14
, and that its regular part
\mathcal{R}^{z^{\prime}}
, under the embedding
\iota_{z^{\prime}}
, coincides with
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
. In general, however, it is not known whether
\iota_{z^{\prime}}(\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}_{(-\infty,0)})=Z^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
holds unconditionally. We will prove in Theorem
7.19
that this equality does hold if the scalar curvature on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}
is uniformly bounded.

On
Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
, we have the following regular-singular decomposition:

\displaystyle Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}=\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}\sqcup\mathcal{S},

where
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
denotes the restriction of
\mathcal{R}
on
\mathbb{I}^{-}
. It can be proved (see Theorem
7.15
) that a point
z
is a regular point if and only if any of its tangent flows is isometric to
(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R},d^{*}_{E,\epsilon_{0}},(\vec{0}^{n},0),\mathfrak{t})
or
(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}_{-},d^{*}_{E,\epsilon_{0}},(\vec{0}^{n},0),\mathfrak{t})
, where
d^{*}_{E,\epsilon_{0}}
denotes the induced
d^{*}
-distance on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
(see Example
3.9
). Here, the concept of isometry between two Ricci flow limit spaces can be found in Definition
5.21
. Equivalently,
z
is a regular point if and only if
\mathcal{N}_{z}(0)\geq-\epsilon_{n}
(see Proposition
7.7
).

The singular set
\mathcal{S}
admits a natural stratification:

\mathcal{S}^{0}\subset\mathcal{S}^{1}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{S}^{n+1}=\mathcal{S},

where a point
z\in\mathcal{S}^{k}
if and only if no tangent flow at
z
is
(k+1)
-symmetric. Here, a tangent flow
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is said to be
k
-symmetric if one of the following holds:

(1)

(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is
k
-splitting and is not a static cone.

(2)

(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is a static cone that is
(k-2)
-splitting.

Roughly speaking, a
static cone
is characterized by
\mathrm{image}(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})=\mathbb{R}
and vanishing Ricci curvature on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
. Notably, Case (1) above may include a
quasi-static cone
, which has vanishing Ricci curvature only on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,t_{a}]}
for some constant
t_{a}\in[0,\infty)
, but not beyond. For precise definitions and related properties of static and quasi-static cones, see Definition
7.17
, Theorem
7.21
and Proposition
7.23
.

Theorem 1.10
.

In the same setting as above, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}^{n-2}.

Theorem
1.10
is derived from
[
Bam20b
, Theorem 2.8]
, where the corresponding metric solitons are excluded (see Theorem
8.8
for details). We can also formulate the following quantitative singular strata as in
[
CN13
]
and
[
Bam20b
]
.

Definition 1.11
.

For
\epsilon>0
and
0<r_{1}<r_{2}<\infty
, the quantitative singular strata

\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,0}_{r_{1},r_{2}}\subset\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,1}_{r_{1},r_{2}}\subset\ldots\subset\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,n-2}_{r_{1},r_{2}}\subset Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}

are defined as follows:

z\in\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,k}_{r_{1},r_{2}}
if and only if
\mathfrak{t}(z)-\epsilon^{-1}r_{2}^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
and for all
r\in[r_{1},r_{2}]
,
z
is not
(k+1,\epsilon,r)
-symmetric.
Here, the precise definition of a point being
(k,\epsilon,r)
-symmetric can be found in Definition
8.10
.

The following identity is clear from the above definitions: for any
L>1
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{k}=\bigcup_{\epsilon\in(0,L^{-1})}\bigcap_{0<r<\epsilon L}\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,k}_{r,\epsilon L}.

(1.3)

Theorem 1.12
.

Given
x_{0}\in Z
,
\epsilon>0
and
r>0
with
\mathfrak{t}(x_{0})-2r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, the following statements are true.

(a)

For any
\delta\in(0,\epsilon)
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert B^{*}_{Z}\left(\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,n-2}_{\delta r,\epsilon r},\delta r\right)\cap B_{Z}^{*}(x_{0},r)\right\rvert\leq C(n,Y,\sigma,\epsilon)\delta^{4-\epsilon}r^{n+2},

where
B^{*}_{Z}\left(A,s\right)
denotes the
s
-neighborhood of a subset
A
with respect to
d_{Z}
. Moreover, for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert B^{*}_{Z}\left(\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,n-2}_{\delta r,\epsilon r},\delta r\right)\cap B_{Z}^{*}(x_{0},r)\cap Z_{t}\right\rvert_{t}\leq C(n,Y,\sigma,\epsilon)\delta^{2-\epsilon}r^{n}.

(b)

For any
\delta\in(0,\epsilon)
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\{r_{\mathrm{Rm}}<\delta r\}\cap B_{Z}^{*}(x_{0},r)\right\rvert\leq C(n,Y,\sigma,\epsilon)\delta^{4-\epsilon}r^{n+2},

where
r_{\mathrm{Rm}}
denotes the curvature radius; see Definition
7.4
. Moreover, for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\{r_{\mathrm{Rm}}<\delta r\}\cap B_{Z}^{*}(x_{0},r)\cap Z_{t}\right\rvert_{t}\leq C(n,Y,\sigma,\epsilon)\delta^{2-\epsilon}r^{n}.

(c)

For any
\epsilon>0
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{B^{*}_{Z}(x_{0},r)\cap\mathcal{R}}|\mathrm{Rm}|^{2-\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z}_{t}}\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{B^{*}_{Z}(x_{0},r)\cap\mathcal{R}}r_{\mathrm{Rm}}^{-4+2\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z}_{t}}\mathrm{d}t\leq C(n,Y,\sigma,\epsilon)r^{n-2+2\epsilon}.

Moreover, for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\int_{B^{*}_{Z}(x_{0},r)\cap\mathcal{R}_{t}}|\mathrm{Rm}|^{1-\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z}_{t}}\leq\int_{B^{*}_{Z}(x_{0},r)\cap\mathcal{R}_{t}}r_{\mathrm{Rm}}^{-2+2\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z}_{t}}\leq C(n,Y,\sigma,\epsilon)r^{n-2+2\epsilon}.

The proof of Theorem
1.12
can be found in Corollary
8.15
and Theorem
8.18
. With Theorem
1.12
, the following result is clear from (
1.3
).

Theorem 1.13
.

The Minkowski dimension with respect to
d_{Z}
satisfies

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathscr{M}}\mathcal{S}\leq n-2.

As an application, we consider a closed Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in[-T,0)}\}
such that
0
is the first singular time. We assume
T<\infty
and that
\mathcal{X}
has entropy bounded below by
-Y
.

We consider the
d^{*}
-distance on
\mathcal{X}_{[-0.99T,0)}
, defined as in Definition
1.2
, using the spacetime distance constant
\epsilon_{0}=\epsilon_{0}(n,Y)>0
. For simplicity, we set
\sigma=1/100
.

We then define

\displaystyle(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})

to be the
metric completion
of
\mathcal{X}_{[-0.98T,0)}
with respect to
d^{*}
. By construction, we have
(Z_{[-0.98T,0)},d_{Z})=(\mathcal{X}_{[-0.98T,0)},d^{*})
; that is, the completion adds only the points in
Z_{0}
. One can show, see Section
9
, that
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
is a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space.

Theorem 1.14
.

With the above assumptions, there exists a constant
C_{\epsilon}
depending on
\epsilon
and the Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}
such that the following statements are true.

(a)

For any small
\epsilon>0

\displaystyle\int_{-T}^{0}\int_{M}|\mathrm{Rm}|^{2-\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{-T}^{0}\int_{M}r_{\mathrm{Rm}}^{-4+2\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}\mathrm{d}t\leq C_{\epsilon}.

Moreover, for any
t\in[-T,0)
,

\displaystyle\int_{M}|\mathrm{Rm}|^{1-\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}\leq\int_{M}r_{\mathrm{Rm}}^{-2+2\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}\leq C_{\epsilon}.

(b)

The limit
V_{0}:=\lim_{t\nearrow 0}|M|_{t}\in[0,\infty)
exists.
V_{0}=0
if and only if
\mathcal{R}_{0}=\emptyset
. In this case, we have

\displaystyle|M|_{t}\leq C_{\epsilon}|t|^{1-\epsilon}

for any
t\in[-T,0)
and any small
\epsilon>0
.

(c)

For any small
\delta>0
and
\epsilon>0
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\{y\in Z_{0}\mid d_{0}^{Z}(y,\mathcal{S})<\delta\right\}\right\rvert_{0}\leq C_{\epsilon}\delta^{2-\epsilon}.

The proof of Theorem
1.14
can be found in Theorem
9.1
, Proposition
9.2
, Corollary
9.5
and Theorem
9.9
.

Figure 1:
Singular set is a segment at
t_{3}
;
\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{t})=\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{t})
for
t<t_{3}
.

Organization of the paper

This paper is organized as follows.

In Section
2
, we introduce the necessary definitions and basic properties of related concepts in metric measure spaces. We also review known results for closed Ricci flows, including estimates for the Nash entropy, heat kernel bounds, and volume bounds.

In Section
3
, we define the spacetime
d^{*}
-distance and establish many of its fundamental properties. This section also contains the proof of Theorem
1.3
.

In Section
4
, we review Bamler’s theory of
\mathbb{F}
-convergence and explain how
\mathbb{F}
-limits relate to the Ricci flow limit space
Z
. Theorem
1.4
is also proved in this section.

Section
5
focuses on the regular part of the Ricci flow limit space. We detail the construction of the Ricci flow spacetime and analyze the associated conjugate heat kernel measures. The proof of Theorem
1.5
is presented here.

In Section
6
, we define the time-slice distance
d^{Z}_{t}
on
Z_{t}
and prove several of its key properties, including Theorem
1.7
.

Section
7
is devoted to the study of tangent flows of the Ricci flow limit space. We prove Theorem
1.9
in this section.

In Section
8
, we investigate the singular set and the quantitative singular strata, providing estimates on their size. Theorems
1.12
and
1.13
are proved here.

In Section
9
, we apply the results established earlier to the first singular time of a closed Ricci flow. Theorem
1.14
is proved in this section.

Section
10
focuses on almost splitting maps. We establish their basic properties and show how they relate to the splitting of the limit space.

Finally, in Section
11
, we extend the main results of this paper to Ricci flows with bounded curvature on each compact time interval. We also study the noncollapsed Ricci flow limit spaces arising as limits of sequences of Kähler Ricci flows.

In Appendix
A
, we derive two versions of estimates for the conjugate heat kernel measures. Appendix
B
establishes the equivalence of various spacetime distances. In Appendix
C
, we explore the relationship between eigenvalues and almost splitting, a result that may be of independent interest. Appendix
D
introduces the notion of the spine of a Ricci shrinker space and investigates its basic properties. Finally, we include a list of notations for reference.

: Hanbing Fang would like to thank his advisor, Prof. Xiuxiong Chen, for his encouragement and support. Hanbing Fang is supported by the Simons Foundation. Yu Li is supported by YSBR-001, NSFC-12201597, NSFC-12522105 and research funds from the University of Science and Technology of China and the Chinese Academy of Sciences.

2
Preliminaries

In this section, we review some basic concepts for metric measure spaces and useful results in closed Ricci flows.

Probability measures on metric spaces

Let
(X,d)
be a complete separable metric space. Denote by
\mathcal{P}(X)
the space of all probability measures on
X
. In particular, we denote by
\delta_{x}\in\mathcal{P}(X)
the Dirac measure at
x\in X
. A tuple
(X,d,\mu)
with
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(X)
is called a
metric measure space
.

Definition 2.1

(Variance and Wasserstein distance)
.

The
variance
between two probability measures
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(X)
is defined by

\mathrm{Var}_{X}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\int_{X}\int_{X}d^{2}(x_{1},x_{2})\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x_{1})\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}(x_{2}).

For simplicity, we set
\mathrm{Var}_{X}(\mu)=\mathrm{Var}_{X}(\mu,\mu)
. For
p\geq 1
, the
W_{p}
-Wasserstein
distance between
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(X)
is defined by

\displaystyle d_{W_{p}}^{X}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\inf_{\Pi}\left(\int_{X\times X}d^{p}(x_{1},x_{2})\,\mathrm{d}\Pi(x_{1},x_{2})\right)^{1/p},

where the infimum is taken over all couplings
\Pi\in\mathcal{P}(M\times M)
between
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
, that is, any such
\Pi
satisfies
(\pi_{i})_{\#}\Pi=\mu_{i}
for
i=1,2
, where
\pi_{i}
is the projection from
X\times X
to the
i
-th copy of
X
.

The following result is immediate from the Kantorovich-Rubinstein duality, see
[
Vil09
, Chapter 5]
.

Lemma 2.2
.

For any
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(X)
, we have

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{X}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\sup_{f\in C_{b}(X),\ \rVert f\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip}}\leq 1}\left(\int_{X}f\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}-\int_{X}f\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}\right),

where
C_{b}(X)
denotes the space of bounded continuous functions on
X
.

Definition 2.3
.

Given two metric measure spaces
(X_{1},d_{1},\mu_{1})
and
(X_{2},d_{2},\mu_{2})
, the
Gromov-
W_{p}
-Wasserstein distance
for
p\geq 1
is defined as

\displaystyle d_{GW_{p}}\left((X_{1},d_{1},\mu_{1}),(X_{2},d_{2},\mu_{2})\right):=\inf d_{W_{p}}^{A}\left((\varphi_{1})_{*}\mu_{1},(\varphi_{2})_{*}\mu_{2}\right),

where the infimum is taken over all isometric embeddings
\varphi_{i}:(X_{i},d_{i})\to(A,d_{A})
for
i=1,2
.

The following lemma from
[
Bam20a
, Lemma 3.2]
gives basic properties of variance:

Lemma 2.4
.

For any
\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3}\in\mathcal{P}(X)
, we have

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}_{X}(\mu_{1},\mu_{3})}\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}_{X}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}+\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}_{X}(\mu_{2},\mu_{3})},

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{X}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}_{X}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\leq d_{W_{1}}^{X}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})+\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}_{X}(\mu_{1})}+\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}_{X}(\mu_{2})}.

Next, we recall that a sequence of
\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{P}(X)
converges
weakly
to
\mu_{\infty}\in\mathcal{P}(X)
if, for any
f\in C_{b}(X)
,

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}\int_{X}f\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}=\int_{X}f\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}.

Proposition 2.5
.

Suppose that a sequence of
\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{P}(X)
converges weakly to
\mu_{\infty}\in\mathcal{P}(X)
. Then the following conclusions hold.

(i)

We have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}_{X}(\mu_{\infty})\leq\liminf_{i\to\infty}\mathrm{Var}_{X}(\mu_{i}).

(ii)

If
\mathrm{Var}_{X}(\mu_{i})\leq C
for a uniform constant
C
, then
\mu_{i}\to\mu_{\infty}
in
d_{W_{p}}^{X}
for any
p\in[1,2)
.

Proof.

(i): This is immediate from the definition of the weak convergence, since
\mu_{i}\otimes\mu_{i}
converges weakly to
\mu_{\infty}\otimes\mu_{\infty}
in
\mathcal{P}(X\times X)
.

(ii): By the definition of the variance, for each
i\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, there exists
x_{i}\in X
such that

\displaystyle\int_{X}d^{2}(x_{i},x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}(x)\leq C.

(2.1)

In particular, this implies that for any
L>0
,

\displaystyle\mu_{i}\left(\{x\in X\mid d(x,x_{i})\geq L\}\right)\leq CL^{-2}.

(2.2)

We claim that there exists
C_{1}>0
such that
d(x_{\infty},x_{i})\leq C_{1}
for any
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Suppose otherwise. Then, by taking a subsequence, we have
\lim_{i\to\infty}d(x_{\infty},x_{i})=+\infty
. Thus, it follows from the weak convergence and (
2.2
) that for any
L>0
,

\displaystyle\mu_{\infty}\left(\{x\in X\mid d(x,x_{\infty})<L\}\right)\leq\liminf_{i\to\infty}\mu_{i}\left(\{x\in X\mid d(x,x_{\infty})<L\}\right)=0,

which is impossible.

By the claim and (
2.1
), we obtain that for any
L\geq 2C_{1}
,

\displaystyle\mu_{i}\left(\{x\in X\mid d(x,x_{\infty})\geq L\}\right)\leq 4CL^{-2}.

Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\int_{X}d^{2}(x,x_{\infty})\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}(x)\leq C_{2}.

Given
p\in[1,2)
, we have for any
L\geq 2C_{1}
,

\displaystyle\int_{d(x,x_{\infty})\geq L}d^{p}(x,x_{\infty})\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}(x)\leq\left(\int_{X}d^{2}(x,x_{\infty})\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}(x)\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}\left(\mu_{i}\left(\{x\in X\mid d(x,x_{\infty})\geq L\}\right)\right)^{1-\frac{p}{2}}\leq C_{3}L^{p-2}.

(2.3)

Consequently, the conclusion follows from (
2.3
) and
[
Vil09
, Theorem 6.9]
.
∎

Preliminary results on the Ricci flow

In this section, we consider a closed Ricci flow solution
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in I}\}
, where
M
is an
n
-dimensional closed manifold,
I
is a closed interval, and
(g(t))_{t\in I}
is a family of smooth metrics on
M
satisfying the Ricci flow equation for all
t\in I
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}g(t)=-2\mathrm{Ric}(g(t)).

For convenience, we use
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
to denote a spacetime point
x^{*}\in M\times I
and define
\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})\in I
as its time component. For any subinterval
I^{\prime}\subset I
, we set
\mathcal{X}_{I^{\prime}}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in I^{\prime}}\}
. We denote by
d_{t}
the distance function on
M
and by
dV_{g(t)}
the volume form induced by
g(t)
. For any
x^{*}=(x,t)\in\mathcal{X}
, let
B_{t}(x,r)
denote the geodesic ball centered at
x
with radius
r
with respect to
g(t)
. The Riemannian curvature, Ricci curvature and scalar curvature of
g(t)
are denoted by
\mathrm{Rm}
,
\mathrm{Ric}
, and
\mathrm{R}
, respectively, with the time parameter
t
omitted when there is no ambiguity. Additionally, we define
R_{\min}
as a lower bound of the scalar curvature. In general, for any
t>t_{0}
with
t,t_{0}\in I
, the scalar curvature satisfies the bound

\displaystyle\mathrm{R}(\cdot,t)\geq-\frac{n}{2(t-t_{0})}

(2.4)

as shown, for instance, in
[
Top06
, Corollary 3.3.5]
.

For the closed Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}
, we denote by
d_{W_{p}}^{t}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
the
W_{p}
-Wasserstein distance and by
\mathrm{Var}_{t}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
the variance between two probability measures
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
on
M
with respect to
g(t)
.

We define the heat operator as
\square:=\partial_{t}-\Delta
and its conjugate operator as
\square^{*}:=-\partial_{t}-\Delta+\mathrm{R}
. Let
K(x,t;y,s)
be the heat kernel on the Ricci flow for
t>s
. More precisely, it satisfies the following system:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&\square K(\cdot,\cdot;y,s)=0,\\
&\square^{*}K(x,t;\cdot,\cdot)=0,\\
&\lim_{t\searrow s}K(\cdot,t;y,s)=\delta_{y},\\
&\lim_{s\nearrow t}K(x,t;\cdot,s)=\delta_{x}.\end{cases}

Definition 2.6
.

The
conjugate heat kernel measure

\nu_{x^{*};s}
based at
x^{*}=(x,t)
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};s}=\mathrm{d}\nu_{x,t;s}:=K(x,t;\cdot,s)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(s)}.

It is clear that
\nu_{x^{*};s}
is a probability measure on
M
. If we set

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};s}=(4\pi(t-s))^{-n/2}e^{-f_{x^{*}}(\cdot,s)}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(s)},

then the function
f_{x^{*}}
is called the
potential function
at
x^{*}
which satisfies:

-\partial_{s}f_{x^{*}}=\Delta f_{x^{*}}-|\nabla f_{x^{*}}|^{2}+\mathrm{R}-\frac{n}{2(t-s)}.

Next, we recall the definitions of the Nash entropy and
\mathcal{W}
-entropy based at
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
.

Definition 2.7
.

The
Nash entropy
based at
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
is defined by

\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}(\tau):=\int_{M}f_{x^{*}}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-\tau}-\frac{n}{2}

for any
\tau>0
with
\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-\tau\in I
, where
f_{x^{*}}
is the potential function at
x^{*}
. Moreover, the
\mathcal{W}
-entropy based at
x^{*}
is defined by

\mathcal{W}_{x^{*}}(\tau):=\int_{M}\tau(2\Delta f_{x^{*}}-|\nabla f_{x^{*}}|^{2}+\mathrm{R})+f_{x^{*}}-n\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-\tau}.

The following proposition follows from a direct calculation; see
[
HN14
]
and
[
Bam20a
, Section 5]
.

Proposition 2.8
.

For any
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
with
\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-\tau\in I
and
\mathrm{R}(\cdot,\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-\tau)\geq R_{\min}
, we have the following inequalities.

(i)

\displaystyle-\frac{n}{2\tau}+R_{\min}\leq\mathchoice{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu\tau}}{\displaystyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu\tau}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu\tau}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu\tau}}}\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}(\tau)\leq 0
.

(ii)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu\tau}}{\displaystyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu\tau}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu\tau}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu\tau}}}\left(\tau\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}(\tau)\right)=\mathcal{W}_{x^{*}}(\tau)\leq 0
.

(iii)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu^{2}}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu\tau^{2}}}{\displaystyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu^{2}}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu\tau^{2}}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu^{2}}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu\tau^{2}}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu^{2}}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu\tau^{2}}}}\left(\tau\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}(\tau)\right)=-2\tau\int_{M}\left\lvert\mathrm{Ric}+\nabla^{2}f_{x^{*}}-\frac{1}{2\tau}g\right\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-\tau}\leq 0
.

We also use the notation
\mathcal{N}_{s}^{*}(x^{*})=\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}(\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-s)
as in
[
Bam20a
, Section 5]
. The following result is from
[
Bam20a
, Corollary 5.11]
:

Proposition 2.9
.

For any
x_{1}^{*},x_{2}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
and
s<t\leq\min\{\mathfrak{t}(x_{1}^{*}),\mathfrak{t}(x_{2}^{*})\}
with
s\in I
and
\mathrm{R}(\cdot,s)\geq R_{\min}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{s}^{*}(x_{1}^{*})-\mathcal{N}_{s}^{*}(x_{2}^{*})\leq\left(\frac{n}{2(t-s)}-R_{\min}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}d_{W_{1}}^{t}(\nu_{x_{1}^{*};t},\nu_{x_{2}^{*};t})+\frac{n}{2}\log\left(\frac{\mathfrak{t}(x_{2}^{*})-s}{t-s}\right).

Definition 2.10
.

For
x^{*}=(x,t)\in\mathcal{X}
, the
curvature radius

r_{\mathrm{Rm}}
is defined to be the supremum over all
r>0
such that
|\mathrm{Rm}|\leq r^{-2}
on the parabolic ball
B_{t}(x,r)\times[t-r^{2},t+r^{2}]\cap I
.

The following
\epsilon
-regularity from
[
Bam20a
, Theorem 10.2]
will be useful later:

Theorem 2.11
.

There exists a dimensional constant
\epsilon_{n}>0
such that the following holds. If
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
satisfies
\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-r^{2}\in I
and
\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}(r^{2})\geq-\epsilon_{n}
, then
r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(x^{*})\geq\epsilon_{n}r
.

Now we recall some monotonicity formulas from
[
Bam20a
, Lemma 2.7, Corollary 3.7]
and their consequences (see also
[
MT10
]
and
[
Top14
]
).

Proposition 2.12
.

Let
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in C^{\infty}(M\times I^{\prime}),\,I^{\prime}\subset I
be two nonnegative solutions to conjugate heat equation
\square^{*}\nu_{1}=\square^{*}\nu_{2}=0
with
\int_{M}\nu_{i}(\cdot,t)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}=1
for
i=1
or
2
and any
t\in I^{\prime}
. If we set
\mathrm{d}\mu_{i,t}=\nu_{i}(\cdot,t)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}
, then

\displaystyle t\mapsto d_{W_{1}}^{t}(\mu_{1,t},\mu_{2,t})\quad\text{and}\quad t\mapsto\mathrm{Var}_{t}(\mu_{1,t},\mu_{2,t})+H_{n}t

are nondecreasing for
t\in I^{\prime}
, where
H_{n}:=(n-1)\pi^{2}/2+4
. In particular, for any
x^{*}_{1},x^{*}_{2}\in\mathcal{X}
,
d_{W_{1}}^{t}(\nu_{x^{*}_{1};t},\nu_{x^{*}_{2};t})
and
\mathrm{Var}_{t}(\nu_{x^{*}_{1};t},\nu_{x^{*}_{2};t})+H_{n}t
are nondecreasing for
t\in I
and
t\leq\min\{\mathfrak{t}(x_{1}^{*}),\mathfrak{t}(x_{2}^{*})\}
.

Definition 2.13
.

A point
(z,t)\in\mathcal{X}
is called an
H
-center
of
x_{0}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
for a constant
H>0
if
t\in I
,
t<\mathfrak{t}(x_{0}^{*})
and

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}_{t}(\delta_{z},\nu_{x_{0}^{*};t})\leq H(\mathfrak{t}(x_{0}^{*})-t).

By Proposition
2.12
, an
H_{n}
-center must exist for any
t\in I
with
t<\mathfrak{t}(x_{0}^{*})
.

We have the following result from
[
Bam20a
, Propositions 3.12, 3.13]
.

Proposition 2.14
.

Any two
H_{n}
-centers
(z_{1},t)
and
(z_{2},t)
of
x_{0}^{*}
satisfy
d_{t}(z_{1},z_{2})\leq 2\sqrt{H_{n}(\mathfrak{t}(x_{0}^{*})-t)}
. Moreover, if
(z,t)
is an
H_{n}
-center of
x_{0}^{*}
, then for any
L>0
, we have

\displaystyle\nu_{x^{*}_{0};t}\left(B_{t}\left(z,\sqrt{LH_{n}(\mathfrak{t}(x_{0}^{*})-t)}\right)\right)\geq 1-L^{-1}.

The following theorem gives a sharp upper bound of the heat kernel, which improves
[
Bam20a
, Theorem 7.2]
; see also
[
LW20
, Theorem 14]
and
[
LW24a
, Theorem 4.15]
.

Theorem 2.15
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in I}\}
be a closed Ricci flow with
[t,t_{0}]\subset I
. Then for any
\epsilon>0,L>0
and
(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
, the following statements hold.

(i)

We have

\displaystyle\nu_{x_{0},t_{0};t}\left(M\setminus B_{t}(z,L)\right)\leq C(n,\epsilon)\exp\left(-\frac{L^{2}}{(4+\epsilon)(t_{0}-t)}\right).

(2.5)

(ii)

If
\mathrm{R}(\cdot,t)\geq R_{\min}
, then for any
(y,t)\in\mathcal{X}
,

K(x_{0},t_{0};y,t)\leq\frac{C\left(n,R_{\min}(t_{0}-t),\epsilon\right)}{(t-s)^{n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{d_{t}^{2}(z,y)}{(4+\epsilon)(t_{0}-t)}-\mathcal{N}_{x_{0},t_{0}}(t_{0}-t)\right),

(2.6)

where
(z,t)
is any
H_{n}
-center of
(x_{0},t_{0})
.

Proof.

The upper bounds in (
2.5
) and (
2.6
) are similar to
[
Bam20a
, Theorems 3.14, 7.2]
but with the constant
8+\epsilon
replaced by
4+\epsilon
, which are sharp in general. For simplicity, we set
\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}=\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{0},t_{0};t}
and define the Laplace transform as

U_{t}(\lambda)=\sup\int_{M}e^{\lambda h}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t},

(2.7)

where the supremum runs over all bounded integrable
1
-Lipschitz functions
h
on
(M,g(t))
satisfying
\int_{M}h\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}=0
. By the proof of
[
HN14
, Theorem 1.30]
, the following bound for the Laplace transform holds:

U_{t}(\lambda)\leq e^{(t_{0}-t)\lambda^{2}}.

(2.8)

For every integrable
1
-Lipschitz function
F:M\to\mathbb{R}
and for every
r\geq 0
, applying
F-\int_{M}F\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}
in (
2.7
), we obtain from (
2.8
) that

\displaystyle\int_{M}e^{\lambda\left(F-\int_{M}F\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\right)}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\leq e^{(t_{0}-t)\lambda^{2}}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\nu_{t}\left(\left\{F\geq\int_{M}F\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}+r\right\}\right)\leq\inf_{\lambda>0}\left(e^{(t_{0}-t)\lambda^{2}-\lambda r}\right)=e^{-\frac{r^{2}}{4(t_{0}-t)}}.

Now we take
F(x)=d_{t}(x,z)
, where
(z,t)
is the
H_{n}
-center of
(x_{0},t_{0})
. Then

\nu_{t}\left(\left\{x\,\big|\,d_{t}(x,z)\geq\int_{M}d_{t}(z,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}+r\right\}\right)\leq e^{-\frac{r^{2}}{4(t_{0}-t)}}.

(2.9)

Recall that by definition of
H_{n}
-center,
\left(\int_{M}d^{2}_{t}(z,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\sqrt{H_{n}(t_{0}-t)}
, and thus by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, we have

\displaystyle\int_{M}d_{t}(z,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\leq\sqrt{H_{n}(t_{0}-t)}.

This implies
\{x\mid d_{t}(x,z)\geq\sqrt{H_{n}(t_{0}-t)}+r\}\subset\{x\mid d_{t}(x,z)\geq\int_{M}d_{t}(z,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}+r\}
. Combining with (
2.9
), we obtain

\nu_{t}\left(\left\{x\mid d_{t}(x,z)\geq\sqrt{H_{n}(t_{0}-t)}+r\right\}\right)\leq e^{-\frac{r^{2}}{4(t_{0}-t)}}.

(2.10)

For any
L>0
, (
2.10
) implies

\displaystyle\nu_{t}\left(M\setminus B_{t}(z,L)\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\left(L-\sqrt{H_{n}(t_{0}-t)}\right)_{+}^{2}}{4(t_{0}-t)}\right)\leq C(n,\epsilon)\exp\left(-\frac{L^{2}}{(4+\epsilon)(t_{0}-t)}\right),

which gives (
2.5
).

Now we can follow the argument in the proof of
[
Bam20a
, Theorem 7.2]
or
[
LW24a
, Theorem 4.15]
to conclude (
2.6
).
∎

We have the following gradient bound from
[
Bam20a
, Theorem 7.5]
:

Theorem 2.16
.

If
[s,t]\subset I
and
\mathrm{R}\geq R_{\min}
on
\mathcal{X}
, then there exists a constant
C=C(n,R_{\min}(t-s))<\infty
such that

\displaystyle\frac{|\nabla_{x}K|(x,t;y,s)}{K(x,t;y,s)}\leq\frac{C}{(t-s)^{1/2}}\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{C\exp\left(-\mathcal{N}_{x,t}(t-s)\right)}{(t-s)^{n/2}K(x,t;y,s)}\right)}.

We also need the following volume estimates from
[
Bam20a
, Theorems 6.1, 6.2, 8.1]
.

Proposition 2.17
.

Assume
[t-r^{2},t]\subset I
and
\mathrm{R}(\cdot,t-r^{2})\geq R_{\min}
.

(i)

For any
1\leq A<\infty
,

\displaystyle|B_{t}(x,Ar)|_{t}\leq C(n,R_{\min}r^{2})\exp\left(\mathcal{N}_{x,t}(r^{2})+C(n)A^{2}\right)r^{n}.

(ii)

If
(z,t-r^{2})
is an
H_{n}
-center of
(x,t)
, then

\displaystyle|B_{t-r^{2}}(z,\sqrt{2H_{n}}r)|_{t-r^{2}}\geq C(n,R_{\min}r^{2})\exp\left(\mathcal{N}_{x,t}(r^{2})\right)r^{n}>0.

(iii)

If
\mathrm{R}\leq r^{-2}
on
B_{t}(x,r)
, then

\displaystyle|B_{t}(x,r)|_{t}\geq C(n)\exp\left(\mathcal{N}_{x,t}(r^{2})\right)r^{n}>0.

Here,
\left\lvert\cdot\right\rvert_{t}
denotes the volume with respect to
g(t)
.

For later applications, we need the following
L^{p}
-Poincaré inequality, proved by
[
HN14
, Theorem 1.10]
and
[
Bam20a
, Theorem 11.1]
.

Theorem 2.18

(Poincaré inequality)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in I}\}
be a closed Ricci flow with
x_{0}^{*}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
. Suppose
\tau>0
with
t_{0}-\tau\in I
, and
h\in C^{1}(M)
with
\int_{M}h\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{0}^{*};t_{0}-\tau}=0
. Then for any
p\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\int_{M}|h|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{0}^{*};t_{0}-\tau}\leq C(p)\tau^{p/2}\int_{M}|\nabla h|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{0}^{*};t_{0}-\tau}.

Here, we can choose
C(1)=\sqrt{\pi}
and
C(2)=2
.

Also, we have the following hypercontractivity from
[
Bam20a
, Theorem 12.1]
.

Theorem 2.19

(Hypercontractivity)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in I}\}
be a closed Ricci flow with
x_{0}^{*}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
. Suppose
0<\tau_{1}<\tau_{2}
with
t_{0}-\tau_{2}\in I
, and
u\in C^{2}(M\times[t_{0}-\tau_{2},t_{0}-\tau_{1}])
is a solution to
\square u=0
or
u\geq 0
with
\square u\leq 0
. If
1<q\leq p<\infty
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{\tau_{2}}{\tau_{1}}\geq\frac{p-1}{q-1},

then we have

\displaystyle\left(\int_{M}|u|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{0}^{*};t_{0}-\tau_{1}}\right)^{1/p}\leq\left(\int_{M}|u|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{0}^{*};t_{0}-\tau_{2}}\right)^{1/q}.

In this paper, we mainly focus on the case where a Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}
has entropy bounded below. To formalize this, we introduce the following definition:

Definition 2.20
.

A closed Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in I}\}
is said to have entropy bounded below by
-Y
at
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
if

\displaystyle\inf_{\tau>0}\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}(\tau)\geq-Y,

(2.11)

where the infimum is taken over all
\tau>0
for which the Nash entropy
\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}(\tau)
is well-defined.

Moreover, we say that the Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}
has entropy bounded below by
-Y
if (
2.11
) holds for all
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
.

Under the assumption of a local scalar curvature bound, we have the following distance distortion estimates.

Proposition 2.21
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in I}\}
be a closed Ricci flow with entropy bounded below by
-Y
. Let
x^{*}=(x,t_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
with
[t_{0}-r^{2},t_{0}]\subset I
. For any constant
R_{0}>0
, there exists a constant
C=C(n,Y,R_{0})>0
such that the following statements hold.

(i)

Assume
|\mathrm{R}|\leq R_{0}r^{-2}
on
\{x\}\times[t_{0}-r^{2},t_{0}]
. If
(z,t)
is an
H_{n}
-center of
x^{*}
with
t\in[t_{0}-r^{2},t_{0}]
, then

\displaystyle d_{t}(x,z)\leq C\sqrt{t_{0}-t}.

(2.12)

(ii)

Assume
|\mathrm{R}|\leq R_{0}r^{-2}
on
\{x\}\times[t_{0}-r^{2},t_{0}]
and
\{y\}\times[t_{0}-r^{2},t_{0}]
. Then for any
t\in[t_{0}-r^{2},t_{0}]
,

\displaystyle d_{t}(x,y)\leq d_{t_{0}}(x,y)+C\sqrt{t_{0}-t}.

(iii)

Assume
|\mathrm{R}|\leq R_{0}r^{-2}
on
B_{t_{0}}(x,r)\times[t_{0}-r^{2},t_{0}+r^{2}]\cap I
. Then any
y\in M
with
d_{t}(x,y)\leq C^{-1}r
for some
t\in[t_{0}-C^{-1}r^{2},t_{0}+C^{-1}r^{2}]\cap I
satisfies

d_{t_{0}}(x,y)\leq r.

Proof.

(i): Equation (
2.12
) can be established using the same argument as in
[
LW23
, Proposition 4.4]
; see also
[
Jia23
, Proposition 3.1]
.

(ii): Let
(z_{1},t)
and
(z_{2},t)
be
H_{n}
-centers of
x^{*}
and
y^{*}:=(y,t_{0})
, respectively. From (
2.12
), we have

\displaystyle d_{t}(x,z_{1})+d_{t}(y,z_{2})\leq C(n,Y,R_{0})\sqrt{t_{0}-t}.

Thus, it follows from Proposition
2.12
that

\displaystyle d_{t}(x,y)\leq

\displaystyle d_{t}(z_{1},z_{2})+C(n,Y,R_{0})\sqrt{t_{0}-t}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{t}(\nu_{x^{*};t},\nu_{y^{*};t})+C(n,Y,R_{0})\sqrt{t_{0}-t}\leq d_{t_{0}}(x,y)+C(n,Y,R_{0})\sqrt{t_{0}-t}.

(iii): This follows from the local distance distortion from
[
BZ17
, Theorem 1.1]
; see also
[
CW20
, Lemma 4.21]
and
[
LW24a
, Lemma 5.8]
. It is worth noting that while
[
BZ17
, Theorem 1.1]
assumes a lower bound on Perelman’s
\nu
-entropy, this condition can, in fact, be relaxed. Applying Theorem
2.15
and Proposition
2.17
, we can verify that it suffices to assume only a lower bound on the Nash entropy.
∎

We also need the following integral estimates from
[
Bam20b
, Proposition 6.2]
.

Proposition 2.22
.

There exists a constant
\bar{\alpha}=\bar{\alpha}(n)>0
such that the following holds. Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in I}\}
be a closed Ricci flow. Suppose
x_{0}^{*}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
with
[t_{0}-2r^{2},t_{0}]\subset I
, and define
\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}=\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{0}^{*};t}=(4\pi\tau)^{-n/2}e^{-f}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}
, where
\tau=t_{0}-t
. Assume that
\mathcal{N}_{x_{0}^{*}}(2r^{2})\geq-Y
for some
r>0
. Then, for any
0<\theta\leq 1/2
and
\alpha\in[0,\bar{\alpha}]
, the following estimates hold:

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}-r^{2}}^{t_{0}-\theta r^{2}}\int_{M}\left(\tau|\mathrm{Ric}|^{2}+\tau|\nabla^{2}f|^{2}+|\nabla f|^{2}+\tau|\nabla f|^{4}+\tau^{-1}e^{\alpha f}+\tau^{-1}\right)e^{\alpha f}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C(n,Y)|\log\theta|,

\displaystyle\int_{M}\left(\tau|\mathrm{R}|+\tau|\Delta f|+\tau|\nabla f|^{2}+e^{\alpha f}+1\right)e^{\alpha f}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t_{0}-r^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,Y).

We end this subsection with the following two-sided pseudolocality from
[
Per02
, Theorem 10.1]
and
[
Bam20b
, Theorem 2.47]
:

Theorem 2.23

(Two-sided pseudolocality theorem)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in I}\}
be a closed Ricci flow. For any
\alpha>0
, there is an
\epsilon(n,\alpha)>0
such that the following holds.

Given
x_{0}^{*}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
and
r>0
with
[t_{0}-r^{2},t_{0}]\subset I
, if
|B_{t_{0}}(x_{0},r)|_{t_{0}}\geq\alpha r^{n}
and
|\mathrm{Rm}|\leq(\alpha r)^{-2}
on
B_{t_{0}}(x_{0},r)
, then

\displaystyle r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(x_{0}^{*})\geq\epsilon r.

3
Spacetime distance and Ricci flow limit spaces

We begin by fixing the time intervals. For a given constant
T\in(0,+\infty]
and a parameter
\sigma\in(0,1/100]
, define

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}^{-}=(-(1-2\sigma)T,0],\quad\mathbb{I}=[-(1-2\sigma)T,0],\quad\mathbb{I}^{+}=[-(1-\sigma)T,0],\quad\mathbb{I}^{++}=[-T,0].

If
T=+\infty
, we set
\mathbb{I}^{-}=\mathbb{I}=\mathbb{I}^{+}=\mathbb{I}^{++}=(-\infty,0]
.

Next, we prove the following lemma on the lower bound of
W_{1}
-distance, which will be used to define a spacetime
d^{*}
-distance:

Lemma 3.1

(Lower bound of
W_{1}
-distance)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}
be a closed Ricci flow with
x_{0}^{*}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in M\times\mathbb{I}^{+}
and
[t_{0}-r^{2},t_{0}]\subset\mathbb{I}^{+}
. Assume
\mathcal{N}_{x_{0}^{*}}(r^{2})\geq-Y
, then we can find a positive constant
c_{1}=c_{1}(n,Y,\sigma)
such that for any
x\in M
,

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{t_{0}-r^{2}}(\nu_{x_{0}^{*};t_{0}-r^{2}},\delta_{x})\geq c_{1}r.

Proof.

By Definition
2.1
,

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{t_{0}-r^{2}}(\nu_{x_{0}^{*};t_{0}-r^{2}},\delta_{x})=\int_{M}d_{t_{0}-r^{2}}(y,x)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{0}^{*};t_{0}-r^{2}}(y).

It follows from Proposition
2.17
(i) that for any
x\in M
,

\displaystyle|B_{t_{0}-r^{2}}(x,2\epsilon r)|\leq C_{1}(n,\sigma)\epsilon^{n}r^{n},

(3.1)

where
\epsilon>0
is a sufficiently small constant to be determined later. In addition, by Theorem
2.15
(ii), we have

\displaystyle K(x_{0},t_{0};y,t_{0}-r^{2})\leq C_{2}(n,\sigma)r^{-n}e^{-\mathcal{N}_{x_{0}^{*}}(r^{2})}\leq C_{2}(n,\sigma)r^{-n}e^{Y}.

Now we choose the constant
\epsilon=\epsilon(n,Y,\sigma)
such that

\epsilon^{n}C_{1}C_{2}e^{Y}\leq 1/2
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\nu_{x_{0}^{*};t_{0}-r^{2}}\big(B_{t_{0}-r^{2}}(x,2\epsilon r)\big)\leq C_{1}C_{2}r^{-n}e^{Y}\epsilon^{n}r^{n}\leq\frac{1}{2},

which implies that
\nu_{x_{0}^{*};t_{0}-r^{2}}\big(M\setminus B_{t_{0}-r^{2}}(x,2\epsilon r)\big)\geq 1/2
.
Thus, using (
3.1
), we obtain

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{t_{0}-r^{2}}(\nu_{x_{0}^{*};t_{0}-r^{2}},\delta_{x})

\displaystyle\geq\int_{M\setminus B_{t_{0}-r^{2}}(x,2\epsilon r)}d_{t_{0}-r^{2}}(y,x)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{0}^{*};t_{0}-r^{2}}(y)

\displaystyle\geq 2\epsilon r\nu_{x_{0}^{*};t_{0}-r^{2}}\big(M\setminus B_{t_{0}-r^{2}}(x,2\epsilon r)\big)\geq\epsilon r.

Therefore, the proof is complete.
∎

Definition 3.2

(Moduli space)
.

For fixed constants
T\in(0,+\infty]
and
Y>0
, the moduli space
\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
consists of all
n
-dimensional closed Ricci flows
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}
with entropy bounded by
-Y
(see Definition
2.20
).

Definition 3.3

(Spacetime distance constant)
.

The
spacetime distance constant

\epsilon_{0}=\epsilon_{0}(n,Y,\sigma)
is defined as the largest constant
c_{1}
in
[0,1]
such that the conclusion of Lemma
3.1
holds for all
\mathcal{X}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
.

Remark 3.4
.

For a closed
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
and
x_{0}^{*}\in M\times\mathbb{I}^{+}
, it follows from Lemma
3.1
that an
H
-center
z^{*}\in M\times\mathbb{I}^{+}
of
x_{0}^{*}
(see Definition
2.13
) exists only if
H\geq\epsilon_{0}^{2}
.

Next, we can define the spacetime distance on
M\times\mathbb{I}^{+}
.

Definition 3.5
.

For
\mathcal{X}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
, we define the distance
d^{*}
on
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
as follows. For any
x^{*}=(x,t),y^{*}=(y,s)\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
with
s\leq t
, we define

\displaystyle d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*}):=\inf_{r\in[\sqrt{t-s},\sqrt{t+(1-\sigma)T})}\left\{r\mid d_{W_{1}}^{t-r^{2}}(\nu_{x^{*};t-r^{2}},\nu_{y^{*};t-r^{2}})\leq\epsilon_{0}r\right\}.

(3.2)

If no such
r
exists, we define
d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*}):=\epsilon_{0}^{-1}d_{W_{1}}^{-(1-\sigma)T}(\nu_{x^{*};-(1-\sigma)T},\nu_{y^{*};-(1-\sigma)T})
. Here,
\epsilon_{0}\in(0,1]
is the spacetime distance constant, depending only on
n
,
Y
and
\sigma
.

Lemma 3.6
.

Assume
x^{*},y^{*}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
for
\mathcal{X}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
. Then for
t\in[-(1-\sigma)T,\min\{\mathfrak{t}(x^{*}),\mathfrak{t}(y^{*})\}]
,
t\mapsto d_{W_{1}}^{t}(\nu_{x^{*};t},\nu_{y^{*};t})
is continuous.

Proof.

Fix
t_{0}\in[-(1-\sigma)T,\min\{\mathfrak{t}(x^{*}),\mathfrak{t}(y^{*})\}]
. We first show

\displaystyle\lim_{t\searrow t_{0}}d_{W_{1}}^{t}(\nu_{x^{*};t},\nu_{y^{*};t})=d_{W_{1}}^{t_{0}}(\nu_{x^{*};t_{0}},\nu_{y^{*};t_{0}}).

(3.3)

If
t_{0}=\min\{\mathfrak{t}(x^{*}),\mathfrak{t}(y^{*})\}
, then (
3.3
) is immediate. Hence assume
t_{0}<\min\{\mathfrak{t}(x^{*}),\mathfrak{t}(y^{*})\}
. Since
t\mapsto d_{W_{1}}^{t}(\nu_{x^{*};t},\nu_{y^{*};t})
is increasing by Proposition
2.12
, if (
3.3
) fails, we can find, by Lemma
2.2
,
t_{i}\searrow t_{0}
and
f_{i}\in C^{1}(M)
with
|\nabla_{g(t_{i})}f_{i}|\leq 1
so that

\displaystyle\int_{M}f_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};t_{i}}-\int_{M}f_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{y^{*};t_{i}}\geq d_{W_{1}}^{t_{0}}(\nu_{x^{*};t_{0}},\nu_{y^{*};t_{0}})+\delta_{0}

(3.4)

for a constant
\delta_{0}>0
. Without loss of generality, we may assume
f_{i}(p)=0
for a fixed point
p\in M
. By taking a subsequence,
f_{i}
converges to a continuous function
f
on
M
with
\mathrm{Lip}_{g(t_{0})}f\leq 1
. Using the continuities of the conjugate heat kernel measures and the corresponding Riemannian metrics, we conclude from (
3.4
) that

\displaystyle\int_{M}f\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};t_{0}}-\int_{M}f\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{y^{*};t_{0}}\geq d_{W_{1}}^{t_{0}}(\nu_{x^{*};t_{0}},\nu_{y^{*};t_{0}})+\delta_{0}.

However, this contradicts Lemma
2.2
, and hence (
3.3
) holds.

Next, we show

\displaystyle\lim_{t\nearrow t_{0}}d_{W_{1}}^{t}(\nu_{x^{*};t},\nu_{y^{*};t})=d_{W_{1}}^{t_{0}}(\nu_{x^{*};t_{0}},\nu_{y^{*};t_{0}}).

(3.5)

For any
\epsilon>0
, it follows from Lemma
2.2
that there exists a continuous function
f
on
M
with
\mathrm{Lip}_{g(t_{0})}f\leq 1
such that

\displaystyle\int_{M}f\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};t_{0}}-\int_{M}f\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{y^{*};t_{0}}\geq d_{W_{1}}^{t_{0}}(\nu_{x^{*};t_{0}},\nu_{y^{*};t_{0}})-\epsilon.

By the continuities of the conjugate heat kernel measures and the corresponding Riemannian metrics, we conclude

\displaystyle\lim_{t\nearrow t_{0}}d_{W_{1}}^{t}(\nu_{x^{*};t},\nu_{y^{*};t})\geq

\displaystyle\lim_{t\nearrow t_{0}}\left(\int_{M}f\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};t}-\int_{M}f\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{y^{*};t}\right)\geq d_{W_{1}}^{t_{0}}(\nu_{x^{*};t_{0}},\nu_{y^{*};t_{0}})-\epsilon.

Since
t\mapsto d_{W_{1}}^{t}(\nu_{x^{*};t},\nu_{y^{*};t})
is increasing and
\epsilon
is arbitrary, the proof of (
3.5
) is complete.
∎

By Definition
3.5
and Lemma
3.6
, we conclude that for
\mathcal{X}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
and
x^{*},y^{*}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
with
d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*})=r
, then

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{t-r^{2}}(\nu_{x^{*};t-r^{2}},\nu_{y^{*};t-r^{2}})=\epsilon_{0}r\quad

\displaystyle\text{if}\quad t-r^{2}>-(1-\sigma)T,

(3.6)

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{-(1-\sigma)T}(\nu_{x^{*};-(1-\sigma)T},\nu_{y^{*};-(1-\sigma)T})=\epsilon_{0}r\quad

\displaystyle\text{if}\quad t-r^{2}\leq-(1-\sigma)T,

(3.7)

where
t=\max\{\mathfrak{t}(x^{*}),\mathfrak{t}(y^{*})\}
.

Lemma 3.7
.

For any
\mathcal{X}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
,
d^{*}
defines a distance function on
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
.

Proof.

If
x^{*}=(x,t),y^{*}=(y,s)
satisfy
d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*})=0
, then by Definition
3.5
,
t=s
and thus by (
3.6
) and (
3.7
), we must have
x=y
, which implies
x^{*}=y^{*}
.

To finish the proof, it suffices to verify the triangle inequality. We take
x_{i}^{*}=(x_{i},t_{i})\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
for
i=1,2,3
. Without loss of generality, we assume
t_{1}\geq t_{2}\geq t_{3}
and set
r=d^{*}(x_{1}^{*},x_{2}^{*})
,
s=d^{*}(x_{2}^{*},x_{3}^{*})
and
l=d^{*}(x_{1}^{*},x_{3}^{*})
.

First, we prove
l\leq r+s
. Note that if
t_{1}-(r+s)^{2}\geq-(1-\sigma)T
, the conclusion follows. Indeed, since
t_{1}-(r+s)^{2}\leq\min\{t_{1}-r^{2},t_{2}-s^{2}\}
, by Proposition
2.12
, we have

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{t_{1}-(r+s)^{2}}(\nu_{x_{1}^{*};t_{1}-(r+s)^{2}},\nu_{x_{3}^{*};t_{1}-(r+s)^{2}})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{t_{1}-(r+s)^{2}}(\nu_{x_{1}^{*};t_{1}-(r+s)^{2}},\nu_{x_{2}^{*};t_{1}-(r+s)^{2}})+d_{W_{1}}^{t_{1}-(r+s)^{2}}(\nu_{x_{2}^{*};t_{1}-(r+s)^{2}},\nu_{x_{3}^{*};t_{1}-(r+s)^{2}})\leq\epsilon_{0}(r+s).

Therefore, by the definition of
d^{*}
,
l\leq r+s
. On the other hand,
suppose
l>r+s
and
t_{1}-(r+s)^{2}<-(1-\sigma)T
, we conclude that

\displaystyle l=

\displaystyle\epsilon_{0}^{-1}d_{W_{1}}^{-(1-\sigma)T}(\nu_{x_{1}^{*};-(1-\sigma)T},\nu_{x_{3}^{*};-(1-\sigma)T})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\epsilon_{0}^{-1}d_{W_{1}}^{-(1-\sigma)T}(\nu_{x_{1}^{*};-(1-\sigma)T},\nu_{x_{2}^{*};-(1-\sigma)T})+\epsilon_{0}^{-1}d_{W_{1}}^{-(1-\sigma)T}(\nu_{x_{2}^{*};-(1-\sigma)T},\nu_{x_{3}^{*};-(1-\sigma)T})\leq r+s.

This gives a contradiction, and thus we have proved
l\leq r+s
.

Next, we prove
r\leq l+s
. If
t_{1}-(l+s)^{2}\geq-(1-\sigma)T
, then since
t_{1}-(l+s)^{2}\leq t_{3}+l^{2}-(l+s)^{2}\leq t_{2}-s^{2}
, we obtain
t_{1}-(l+s)^{2}\leq\min\{t_{1}-l^{2},t_{2}-s^{2}\}
. Thus, by Proposition
2.12
, we have

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{t_{1}-(l+s)^{2}}(\nu_{x_{1}^{*};t_{1}-(l+s)^{2}},\nu_{x_{2}^{*};t_{1}-(l+s)^{2}})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{t_{1}-(l+s)^{2}}(\nu_{x_{1}^{*};t_{1}-(l+s)^{2}},\nu_{x_{3}^{*};t_{1}-(l+s)^{2}})+d_{W_{1}}^{t_{1}-(l+s)^{2}}(\nu_{x_{2}^{*};t_{1}-(l+s)^{2}},\nu_{x_{3}^{*};t_{1}-(l+s)^{2}})\leq\epsilon_{0}(l+s),

which implies
r\leq l+s
. If
t_{1}-(l+s)^{2}\leq-(1-\sigma)T
and
r>l+s
, then

\displaystyle r=

\displaystyle\epsilon_{0}^{-1}d_{W_{1}}^{-(1-\sigma)T}(\nu_{x_{1}^{*};-(1-\sigma)T},\nu_{x_{2}^{*};-(1-\sigma)T})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\epsilon_{0}^{-1}d_{W_{1}}^{-(1-\sigma)T}(\nu_{x_{1}^{*};-(1-\sigma)T},\nu_{x_{3}^{*};-(1-\sigma)T})+\epsilon_{0}^{-1}d_{W_{1}}^{-(1-\sigma)T}(\nu_{x_{2}^{*};-(1-\sigma)T},\nu_{x_{3}^{*};-(1-\sigma)T})\leq l+s,

which gives a contradiction, and thus we have proved
r\leq l+s
.

Finally, we prove
s\leq r+l
. If
t_{2}-(r+l)^{2}\geq-(1-\sigma)T
, then using the fact that
t_{2}-(r+l)^{2}\leq\min\{t_{1}-r^{2},t_{1}-l^{2}\}
, by Proposition
2.12
, we have

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{t_{2}-(r+l)^{2}}(\nu_{x_{2}^{*};t_{2}-(r+l)^{2}},\nu_{x_{3}^{*};t_{2}-(r+l)^{2}})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{t_{2}-(r+l)^{2}}(\nu_{x_{1}^{*};t_{2}-(r+l)^{2}},\nu_{x_{2}^{*};t_{2}-(r+l)^{2}})+d_{W_{1}}^{t_{2}-(r+l)^{2}}(\nu_{x_{1}^{*};t_{1}-(r+l)^{2}},\nu_{x_{3}^{*};t_{2}-(r+l)^{2}})\leq\epsilon_{0}(r+l),

which implies
s\leq r+l
. If
t_{2}-(r+l)^{2}\leq-(1-\sigma)T
and
s>r+l
, then by Proposition
2.12
,

\displaystyle s=

\displaystyle\epsilon_{0}^{-1}d_{W_{1}}^{-(1-\sigma)T}(\nu_{x_{2}^{*};-(1-\sigma)T},\nu_{x_{3}^{*};-(1-\sigma)T})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\epsilon_{0}^{-1}d_{W_{1}}^{-(1-\sigma)T}(\nu_{x_{1}^{*};-(1-\sigma)T},\nu_{x_{2}^{*};-(1-\sigma)T})+\epsilon_{0}^{-1}d_{W_{1}}^{-(1-\sigma)T}(\nu_{x_{1}^{*};-(1-\sigma)T},\nu_{x_{3}^{*};-(1-\sigma)T})\leq r+l,

which gives a contradiction, and hence we have proved
s\leq r+l
.
∎

With
d^{*}
-distance, we can define the
d^{*}
-balls as follows:

Definition 3.8

(
d^{*}
-balls)
.

For any
\mathcal{X}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
,
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
and
r>0
, we define

\displaystyle B^{*}(x^{*},r):=\{y^{*}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}\mid d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*})<r\}.

In particular, it follows from (
3.6
), (
3.7
) and Proposition
2.12
that for any
y^{*}\in B^{*}(x^{*},r)
,

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{\max\{\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-r^{2},\mathfrak{t}(y^{*})-r^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}}\left(\nu_{x^{*};\max\{\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-r^{2},\mathfrak{t}(y^{*})-r^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}},\nu_{y^{*};\max\{\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-r^{2},\mathfrak{t}(y^{*})-r^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}}\right)<\epsilon_{0}r.

(3.8)

Example 3.9
.

Let
\mathcal{X}^{\mathbb{E}}=\{\mathbb{R}^{n},(g(t)=g_{E})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\}
be the standard static Ricci flow on the Euclidean space. We denote by
d^{*}_{E,\epsilon_{0}}
the spacetime distance, defined with respect to a spacetime distance constant
\epsilon_{0}=\epsilon_{0}(n,Y,\sigma)
.

Given
x^{*}=(x,0),y^{*}=(y,0)\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathbb{E}}
,
d^{*}_{E,\epsilon_{0}}(x^{*},y^{*})=\epsilon_{0}^{-1}|x-y|
. To see this, for
t<0
,

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{t}(\nu_{x^{*};t},\nu_{y^{*};t})

\displaystyle=\sup_{f\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\ \rVert f\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip}}\leq 1}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(z)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};t}(z)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(z)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{y^{*};t}(z)\right)

\displaystyle=(4\pi)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\sup_{f\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\ \rVert f\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip}}\leq 1}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(f(x+|t|z)-f(y+|t|z)\right)e^{-\frac{|z|^{2}}{4}}\,\mathrm{d}z\right)

\displaystyle=(4\pi)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|x-y|e^{-\frac{|z|^{2}}{4}}\,\mathrm{d}z=|x-y|,

where, to obtain the third equality, we need to choose the test function
f
to be an appropriate linear function with a cutoff. Thus, by definition,

\displaystyle d^{*}_{E,\epsilon_{0}}(x^{*},y^{*})=\epsilon_{0}^{-1}|x-y|.

Although an explicit formula for the
d^{*}
-distance between arbitrary spacetime points of
\mathcal{X}^{\mathbb{E}}
is difficult to obtain, we show that
d^{*}
is comparable to the standard parabolic distance. More generally, we have:

Proposition 3.10
.

Given
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
, suppose
r\in(0,\sqrt{T})
and
|\mathrm{R}|\leq R_{0}r^{-2}
on
P(x^{*},r)
. Then there exist constants
\rho_{1}=\rho_{1}(n,Y,R_{0})\in(0,1)
and
\rho_{2}=\rho_{2}(n,Y,R_{0},\sigma)\in(0,1)
such that

P(x^{*},\rho_{1}\epsilon_{0}r)\subset B^{*}(x^{*},r)\quad\text{and}\quad B^{*}(x^{*},\rho_{2}r)\subset P(x^{*},r).

(3.9)

Here,
P(x^{*},s):=B_{t_{0}}(x,s)\times[t_{0}-s^{2},t_{0}+s^{2}]\bigcap\mathbb{I}^{+}
and
x^{*}=(x,t_{0})
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
r=1
.

First, we show that for any
l\in(0,1)
, if
y^{*}=(y,s)\in P(x^{*},l)
, then
d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*})\leq C(n,Y,R_{0})\epsilon_{0}^{-1}l
. This will give the first inclusion in (
3.9
).

We set
t_{1}=\max\{-(1-\sigma)T,t_{0}-l^{2}\}
and choose an
H_{n}
-center
(z,t_{1})
of
x^{*}
. By Theorem
2.15
(i) and Proposition
2.21
(i), we know that

\displaystyle\int_{M}d_{t_{1}}(x,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};t_{1}}\leq

\displaystyle C(n,Y,R_{0})\sqrt{t_{0}-t_{1}}+\int_{M}d_{t_{1}}(z,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};t_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C(n,Y,R_{0})\sqrt{t_{0}-t_{1}}+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\int_{\{k\sqrt{t_{0}-t_{1}}\leq d_{t_{1}}(z,\cdot)\leq(k+1)\sqrt{t_{0}-t_{1}}\}}d_{t_{1}}(z,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};t_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(n,Y,R_{0})\sqrt{t_{0}-t_{1}}+\sqrt{t_{0}-t_{1}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(k+1)\nu_{x^{*};t_{1}}\left(\{d_{t_{1}}(z,\cdot)\geq k\sqrt{t_{0}-t_{1}}\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(n,Y,R_{0})\sqrt{t_{0}-t_{1}}+C(n)\sqrt{t_{0}-t_{1}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(k+1)e^{-\frac{k^{2}}{5}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(n,Y,R_{0})\sqrt{t_{0}-t_{1}}\leq C(n,Y,R_{0})l.

(3.10)

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\int_{M}d_{t_{1}}(y,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{y^{*};t_{1}}\leq C(n,Y,R_{0})l.

(3.11)

Now, by Definition
2.1
, we estimate

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{t_{1}}(\nu_{x^{*};t_{1}},\nu_{y^{*};t_{1}})

\displaystyle\leq\int_{M}\int_{M}d_{t_{1}}(z_{1},z_{2})\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};t_{1}}(z_{1})\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{y^{*};t_{1}}(z_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\int_{M}\int_{M}\big(d_{t_{1}}(z_{1},x)+d_{t_{1}}(z_{2},y)+d_{t_{1}}(x,y)\big)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};t_{1}}(z_{1})\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{y^{*};t_{1}}(z_{2})\leq C(n,Y,R_{0})l,

where in the last inequality, we have used (
3
), (
3.11
), and the fact that
d_{t_{1}}(x,y)\leq C(n,Y,R_{0})l
by Proposition
2.21
(ii).

By Definition
3.5
, this gives

\displaystyle d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*})\leq C(n,Y,R_{0})\epsilon_{0}^{-1}l.

Next, we prove the second inclusion in (
3.9
). Given
y^{*}=(y,s)\in B^{*}(x^{*},\rho)
, where
\rho=\rho(n,Y,R_{0},\sigma)\in(0,1)
is a constant to be determined later.

We set
t_{2}:=\max\{-(1-\sigma)T,t_{0}-\rho^{2}\}
. Then, by our assumption and (
3.8
), we have

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{t_{2}}(\nu_{x^{*};t_{2}},\nu_{y^{*};t_{2}})<\epsilon_{0}\rho\leq\rho.

(3.12)

Set
(z,t_{2})
to be an
H_{n}
-center of
y^{*}
, then it follows from Proposition
2.21
(i) and (
3.12
) that

\displaystyle d_{t_{2}}(x,z)\leq C(n,Y,R_{0})\rho.

Thus, it follows from the same argument of
[
LW24a
, Proposition 5.13]
that if
\rho\leq\rho(n,Y,R_{0},\sigma)
, then
d_{t_{0}}(x,y)<1
, which finishes the proof.
∎

An immediate consequence of Proposition
3.10
is the following:

Corollary 3.11
.

Given
\mathcal{X}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
, the topology on
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
induced by the
d^{*}
-distance agrees with the standard topology.

Proposition 3.12
.

\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
, the following properties hold:

(1)

For any
x,y\in M
and
t\in\mathbb{I}^{+}
,
d^{*}\big((x,t),(y,t)\big)\leq\epsilon_{0}^{-1}d_{t}(x,y)
;

(2)

For any
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
,
\mathfrak{t}(B^{*}(x^{*},r))\subset(t-r^{2},t+r^{2})\bigcap\mathbb{I}^{+}
. Moreover, the time-function
\mathfrak{t}
is a
2
-Hölder function, i.e. for any
x^{*},y^{*}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
,

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-\mathfrak{t}(y^{*})|\leq d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*})^{2}.

Proof.

For (1), let
r=d^{*}\left((x,t),(y,t)\right)
. If
t-r^{2}>-(1-\sigma)T
, then by (
3.6
),

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{t-r^{2}}(\nu_{x,t;t-r^{2}},\nu_{y,t;t-r^{2}})=\epsilon_{0}r.

Thus, by Proposition
2.12
,

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{t-r^{2}}(\nu_{x,t;t-r^{2}},\nu_{y,t;t-r^{2}})\leq d_{W_{1}}^{t}(\delta_{x},\delta_{y})=d_{t}(x,y),

which implies
d^{*}\left((x,t),(y,t)\right)=r\leq\epsilon_{0}^{-1}d_{t}(x,y)
. The case
t-r^{2}\leq-(1-\sigma)T
can be proved similarly.

(2) follows directly from Definition
3.5
.
∎

Next, we prove

Lemma 3.13
.

Given
\mathcal{X}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
, the
d^{*}
-distance on
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
is complete.

Proof.

We set
\mathcal{X}=\{M,(g(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}
. Given a Cauchy sequence
x_{i}^{*}=(x_{i},t_{i})\in M\times\mathbb{I}^{+}
with respect to
d^{*}
, it follows from Proposition
3.12
(2) that
\{t_{i}\}
is a Cauchy sequence in
\mathbb{R}
. Without loss of generality, we assume
t_{i}\to t_{\infty}\in\mathbb{I}^{+}
.

Moreover, since
M
is closed, we can take a subsequence (if necessary) such that
x_{i}\to x_{\infty}
with respect to
g(0)
. Then, by Corollary
3.11
, we conclude that
x_{i}^{*}
converges to
(x_{\infty},t_{\infty})
with respect to
d^{*}
.
∎

Next, we recall the following definition of the parabolic neighborhoods in
[
Bam20a
, Definition 9.2]
, slightly adapted to our setting.

Definition 3.14

(
P^{*}
-neighborhoods)
.

For any
\mathcal{X}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
,
x^{*}=(x,t)\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
,
A,T^{+},T^{-}\geq 0
,
P^{*}(x,t;A,-T^{-},T^{+})\subset\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
is defined as the set of points
y^{*}=(y,s)\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
with
s\in[t-T^{-},t+T^{+}]\cap\mathbb{I}^{+}
and

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{\max\{t-T^{-},-(1-\sigma)T\}}\big(\nu_{x^{*};\max\{t-T^{-},-(1-\sigma)T\}},\nu_{y^{*};\max\{t-T^{-},-(1-\sigma)T\}}\big)<A.

Moreover, we set
P^{*}(x^{*};r)=P^{*}(x,t;r,-r^{2},r^{2})
.

The following proposition shows that
P^{*}
-neighborhoods are essentially equivalent to
d^{*}
-balls:

Proposition 3.15
.

For
x^{*}=(x,t)\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
and
r>0
,

P^{*}(x,t;\epsilon_{0}r,-r^{2}/2,r^{2}/2)\subset B^{*}\left(x^{*},r\right)\subset P^{*}(x,t;\epsilon_{0}r,-r^{2},r^{2}).

In particular,

P^{*}(x^{*};\epsilon_{0}r)\subset B^{*}\left(x^{*},r\right)\subset P^{*}(x^{*};r).

Proof.

Given
y^{*}=(y,s)\in B^{*}(x^{*},r)
. If
s\leq t
, then by (
3.8
),

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{\max\{t-r^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}}\left(\nu_{x^{*};\max\{t-r^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}},\nu_{y^{*};\max\{t-r^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}}\right)<\epsilon_{0}r.

Thus,
(y,s)\in P^{*}(x,t;\epsilon_{0}r,-r^{2},0).
If
s\geq t
, then by (
3.8
) again,

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{\max\{s-r^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}}\left(\nu_{x^{*};\max\{s-r^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}},\nu_{y^{*};\max\{s-r^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}}\right)<\epsilon_{0}r.

Since
t-r^{2}\leq s-r^{2}\leq t
, by Proposition
2.12
, we have

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{\max\{t-r^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}}(\nu_{x^{*};\max\{t-r^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}},\nu_{y^{*};\max\{t-r^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{\max\{s-r^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}}(\nu_{x^{*};\max\{s-r^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}},\nu_{y^{*};\max\{s-r^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}})<\epsilon_{0}r,

which implies
y^{*}\in P^{*}(x,t;\epsilon_{0}r,-r^{2},r^{2}).

Combining the above two cases, we conclude that

\displaystyle B^{*}\left(x^{*},r\right)\subset P^{*}(x,t;\epsilon_{0}r,-r^{2},r^{2}).

On the other hand, for
y^{*}=(y,s)\in P^{*}(x,t;\epsilon_{0}r,-r^{2}/2,r^{2}/2)
with
s\leq t
, we have

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{\max\{t-r^{2}/2,-(1-\sigma)T\}}\left(\nu_{x^{*};\max\{t-r^{2}/2,-(1-\sigma)T\}},\nu_{y^{*};\max\{t-r^{2}/2,-(1-\sigma)T\}}\right)<\epsilon_{0}r.

(3.13)

Then, by Definition
3.5
, we obtain
d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*})<r
.

For
y^{*}=(y,s)\in P^{*}(x,t;\epsilon_{0}r,-r^{2}/2,r^{2}/2)
with
s\geq t
, since
s\leq t+r^{2}/2
, it follows from (
3.13
) and Proposition
2.12
that

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{\max\{s-r^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}}\left(\nu_{x^{*};\max\{s-r^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}},\nu_{y^{*};\max\{s-r^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}}\right)<\epsilon_{0}r,

which also implies
d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*})<r
. Thus, we have proved
P^{*}(x,t;\epsilon_{0}r,-r^{2}/2,r^{2}/2)\subset B^{*}(x^{*},r)
.
∎

Lemma 3.16
.

For
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
and
s\in[-(1-\sigma)T,\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})]
, let
z^{*}=(z,s)
be an
H
-center of
x^{*}
. Then

\displaystyle d^{*}(x^{*},z^{*})\leq\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\sqrt{H(\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-s)}.

Proof.

By the definition of an
H
-center (see Definition
2.13
),

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{s}(\nu_{x^{*};s},\delta_{z})\leq\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}_{s}(\nu_{x^{*};s},\delta_{z})}\leq\sqrt{H(\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-s)}.

By Proposition
2.12
, we have

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{\max\{-(1-\sigma)T,t_{1}\}}\big(\nu_{x^{*};\max\{-(1-\sigma)T,t_{1}\}},\nu_{z^{*};\max\{-(1-\sigma)T,t_{1}\}}\big)\leq\sqrt{H(\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-t_{1})},

where
t_{1}:=\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-\epsilon_{0}^{-2}H(\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-s)
, since
t_{1}\leq s
by Remark
3.4
. From this, the conclusion easily follows.
∎

Proposition 3.17
.

For
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
and
r>0
with
\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-r^{2}\geq-(1-\sigma)T
, the following conclusion holds.

(i)

For any
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\left\lvert B^{*}(x^{*},r)\bigcap M\times\{t\}\right\rvert_{t}\leq C(n,\sigma)r^{n},

where
|\cdot|_{t}
denotes the volume with respect to
dV_{g(t)}
.

(ii)

We have

0<c(n,Y,\sigma)r^{n+2}\leq|B^{*}(x^{*},r)|\leq C(n,\sigma)r^{n+2},

where
|\cdot|
denotes the spacetime volume with respect to
dV_{g(t)}\mathrm{d}t
.

Proof.

The conclusion in (i) and the upper bound in (ii) follow from
[
Bam20a
, Theorem 9.8]
and Proposition
3.15
.

For the lower bound in (ii), we set
t=\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})
and take any
s
with
r/2\leq 3\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\sqrt{H_{n}}s\leq r
. Moreover, we assume
z^{*}=(z,t-s^{2})
is an
H_{n}
-center of
x^{*}
. By Proposition
2.17
(ii), we have

\displaystyle\big|B_{t-s^{2}}(z,\sqrt{2H_{n}}s)\big|_{t-s^{2}}\geq C(R_{\min}s^{2})\exp\left(\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}(s^{2})\right)s^{n},

where
\mathrm{R}(\cdot,t-s^{2})\geq R_{\min}
. Since
\mathcal{X}
is defined on
\mathbb{I}^{++}=[-T,0]
and
s^{2}\leq(1-\sigma)T
, it follows from (
2.4
) and the assumption on the entropy that

\displaystyle\big|B_{t-s^{2}}(z,\sqrt{2H_{n}}s)\big|_{t-s^{2}}\geq c(n,Y,\sigma)s^{n}>0.

(3.14)

By Lemma
3.16
, we have

\displaystyle d^{*}(x^{*},z^{*})\leq\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\sqrt{H_{n}}s.

In addition, by Proposition
3.12
(1), we conclude that

\displaystyle B_{t-s^{2}}(z,\sqrt{2H_{n}}s)\subset B^{*}\big(z^{*},2\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\sqrt{H_{n}}s\big)\subset B^{*}\big(x^{*},3\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\sqrt{H_{n}}s\big).

Combining with (
3.14
), we get
