\displaystyle\big|B^{*}\big(x^{*},r\big)\bigcap\mathcal{X}_{t-s^{2}}\big|_{t-s^{2}}\geq\big|B^{*}\big(x^{*},3\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\sqrt{H_{n}}s\big)\bigcap\mathcal{X}_{t-s^{2}}\big|_{t-s^{2}}\geq\big|B_{t-s^{2}}(z,\sqrt{2H_{n}}s)\big|_{t-s^{2}}\geq c(n,Y,\sigma)s^{n}.

Consequently, the conclusion follows by integrating
s
with
r/2\leq 3\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\sqrt{H_{n}}s\leq r
.
∎

Proposition 3.18
.

For any
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
and
L>0
, the following statements hold.

(i)

If
T<\infty
, we have

\displaystyle|B^{*}(x^{*},L\sqrt{T})|\leq C(n,\sigma,L)T^{\frac{n}{2}+1}.

(ii)

If
T=+\infty
, we have

\displaystyle|B^{*}(x^{*},L)|\leq C(n)L^{n+2}.

Proof.

We prove only (i), as the proof of (ii) follows by a similar argument.

For any
y^{*}\in B^{*}(x^{*},L\sqrt{T})
, it follows from Proposition
2.12
and Definition
3.5
that

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{-(1-\sigma)T}(\nu_{x^{*};-(1-\sigma)T},\nu_{y^{*};-(1-\sigma)T})\leq\epsilon_{0}L\sqrt{T}.

Let
(z_{0},-(1-\sigma)T)
and
(z,-(1-\sigma)T)
be
H_{n}
-centers of
x^{*}
and
y^{*}
, respectively. Then,

\displaystyle d_{-(1-\sigma)T}(z_{0},z)\leq\epsilon_{0}L\sqrt{T}+2\sqrt{H_{n}T}\leq C_{1}(n,L)\sqrt{T}.

For the ball
B=B_{-(1-\sigma)T}(z_{0},2C_{1}\sqrt{T})
, we have
\nu_{y^{*};-(1-\sigma)T}(B)\geq 1/2
by Proposition
2.14
. Let
u
be the solution to the heat equation with
u=\chi_{B}
at
t=-(1-\sigma)T
, then we have
u(y^{*})\geq 1/2
. Thus we obtain that for any
t\geq-(1-\sigma)T
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\big|B^{*}(x^{*},L\sqrt{T})\cap\mathcal{X}_{t}\big|_{t}\leq\int_{M}u(\cdot,t)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}\leq C(n,\sigma)|B|_{-(1-\sigma)T}.

(3.15)

Here, the second inequality holds since
\mathrm{R}\geq-\frac{n}{2\sigma T}
on
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
and hence for
t\geq-(1-\sigma)T
,

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}{\displaystyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}\int_{M}u\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}=-\int_{M}u\mathrm{R}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}\leq\frac{n}{2\sigma T}\int_{M}u\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}.

Thus, for
t\geq-(1-\sigma)T
,
\int_{M}u(\cdot,t)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}\leq C(n,\sigma)|B|_{-(1-\sigma)T}
.

By Proposition
2.17
(i), we have

\displaystyle|B|_{-(1-\sigma)T}\leq C(n,\sigma,L)T^{\frac{n}{2}}.

Therefore, the conclusion follows by integrating (
3.15
) on
t\in\mathbb{I}^{+}
.
∎

Next, we prove the Lipschitz property of the Nash entropy with respect to the
d^{*}
-distance.

Proposition 3.19
.

Given
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
and
s\in[-(1-\sigma)T,0)
,
\mathcal{N}_{s}^{*}(\cdot)
is locally uniformly Lipschitz on
\mathcal{X}_{(s,0]}
with respect to
d^{*}
in the sense that for any
x^{*},y^{*}\in\mathcal{X}_{(s,0]}
with
r:=d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*})
and
\max\{\mathfrak{t}(x^{*}),\mathfrak{t}(y^{*})\}-r^{2}\geq s/2
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{N}_{s}^{*}(x^{*})-\mathcal{N}_{s}^{*}(y^{*})\right\rvert\leq Cr,

(3.16)

where
C=C(n,s,\sigma T)
. In particular, if
T=+\infty
, we can choose
C=2n/\sqrt{|s|}
.

Proof.

We set
t_{1}:=\max\{\mathfrak{t}(x^{*}),\mathfrak{t}(y^{*})\}
. By our assumption,
t_{1}-r^{2}>s/2
, which implies by (
3.6
) that

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{t_{1}-r^{2}}\left(\nu_{x^{*};t_{1}-r^{2}},\nu_{y^{*};t_{1}-r^{2}}\right)=\epsilon_{0}r\leq r.

Then, it follows from Proposition
2.9
that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{N}_{s}^{*}(x^{*})-\mathcal{N}_{s}^{*}(y^{*})\right\rvert\leq

\displaystyle\left(\frac{n}{2(t_{1}-r^{2}-s)}-R_{\min}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}d_{W_{1}}^{t_{1}-r^{2}}(\nu_{x^{*};t_{1}-r^{2}},\nu_{y^{*};t_{1}-r^{2}})+\frac{n}{2}\log\left(\frac{t_{1}-s}{t_{1}-r^{2}-s}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\frac{n}{2(t_{1}-r^{2}-s)}-R_{\min}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}r+\frac{n}{2}\frac{r^{2}}{t_{1}-r^{2}-s}.

By our assumption, we have
t_{1}-r^{2}-s\geq|s|/2
and
r^{2}\leq|s|/2
. Thus, (
3.16
) holds. The last conclusion follows since
R_{\min}=0
.
∎

Similarly, we have

Proposition 3.20
.

Given
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
, for any
x^{*},y^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
, we assume
t_{1}=\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})\geq\mathfrak{t}(y^{*})=t_{2}
and
r=d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*})
. Suppose
t_{2}-\tau>-(1-\sigma)T
and
t_{1}-r^{2}\geq(t_{2}-\tau)/2
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}(\tau)-\mathcal{N}_{y^{*}}(\tau)\right\rvert\leq Cr

where
C=C(n,\tau,\sigma T)
. In particular, if
T=+\infty
, we can choose
C=C(n)/\sqrt{\tau}
.

Proof.

We set
s=t_{2}-\tau
. By Proposition
3.19
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{N}_{s}^{*}(x^{*})-\mathcal{N}_{s}^{*}(y^{*})\right\rvert\leq C(n,s,\sigma T)r=C(n,\tau,\sigma T)r.

(3.17)

On the other hand, if we set
\tau_{1}=\tau+t_{1}-t_{2}
, then it follows from Proposition
2.8
(i) that

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}(\tau)\geq\mathcal{N}_{s}^{*}(x^{*})\geq\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}(\tau)-\frac{n}{2}\log\left(\frac{\tau_{1}}{\tau}\left(1-\frac{2}{n}R_{\min}(\tau_{1}-\tau)\right)\right).

Since
\tau\leq\tau_{1}\leq\tau+r^{2}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}(\tau)-\mathcal{N}_{s}^{*}(x^{*})\right\rvert\leq C(n,\tau,\sigma T)r.

Combined with (
3.17
), we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}(\tau)-\mathcal{N}_{y^{*}}(\tau)\right\rvert\leq Cr.

The case
T=+\infty
can be proved similarly. Thus, the proof is complete.
∎

Next, we prove the Lipschitz property of the curvature radius (see Definition
2.10
) with respect to
d^{*}
.

Proposition 3.21
.

There exists a constant
C=C(n,Y)>0
such that for
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
, we have

\displaystyle|r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(x^{*})-r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(y^{*})|\leq Cd^{*}(x^{*},y^{*}),\quad\forall x^{*},y^{*}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}}.

Proof.

We set
x^{*}=(x,t),y^{*}=(y,s)
, and assume without loss of generality that
d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*})=1
. It suffices to show that

\displaystyle r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(x^{*})\leq L+r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(y^{*}),

(3.18)

where the constant
L=L(n,Y)\gg 1
is a constant to be determined in the proof.

If
r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(x^{*})\leq L
, the estimate is trivial. Therefore, assume
r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(x^{*})>L
. Then on the parabolic neighborhood

\displaystyle P:=B_{t}(x,L)\times[t-L^{2},t+L^{2}]\cap\mathbb{I}^{++},

we have
|\mathrm{Rm}|\leq L^{-2}<1
. By Proposition
3.12
(2), it follows that
|t-s|\leq 1
. Let

\displaystyle t_{1}:=\max\{\max\{t,s\}-1,(1-\sigma)T\}

and set
z_{1}^{*}=(z_{1},t_{1}),z_{2}^{*}=(z_{2},t_{1})
to be
H_{n}
-centers of
x^{*},y^{*}
, respectively. By the definition of
d^{*}
, we obtain

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{t_{1}}(\nu_{x^{*};t_{1}},\nu_{y^{*};t_{1}})=\epsilon_{0}\leq 1,

which gives

\displaystyle d_{t_{1}}(z_{1},z_{2})\leq d_{W_{1}}^{t_{1}}(\nu_{x^{*};t_{1}},\nu_{y^{*};t_{1}})+d_{W_{1}}^{t_{1}}(\nu_{x^{*};t_{1}},\delta_{z_{1}})+d_{W_{1}}^{t_{1}}(\nu_{y^{*};t_{1}},\delta_{z_{2}})\leq C_{1}(n).

By Proposition
2.21
(i), we also have

\displaystyle d_{t_{1}}(x,z_{1})\leq C_{2}(n,Y),

so that

\displaystyle d_{t_{1}}(x,z_{2})\leq C_{1}(n)+C_{2}(n,Y).

Choose
L\geq e^{100n}(C_{1}(n)+C_{2}(n,Y))
. We claim:

\displaystyle d_{t}(x,z_{2})\leq e^{(n-1)}(C_{1}(n)+C_{2}(n,Y)).

To see this, let
\gamma:[0,d_{t_{1}}(x,z_{2})]\in M
be a unit-speed minimizing geodesic between
x
and
z_{2}
with respect to
g(t_{1})
, so that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Length}_{t_{1}}(\gamma)\leq C_{1}(n)+C_{2}(n,Y).

Define

\displaystyle\bar{r}:=\sup\{r\in[0,d_{t_{1}}(x,z_{2})]\mid\gamma|_{[0,r]}\times[t_{1},t]\subset P\}.

If
\bar{r}<d_{t_{1}}(x,z_{2})
, then the standard distance distortion estimate yields

\displaystyle\mathrm{Length}_{t}(\gamma|_{[0,\bar{r}]})\leq e^{|t-t_{1}|(n-1)}\mathrm{Length}_{t_{1}}(\gamma|_{[0,\bar{r}]})\leq e^{(n-1)}(C_{1}(n)+C_{2}(n,Y))\leq L/10,

which contradicts the definition of
\bar{r}
. Therefore,
\bar{r}=d_{t_{1}}(x,z_{2})
, and
\gamma\times[t_{1},t]\subset P
, implying

\displaystyle d_{t^{\prime}}(x,z_{2})\leq e^{(n-1)}(C_{1}(n)+C_{2}(n,Y))\leq e^{-99n}L,\quad\forall t^{\prime}\in[t_{1},\max\{t,s\}].

Similarly, we obtain

\displaystyle B_{s}(z_{2},e^{-n}L)\times[t_{1},s]\subset P.

(3.19)

Now choose

\displaystyle L\geq\max\{100c(H_{n})^{-1/2},e^{100n}(C_{1}(n)+C_{2}(n,Y))\},

where
c(H_{n})
is the constant from
[
Bam23
, Proposition 9.16 (b)]
. Then by (
3.19
) and that proposition, we obtain

\displaystyle d_{s}(z_{2},y)\leq C_{3}(n),

and hence

\displaystyle d_{s}(x,y)\leq e^{(n-1)}(C_{1}(n)+C_{2}(n,Y))+C_{3}(n).

Next, choose

\displaystyle L\geq\max\{100c(H_{n})^{-1/2},e^{100n^{2}}(C_{1}(n)+C_{2}(n,Y)+C_{3}(n))\}.

Then for any
t^{\prime}\in[t_{1},\max\{t,s\}]
, another distance distortion argument gives

\displaystyle d_{t^{\prime}}(x,y)\leq e^{2(n-1)}(C_{1}(n)+C_{2}(n,Y)+C_{3}(n)).

Thus, we have

\displaystyle B_{s}(y,r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(x^{*})-L/2)\subset B_{s}(x,r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(x^{*})-L/3).

On the other hand, for any
z^{*}=(z,s)\in B_{s}(x,r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(x^{*})-L/3)
, the distance distortion estimate gives

\displaystyle d_{t}(z,x)\leq

\displaystyle e^{(n-1)|t-s|r_{\mathrm{Rm}}^{-2}(x^{*})}d_{s}(z,x)\leq e^{(n-1)|t-s|r_{\mathrm{Rm}}^{-2}(x^{*})}(r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(x^{*})-L/3)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(1+2(n-1)r_{\mathrm{Rm}}^{-2}(x^{*})\right)(r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(x^{*})-L/3)\leq r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(x^{*})-L/4.

This implies

\displaystyle B_{s}(x,r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(x^{*})-L/3)\subset B_{t}(x,r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(x^{*})-L/4).

Therefore,
B_{s}(y,r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(x^{*})-L/2)\subset B_{t}(x,r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(x^{*})-L/4)
, which shows that the curvature radius at
y^{*}
satisfies

\displaystyle r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(y^{*})\geq r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(x^{*})-L/2.

This proves (
3.18
) and completes the proof.
∎

Definition 3.22

(Parabolic metric space)
.

A
parabolic metric space

(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
over an interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
is a metric space
(Z,d_{Z})
coupled with a
time-function

\mathfrak{t}:Z\to I
, which satisfies for any
x,y\in Z
,

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{t}(x)-\mathfrak{t}(y)|\leq d_{Z}^{2}(x,y).

(3.20)

Additionally, for any set
J\subset I
, we define
Z_{J}:=\mathfrak{t}^{-1}(J)
. A sequence of parabolic metric spaces is said to converge if the underlying metric spaces converge in the (pointed) Gromov–Hausdorff sense and the corresponding time functions also converge.

In the following, we will focus on
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}}
for
\mathcal{X}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
and restrict the
d^{*}
-distance from
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
to
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}}
.

Theorem 3.23
.

Consider a sequence

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}^{i}=\{M_{i}^{n},(g_{i}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)

with base point
p_{i}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}^{i}_{\mathbb{I}}
. When
T=+\infty
, we additionally assume
\limsup_{i\to\infty}\mathfrak{t}_{i}(p_{i}^{*})>-\infty
.

By taking a subsequence if necessary, we obtain the pointed Gromov–Hausdorff convergence

\displaystyle(M_{i}\times\mathbb{I},d^{*}_{i},p_{i}^{*},\mathfrak{t}_{i})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathrm{pGH}\hskip 8.19447pt}(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t}),

where
d^{*}_{i}
denotes the
d^{*}
-distance associated with
\mathcal{X}^{i}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
, when restricted on
\mathcal{X}^{i}_{\mathbb{I}}
,
\mathfrak{t}_{i}
is the standard time-function on
M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
, and
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
is a complete, separable, locally compact parabolic metric space over
\mathbb{I}
.

Moreover, there exists a constant
t_{e}\in[\mathfrak{t}(p_{\infty}),0]
such that
\mathrm{image}(\mathfrak{t})=[-(1-2\sigma)T,t_{e}]
or
[-(1-2\sigma)T,t_{e})
.

Proof.

For any
L>0,\epsilon\in(0,\sqrt{\sigma T})
, let
\{x_{j}^{*}\}_{1\leq j\leq N}\in\mathcal{X}^{i}_{\mathbb{I}}
be any maximal
\epsilon
-separated set in
B^{*}(p_{i}^{*},L)\cap\mathcal{X}^{i}_{\mathbb{I}}
. Then, by comparing the volumes on
M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}^{+}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}|B^{*}(x_{j}^{*},\epsilon)|_{M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}^{+}}\leq|B^{*}(p_{i}^{*},L)|_{M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}^{+}}.

By Proposition
3.17
and Proposition
3.18
, we get

\displaystyle 0<Nc(n,Y,\sigma)\epsilon^{n+2}\leq C(n,\sigma,T,L),

which implies
N\leq C(n,Y,\sigma,\epsilon,L,T)
.

Thus, by
[
Bur01
, Theorem 8.1.10]
, we can take a subsequence, which converges to a limit metric space in the pointed Gromov–Hausdorff sense. Since the time-function
\mathfrak{t}_{i}
satisfies

\displaystyle\sqrt{|\mathfrak{t}_{i}(x^{*})-\mathfrak{t}_{i}(y^{*})|}\leq d_{i}^{*}(x^{*},y^{*}),

the limit of
\mathfrak{t}_{i}
, denoted by
\mathfrak{t}
, exists by taking a subsequence, satisfying (
3.20
). The fact that
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
is a complete, separable, locally compact space follows from the fact that
(M_{i}\times\mathbb{I},d^{*}_{i},p_{i}^{*},\mathfrak{t}_{i})
is complete, separable and locally compact, which is immediate from Corollary
3.11
and Lemma
3.13
.

To prove the last conclusion, we only need to prove that if
t\in\mathrm{image}(\mathfrak{t})
, then
s\in\mathrm{image}(\mathfrak{t})
for any
s\in[-(1-2\sigma)T,t)
. Fix
z\in Z
with
\mathfrak{t}(z)=t
. We choose a sequence
z_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
converging to
z
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense. Then we take an
H_{n}
-center
w_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\{s\}
of
z_{i}^{*}
. Note that this is possible if
i
is sufficiently large. By Lemma
3.16
,
d_{i}^{*}(z_{i}^{*},w_{i}^{*})
and hence
d_{i}^{*}(p_{i}^{*},w_{i}^{*})
are uniformly bounded. After passing to a subsequence, we assume
w_{i}^{*}\to w\in Z
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense with
\mathfrak{t}(w)=s
. This completes the proof.
∎

Definition 3.24
.

Any pointed Gromov–Hausdorff limit
(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t})
from Theorem
3.23
is called a
noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space
.

For a Ricci flow limit space
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
, we always use
x,y,z
, etc., to denote spacetime points and
\mathfrak{t}(x),\mathfrak{t}(y),\mathfrak{t}(z)
, etc., to represent their respective time components. We denote metric balls in
Z
by
B_{Z}^{*}(x,r)
.

Remark 3.25
.

One can also consider a more general setting. Let
T_{i}>0
be a sequence with
T_{i}\to T\in(0,+\infty]
, and consider a sequence of Ricci flows

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}^{i}=\{M_{i}^{n},(g_{i}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}_{i}^{++}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T_{i})

with base points
p_{i}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}^{i}_{\mathbb{I}_{i}}
, where
\mathbb{I}_{i}=[-(1-2\sigma)T_{i},0]
.

If
T<+\infty
, then by an argument similar to that in Theorem
3.23
, we may take a subsequence such that

\displaystyle(M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}_{i},d^{*}_{i},p_{i}^{*},\mathfrak{t}_{i})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathrm{pGH}\hskip 8.19447pt}(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t}),

where
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
is a parabolic metric space over
[-(1-2\sigma)T,0]
.

If
T=+\infty
, by taking a subsequence, we assume

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}\mathfrak{t}_{i}(p_{i}^{*})=t_{0}\in[-\infty,0]\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{i\to\infty}\left(\mathfrak{t}_{i}(p_{i}^{*})+(1-2\sigma)T_{i}\right)=a\in[0,+\infty].

Then, we consider the following subcases.

•

If
a<+\infty
, then we consider the shifted time functions
\mathfrak{t}_{i}-\mathfrak{t}_{i}(p_{i}^{*})
, and obtain

\displaystyle(M_{i}\times[-(1-2\sigma)T_{i},0],d^{*}_{i},p_{i}^{*},\mathfrak{t}_{i}-\mathfrak{t}_{i}(p_{i}^{*}))\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathrm{pGH}\hskip 8.19447pt}(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t}),

so that
\mathfrak{t}(p_{\infty})=0
and
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
is a parabolic metric space with
[-a,0]\subset\mathrm{image}(\mathfrak{t})
.

•

If
a=+\infty
and
t_{0}>-\infty
, then a similar argument yields

\displaystyle(M_{i}\times[-(1-2\sigma)T_{i},0],d^{*}_{i},p_{i}^{*},\mathfrak{t}_{i})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathrm{pGH}\hskip 8.19447pt}(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t}),

so that
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
is a parabolic metric space with
(-\infty,t_{0}]\subset\mathrm{image}(\mathfrak{t})
.

•

If
a=+\infty
and
t_{0}=-\infty
, then a similar argument yields

\displaystyle(M_{i}\times[-(1-2\sigma)T_{i},0],d^{*}_{i},p_{i}^{*},\mathfrak{t}_{i}-\mathfrak{t}_{i}(p_{i}^{*}))\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathrm{pGH}\hskip 8.19447pt}(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t}),

(3.21)

so that
\mathfrak{t}(p_{\infty})=0
and
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
is a parabolic metric space with
\mathbb{R}_{-}:=(-\infty,0]\subset\mathrm{image}(\mathfrak{t})
.

Now we introduce the following notation.

Notation 3.26
.

We write
(Z,d_{Z},z,\mathfrak{t})\in\overline{\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)}
if it arises as the pointed Gromov–Hausdorff limit of a sequence in
\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
. In general, we write
(Z,d_{Z},z,\mathfrak{t})\in\overline{\mathcal{M}(n,Y)}
if
(Z,d_{Z},z,\mathfrak{t})
is a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space obtained as the pointed Gromov–Hausdorff limit of a sequence in
\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T_{i})
for some sequence
\{T_{i}\}
with a finite or infinite limit.

In this paper, all results concerning noncollapsed Ricci flow limit spaces in
\overline{\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)}
remain valid even in
\overline{\mathcal{M}(n,Y)}
.

4
\mathbb{F}
-limits of Ricci flows

In this section, we relate our Ricci flow limit spaces to Bamler’s
\mathbb{F}
-limits developed in
[
Bam23
]
and
[
Bam20b
]
.

We first recall the following definition of a metric flow (see
[
Bam23
, Definition 3.2]
).

Definition 4.1

(Metric flow)
.

A metric flow over a subset
I
of
\mathbb{R}
is a tuple of the form

\displaystyle(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{t},(d_{t})_{t\in I},(\nu_{x;s})_{x\in\mathcal{X},s\in I,s\leq\mathfrak{t}(x)})

with the following properties:

(1)

\mathcal{X}
is a set consisting of points.

(2)

\mathfrak{t}:\mathcal{X}\to I
is a map called time-function.
Its level sets
\mathcal{X}_{t}:=\mathfrak{t}^{-1}(t)
are called time-slices and the preimages
\mathcal{X}_{I^{\prime}}:=\mathfrak{t}^{-1}(I^{\prime})
,
I^{\prime}\subset I
, are called time-slabs.

(3)

(\mathcal{X}_{t},d_{t})
is a complete and separable metric space for all
t\in I
.

(4)

\nu_{x;s}
is a probability measure on
\mathcal{X}_{s}
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
,
s\in I
,
s\leq\mathfrak{t}(x)
. For any
x\in\mathcal{X}
the family
(\nu_{x;s})_{s\in I,s\leq\mathfrak{t}(x)}
is called the conjugate heat kernel at
x
.

(5)

\nu_{x;\mathfrak{t}(x)}=\delta_{x}
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
.

(6)

For all
s,t\in I
,
s<t
,
L\geq 0
and any measurable function
u_{s}:\mathcal{X}_{s}\to[0,1]
with the property that if
L>0
, then
u_{s}=\Phi\circ f_{s}
for some
L^{-1/2}
-Lipschitz function
f_{s}:\mathcal{X}_{s}\to\mathbb{R}

(
if
L=0
, then there is no additional assumption on
u_{s}
)
, the following is true.
The function

\displaystyle u_{t}:\mathcal{X}_{t}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R},\qquad x\longmapsto\int_{\mathcal{X}_{s}}u_{s}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x;s}

is either constant or of the form
u_{t}=\Phi\circ f_{t}
, where
f_{t}:\mathcal{X}_{t}\to\mathbb{R}
is
(t-s+L)^{-1/2}
-Lipschitz. Here,
\Phi
is given by

\mathchoice{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mux}}{\displaystyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mux}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mux}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mux}}}\Phi(x)=(4\pi)^{-1/2}e^{-x^{2}/4},\ \ \ \lim_{x\to-\infty}\Phi(x)=0,\ \ \ \lim_{x\to\infty}\Phi(x)=1.

(7)

For any
t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}\in I
,
t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq t_{3}
,
x\in\mathcal{X}_{t_{3}}
we have the reproduction formula

\nu_{x;t_{1}}=\int_{\mathcal{X}_{t_{2}}}\nu_{\cdot;t_{1}}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x;t_{2}},

meaning that for any Borel set
S\subset\mathcal{X}_{t_{1}}

\nu_{x;t_{1}}(S)=\int_{\mathcal{X}_{t_{2}}}\nu_{y;t_{1}}(S)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x;t_{2}}(y).

Given a metric flow
\mathcal{X}
over
I
, we recall the following definitions from
[
Bam23
, Definition 3.13]
.

Definition 4.2

(Conjugate heat flow)
.

A family of probability measures
(\mu_{t}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}_{t}))_{t\in I^{\prime}}
over
I^{\prime}\subset I
is called a
conjugate heat flow
if for all
s,t\in I^{\prime}
,
s\leq t
we have

\displaystyle\mu_{s}=\int_{\mathcal{X}_{t}}\nu_{x;s}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(x).

Next, we recall the definition of the metric flow pair from
[
Bam23
, Definitions 5.1, 5.2]
. Roughly speaking, two metric flow pairs are equivalent if they are the same in the metric measure sense almost everywhere.

Definition 4.3

(Metric flow pair)
.

A pair
(\mathcal{X},(\mu_{t})_{t\in I^{\prime}})
is called a metric flow pair over
I\subset\mathbb{R}
if:

(1)

I^{\prime}\subset I
with
|I\setminus I^{\prime}|=0
.

(2)

\mathcal{X}
is a metric flow over
I^{\prime}
.

(3)

(\mu_{t}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}_{t}))_{t\in I^{\prime}}
is a conjugate heat flow on
\mathcal{X}
with
\text{supp}\,\mu_{t}=\mathcal{X}_{t}
for all
t\in I^{\prime}
. That is, for all
s,t\in I^{\prime}
,
s\leq t
we have

\displaystyle\mu_{s}=\int_{\mathcal{X}_{t}}\nu_{x;s}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(x).

If
J\subset I^{\prime}
, then we say that
(\mathcal{X},(\mu_{t})_{t\in I^{\prime}})
is fully defined over
J
. We denote by
\mathbb{F}_{I}^{J}
the set of equivalence classes of metric flow pairs over
I
that are fully defined over
J
. Here, two metric flow pairs
(\mathcal{X}^{i},(\mu^{i}_{t})_{t\in I^{\prime,i}})
,
i=1,2
, that are fully defined over
J
are equivalent if there exists an almost always isometry
\phi
between
\mathcal{X}^{1}
and
\mathcal{X}^{2}

(cf.
[
Bam23
, Definition 5.1]
)
such that
|I^{\prime,1}\setminus I^{\prime}|=|I^{\prime,2}\setminus I^{\prime}|=0
,
(\phi_{t})_{*}\mu^{1}_{t}=\mu^{2}_{t}
for all
t\in I^{\prime}
and
J\subset I^{\prime}
.

Next, for a sequence of metric flow pairs, we recall the following definition of a correspondence from
[
Bam23
, Definition 5.4]
, which can be regarded as embeddings into an ambient space.

Definition 4.4

(Correspondence)
.

Let
(\mathcal{X}^{i},(\mu^{i}_{t})_{t\in I^{\prime,i}})
be metric flow pairs over
I
, indexed by some
i\in\mathcal{I}
.
A correspondence between these metric flows over
I^{\prime\prime}
is a pair of the form

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}:=\big((A_{t},d^{A}_{t})_{t\in I^{\prime\prime}},(\varphi^{i}_{t})_{t\in I^{\prime\prime,i},i\in\mathcal{I}}\big),

where:

(1)

(A_{t},d^{A}_{t})
is a metric space for any
t\in I^{\prime\prime}
.

(2)

I^{\prime\prime,i}\subset I^{\prime\prime}\cap I^{\prime,i}
for any
i\in\mathcal{I}
.

(3)

\varphi^{i}_{t}:(\mathcal{X}^{i}_{t},d^{i}_{t})\to(A_{t},d^{A}_{t})
is an isometric embedding for any
i\in\mathcal{I}
and
t\in I^{\prime\prime,i}
.

If
J\subset I^{\prime\prime,i}
for all
i\in\mathcal{I}
, we say that
\mathfrak{C}
is fully defined over
J
.

Given a correspondence, one can define the corresponding
\mathbb{F}
-distance. In general, the
\mathbb{F}
-distance between metric flow pairs is the infimum for all correspondences (see
[
Bam23
, Definitions 5.6, 5.8]
).

Definition 4.5

(
\mathbb{F}
-distance)
.

We define the
\mathbb{F}
-distance between two metric flow pairs within
\mathfrak{C}
(uniform over
J
),

d_{\mathbb{F}}^{\,\mathfrak{C},J}\big((\mathcal{X}^{1},(\mu^{1}_{t})_{t\in I^{\prime,1}}),(\mathcal{X}^{2},(\mu^{2}_{t})_{t\in I^{\prime,2}})\big),

to be the infimum over all
r>0
with the property that there is a measurable subset
E\subset I^{\prime\prime}
with

J\subset I^{\prime\prime}\setminus E\subset I^{\prime\prime,1}\cap I^{\prime\prime,2}

and a family of couplings
(q_{t})_{t\in I^{\prime\prime}\setminus E}
between
\mu^{1}_{t},\mu^{2}_{t}
such that:

(1)

|E|\leq r^{2}
.

(2)

For all
s,t\in I^{\prime\prime}\setminus E
,
s\leq t
, we have

\int_{\mathcal{X}^{1}_{t}\times\mathcal{X}_{t}^{2}}d_{W_{1}}^{A_{s}}((\varphi^{1}_{s})_{*}\nu^{1}_{x^{1};s},(\varphi^{2}_{s})_{*}\nu^{2}_{x^{2};s})\,\mathrm{d}q_{t}(x^{1},x^{2})\leq r.

The
\mathbb{F}
-distance between two metric flow pairs (uniform over
J
),

d_{\mathbb{F}}^{J}\big((\mathcal{X}^{1},(\mu^{1}_{t})_{t\in I^{\prime,1}}),(\mathcal{X}^{2},(\mu^{2}_{t})_{t\in I^{\prime,2}})\big),

is defined as the infimum of

d_{\mathbb{F}}^{\,\mathfrak{C},J}\big((\mathcal{X}^{1},(\mu^{1}_{t})_{t\in I^{\prime,1}}),(\mathcal{X}^{2},(\mu^{2}_{t})_{t\in I^{\prime,2}})\big),

over all correspondences
\mathfrak{C}
between
\mathcal{X}^{1},\mathcal{X}^{2}
over
I^{\prime\prime}
that are fully defined over
J
.

With the
\mathbb{F}
-distance, one can define the
\mathbb{F}
-convergence of a sequence of metric flow pairs. In general,
\mathbb{F}
-convergence implies
\mathbb{F}
-convergence within a correspondence; see
[
Bam23
, Theorems 6.5, 6.6]
. More precisely,

Theorem 4.6
.

Let
(\mathcal{X}^{i},(\mu^{i}_{t})_{t\in I^{\prime,i}})
,
i\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, be metric flow pairs over
I
that are fully defined over some
J\subset I
.
Suppose that for any compact subinterval
I_{0}\subset I

d_{\mathbb{F}}^{J\cap I_{0}}\big((\mathcal{X}^{i},(\mu^{i}_{t})_{t\in I_{0}\cap I^{\prime,i}}),(\mathcal{X}^{\infty},(\mu^{\infty}_{t})_{t\in I_{0}\cap I^{\prime,\infty}})\big)\to 0.

Then there is a correspondence
\mathfrak{C}
between the metric flows
\mathcal{X}^{i}
,
i\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, over
I
such that

\displaystyle(\mathcal{X}^{i},(\mu^{i}_{t})_{t\in I^{\prime,i}})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathbb{F},\mathfrak{C},J\hskip 8.19447pt}(\mathcal{X}^{\infty},(\mu^{\infty}_{t})_{t\in I^{\prime,\infty}})

on compact time intervals, in the sense that

\displaystyle d_{\mathbb{F}}^{\,\mathfrak{C},J\cap I_{0}}\big((\mathcal{X}^{i},(\mu^{i}_{t})_{t\in I_{0}\cap I^{\prime,i}}),(\mathcal{X}^{\infty},(\mu^{\infty}_{t})_{t\in I_{0}\cap I^{\prime,\infty}})\big)\to 0

for any compact subinterval
I_{0}\subset I
.

Next, we recall the notion of convergence of points within correspondence, see
[
Bam23
, Definitions 6.7, 6.10, 6.12]
.

Definition 4.7
.

Let
\mathcal{X}^{i}
be metric flows over
I
and consider a correspondence
\mathfrak{C}
as in Definition
4.4
between
\mathcal{X}^{i}
over
I^{\prime\prime}
.

Let
(\mu_{t}^{i})_{t\in I_{*}^{\prime}},i\in\mathbb{N}\bigcup\{\infty\}
be conjugate heat flows on
\mathcal{X}^{i}
, where
I_{*}^{i}=I^{\prime,i}\bigcap(-\infty,T_{i})
or
I^{\prime,i}\bigcap(-\infty,T_{i}]
for some
T_{i}\in(-\infty,+\infty]
. We say that the conjugate heat flows
(\mu_{t}^{i})_{t\in I_{*}^{\prime}},i\in\mathbb{N}\bigcup\{\infty\}

converge
to
(\mu_{t}^{\infty})_{t\in I_{*}^{\infty}}
within
\mathfrak{C}
and that the convergence is uniform over
J
if
J\subset I_{*}^{\prime}
and there exist measurable subsets
E_{i}\subset I^{\prime\prime},i\in\mathbb{N}
such that:

(1)

J\bigcap I_{*}^{\infty}\subset(I_{*}^{i}\bigcap I^{\prime\prime})\setminus E_{i}=(I_{*}^{\infty}\bigcap I^{\prime\prime})\setminus E_{i}\subset I^{\prime\prime,i}\bigcap I^{\prime\prime,\infty}
for large
i
.

(2)

|E_{i}|\to 0
.

(3)

\sup_{t\in(I_{*}^{\infty}\bigcap I^{\prime\prime})\setminus E_{i}}d_{W_{1}}^{A_{t}}((\varphi_{t}^{i})_{*}\mu_{t}^{i},(\varphi_{t}^{\infty})_{*}\mu_{t}^{\infty})\to 0
.

We write this convergence as

\displaystyle(\mu_{t}^{i})_{t\in I_{*}^{i}}\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathfrak{C},J\hskip 8.19447pt}(\mu_{t}^{\infty})_{t\in I_{*}^{\infty}}.

(4.1)

We say that (
4.1
) holds on compact intervals and is uniform over
J
if for any compact subinterval
I_{0}\subset I_{*}^{\infty}
, (
4.1
) holds after replacing
\mathfrak{C},J
by
\mathfrak{C}|_{I^{\prime\prime}\bigcap I_{0}},J\bigcap I_{0}
. We say that (
4.1
) is uniform at time
t\in I^{\prime\prime}
if (
4.1
) holds after replacing
J
by
J\bigcup\{t\}
. Let
T_{i}\in I^{\prime,i}
and
\mu^{i}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}^{i}_{T^{i}})
. We say that
\mu^{i}
converge to
\mu^{\infty}
within
\mathfrak{C}
(and uniform over
J
)
, and write

\displaystyle\mu^{i}\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathfrak{C},J\hskip 8.19447pt}\mu^{\infty},

if
T_{i}\to T_{\infty}
and if for the conjugate heat flows
(\tilde{\mu}_{t}^{i})_{t\in I^{\prime,i}\bigcap(-\infty,T_{i}]}
,
i\in\mathbb{N}\bigcap\{\infty\}
with initial condition
\tilde{\mu}_{T_{i}}^{i}=\mu^{i}
, we have the following convergence on compact time intervals

\displaystyle(\tilde{\mu}_{t}^{i})_{t\in I_{*}^{i}}\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathfrak{C},J\hskip 8.19447pt}(\tilde{\mu}_{t}^{\infty})_{t\in I_{*}^{\infty}}.

Fix some
T\in I^{\prime\prime}
and
\mu^{i}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}_{T}^{i})
. We say that
\mu^{i}

strictly converge
to
\mu^{\infty}
within
\mathfrak{C}
if

\displaystyle(\varphi_{T}^{i})_{*}\mu^{i}\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{W_{1}}(\varphi_{T}^{\infty})_{*}\mu^{\infty}.

For a sequence of points
x_{i}\in\mathcal{X}^{i}_{T_{i}},i\in\mathbb{N}\bigcup\{\infty\}
, we say that
x_{i}

converge
to
x_{\infty}
within
\mathfrak{C}
(and uniform over
J
) if
\delta_{x_{i}}\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathfrak{C},J\hskip 8.19447pt}\delta_{x_{\infty}}
. We write this convergence as

\displaystyle x_{i}\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathfrak{C},J\hskip 8.19447pt}x_{\infty}.

For any sequence of points
x_{i}\in\mathcal{X}^{i}_{T},i\in\mathbb{N}\bigcup\{\infty\}
, we say that
x_{i}

strictly converge
to
x_{\infty}
within
\mathfrak{C}
if

\displaystyle(\varphi_{T}^{i})(x_{i})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{}(\varphi_{T}^{\infty})(x_{\infty}).

Next, we recall the following definition from
[
Bam23
, Definition 3.21]
.

Definition 4.8

(
H
-concentration)
.

Given a constant
H>0
, a metric flow
\mathcal{X}
is called
H
-concentrated
if for any
s\leq t
,
s,t\in I
,
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathcal{X}_{t}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}(\nu_{x_{1};s},\nu_{x_{2};s})\leq d^{2}_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})+H(t-s).

We note that Definition
4.7
has defined two notions of convergence of measures or points. Strict convergence is useful if the
\mathfrak{C}
-convergence is time-wise at time
T
, see
[
Bam23
, Theorems 6.13, 6.15]
. The following theorem from
[
Bam23
, Theorem 6.19]
shows how to represent points as limits of sequences:

Theorem 4.9
.

Let
\mathcal{X}^{i}
be metric flows over subset
I^{\prime,i}\subset\mathbb{R},i\in\mathbb{N}\bigcup\{\infty\}
and consider a correspondence
\mathfrak{C}
as in Definition
4.4
between
\mathcal{X}^{i}
. Suppose for some
J\subset\mathbb{R}
, we have on compact time-intervals,

\displaystyle(\mathcal{X}^{i},(\mu^{i}_{t})_{t\in I^{\prime,i}})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathbb{F},\mathfrak{C},J\hskip 8.19447pt}(\mathcal{X}^{\infty},(\mu^{\infty}_{t})_{t\in I^{\prime,\infty}})

and all
\mathcal{X}^{i}
are
H
-concentrated for some uniform constant
H
. Consider some
x_{\infty}\in\mathcal{X}^{\infty}_{t_{\infty}}
with
t_{\infty}>\inf\,I_{\infty}
and a sequence of times
t_{i}\in I^{\prime,i}
with
t_{i}\to t_{\infty}
. Then there exist points
x_{i}\in\mathcal{X}^{i}_{t_{i}}
such that

\displaystyle x_{i}\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathfrak{C},J\hskip 8.19447pt}x_{\infty}.

In particular, if
t_{\infty}\in I^{\prime,i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, then we can choose all
x_{i}\in\mathcal{X}^{i}_{t_{\infty}}
.

In this paper, we will focus on metric flows induced by closed Ricci flows and their limits. For any pointed Ricci flow
\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in[-L,0]},p^{*}=(p,0)\}
, one can define
(\mathcal{X},(\mu_{t})_{t\in[-L,0]})
as follows:

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{X}:=M\times[-L,0)\sqcup p^{*},\mathfrak{t}:=\text{proj}_{[-L,0]},(d_{t})_{t\in[-L,0]},(\nu_{x^{*};s})_{x^{*}\in M\times[-L,0],s\in[-L,0],s\leq\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})},\mu_{t}:=\nu_{p^{*};t}\right).

(4.2)

Here, if
L=\infty
, we set
[-L,0]=(-\infty,0]
.

Then by Proposition
2.12
, we have:

Proposition 4.10
.

The pair
(\mathcal{X},(\mu_{t})_{t\in[-L,0]})
defined in (
4.2
) is an
H_{n}
-concentrated metric flow pair that is fully defined over
[-L,0]
.

For a sequence of closed Ricci flows, we have the following compactness theorem from
[
Bam23
, Theorem 7.4, Corollary 7.5, Theorem 7.6]
.

Theorem 4.11

(
\mathbb{F}
-limit)
.

Let
(M_{i}^{n},g_{i}(t),p_{i}^{*}=(p_{i},0))_{t\in[-L,0]}
be a sequence of closed Ricci flows with the corresponding metric flow pairs
(\mathcal{X}^{i},(\mu_{t}^{i})_{t\in[-L,0]})
as described in (
4.2
).

For any finite set
J\subset[-L,0]
containing
0
, after passing to a subsequence, there exist an
H_{n}
-concentrated metric flow pair
(\mathcal{X}^{\infty},(\mu^{\infty}_{t})_{t\in[-L,0]})
and a correspondence
\mathfrak{C}
between the metric flows
\mathcal{X}^{i}
,
i\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, over
[-L,0]
such that (on compact time-intervals if
L=+\infty
)

(\mathcal{X}^{i},(\mu^{i}_{t})_{t\in[-L,0]})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathbb{F},\mathfrak{C},J\hskip 8.19447pt}(\mathcal{X}^{\infty},(\mu^{\infty}_{t})_{t\in[-L,0]}).

(4.3)

where
\mathcal{X}^{\infty}_{0}
consists of a single point
p_{\infty}
, and
\mu^{\infty}_{t}=\nu_{p_{\infty};t}
. Moreover, the convergence (
4.3
) is uniform over any compact
J^{\prime}\subset[-L,0]
that only contains times at which
\mathcal{X}^{\infty}
is continuous. The limit metric flow pair
(\mathcal{X}^{\infty},(\mu^{\infty}_{t})_{t\in[-L,0]})
is called an
\mathbb{F}
-limit
of the sequence.

In addition, after passing to a subsequence, there exists a unique
\mathbb{F}
-limit
(\mathcal{X}^{\mathbb{F}},(\nu_{p_{\infty};t})_{t\in[-L,0]})
such that
\mathcal{X}^{\mathbb{F}}
is future continuous in the sense of
[
Bam23
, Definition 4.7]
.

Remark 4.12
.

In general, the set of discontinuous times of
\mathcal{X}^{\infty}
is at most countable; see
[
Bam23
, Corollary 4.11]
.

Generally speaking, an
\mathbb{F}
-limit
(\mathcal{X}^{\infty},(\nu_{p_{\infty};t})_{t\in[-L,0]})
carries limited geometric information. However, if we assume that the Nash entropies of all closed Ricci flows are uniformly bounded, an assumption equivalent to a certain non-collapsing condition, then the
\mathbb{F}
-limit
(\mathcal{X}^{\infty},(\mu^{\infty}_{t})_{t\in[-L,0]})
reveals significantly richer structural properties.

Let us first recall the following definitions from
[
Bam23
, Definitions 6.22, 3.42, 3.40, 3.46]
:

Definition 4.13

(Tangent metric flow)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a metric flow over
I
and
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{t_{0}}
a point.
We say that a metric flow pair
(\mathcal{X}^{\prime},(\nu^{\prime}_{x_{\max};t})_{t\in(-\infty,0]})
is a tangent metric flow of
\mathcal{X}
at
x_{0}
if there is a sequence of scales
\lambda_{k}>0
with
\lambda_{k}\to\infty
such that for any
L>0
the parabolic rescalings (see
[
Bam23
, Lemma 3.4]
for the notations)

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{X}^{-t_{0},\lambda_{k}}_{[-L,0]},(\nu_{x_{0};t}^{-t_{0},\lambda_{k}})_{\lambda_{k}^{-2}t+t_{0}\in I,t\in[-L,0]}\big)

\mathbb{F}
-converge to
\big(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{[-L,0]},(\nu^{\prime}_{x_{\max};t})_{t\in[-L,0]}\big)
.

Definition 4.14

(Metric soliton)
.

A metric flow pair
(\mathcal{X},(\mu_{t})_{t\in(-\infty,0]})
is called a
metric soliton
if there is a tuple

\big(X,d,\mu,(\nu^{\prime}_{x;t})_{x\in X;t<0}\big)

and a map
\phi:\mathcal{X}\to X
such that the following holds:

(1)

(X,d,\mu)
is a metric measure space and for any
t<I
, the map
\phi_{t}:(\mathcal{X}_{t},d_{t},\mu_{t})\to(X,\sqrt{|t|}d,\mu)
is an isometry between metric measure spaces.

(2)

For any
x\in\mathcal{X}_{t}
,
s\in I
with
s\leq t
, we have
(\phi_{s})_{*}\nu_{x;s}=\nu^{\prime}_{\phi_{t}(x);\log(s/t)}
.

Definition 4.15

(Static cone)
.

A metric flow
\mathcal{X}
over
(-\infty,0]
is called a
static cone
if there is a tuple

\big(X,d,(\nu^{\prime}_{x;t})_{x\in X;t\leq 0}\big)

and a map
\phi:\mathcal{X}\to X
such that the following holds:

1.

(X,d)
is a metric cone with vertex
q
such that for any
\lambda\in(0,1]
, if
\psi_{\lambda}:X\to X
is the radial dilation by
\lambda
preserving
q
, then
(\psi_{\lambda})_{*}\nu^{\prime}_{x;t}=\nu^{\prime}_{\psi_{\lambda}(x);\lambda^{2}t}
for any
x\in X
and
t\leq 0
.

2.

For any
t<0
, the map
\phi_{t}:(\mathcal{X}_{t},d_{t})\to(X,d)
is an isometry.

3.

For any
x\in\mathcal{X}_{t}
and
s\leq t
, we have
(\phi_{s})_{*}\nu_{x;s}=\nu^{\prime}_{\phi_{t}(x);t-s}
.

We consider a sequence of closed Ricci flows with entropy bounded below at the base point. First, we recall the following definition.

Definition 4.16

(Ricci flow spacetime)
.

An
n
-dimensional Ricci flow spacetime over an interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
is a tuple
(\mathcal{U},\mathfrak{t},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}},g)
with the following properties:

(1)

\mathcal{U}
is an
(n+1)
-dimensional smooth manifold with smooth boundary
\partial\mathcal{U}
, and
\partial\mathcal{U}
is a disjoint union of smooth manifolds of dimension
n
.

(2)

\mathfrak{t}:\mathcal{U}\to I
is a smooth function without critical points. For any
t\in I
we denote by
\mathcal{U}_{t}:=\mathfrak{t}^{-1}(t)\subset\mathcal{U}
the time-
t
-slice of
\mathcal{U}
.

(3)

\mathfrak{t}(\partial\mathcal{U})\subset\partial I
.

(4)

\partial_{\mathfrak{t}}
is a smooth vector field on
\mathcal{U}
that satisfies
\partial_{\mathfrak{t}}\mathfrak{t}\equiv 1
.

(5)

g
is a smooth inner product on the spatial subbundle
\ker(d\mathfrak{t})\subset T\mathcal{U}
.
For any
t\in I
we denote by
g_{t}
the restriction of
g
to the time-
t
-slice
\mathcal{U}_{t}
.

(6)

g
satisfies the Ricci flow equation:
\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{\mathfrak{t}}}g=-2\mathrm{Ric}(g)
.
Here
\mathrm{Ric}(g)
denotes the symmetric
(0,2)
-tensor on
\ker(d\mathfrak{t})
that restricts to the Ricci tensor of
(\mathcal{U}_{t},g_{t})
for all
t\in I
.

Obviously, a conventional Ricci flow
(M,g(t))_{t\in I}
is a Ricci flow spacetime by setting
\mathcal{M}=M\times I
,
\mathfrak{t}
to be the projection on the time factor, and
\partial_{\mathfrak{t}}
to be the unit vector on
I
.

The following structure theorem follows from
[
Bam20b
, Theorems 2.3, 2.4, 2.5, 2.6, 2.46]
and
[
Bam23
, Theorem 9.21]
.

Theorem 4.17
.

Let
\mathcal{X}^{i}=\{M_{i}^{n},(g_{i}(t))_{t\in[-L,0]},p_{i}^{*}=(p_{i},0)\}
be a sequence of pointed closed Ricci flows with entropy bounded below by
-Y
at
p_{i}^{*}
(see Definition
2.20
). Suppose
(\mathcal{X}^{\infty},(\mu^{\infty}_{t})_{t\in[-L,0]})
is a future continuous
\mathbb{F}
-limit obtained in Theorem
4.11
. Then the following properties hold.

(1)

There exists a decomposition

\mathcal{X}^{\infty}_{0}=\{p_{\infty}\},\quad\mathcal{X}^{\infty}_{(-L,0)}=\mathcal{R}^{\mathbb{F}}\sqcup\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{F}},

(4.4)

such that
\mathcal{R}^{\mathbb{F}}
is given by an
n
-dimensional Ricci flow spacetime
(\mathcal{R}^{\mathbb{F}},\mathfrak{t},\partial^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{t}},g^{\infty})
and
\emph{\text{dim}}_{\mathcal{M}^{*}}(\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{F}})\leq n-2
, where
\emph{\text{dim}}_{\mathcal{M}^{*}}
denotes the
*
-Minkowski dimension in
[
Bam23
, Definition 3.31]
. Moreover,
\mathcal{R}^{\mathbb{F}}_{t}
is a connected open set and
\mu^{\infty}_{t}(\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{F}}_{t})=0
for any
t\in(-L,0)
.

(2)

Every tangent flow
(\mathcal{X}^{\prime},(\nu_{x_{\infty}^{\prime};t})_{t\leq 0})
at every point
x\in\mathcal{X}^{\infty}
is a metric soliton. Moreover,
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
is the Gaussian soliton iff
x\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathbb{F}}
. If
x\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{F}}
, the singular set of
(\mathcal{X}^{\prime},(\nu_{x_{\infty}^{\prime};t})_{t\leq 0})
on each
t<0
has Minkowski dimension at most
n-4
. In particular, if
n=3
, the metric soliton is a smooth Ricci flow associated with a
3
-dimensional Ricci shrinker. If
n=4
, each slice of the metric soliton is a smooth Ricci shrinker orbifold with isolated singularities.

(3)

The convergence (
4.3
) is smooth on
\mathcal{R}^{\mathbb{F}}
, in the following sense. There exists an increasing sequence
U_{1}\subset U_{2}\subset\ldots\subset\mathcal{R}^{\mathbb{F}}
of open subsets with
\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}U_{i}=\mathcal{R}^{\mathbb{F}}
, open subsets
V_{i}\subset M_{i}\times(-L,0)
, time-preserving diffeomorphisms
\phi_{i}:U_{i}\to V_{i}
and a sequence
\epsilon_{i}\to 0
such that the following holds:

(a)

We have

\displaystyle\|\phi_{i}^{*}g^{i}-g^{\infty}\|_{C^{[\epsilon_{i}^{-1}]}(U_{i})}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon_{i},

\displaystyle\|\phi_{i}^{*}\partial_{\mathfrak{t}_{i}}-\partial^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{t}}\|_{C^{[\epsilon_{i}^{-1}]}(U_{i})}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon_{i},

\displaystyle\|w^{i}\circ\phi_{i}-w^{\infty}\|_{C^{[\epsilon_{i}^{-1}]}(U_{i})}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon_{i},

where
g^{i}
is the spacetime metric induced by
g_{i}(t)
, and
w^{i}
is the conjugate heat kernel defined by
d\nu_{p_{i}^{*};\cdot}=w^{i}dg^{i}
,
i\in\mathbb{N}\bigcup\{\infty\}
.

(b)

Let
y_{\infty}\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathbb{F}}
and
y_{i}\in M_{i}\times(-L,0)
.
Then
y_{i}
converges to
y_{\infty}
within
\mathfrak{C}

(cf. Definition
4.7
if and only if
y_{i}\in V_{i}
for large
i
and
\phi_{i}^{-1}(y_{i})\to y_{\infty}
in
\mathcal{R}^{\mathbb{F}}
.

(c)

If the convergence (
4.3
) is uniform at some time
t\in(-L,0)
, then for any compact subset
K\subset\mathcal{R}^{\mathbb{F}}_{t}
and for the same subsequence we have

\sup_{x\in K\cap U_{i}}d^{A}_{t}(\varphi^{i}_{t}(\phi_{i}(x)),\varphi^{\infty}_{t}(x))\longrightarrow 0.

(4)

For any
t\in(-T,0)
, the restriction of
d_{t}
on
\mathcal{R}^{\mathbb{F}}_{t}
agrees with the length metric of
g(t)
.

The singular set
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{F}}
in (
4.4
) has a natural stratification; see
[
Bam20b
, Theorem 1.9]
:

Theorem 4.18
.

There is a stratification of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{F}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{0,\mathbb{F}}\subset\mathcal{S}^{1,\mathbb{F}}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{S}^{n-2,\mathbb{F}}=\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{F}},

such that for each
k=0,\ldots,n-2
,

1.

\emph{\text{dim}}_{\mathcal{M}^{*}}(\mathcal{S}^{k,\mathbb{F}})\leq k
;

2.

Every point
x\in\mathcal{X}^{\mathbb{F}}_{<0}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{k-1,\mathbb{F}}
has a tangent flow
(\mathcal{X}^{\prime},(\nu_{x^{\prime};t})_{t\leq 0})
that is a metric soliton and satisfies one of the following:

(a)

\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{<0}=\mathcal{X}_{<0}^{\prime\prime}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
(\nu_{x^{\prime};t})_{t\leq 0}=(\mu_{t}^{\prime\prime}\otimes\mu_{t}^{\mathbb{R}^{k}})_{t<0}
for some metric soliton
(\mathcal{X}^{\prime\prime},(\mu_{t}^{\prime\prime})_{t<0})
;

(b)

\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{<0}=\mathcal{X}_{<0}^{\prime\prime}\times\mathbb{R}^{k-2}
and
(\nu_{x^{\prime};t})_{t\leq 0}=(\mu_{t}^{\prime\prime}\otimes\mu_{t}^{\mathbb{R}^{k-2}})_{t<0}
for some static cone
(\mathcal{X}^{\prime\prime},(\mu_{t}^{\prime\prime})_{t<0})
.

For later use, we record the following splitting result for the
\mathbb{F}
-limit, which is essentially a consequence of
[
Bam20b
, Theorem 15.50]
. We sketch the proof for readers’ convenience.

Theorem 4.19
.

Let
\mathcal{X}^{\infty}
be the limit metric flow from Theorem
4.17
, and let
(-T_{1},-T_{2})\subset[-L,0]
. Suppose that there exist
k
smooth functions
y_{1},\ldots,y_{k}
on
\mathcal{R}^{\mathbb{F}}_{(-T_{1},-T_{2})}
such that for all
a,b\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
,

\displaystyle\langle\nabla y_{a},\nabla y_{b}\rangle=\delta_{ab},\quad\nabla^{2}y_{a}=0,\quad\partial_{\mathfrak{t}}y_{a}=0,

then the vector fields
\nabla y_{a}
induce an isometric splitting of the form
\mathcal{X}^{\infty}_{(-T_{1},-T_{2})}=\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{(-T_{1},-T_{2})}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
for some metric flow
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
over
(-T_{1},-T_{2})
.

Proof.

It suffices to establish the claim on any closed subinterval
[-T_{1}^{\prime},-T_{2}^{\prime}]\subset(-T_{1},-T_{2})
. Using the cutoff functions constructed in
[
Bam20b
, Lemma 15.27]
and the smooth convergence, we obtain
\epsilon_{i}\to 0
and functions
u_{a}^{i}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{X}^{i})
,
a\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
, such that for all
a,b\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
,

\displaystyle\int_{-\frac{T_{1}^{\prime}+T_{1}}{2}}^{-\frac{T_{2}^{\prime}+T_{2}}{2}}\int_{M_{i}}|\square u_{a}^{i}|\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{p_{i}^{*}}\mathrm{d}t\leq\epsilon_{i},\quad\int_{-\frac{T_{1}^{\prime}+T_{1}}{2}}^{-\frac{T_{2}^{\prime}+T_{2}}{2}}\int_{M_{i}}|\langle\nabla u_{a}^{i},\nabla u_{b}^{i}\rangle-\delta_{ab}|\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{p_{i}^{*};t}\mathrm{d}t\leq\epsilon_{i}.

Moreover,
u_{a}^{i}\to y_{a}
on
\mathcal{R}_{[-T_{1}^{\prime},-T_{2}^{\prime}]}
.

By
[
Bam20b
, Proposition 12.1]
, there exist
\tilde{u}_{a}^{i}
on
\mathcal{X}^{i}_{[-T^{\prime}_{1},-T^{\prime}_{2}]}
for
a\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
with
\square\tilde{u}_{a}^{i}=0
such that, for all
a,b
,

\displaystyle\int_{-T_{1}}^{-T_{2}}\int_{M_{i}}|\langle\nabla\tilde{u}^{i}_{a},\nabla\tilde{u}^{i}_{b}\rangle-\delta_{ab}|\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{p_{i}^{*};t}\mathrm{d}t\leq\epsilon^{\prime}_{i},\quad\tilde{u}_{a}^{i}(p_{i}^{*})=0,

and

\displaystyle\int_{-T_{1}}^{-T_{2}}\int_{M_{i}}|\nabla^{2}\tilde{u}^{i}_{a}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{p_{i}^{*};t}\mathrm{d}t\leq\epsilon^{\prime}_{i},

where
\epsilon_{i}^{\prime}\to 0
as
i\to\infty
. Furthermore,
\tilde{u}_{a}^{i}\to y_{a}
on
\mathcal{R}_{[-T_{1}^{\prime},-T_{2}^{\prime}]}
. The remainder of the argument follows verbatim from
[
Bam20b
, Theorem 15.50]
.
∎

One can define, even in smooth Ricci flows, the quantitative singular strata as in
[
CN13
]
. The following definition is from
[
Bam20b
, Definition 2.22]
, slightly adapted to our setting.

Definition 4.20
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
. For any
\epsilon>0
and
0<r_{1}<r_{2}<\infty
, we have the following quantitative strata:

\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,0,\mathbb{F}}_{r_{1},r_{2}}\subset\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,1,\mathbb{F}}_{r_{1},r_{2}}\subset\ldots\subset\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,n-2,\mathbb{F}}_{r_{1},r_{2}}\subset M\times\mathbb{I}^{-}

defined as:
x^{*}\in\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,k,\mathbb{F}}_{r_{1},r_{2}}
if and only if
\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-\epsilon^{-1}r_{2}^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
and
x^{*}
is not
(k+1,\epsilon,r^{\prime})
-
\mathbb{F}
-symmetric for any
r^{\prime}\in[r_{1},r_{2}]
.

Here, a point
x_{0}^{*}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
is called
(k,\epsilon,r)
-
\mathbb{F}
-symmetric if
\mathfrak{t}(x_{0}^{*})-\epsilon^{-1}r^{2}>-(1-2\sigma)T
and there exists a metric flow pair
(\mathcal{X}^{\prime},(\nu_{x^{\prime};t})_{t\leq 0})
over
(-\infty,0]
that arises as a noncollapsed
\mathbb{F}
-limit of closed Ricci flows as in Theorem
4.17
and satisfies Theorem
4.18
2. (a) or (b) such that the following is true. Consider the metric flow pair

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{X}_{[t_{0}-\epsilon^{-1}r^{2},t_{0}]},(\nu_{x_{0}^{*};t})_{[t_{0}-\epsilon^{-1}r^{2},t_{0}]}\right).

After a time-shift by
-t_{0}
and parabolic rescaling by
r^{-1}
, this metric flow pair has
d_{\mathbb{F}}
-distance smaller than
\epsilon
to the metric flow pair
(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{[-\epsilon^{-1},0]},(\nu_{x^{\prime};t})_{t\in[-\epsilon^{-1},0]})
.

By
[
Bam20b
, Theorems 2.25, 2.28]
, we have the following estimates which can be regarded as parabolic versions of
[
CN13
, Theorem 1.3, Corollary 1.11]
.

Theorem 4.21
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
with
x_{0}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
. Given
\epsilon>0
and
r>0
with
\mathfrak{t}(x_{0}^{*})-2r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, for any
\delta\in(0,\epsilon)
, there exist
x_{1}^{*},x_{2}^{*},\ldots,x_{N}^{*}\in\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,k,\mathbb{F}}_{\delta r,\epsilon r}\cap P^{*}(x_{0}^{*};r)
with
N\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon)\delta^{-k-\epsilon}
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,k,\mathbb{F}}_{\delta r,\epsilon r}\cap P^{*}(x_{0}^{*};r)\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}P^{*}(x_{i}^{*};\delta r).

Moreover, if
\epsilon\leq\epsilon(n,Y)
, then

\displaystyle r_{\mathrm{Rm}}\geq\delta r,\quad on\quad P^{*}(x_{0}^{*};r)\cap M\times\mathbb{I}^{-}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,n-2,\mathbb{F}}_{\delta r,\epsilon r},

where
r_{\mathrm{Rm}}
is the curvature radius from Definition
2.10
. Moreover, the following integral estimate holds for any small
\epsilon>0
:

\displaystyle\int_{[\mathfrak{t}(x_{0}^{*})-r^{2},\mathfrak{t}(x_{0}^{*})+r^{2}]\cap\mathbb{I}^{-}}\int_{P^{*}(x_{0}^{*};r)\cap M\times\{t\}}|\mathrm{Rm}|^{2-\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{[\mathfrak{t}(x_{0}^{*})-r^{2},\mathfrak{t}(x_{0}^{*})+r^{2}]\cap\mathbb{I}^{-}}\int_{P^{*}(x_{0}^{*};r)\cap M\times\{t\}}r_{\mathrm{Rm}}^{-4+2\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}\mathrm{d}t\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon)r^{n-2+2\epsilon}.

For the rest of the section, we fix a sequence
\mathcal{X}^{i}=\{M_{i}^{n},(g_{i}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
with base point
p_{i}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}^{i}_{\mathbb{I}}
. Then it follows from Theorem
3.23
that, by taking a subsequence if necessary, we have the following pointed Gromov–Hausdorff convergence:

(M_{i}\times\mathbb{I},d^{*}_{i},p_{i}^{*},\mathfrak{t}_{i})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathrm{pGH}\hskip 8.19447pt}(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t}).

Given
z\in Z
, we choose a sequence
z_{i}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}^{i}_{\mathbb{I}}
converging to
z
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense. Set
J=\{-(1-\sigma)T\}
. Then, by Theorem
4.11
and Theorem
4.17
, and after passing to a further subsequence if necessary, there exists a correspondence
\mathfrak{C}
such that

(\mathcal{X}^{i},(\nu_{z_{i}^{*};t})_{t\in[-T,\mathfrak{t}_{i}(z_{i}^{*})]})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathbb{F},\mathfrak{C},J\hskip 8.19447pt}(\mathcal{X}^{z},(\nu_{z;t})_{t\in[-T,\mathfrak{t}(z)]})

(4.5)

such that the metric flow
\mathcal{X}^{z}
is future continuous for all
t\in[-T,\mathfrak{t}(z)]
except possibly at
t=-(1-\sigma)T
. Here, we require that the convergence
(
4.5
)
is uniform at
-(1-\sigma)T
. We call
\mathcal{X}^{z}
obtained in this manner
a metric flow associated with
z
with a time-function denoted by
\mathfrak{t}^{z}
. Notably,
\mathcal{X}^{z}
depends on the choice of
z_{i}^{*}
and the subsequence of
\mathcal{X}^{i}
.

Notice that
\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathfrak{t}(z)}
consists of a single point, also denoted by
z
, and by Theorem
4.17
(1), we have the regular-singular decomposition:

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}^{z}_{(-(1-\sigma)T,\mathfrak{t}(z))}=\mathcal{R}^{z}\sqcup\mathcal{S}^{z}.

(4.6)

We set
d^{z}_{t},d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{t}},\mathrm{Var}_{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{t}}
for
t\in[-T,\mathfrak{t}(z))
to be the space distance,
W_{1}
-Wasserstein and variance on
\mathcal{X}^{z}
.

As in Definition
3.5
, the distance
d^{*}_{z}
on
\mathcal{X}^{z}
is defined as follows:

Definition 4.22
.

For any
x,y\in\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
with
t=\mathfrak{t}^{z}(x)\geq s=\mathfrak{t}^{z}(y)
, we define

\displaystyle d^{*}_{z}(x,y):=\inf_{r\in[\sqrt{t-s},\sqrt{t+(1-\sigma)T})}\left\{r\bigm|d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{t-r^{2}}}(\nu_{x;t-r^{2}},\nu_{y;t-r^{2}})\leq\epsilon_{0}r\right\}.

(4.7)

If no such
r
exists, we define

\displaystyle d^{*}_{z}(x,y):=\epsilon_{0}^{-1}d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{-(1-\sigma)T}}(\nu_{x;-(1-\sigma)T},\nu_{y;-(1-\sigma)T}).

Here,
\epsilon_{0}
is the spacetime distance constant (see Definition
3.3
).

Note that by taking the limit at continuous time and monotonicity, we see that Lemma
3.1
also holds on
\mathcal{X}^{z}
.
The equality (
3.6
) may not be true for
d_{z}^{*}
, due to the fact that
d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{s}}(\nu_{x;s},\nu_{y;s})
may not be continuous (cf. Lemma
3.6
). In fact, by Proposition
2.12
and Definition
4.22
, for
x,y\in\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
with
r=d_{z}^{*}(x,y)
and
\mathfrak{t}^{z}(y)\leq t=\mathfrak{t}^{z}(x)
, if
t-r^{2}>-(1-\sigma)T
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{s\nearrow t-r^{2}}d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{s}}(\nu_{x;s},\nu_{y;s})\leq\epsilon_{0}r\leq\lim_{s\searrow t-r^{2}}d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{s}}(\nu_{x;s},\nu_{y;s}),

(4.8)

and if
t-r^{2}\leq-(1-\sigma)T
, then

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{-(1-\sigma)T}}(\nu_{x;-(1-\sigma)T},\nu_{y;-(1-\sigma)T})=\epsilon_{0}r.

(4.9)

Proposition 4.23
.

d^{*}_{z}
defines a metric on
\mathcal{R}^{z}
(see (
4.6
)) and a pseudo-metric on
\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
.

Proof.

We first show that if
x,y\in\mathcal{R}^{z}
with
d_{z}^{*}(x,y)=0
, then
x=y
. By (
4.7
),
\mathfrak{t}^{z}(x)=\mathfrak{t}^{z}(y)=t<\mathfrak{t}^{z}(z)
. If
d:=d^{z}_{t}(x,y)>0
, by
[
Bam23
, Definition 9.11]
, there exists a small constant
r>0
such that
P(x,r):=\bigcup_{s\in[t-r^{2},t]}B_{s}(x,r)
and
P(y,r):=\bigcup_{s\in[t-r^{2},t]}B_{s}(x,r)
are disjoint and both contained in
\mathcal{R}^{z}
. Thus, it follows from Proposition
2.21
, by taking a smaller
r
if necessary, that
d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{s}}(\nu_{x;s},\nu_{y;s})>r
for
s
close to
t
. However, this contradicts
d_{z}^{*}(x,y)=0
. Thus, we must have
d^{z}_{t}(x,y)=0
, and hence
x=y
.

Now, the triangle inequality can be proved similarly to Lemma
3.7
. Consequently, the proof is complete.
∎

Remark 4.24
.

If
\mathcal{X}^{z}
is assumed to be past continuous, then
d^{*}_{z}
also defines a metric on
\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
. In fact, we only need to check that
d_{z}^{*}
is positive definite. By the argument in the proof of Proposition
4.23
, if
x,y\in\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
with
d_{z}^{*}(x,y)=0
, then
\mathfrak{t}^{z}(x)=\mathfrak{t}^{z}(y)=t<\mathfrak{t}^{z}(z)
. By the past continuity and
[
Bam23
, (4.22)]
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{s\nearrow t}d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{s}}(\nu_{x;s},\nu_{y;s})=d^{z}_{t}(x,y).

Thus, if
d^{z}_{t}(x,y)>0
, then for
s<t
which is sufficiently close to
t
,
d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{s}}(\nu_{x;s},\nu_{y;s})\geq\frac{1}{2}d^{z}_{t}(x,y)
, which, by (
4.7
), implies
d_{z}^{*}(x,y)>0
. This contradicts the assumption
d_{z}^{*}(x,y)=0
.

With the
d^{*}_{z}
-distance, one can prove in the same manner that Propositions
3.12
,
3.15
and Lemma
3.16
in Section 4 hold for
\mathcal{X}^{z}
.

Lemma 4.25
.

\mathcal{R}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}
is dense in
\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}
with respect to the
d_{z}^{*}
-distance.

Proof.

Given
x\in\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}
with
t=\mathfrak{t}^{z}(x)
, we choose a small
r>0
. Let
z\in\mathcal{X}^{z}_{t-r^{2}}
be an
H_{n}
-center of
x
. Since
(\mathcal{X}^{z}_{t-r^{2}},d^{z}_{t-r^{2}})
is the metric completion of
(\mathcal{R}^{z}_{t-r^{2}},g^{z}_{t-r^{2}})
by Theorem
4.17
, we can find
z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}^{z}_{t-r^{2}}
with
d^{z}_{t-r^{2}}(z,z^{\prime})<r
.

By Proposition
3.12
and Lemma
3.16
, we have

\displaystyle d^{*}_{z}(x,z^{\prime})\leq d^{*}_{z}(x,z)+d^{*}_{z}(z,z^{\prime})\leq\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\sqrt{H_{n}}r+\epsilon_{0}^{-1}r.

Therefore, the conclusion follows if we choose a sequence
r=r_{i}\to 0
.
∎

We set
(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}},d^{*}_{z},\mathfrak{t}^{z})
to be the quotient space of
(\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}},d^{*}_{z})
by the relation that
x=y
if and only if
d^{*}_{z}(x,y)=0
. Here,
\mathfrak{t}^{z}
is the natural quotient of the time-function
\mathfrak{t}^{z}
on
\mathcal{X}^{z}
. Note that by Lemma
4.25
, we can regard
\mathcal{R}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}
as a dense open subset of
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}}
.

Next, we prove the following lemma.

Lemma 4.26
.

For any
x,y\in\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}
, suppose

\displaystyle x_{i}^{*}\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathfrak{C},J\hskip 8.19447pt}x\quad\text{and}\quad y_{i}^{*}\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathfrak{C},J\hskip 8.19447pt}y.

Then, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{i}^{*}(x^{*}_{i},y^{*}_{i})=d^{*}_{z}(x,y).

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
t=\mathfrak{t}^{z}(x)\geq s=\mathfrak{t}^{z}(y)
. Moreover, we set
t_{i}=\mathfrak{t}_{i}(x_{i}^{*})
,
s_{i}=\mathfrak{t}_{i}(y_{i}^{*})
,
r_{i}=d_{i}^{*}(x^{*}_{i},y^{*}_{i})
and
r_{\infty}=d^{*}_{z}(x,y)
. Note that
\lim_{i\to\infty}t_{i}=t
and
\lim_{i\to\infty}s_{i}=s
. By taking a subsequence,
\lim_{i\to\infty}r_{i}=r_{\infty}^{\prime}
. It suffices to show that
r_{\infty}^{\prime}=r_{\infty}
.

Case 1
:
t-(r_{\infty}^{\prime})^{2}>-(1-\sigma)T
.

By (
3.6
), we have for large
i
,

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{\max\{t_{i},s_{i}\}-r_{i}^{2}}(\nu_{x_{i}^{*};\max\{t_{i},s_{i}\}-r_{i}^{2}},\nu_{y_{i}^{*};\max\{t_{i},s_{i}\}-r_{i}^{2}})=\epsilon_{0}r_{i}.

If
r_{\infty}^{\prime}<r_{\infty}
, by (
4.8
) and (
4.9
), we have

\displaystyle\lim_{t^{\prime}\searrow\max\{t-r_{\infty}^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}}d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{t^{\prime}}}(\nu_{x;t^{\prime}},\nu_{y;t^{\prime}})\geq\epsilon_{0}r_{\infty}.

Choose a positive
\delta<\min\{\frac{1}{100}(r_{\infty}^{2}-(r_{\infty}^{\prime})^{2}),t-(r_{\infty}^{\prime})^{2}+(1-\sigma)T\}
such that the convergence (
4.5
) is uniform at
t-(r_{\infty}^{\prime})^{2}-\delta
. Thus, it follows from
[
Bam23
, Theorem 6.15]
that
x_{i}^{*}\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\mathfrak{C},J}x
and
y_{i}^{*}\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\mathfrak{C},J}y
are uniformly at time
t-(r_{\infty}^{\prime})^{2}-\delta
. Then by Definition
4.7
, we know that within correspondence (which we omit the map
\varphi
for simplicity)

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{W_{1}}^{t-(r_{\infty}^{\prime})^{2}-\delta}(\nu_{x_{i}^{*};t-(r_{\infty}^{\prime})^{2}-\delta},\nu_{x;t-(r_{\infty}^{\prime})^{2}-\delta})=0,

and similarly,

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{W_{1}}^{t-(r_{\infty}^{\prime})^{2}-\delta}(\nu_{y_{i}^{*};t-(r_{\infty}^{\prime})^{2}-\delta},\nu_{y;t-(r_{\infty}^{\prime})^{2}-\delta})=0.

Thus, by Proposition
2.12
, we obtain

\displaystyle\epsilon_{0}r_{\infty}^{\prime}=\lim_{i\to\infty}\epsilon_{0}r_{i}

\displaystyle=\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{W_{1}}^{\max\{t_{i},s_{i}\}-r_{i}^{2}}(\nu_{x_{i}^{*};\max\{t_{i},s_{i}\}-r_{i}^{2}},\nu_{y_{i}^{*};\max\{t_{i},s_{i}\}-r_{i}^{2}})

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{W_{1}}^{t-(r_{\infty}^{\prime})^{2}-\delta}(\nu_{x_{i}^{*};t-(r_{\infty}^{\prime})^{2}-\delta},\nu_{y_{i}^{*};t-(r_{\infty}^{\prime})^{2}-\delta})

\displaystyle=d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{t-(r_{\infty}^{\prime})^{2}-\delta}}(\nu_{x;t-(r_{\infty}^{\prime})^{2}-\delta},\nu_{y;t-(r_{\infty}^{\prime})^{2}-\delta})

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{t^{\prime}\searrow\max\{t-r_{\infty}^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}}d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{t^{\prime}}}(\nu_{x;t^{\prime}},\nu_{y;t^{\prime}})\geq\epsilon_{0}r_{\infty}.

This contradicts the assumption that
r_{\infty}^{\prime}<r_{\infty}
. Similarly, if
r_{\infty}^{\prime}>r_{\infty}>0
, then by (
4.8
),

\displaystyle\lim_{t^{\prime}\nearrow t-r_{\infty}^{2}}d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{t^{\prime}}}(\nu_{x;t^{\prime}},\nu_{y;t^{\prime}})\leq\epsilon_{0}r_{\infty}.

Choose
\delta<\min\{\frac{1}{100}((r_{\infty}^{\prime})^{2}-r_{\infty}^{2}),t-r_{\infty}^{2}+(1-\sigma)T\}
such that the convergence is uniform at
t-r_{\infty}^{2}-\delta
, then, for the same reason as before, we obtain

\displaystyle\epsilon_{0}r_{\infty}

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{t^{\prime}\nearrow t-r_{\infty}^{2}}d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{t^{\prime}}}(\nu_{x;t^{\prime}},\nu_{y;t^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\geq d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{t-r_{\infty}^{2}-\delta}}(\nu_{x;t-r_{\infty}^{2}-\delta},\nu_{y;t-r_{\infty}^{2}-\delta})

\displaystyle=\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{W_{1}}^{t-r_{\infty}^{2}-\delta}(\nu_{x_{i}^{*};t-r_{\infty}^{2}-\delta},\nu_{y_{i}^{*};t-r_{\infty}^{2}-\delta})

\displaystyle\geq\liminf_{i\to\infty}d_{W_{1}}^{\max\{t_{i},s_{i}\}-r_{i}^{2}}(\nu_{x_{i}^{*};\max\{t_{i},s_{i}\}-r_{i}^{2}},\nu_{y_{i}^{*};\max\{t_{i},s_{i}\}-r_{i}^{2}})=\epsilon_{0}r_{\infty}^{\prime}.

This contradicts the assumption that
r_{\infty}^{\prime}>r_{\infty}
. In conclusion, we have shown
r_{\infty}^{\prime}=r_{\infty}
in this case.

Case 2
:
t-(r_{\infty}^{\prime})^{2}\leq-(1-\sigma)T
.

By (
3.6
) and (
3.7
), we have

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{-(1-\sigma)T}(\nu_{x_{i}^{*};-(1-\sigma)T},\nu_{y_{i}^{*};-(1-\sigma)T})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{\max\{t_{i}-r_{i}^{2},s_{i}-r_{i}^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}}(\nu_{x_{i}^{*};\max\{t_{i}-r_{i}^{2},s_{i}-r_{i}^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}},\nu_{y_{i}^{*};\max\{t_{i}-r_{i}^{2},s_{i}-r_{i}^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}})=\epsilon_{0}r_{i}.

If
r_{\infty}^{\prime}<r_{\infty}
, then by (
4.9
), we have

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{-(1-\sigma)T}}(\nu_{x;-(1-\sigma)T},\nu_{y;-(1-\sigma)T})=\epsilon_{0}r_{\infty}.

Since the convergence (
4.5
) is uniform at
-(1-\sigma)T
, by the same argument as above, we can obtain

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{-(1-\sigma)T}}(\nu_{x;-(1-\sigma)T},\nu_{y;-(1-\sigma)T})

\displaystyle=\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{W_{1}}^{-(1-\sigma)T}(\nu_{x_{i}^{*};-(1-\sigma)T},\nu_{y_{i}^{*};-(1-\sigma)T})

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{W_{1}}^{\max\{t_{i}-r_{i}^{2},s_{i}-r_{i}^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}}(\nu_{x_{i}^{*};\max\{t_{i}-r_{i}^{2},s_{i}-r_{i}^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}},\nu_{y_{i}^{*};\max\{t-r_{i}^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}})=\epsilon_{0}r_{\infty}^{\prime},

which contradicts the assumption
r_{\infty}^{\prime}<r_{\infty}
. If
r_{\infty}^{\prime}>r_{\infty}
and
t-r_{\infty}^{2}\leq-(1-\sigma)T
, then
t-(r_{\infty}^{\prime})^{2}<-(1-\sigma)T
. By (
3.7
) and (
4.9
), we have for large
i

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{-(1-\sigma)T}(\nu_{x_{i}^{*};-(1-\sigma)T},\nu_{y_{i}^{*};-(1-\sigma)T})=\epsilon_{0}r_{i}

and

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{-(1-\sigma)T}}(\nu_{x;-(1-\sigma)T},\nu_{y;-(1-\sigma)T})=\epsilon_{0}r_{\infty}.

Since the convergence (
4.5
) is uniform at
-(1-\sigma)T
, we obtain
r_{\infty}=r_{\infty}^{\prime}
, which contradicts
r_{\infty}^{\prime}>r_{\infty}
. If
r_{\infty}^{\prime}>r_{\infty}
and
t-r_{\infty}^{2}>-(1-\sigma)T
, then by (
4.8
),

\displaystyle\lim_{t^{\prime}\nearrow t-r_{\infty}}d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{t^{\prime}}}(\nu_{x;t^{\prime}},\nu_{y;t^{\prime}})\leq\epsilon_{0}r_{\infty}.

Choose
\delta<\min\{\frac{1}{100}((r^{\prime}_{\infty})^{2}-r_{\infty}^{2}),t-r_{\infty}^{2}+(1-\sigma)T\}
such that the convergence is uniform at
t-r_{\infty}^{2}-\delta
, then, by the same reason, we obtain

\displaystyle\epsilon_{0}r_{\infty}

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{t^{\prime}\nearrow t-r_{\infty}^{2}}d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{t^{\prime}}}(\nu_{x,t^{\prime}},\nu_{y,t^{\prime}})\geq d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{t-r_{\infty}^{2}-\delta}}(\nu_{x;t-r_{\infty}^{2}-\delta},\nu_{y;t-r_{\infty}^{2}-\delta})

\displaystyle=\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{W_{1}}^{t-r_{\infty}^{2}-\delta}(\nu_{x_{i}^{*};t-r_{\infty}^{2}-\delta},\nu_{y_{i}^{*};t-r_{\infty}^{2}-\delta})

\displaystyle\geq\liminf_{i\to\infty}d_{W_{1}}^{\max\{t_{i}-r_{i}^{2},s_{i}-r_{i}^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}}(\nu_{x_{i}^{*};\max\{t_{i}-r_{i}^{2},s_{i}-r_{i}^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}},\nu_{y_{i}^{*};\max\{t_{i}-r_{i}^{2},s_{i}-r_{i}^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}})=\epsilon_{0}r_{\infty}^{\prime},

which gives a contradiction. Consequently, the proof of Lemma
4.26
is complete.
∎

The main result of this section is the following identification, which states that
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}}
, equipped with
d_{z}^{*}
-distance, can be isometrically embedded in
Z
with respect to the
d_{z}^{*}
-distance.

Theorem 4.27
.

For any
z\in Z
, there exists an isometric embedding

\displaystyle\iota_{z}:(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}},d^{*}_{z})\longrightarrow(Z,d_{Z})

such that
\iota_{z}(z)=z
and
\mathfrak{t}\circ\iota_{z}=\mathfrak{t}^{z}
. Moreover, for any
y_{i}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}^{i}_{\mathbb{I}}
and
y_{\infty}\in\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}
,

\displaystyle y_{i}^{*}\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathfrak{C},J\hskip 8.19447pt}y_{\infty}

if and only if
y_{i}^{*}\to\iota_{z}(\tilde{y}_{\infty})
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense, where
\tilde{y}_{\infty}
is the quotient of
y_{\infty}
from
\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}
to
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}}
.

Proof.

We choose a countable dense set
\{x_{k}\}
of
\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}
with respect to
d_{z}^{*}
. For each
x_{k}
, it follows from Proposition
4.9
that there exists a sequence
x_{k,i}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}^{i}_{\mathbb{I}}
such that

\displaystyle x_{k,i}^{*}\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathfrak{C},J\hskip 8.19447pt}x_{k}.

By Lemma
4.26
, we have for any
k,l
,

\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{i}^{*}(x_{k,i}^{*},x_{l,i}^{*})=d^{*}_{z}(x_{k},x_{l}).

(4.10)

Next, we assume
x_{k,i}^{*}\to a_{k}\in Z
as
i\to\infty
in the Gromov-Hausdorff sense. It is clear from (
4.10
) that for any
k,l
,

\displaystyle d^{*}_{z}(x_{k},x_{l})=d_{Z}(a_{k},a_{l}).

(4.11)

We define the map
\iota_{z}
that sends
z
to
z
and maps each
\tilde{x}_{k}
to
a_{k}
, where the tilde denotes the quotient from
\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}
to
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}}
. Since
\{\tilde{x}_{k}\}
is dense in
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}}
and
Z
is complete, it follows from (
4.11
) that
\iota_{z}
extends to an isometric embedding
\iota_{z}:\widetilde{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}}\to Z
.

Now, suppose

\displaystyle y_{i}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}}^{i}\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathfrak{C},J\hskip 8.19447pt}y_{\infty}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}}^{z},

and let
y_{\infty}^{\prime}\in Z
be a limit of
y_{i}^{*}
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense.

By our construction, we can find a sequence
x_{k_{j}}\to y_{\infty}
in
d_{z}^{*}
as
j\to\infty
. Since

\displaystyle x_{k_{j},i}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}}^{i}\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathfrak{C},J\hskip 8.19447pt}x_{k_{j}}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}}^{z},

it follows from Lemma
4.26
that
\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{i}^{*}(y_{i}^{*},x_{k_{j},i}^{*})=d_{z}^{*}(y_{\infty},x_{k_{j}})
. Then, we have

\displaystyle d_{Z}(y_{\infty}^{\prime},a_{k_{j}})=\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{i}^{*}(y_{i}^{*},x_{k_{j},i}^{*})=d_{z}^{*}(y_{\infty},x_{k_{j}}).

From our definition of
\iota_{z}
, we conclude that
\iota_{z}(\tilde{y}_{\infty})=y_{\infty}^{\prime}
.

Conversely, suppose

\displaystyle y_{i}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}}^{i}\longrightarrow\iota_{z}(\tilde{y}_{\infty})\in Z

in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense. By Proposition
4.9
, we obtain a sequence
w_{i}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}}^{i}
such that

\displaystyle w_{i}^{*}\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathfrak{C},J\hskip 8.19447pt}y_{\infty}.

In particular, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}\mathfrak{t}_{i}(y_{i}^{*})=\lim_{i\to\infty}\mathfrak{t}_{i}(w_{i}^{*})=\mathfrak{t}^{z}(y_{\infty}).

Moreover,
w_{i}^{*}
converges to
\iota_{z}(\tilde{y}_{\infty})
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense, and hence

\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{i}^{*}(y_{i}^{*},w_{i}^{*})=0.

(4.12)

By Definition
4.7
, we can find
E_{i}\subset[-T,\mathfrak{t}^{z}(y_{\infty}))
such that

\displaystyle|E_{i}|\to 0,\quad\sup_{t\in[-T,\mathfrak{t}^{z}(y_{\infty}))\setminus E_{i}}d_{W_{1}}^{A_{t}}((\varphi_{t}^{i})_{*}\nu_{w_{i}^{*};t},(\varphi_{t}^{\infty})_{*}\nu_{y_{\infty};t})\to 0,

(4.13)

where
\varphi_{t}
is the embedding defined in Definition
4.4
.

By (
4.12
), for any small
\delta>0
, if
i
is sufficiently large,

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{\max\{\mathfrak{t}_{i}(y_{i}^{*})-\delta^{2},\mathfrak{t}_{i}(w_{i}^{*})-\delta^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}}\left(\nu_{y_{i}^{*};\max\{\mathfrak{t}_{i}(y_{i}^{*})-\delta^{2},\mathfrak{t}_{i}(w_{i}^{*})-\delta^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}},\nu_{w_{i}^{*};\max\{\mathfrak{t}_{i}(y_{i}^{*})-\delta^{2},\mathfrak{t}_{i}(w_{i}^{*})-\delta^{2},-(1-\sigma)T\}}\right)\leq\epsilon_{0}\delta,

which implies

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[-T,\max\{\mathfrak{t}_{i}(y_{i}^{*}),\mathfrak{t}_{i}(w_{i}^{*})\}-\delta^{2})}d_{W_{1}}^{t}(\nu_{y_{i}^{*};t},\nu_{w_{i}^{*};t})\leq\epsilon_{0}\delta.

(4.14)

Combining (
4.13
) and (
4.14
), we can find
E_{i}^{\prime}\subset[-T,\mathfrak{t}^{z}(y_{\infty}))
with

\displaystyle|E_{i}^{\prime}|\to 0,\quad\sup_{t\in[-T,\mathfrak{t}^{z}(y_{\infty}))\setminus E^{\prime}_{i}}d_{W_{1}}^{A_{t}}((\varphi_{t}^{i})_{*}\nu_{y_{i}^{*};t},(\varphi_{t}^{\infty})_{*}\nu_{y_{\infty};t})\to 0.

In other words, we conclude that
y_{i}^{*}\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\mathfrak{C},J}y_{\infty}
.
∎

Note that by Proposition
4.23
and Theorem
4.27
,
\mathcal{R}^{z}
can be regarded as a subset of
Z
through the map
\iota_{z}
.

5
Smooth convergence on the regular part

In this section, we consider a Ricci flow limit space
(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t})
obtained from

(M_{i}\times\mathbb{I},d^{*}_{i},p_{i}^{*},\mathfrak{t}_{i})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathrm{pGH}\hskip 8.19447pt}(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t}),

(5.1)

where
\mathcal{X}^{i}=\{M_{i}^{n},(g_{i}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
with base point
p_{i}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}^{i}_{\mathbb{I}}
.

We first introduce the following definition, which is similar to
[
Bam23
, Definition 9.20]
.

Definition 5.1

(Smooth convergence)
.

The Gromov-Hausdorff convergence (
5.1
) is
smooth
at
z\in Z
if there exist a constant
r>0
and a sequence
z_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
converging to
z
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense such that for all
i
,

\displaystyle r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(z_{i}^{*})\geq r,

where
r_{\mathrm{Rm}}
denotes the curvature radius as defined in Definition
2.10
. We denote by
\mathcal{R}\subset Z
the set of points at which (
5.1
) is smooth.

The first main result of this section is the following theorem.

Theorem 5.2
.

The set
\mathcal{R}
, which is open in
Z
, can be realized as a Ricci flow spacetime
(\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{t},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}},g^{Z}_{t})
over
\mathbb{I}
(see Definition
4.16
). Moreover, there exists an increasing sequence of open subsets
U_{1}\subset U_{2}\subset\ldots\subset\mathcal{R}
such that
\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}U_{i}=\mathcal{R}
, and for sufficiently large
i
, there exist open subsets
V_{i}\subset M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
, time-preserving diffeomorphisms
\phi_{i}:U_{i}\to V_{i}
, and a sequence
\epsilon_{i}\to 0
such that the following properties hold:

(a)

We have

\displaystyle\|\phi_{i}^{*}g^{i}-g^{Z}\|_{C^{[\epsilon_{i}^{-1}]}(U_{i})}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon_{i},

\displaystyle\|\phi_{i}^{*}\partial_{\mathfrak{t}_{i}}-\partial_{\mathfrak{t}}\|_{C^{[\epsilon_{i}^{-1}]}(U_{i})}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon_{i},

where
g^{i}
is the spacetime metric induced by
g_{i}(t)
, and
\partial_{\mathfrak{t}_{i}}
is the standard time vector field.

(b)

For
U_{i}^{(2)}=\{(x,y)\in U_{i}\times U_{i}\mid\mathfrak{t}(x)>\mathfrak{t}(y)+\epsilon_{i}\}
,
V_{i}^{(2)}=\{(x^{*},y^{*})\in V_{i}\times V_{i}\mid\mathfrak{t}_{i}(x^{*})>\mathfrak{t}_{i}(y^{*})+\epsilon_{i}\}
and
\phi_{i}^{(2)}:=(\phi_{i},\phi_{i}):U_{i}^{(2)}\to V_{i}^{(2)}
, we have

\displaystyle\|(\phi_{i}^{(2)})^{*}K^{i}-K_{Z}\|_{C^{[\epsilon_{i}^{-1}]}(U_{i}^{(2)})}\leq\epsilon_{i},

where
K^{i}
and
K_{Z}
denote the heat kernels
(M_{i}\times\mathbb{I},g_{i}(t))
and
(\mathcal{R},g^{Z})
, respectively.

(c)

Let
y\in\mathcal{R}
and
y_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
. Then
y_{i}^{*}\to y
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense if and only if
y_{i}^{*}\in V_{i}
for large
i
and
\phi_{i}^{-1}(y_{i}^{*})\to y
in
\mathcal{R}
.

The main idea of the proof is to show that each point
z\in\mathcal{R}
admits an open neighborhood
U_{z}
such that the statements in Theorem
5.2
hold on
U_{z}
. These local neighborhoods are then combined in a standard fashion to construct the desired global structure.

First, we prove

Lemma 5.3
.

For any
z\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{t}<0}
, there exists an open neighborhood
z\in U_{z}\subset\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{t}<0}
such that
U_{z}
is realized as a Ricci flow spacetime
(U_{z},\mathfrak{t},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{z}},g_{t}^{z})
defined on a product domain. That is, the Ricci flow spacetime arises from a conventional Ricci flow on
M^{\prime}\times I^{\prime}
, where
M^{\prime}
is an open manifold and
I^{\prime}
is an open interval.

Moreover, for sufficiently large
i
, there exist open subsets
V_{i}\subset M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
, time-preserving and
\partial_{\mathfrak{t}}
-preserving diffeomorphisms
\phi_{i}:U_{z}\to V_{i}
(that is,
\mathfrak{t}^{z}(\phi_{i})=\mathfrak{t}_{i}
and
\phi_{*}(\partial_{\mathfrak{t}})=\partial_{\mathfrak{t}_{i}}
), and a sequence
\epsilon_{i}\to 0
such that statements (a), (b) and (c) in Theorem
5.2
hold with
U_{i}
replaced by
U_{z}
, and in (c), the point
y
is required to lie in
U_{z}
.

In the proof of Lemma
5.3
, we are free to pass to any further subsequence of
\mathcal{X}^{i}
. Indeed, if Lemma
5.3
does not hold for a given sequence
\mathcal{X}^{i}
, then, after passing to a subsequence, for any large
i
, either a diffeomorphism
\phi_{i}:U_{z}\to V_{i}\subset M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
cannot be found, or or such a diffeomorphism exists but no sequence
\epsilon_{i}\to 0
can be chosen so that statements (a), (b), and (c) all hold. However, since we may always extract a further subsequence so that Lemma
5.3
holds, a diffeomorphism
\phi_{i}:U_{z}\to V_{i}
can always be found for all large
i
. If, for instance, statement (a) fails to hold for such
\phi_{i}
, then we would have

\displaystyle\|\phi_{i}^{*}g^{i}-g^{z}\|_{C^{[\epsilon_{i}^{-1}]}(U_{z})}+\|\phi_{i}^{*}\partial_{\mathfrak{t}_{i}}-\partial_{\mathfrak{t}}\|_{C^{[\epsilon_{i}^{-1}]}(U_{z})}\geq\epsilon>0

for some constant
\epsilon>0
and all large
i
. But this contradicts the possibility of taking a further subsequence, hence such a violation cannot occur.

Proof.

Given
z\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{t}<0}
, by Definition
5.1
, there exist a sequence
z_{i}^{*}=(z_{i},t_{i})\in M_{i}\times[-(1-2\sigma)T,0)
converging to
z
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense and a constant
r>0
such that

\displaystyle r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(z_{i}^{*})\geq r.

We choose a small constant
\delta\in(0,1/10)
to be determined later and set
w_{i}^{*}:=(z_{i},t_{i}+3\delta^{2}r^{2})\in M_{i}\times(-(1-2\sigma)T,0)
. By the definition of the curvature radius, we have for any
t\in[t_{i},t_{i}+3\delta^{2}r^{2}]
,

\displaystyle|\mathrm{Rm}_{g_{i}}(z_{i},t)|\leq r^{-2}.

By Proposition
2.21
(i), Proposition
3.12
(1), and Lemma
3.16
, we have
d_{i}^{*}(z_{i}^{*},w_{i}^{*})\leq C_{1}\delta r
for a constant
C_{1}=C_{1}(n,Y,\sigma)
. By passing to a subsequence, we may assume that
w_{i}^{*}\to w\in Z_{(-(1-2\sigma)T,0)}
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense. Furthermore, by extracting a further subsequence, there exists a correspondence
\mathfrak{C}
such that

(\mathcal{X}^{i},(\nu_{w_{i}^{*};t})_{t\in[-T,\mathfrak{t}(w_{i}^{*})]})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathbb{F},\mathfrak{C},J\hskip 8.19447pt}(\mathcal{X}^{w},(\nu_{w;t})_{t\in[-T,\mathfrak{t}(w)]}).

(5.2)

Next, we choose a time
s\in[\mathfrak{t}(z)+\delta^{2}r^{2},\mathfrak{t}(z)+2\delta^{2}r^{2}]
such that the convergence (
5.2
) is uniform at time
s
. By Proposition
2.14
and Proposition
2.21
(i), we have

\nu_{w_{i}^{*};s}\left(B_{g_{i}(s)}(z_{i},C_{2}\delta r)\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}

for some constant
C_{2}=C_{2}(n)>0
. It follows from the definition of
d_{GW_{1}}
-convergence that, after passing to a further subsequence, there exists
y_{i}^{*}=(y_{i},s)
with
y_{i}\in B_{g_{i}(s)}(z_{i},C_{2}\delta r)
so that
y_{i}^{*}
strictly converges to a point
y\in\mathcal{X}^{w}_{s}
. By
[
Bam23
, Theorem 6.13 (b)]
, this implies
y_{i}^{*}\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathfrak{C},J\hskip 8.19447pt}y
.

Now, we can choose
\delta
to be small so that on the parabolic ball
P_{i}:=B_{g_{i}(s)}(y_{i},r/2)\times[s-r^{2}/4,s]
, the curvature
|\mathrm{Rm}_{g_{i}}|
is bounded by
4r^{-2}
, and
z_{i}\in B_{g_{i}(s)}(y_{i},r/4)
. Then, it follows from
[
Bam23
, Theorem 9.24]
and the smooth convergence in Theorem
4.17
that, by taking a further subsequence,
z_{i}^{*}\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathfrak{C},J\hskip 8.19447pt}z_{\infty}\in\mathcal{R}^{w}
. By Theorem
4.27
and the fact that
z_{i}^{*}
converges to
z
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense, it follows that
\iota_{w}(z_{\infty})=z
.

Finally, the conclusion follows by observing that around
z_{\infty}
, we can find an open set
U\subset\mathcal{R}^{w}
for which the statements in the lemma hold (see Theorem
4.17
(3)). Through the isometric embedding
\iota_{w}:\mathcal{R}^{w}\to Z
, we define
U_{z}=\iota_{w}(U)
.
∎

After possibly shrinking
U_{z}
, we may find a locally finite cover
\{U_{z_{i}}\}
of
\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{t}<0}
. Then, using a standard center of mass construction (see, for instance,
[
Bam23
, Page 1268]
), we can glue all those
\{U_{z_{i}}\}
so that the following result holds.

Lemma 5.4
.

The set
\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{t}<0}
, which is open in
Z
, can be realized as a Ricci flow spacetime
(\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{t}<0},\mathfrak{t},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}},g^{Z}_{t})
. Moreover, there exists an increasing sequence
U_{1}\subset U_{2}\subset\ldots\subset\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{t}<0}
of open subsets with
\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}U_{i}=\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{t}<0}
. In addition, for sufficiently large
i
, there exist open subsets
V_{i}\subset M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
, time-preserving diffeomorphisms
\phi_{i}:U_{i}\to V_{i}
and a sequence
\epsilon_{i}\to 0
such that statements (a), (b) and (c) in Theorem
5.2
hold.

Next, we extend the Ricci flow spacetime
\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{t}<0}
to
\mathcal{R}
so that Theorem
5.2
holds. Notice that this follows from the next lemma. Indeed, once this lemma is proved, one can find a locally finite cover
\{U_{z_{i}}\}
of
\mathcal{R}
and then glue them together as Lemma
5.4
.

Lemma 5.5
.

For any
z\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
, there exists an open neighborhood
z\in U_{z}\subset\mathcal{R}
such that
U_{z}
is realized as a Ricci flow spacetime
(U_{z},\mathfrak{t},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{z}},g_{t}^{z})
defined on a product domain. Moreover, for sufficiently large
i
, there exist open subsets
V_{i}\subset M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
, time-preserving and
\partial_{\mathfrak{t}}
-preserving diffeomorphisms
\phi_{i}:U_{z}\to V_{i}
, and a sequence
\epsilon_{i}\to 0
such that statements (a), (b) and (c) in Theorem
5.2
hold with
U_{i}
replaced by
U_{z}
, and in (c), the point
y
is required to lie in
U_{z}
.

Proof.

Given
z\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
, by Definition
5.1
, there exist a sequence
z_{i}^{*}=(z_{i},t_{i})\in M_{i}\times(-(1-2\sigma)T,0]
converging to
z
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense and a constant
r>0
such that on
B_{g_{i}(-r^{2})}(z_{i},r)\times[-r^{2},0]
,

r_{\mathrm{Rm}}\geq r.

(5.3)

We choose
w_{i}^{*}:=(z_{i},-r^{2})\in M_{i}\times(-(1-2\sigma)T,0)
. By taking a subsequence, we assume
w_{i}^{*}\to w\in\mathcal{R}_{(-(1-2\sigma)T,0)}
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense. By (
5.3
) and Lemma
5.4
, we have a product domain:

\displaystyle B_{g^{Z}_{-r^{2}}}(w,r/2)\times[-r^{2},0)\to\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{t}<0}.

In addition, under this identification, the curvature of any spacetime point in
B_{g^{Z}_{-r^{2}}}(w,r/2)\times[-r^{2},0)
is bounded by
4r^{-2}
. Therefore, the metric
g^{Z}_{t}
, restricted on
B_{g^{Z}_{-r^{2}}}(w,r/2)\times[-r^{2},0)
, can be extended to a Ricci flow spacetime
B_{g^{Z}_{-r^{2}}}(w,r/2)\times[-r^{2},0]
.

For simplicity, for any
x\in B_{g^{Z}_{-r^{2}}}(w,r/2)
and
t\in[-r^{2},0)
, we set
x^{t}
to be the flow of
x
along
\partial_{\mathfrak{t}}
such that
x^{-r^{2}}=x
.

Claim 1:

w^{t}
converges to
z
in
d_{Z}
as
t\to 0
. Moreover, for any
x\in B_{g^{Z}_{-r^{2}}}(w,r/2)
,
x^{t}
converges to a point in
Z_{0}
as
t\to 0
.

Proof of Claim 1
: Given
s,t\in[-r^{2},0)
with
s\leq t
, it follows from Lemma
5.4
that
\phi_{i}^{-1}(z_{i},t)\to w^{t}
and
\phi_{i}^{-1}(z_{i},s)\to w^{s}
as
i\to\infty
. By Proposition
2.21
and Lemma
3.16
, we have

\displaystyle d_{i}^{*}((z_{i},t),(z_{i},s))\leq C(n,Y,\sigma)\sqrt{t-s}\quad\text{and}\quad d_{i}^{*}((z_{i},t),z_{i}^{*})\leq C(n,Y,\sigma)\sqrt{|t|}.

By the convergence (
5.1
), we conclude that

\displaystyle d_{Z}(w^{t},w^{s})\leq C(n,Y,\sigma)\sqrt{t-s}\quad\text{and}\quad d_{Z}(w^{t},z)\leq C(n,Y,\sigma)\sqrt{|t|}.

Thus,
w^{t}\to z
in
d_{Z}
as
t\to 0
. The other conclusion can be proved similarly.

Next, we define a map
\psi:B:=B_{g^{Z}_{-r^{2}}}(w,r/4)\to Z_{0}
so that
\psi(x)=\lim_{t\to 0}x^{t}
for any
x\in B
.

Claim 2:

\psi
is injective. Moreover,
\psi(B)
contains an open neighborhood of
z
in
Z_{0}
.

Proof of Claim 2
: For any
a,b\in B
with
a\neq b
and
t\in[-r^{2},0)
, we can find
a_{i}^{*}=(a_{i},t),b_{i}^{*}=(b_{i},t)
with
a_{i},b_{i}\in B_{g_{i}(-r^{2})}(z_{i},r/2)
such that
(a_{i},-r^{2})\to a
and
(b_{i},-r^{2})\to b
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense. By smooth convergence and distance distortion, we have for large
i
,

\displaystyle d_{g_{i}(t)}(a_{i}^{*},b_{i}^{*})\geq c_{0}d_{g^{Z}_{-r^{2}}}(a,b)

for a constant
c_{0}>0
. Since
r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(a_{i}^{*})\geq r
, it follows from Proposition
2.21
(i) that

\displaystyle d_{i}^{*}(a_{i}^{*},b_{i}^{*})\geq c_{1}d_{g^{Z}_{-r^{2}}}(a,b)

for a constant
c_{1}>0
. By taking the limit, we obtain
d_{Z}(a^{t},b^{t})\geq c_{1}d_{g^{Z}_{-r^{2}}}(a,b)
and hence
\psi(a)\neq\psi(b)
.

Suppose
y\in Z_{0}
with
d_{Z}(z,y)\leq\epsilon r
for a small constant
\epsilon
to be determined later. We can choose
y_{i}^{*}=(y_{i},0)\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
so that
y_{i}^{*}\to y
. For sufficiently large
i
, we know
d_{i}^{*}(y_{i}^{*},z_{i}^{*})\leq 2\epsilon r
. If
\epsilon\leq\epsilon(n)
, we conclude that
y_{i}\in B_{g_{i}(-r^{2})}(z_{i},r/10)
by the definition of the
d^{*}
-distance and
[
Bam23
, Proposition 9.16 (b)]
. By taking a subsequence, we assume that
(y_{i},-r^{2})
converges to
a\in B
. Then, it is clear that
y=\psi(a)
.

By Claim 1 and Claim 2, we obtain an embedding
