B_{g^{Z}_{-r^{2}}}(w,c_{3}r)\times[-r^{2},0]
into
Z
for a small constant
c_{3}=c_{3}(n)>0
, whose image is an open neighborhood of
z
. Moreover, there exists an embedding
\phi_{i}^{z}:U_{z}:=B_{g^{Z}_{-r^{2}}}(w,c_{3}r)\times[-r^{2},0]\to B_{g^{i}_{-r^{2}}}(z_{i},r)\times[-r^{2},0]
. Then, it can be checked easily that statements (a), (b) and (c) in Theorem
5.2
hold.
∎

Next, we prove that the map
\iota_{z}
obtained in Theorem
4.27
is an isometric embedding for Ricci flow spacetimes.

Proposition 5.6
.

For any
z\in Z
, let
\mathcal{X}^{z}
denote the metric flow associated with
z
, and let
(\mathcal{R}^{z},\mathfrak{t},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{z}},g^{z})
be the Ricci flow spacetime of
\mathcal{X}^{z}
obtained in Theorem
4.17
(1). Then, the time-preserving map

\displaystyle\iota_{z}:(\mathcal{R}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}},\mathfrak{t}^{z})\to(\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{t}),

which is the restriction of the map
\iota_{z}
from Theorem
4.27
to
\mathcal{R}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}
, satisfies the following properties:

(i)

(\iota_{z})_{*}(\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{z}})=\partial_{\mathfrak{t}}
and
(\iota_{z})^{*}g^{Z}=g^{z}
.

(ii)

For any
x,y\in\mathcal{R}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}
,
K_{Z}(\iota_{z}(x);\iota_{z}(y))=K^{z}(x;y)
, where
K^{z}
denotes the heat kernel of
\mathcal{R}^{z}
.

(iii)

If
z\in\mathcal{R}
, then
K_{Z}(z;\iota_{z}(y))=K^{z}(z;y)
for any
y\in\mathcal{R}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}
.

Proof.

Given
z\in Z
, we choose a sequence
z_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
converging to
z
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense and a correspondence
\mathfrak{C}
such that

(\mathcal{X}^{i},(\nu_{z_{i}^{*};t})_{t\in[-T,\mathfrak{t}(z_{i}^{*})]})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathbb{F},\mathfrak{C},J\hskip 8.19447pt}(\mathcal{X}^{z},(\nu_{z;t})_{t\in[-T,\mathfrak{t}(z)]}).

By Theorem
4.17
(3), there exists an increasing sequence
U^{z}_{1}\subset U^{z}_{2}\subset\ldots\subset\mathcal{R}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}
of open subsets with
\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}U^{z}_{i}=\mathcal{R}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}
. In addition, for sufficiently large
i
, there exist open subsets
V^{z}_{i}\subset M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
, time-preserving diffeomorphisms
\phi^{z}_{i}:U^{z}_{i}\to V^{z}_{i}
and a sequence
\epsilon_{i}\to 0
such that all statements in Theorem
4.17
(3) hold.

On the other hand, there exists an increasing sequence
U_{1}\subset U_{2}\subset\ldots\subset\mathcal{R}
of open subsets with
\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}U_{i}=\mathcal{R}
. In addition, for sufficiently large
i
, there exist open subsets
V_{i}\subset M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
, time-preserving diffeomorphisms
\phi_{i}:U_{i}\to V_{i}
and a sequence
\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}\to 0
such that statements (a), (b) and (c) in Theorem
5.2
hold, with
\epsilon_{i}
replaced by
\epsilon_{i}^{\prime}
.

(i): For any
w\in\mathcal{R}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}
, we choose a sequence
w_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
so that
w_{i}^{*}\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathfrak{C},J\hskip 8.19447pt}w
. By Theorem
4.17
(3)(b), we conclude that

\displaystyle(\phi_{i}^{z})^{-1}(w_{i}^{*})\to w\quad\text{in}\quad\mathcal{R}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}.

By Lemma
4.26
,
d_{i}^{*}(z_{i}^{*},w_{i}^{*})
is uniformly bounded. Moreover, by the smooth convergence, we know that
r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(w_{i}^{*})\geq r>0
for a constant
r
. Now, we assume, by taking a subsequence,
w_{i}^{*}\to w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense. Then by Theorem
5.2
(c), we know

\displaystyle(\phi_{i})^{-1}(w_{i}^{*})\to w^{\prime}\quad\text{in}\quad\mathcal{R}.

Then, we can find small open neighborhoods
U_{w}\subset\mathcal{R}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}
and
U_{w^{\prime}}\subset\mathcal{R}
around
w
and
w^{\prime}
, respectively, such that map
\psi_{i}:=\phi_{i}^{-1}\circ\phi_{i}^{z}:U_{w}\to U_{w^{\prime}}
, for sufficiently large
i
, is well defined and a diffeomorphism. Moreover, by Theorem
4.17
(3)(a) and Theorem
5.2
(a), we may assume, by shrinking the open neighborhoods and taking a further subsequence, that
\psi_{i}
converges smoothly to a diffeomorphism
\psi_{\infty}
. In addition, it follows from Theorem
4.17
(3)(a) and Theorem
5.2
(a) again that

\displaystyle(\psi_{\infty})_{*}(\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{z}})=\partial_{\mathfrak{t}}\quad\text{and}\quad(\psi_{\infty})^{*}g^{Z}=g^{z}.

Note that by Theorem
4.27
,
\iota_{z}
agrees with
\psi_{\infty}
on
U_{w}
. Thus, we have proved that on
\mathcal{R}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}
,

\displaystyle(\iota_{z})_{*}(\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{z}})=\partial_{\mathfrak{t}}\quad\text{and}\quad(\iota_{z})^{*}g^{Z}=g^{z}.

(ii): For any
x,y\in\mathcal{R}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}
with
\mathfrak{t}^{z}(x)>\mathfrak{t}^{z}(y)
, we find an open set
U\subset\mathcal{R}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}
containing
x
and
y
so that the closure
\bar{U}
of
U
is a compact set in
\mathcal{R}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}
. Then for sufficiently large
i
, we have
\bar{U}\subset U_{i}^{z}
. By smooth convergence, we conclude that

\displaystyle r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(w^{*})\geq r>0

for a constant
r
and any
w^{*}\in D_{i}:=\phi_{i}^{z}(\bar{U})
. Since
\bar{U}
is compact, it is easy to see, by using Proposition
2.21
(i), Proposition
3.12
(1) and Lemma
3.16
, that

\displaystyle\sup_{w\in\bar{U}}d_{z}^{*}(z,w)<\infty.

By Lemma
4.26
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\sup_{w^{*}\in D_{i}}d_{i}^{*}(z_{i}^{*},w^{*})\leq C

for a constant
C
. After passing to a subsequence, we assume
D_{i}\to D\subset\mathcal{R}
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense. Arguing as before,
\iota_{z}|_{\bar{U}}
is the smooth limit of
\psi_{i}
. Thus, it follows from Theorem
4.17
(3)(a) and Theorem
5.2
(b) that

\displaystyle K_{Z}(\iota_{z}(x);\iota_{z}(y))=K^{z}(x;y).

(iii): If
z\in\mathcal{R}
, then it follows from Theorem
4.17
(3)(a) that for any
x\in\mathcal{R}^{z}_{[-T,\mathfrak{t}(z))}
,

\displaystyle K^{z}(z;x)=\lim_{i\to\infty}K^{i}(z_{i}^{*};x_{i}^{*}),

where
x_{i}^{*}=\phi_{i}^{z}(x)
. By Theorem
5.2
(b), we conclude that

\displaystyle K^{z}(z;x)=K_{Z}(z;\iota_{z}(x)).

In summary, this completes the proof.
∎

As a corollary of Proposition
5.6
, we prove:

Corollary 5.7
.

\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
is dense in
Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
with respect to
d_{Z}
.

Proof.

For any
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
, we consider its associated metric flow
\mathcal{X}^{z}
. For any
s<\mathfrak{t}(z)
, we choose an
H_{n}
-center
w\in\mathcal{R}^{z}_{s}
. By Lemma
3.16
, we have

\displaystyle d_{z}^{*}(z,w)\leq\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\sqrt{H_{n}(\mathfrak{t}(z)-s)}.

Then, it follows from Theorem
4.27
that

\displaystyle d_{Z}(z,\iota_{z}(w))\leq\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\sqrt{H_{n}(\mathfrak{t}(z)-s)}.

By choosing
s=s_{i}\nearrow\mathfrak{t}(z)
, the conclusion follows.
∎

Next, we define conjugate heat kernel measures on
\mathcal{R}
.

Definition 5.8

(Conjugate heat kernel measures on
\mathcal{R}
)
.

For any
x\in\mathcal{R}
, we define the
conjugate heat kernel measure

\nu_{x;s}
based at
x
, to be the Borel measure on
(\mathcal{R}_{s},g^{Z}_{s})
given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\nu_{x;s}:=K_{Z}(x;\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z}_{s}}

for any
s<\mathfrak{t}(x)
, and set
\nu_{x;\mathfrak{t}(x)}=\delta_{x}
.

Lemma 5.9
.

For any
x\in\mathcal{R}
,
\nu_{x;s}
is a probability measure on
(\mathcal{R}_{s},g^{Z}_{s})
.

Proof.

Given
x\in\mathcal{R}
and
s<\mathfrak{t}(x)
, we choose
x_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
so that
x_{i}^{*}\to x
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense. Then, it follows from Theorem
5.2
(b) that

\displaystyle\nu_{x;s}(\mathcal{R}_{s})\leq\liminf_{i\to\infty}\nu_{x_{i}^{*};s}(M_{i})=1.

On the other hand, it follows from Proposition
5.6
(iii) that
K_{Z}(x;\iota_{x}(y))=K^{x}(x;y)
for any
y\in\mathcal{R}^{x}_{\mathbb{I}}
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\nu_{x;s}(\mathcal{R}_{s})\geq\nu_{x;s}(\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{s}))=1.

Combining these inequalities, we conclude
\nu_{x;s}(\mathcal{R}_{s})=1
.
∎

In the proof of Lemma
5.9
, we have the following corollary.

Corollary 5.10
.

For any
x\in\mathcal{R}
and
s<\mathfrak{t}(x)
,

\displaystyle\{y\in\mathcal{R}_{s}\mid K_{Z}(x;y)>0\}=\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{s}).

In particular,
\nu_{x;s}\left(\mathcal{R}_{s}\setminus\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{s})\right)=0
.

Lemma 5.11
.

For any
x,y\in\mathcal{R}
and
s<\min\{\mathfrak{t}(x),\mathfrak{t}(y)\}
, either
\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{s})=\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{s})
or
\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{s})\cap\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{s})=\emptyset
. Moreover, if the latter happens, we have
\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{t})\cap\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{t})=\emptyset
for any
t\in[s,\min\{\mathfrak{t}(x),\mathfrak{t}(y)\})
.

Proof.

If
\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{s})\neq\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{s})
and
\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{s})\cap\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{s})\neq\emptyset
, then we may assume, without loss of generality, that there exists
w\in\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{s})
which lies in the boundary of
\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{s})
. By Corollary
5.10
,
K_{Z}(x;w)=0
. Since
K_{Z}(x;\cdot)
satisfies the conjugate heat equation, it follows from the strong maximum principle that on
B_{g^{Z}_{s}}(w,\delta)\times(s,s+\delta]\subset\mathcal{R}
,
K_{Z}(x;\cdot)
vanishes. However, there exists a point
w^{\prime}\in B_{g^{Z}_{s}}(w,\delta)\times(s,s+\delta]\bigcap\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{s})
by our assumption. This contradicts Corollary
5.10
.

If
\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{s})\cap\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{s})=\emptyset
and
\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{t})=\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{t})
for some
t\in[s,\min\{\mathfrak{t}(x),\mathfrak{t}(y)\})
, then, for any
w\in\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{t})
, it follows from Proposition
5.6
(ii) that
\nu_{w;s}(\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{s}))=1
and
\nu_{w;s}(\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{s}))=1
, which yields a contradiction.

Therefore, the proof is complete.
∎

Corollary 5.12
.

For any
x\in\mathcal{R}
and
s<\mathfrak{t}(x)
,
\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{s})
is a connected component of
\mathcal{R}_{s}
.

Proof.

Argued as in the proof of Lemma
5.3
, one can prove that any
w\in\mathcal{R}_{s}
is contained in
\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{s})
for some
y\in\mathcal{R}
with
\mathfrak{t}(y)>s
. Thus, we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{s}=\bigcup_{y\in\mathcal{R}_{(s,0]}}\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{s}).

Consequently, the conclusion follows from Lemma
5.11
.
∎

Lemma 5.13
.

For any
x\in\mathcal{R}
,
\nu_{x;s}
satisfies the reproduction formula. That is, for any
s<t<\mathfrak{t}(x)
,

\displaystyle\nu_{x;s}=\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\nu_{y;s}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x;t}(y).

Proof.

Since
\nu_{x;s}
satisfies the reproduction formula in
\mathcal{X}^{x}
, we conclude that for any Borel set
S\subset\mathcal{R}_{s}^{x}
,

\nu_{x;s}(S)=\int_{\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{t})}\nu_{y;s}(\iota_{x}(S))\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x;t}(y).

(5.4)

On the other hand, for any
y\in\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{t})
, it follows from Proposition
5.6
(ii) that
\nu_{y;s}(\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{s}))=1
. Therefore, the conclusion follows from (
5.4
) and Corollary
5.10
.
∎

Next, we define the conjugate heat kernel measure at any
z\in Z\setminus\mathcal{R}
. Let
\mathcal{X}^{z}
be a metric flow associated with
z
. For any
s\leq\mathfrak{t}(z)
, the conjugate heat kernel measure
\nu_{z;s}
on
\mathcal{X}^{z}_{s}
, when restricted to
\mathcal{R}^{z}_{s}
, can be regarded through the map
\iota_{z}
as a probability measure on
\mathcal{R}_{s}
. More precisely,

Definition 5.14

(Conjugate heat kernel measures on
Z\setminus\mathcal{R}
)
.

For any
z\in Z\setminus\mathcal{R}
, we define the
conjugate heat kernel measure

\nu_{z;s}
based at
z
, to be the Borel measure on
(\mathcal{R}_{s},g^{Z}_{s})
given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\nu_{z;s}=K_{Z}(z;\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z}_{s}},

for any
s<\mathfrak{t}(z)
, and set
\nu_{z;\mathfrak{t}(z)}=\delta_{z}
. Here,
K_{Z}(z;\cdot):=K^{z}(z;\iota_{z}^{-1}(\cdot))
.

We will prove in Lemma
5.19
that
\nu_{z;s}
is independent of the choice of the associated
\mathcal{X}^{z}
.

It is clear that the conjugate heat kernel measure
\nu_{z;s}
is a probability measure on
(\mathcal{R}_{s},g^{Z}_{s})
and

\displaystyle\nu_{z;s}(\iota_{z}(\mathcal{R}^{z}_{s}))=1.

Moreover, it satisfies the reproduction formula as Lemma
5.13
: for any
s<t<\mathfrak{t}(z)
,

\nu_{z;s}=\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\nu_{y;s}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z;t}(y).

(5.5)

In addition, by the same proof of Lemma
5.11
, we have

Lemma 5.15
.

For any
x,y\in Z
and
s<\min\{\mathfrak{t}(x),\mathfrak{t}(y)\}
, either
\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{s})=\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{s})
or
\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{s})\cap\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{s})=\emptyset
. Moreover, if the latter happens, we have
\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{t})\cap\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{t})=\emptyset
for any
t\in[s,\min\{\mathfrak{t}(x),\mathfrak{t}(y)\})
. In particular,
\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{s})
is a connected component of
\mathcal{R}_{s}
for any
x\in Z
.

Conversely, we have

Proposition 5.16
.

For any
x,y\in Z
, if
\max\{\mathfrak{t}(x),\mathfrak{t}(y)\}-d^{2}_{Z}(x,y)>-(1-2\sigma)T
, then
\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{t})=\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{t})
for any
t\in[-(1-2\sigma)T,\max\{\mathfrak{t}(x),\mathfrak{t}(y)\}-d^{2}_{Z}(x,y))
. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{t\nearrow\max\{\mathfrak{t}(x),\mathfrak{t}(y)\}-d^{2}_{Z}(x,y)}d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{x}_{t}}\left(\nu_{x;t},(\iota^{-1}_{x})_{*}(\nu_{y;t})\right)\leq\epsilon_{0}d_{Z}(x,y),

(5.6)

where we regard
(\iota^{-1}_{x})_{*}(\nu_{y;t})
as a probability measure on
\mathcal{X}^{x}_{t}
by extension from
\mathcal{R}^{x}_{t}
.

Proof.

We set
r=d_{Z}(x,y)
and
t_{0}=\max\{\mathfrak{t}(x),\mathfrak{t}(y)\}-r^{2}
. Then we choose
x^{*}_{i},y^{*}_{i}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
so that
x^{*}_{i}\to x
and
y^{*}_{i}\to y
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense. In particular,

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{i}^{*}(x_{i}^{*},y_{i}^{*})=r.

Then, for sufficiently large
i
, by Definition
3.5
and (
3.6
), we have

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{t_{i}}(\nu_{x_{i}^{*};t_{i}},\nu_{y_{i}^{*};t_{i}})=\epsilon_{0}r_{i},

(5.7)

where
r_{i}:=d_{i}^{*}(x_{i}^{*},y_{i}^{*})
and
t_{i}=\max\{\mathfrak{t}(x_{i}^{*}),\mathfrak{t}(y_{i}^{*})\}-r_{i}^{2}
.

We take
-(1-2\sigma)T<s^{\prime}<s<t_{0}
so that
\mathcal{X}^{x}
is continuous at time
s^{\prime}
. Then, by (
5.7
),

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{s}(\nu_{x_{i}^{*};s},\nu_{y_{i}^{*};s})\leq 2\epsilon_{0}r

(5.8)

for large
i
. Next, we set
a_{i}^{*},b_{i}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}^{i}_{s}
to be
H_{n}
-centers of
x_{i}^{*}
and
y_{i}^{*}
, respectively. Then, by (
5.8
), we have

\displaystyle d_{g_{i}(s)}(a_{i}^{*},b_{i}^{*})\leq D_{0}

for a constant
D_{0}
. Thus, for a sufficiently large constant
D_{1}>0
to be determined later, we have

\displaystyle\quad\nu_{x_{i}^{*};s}\left(B_{g_{i}(s)}(a_{i}^{*},D_{1})\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}.

We fix a correspondence
\mathfrak{C}
as in Definition
4.4
. By our assumption, the
\mathbb{F}
-convergence is uniform at
s^{\prime}
. We choose a compact set
K_{\epsilon}\subset\mathcal{X}^{x}_{s^{\prime}}
such that
\nu_{x;s^{\prime}}(K_{\epsilon})\geq 1-\epsilon
. Then, we define

\displaystyle K_{i,\epsilon}:=(\varphi_{s^{\prime}}^{i})^{-1}\left(B_{A_{s^{\prime}}}(\varphi_{s^{\prime}}^{\infty}(K_{\epsilon}),\epsilon)\right)

and hence for sufficiently large
i
,

\displaystyle\nu_{x_{i}^{*};s^{\prime}}(K_{i,\epsilon})\geq 1-2\epsilon.

(5.9)

By the reproduction formula and Definition
4.1
(6), we have for any
w_{i}^{*}\in B_{g_{i}(s)}(a_{i}^{*},D_{1})
,

\displaystyle\nu_{x_{i}^{*};s^{\prime}}(K_{i,\epsilon})=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{X}^{i}_{s}}\nu_{z^{*};s^{\prime}}(K_{i,\epsilon})\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{i}^{*};s}(z^{*})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\nu_{x_{i}^{*};s}\left(\mathcal{X}^{i}_{s}\setminus B_{g_{i}(s)}(w_{i}^{*},2D_{1})\right)+\Phi\left(\Phi^{-1}(\nu_{w_{i}^{*};s^{\prime}}(K_{i,\epsilon}))+2(s-s^{\prime})^{-\frac{1}{2}}D_{1}\right)\nu_{x_{i}^{*};s}\left(B_{g_{i}(s)}(w_{i}^{*},2D_{1})\right).

(5.10)

Since
\nu_{x_{i}^{*};s}\left(B_{g_{i}(s)}(w_{i}^{*},2D_{1})\right)\geq\nu_{x_{i}^{*};s}\left(B_{g_{i}(s)}(a_{i}^{*},D_{1})\right)\geq 1/2
, we obtain from (
5.9
) and (
5.10
) that

\displaystyle\Phi\left(\Phi^{-1}(\nu_{w_{i}^{*};s^{\prime}}(K_{i,\epsilon}))+2(s-s^{\prime})^{-\frac{1}{2}}D_{1}\right)\geq 1-4\epsilon.

Thus, for sufficiently large
i
,

\displaystyle\nu_{w_{i}^{*};s^{\prime}}(K_{i,\epsilon})\geq 1-\Psi(\epsilon|s-s^{\prime},D_{1}),

where
\Psi(\epsilon|s-s^{\prime},D_{1})
denotes a function that goes to
0
as
\epsilon\to 0
, while the other arguments are fixed. By the reproduction formula again, we have

\displaystyle\nu_{y_{i}^{*};s^{\prime}}(K_{i,\epsilon})=\int_{\mathcal{X}^{i}_{s}}\nu_{z^{*};s^{\prime}}(K_{i,\epsilon})\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{y_{i}^{*};s}(z^{*})\geq\left(1-\Psi(\epsilon|s-s^{\prime},D_{1})\right)\nu_{y_{i}^{*};s}\left(B_{g_{i}(s)}(a_{i}^{*},D_{1})\right).

Thus, we can first choose a sufficiently large
D_{1}
so that
\nu_{y_{i}^{*};s}\left(B_{g_{i}(s)}(a_{i}^{*},D_{1})\right)
is almost
1
and then choose
\epsilon
to be small. In other words, we have shown that the sequence
(\varphi_{s^{\prime}}^{i})_{*}\nu_{y_{i}^{*};s^{\prime}}
is tight. By taking a subsequence, this sequence converges weakly to a probability measure
\mu^{\infty}
on
\mathcal{X}^{x}_{s^{\prime}}
. Moreover, by the definition of
K_{i,\epsilon}
, we conclude that
\mathrm{supp}\,\mu^{\infty}\subset\varphi_{s^{\prime}}^{\infty}(\mathcal{X}^{x}_{s^{\prime}})
. By Proposition
2.5
,
(\varphi_{s^{\prime}}^{i})_{*}\nu_{y_{i}^{*};s^{\prime}}
converges to
\mu^{\infty}
in the
d_{W_{1}}
-sense.

Now, we regard
\mu^{\infty}
as a probability measure on
\mathcal{X}^{x}_{s^{\prime}}
and let
\mu_{t}
be the conjugate heat flow on
\mathcal{X}^{z}
for
t\leq s^{\prime}
with
\mu_{s^{\prime}}=\mu^{\infty}
. By
[
Bam23
, Theorem 6.13]
, we conclude that

\displaystyle(\nu_{y_{i}^{*};t})_{t\in[-(1-2\sigma)T,s^{\prime}]}\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathfrak{C},J\hskip 8.19447pt}(\mu_{t})_{t\in[-(1-2\sigma)T,s^{\prime}]}.

Therefore, it follows from
[
Bam23
, Theorem 9.21(f)]
and our construction of
\iota_{x}
that

\displaystyle(\iota_{x})_{*}(\mu_{t}|_{\mathcal{R}^{x}_{t}})=K(y;\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z}_{t}}.

for any
t\in[-(1-2\sigma)T,s^{\prime}]
.

Since
s^{\prime}
can be chosen as close as we want to
t_{0}
, we conclude that
\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{t})=\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{t})
for any
t\in[-(1-2\sigma)T,t_{0})
.

Moreover, by the
d_{W_{1}}
-convergence at
s^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{W_{1}}^{s^{\prime}}(\nu_{x_{i}^{*};s^{\prime}},\nu_{y_{i}^{*};s^{\prime}})=d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{x}_{s^{\prime}}}(\nu_{x;s^{\prime}},\mu^{\infty})=d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{x}_{s^{\prime}}}(\nu_{x;s^{\prime}},(\iota^{-1}_{x})_{*}(\nu_{y;s^{\prime}})).

By monotonicity, the last conclusion holds.
∎

The proof of Proposition
5.16
also yields the following result:

Lemma 5.17
.

For any
x,y\in Z
, suppose that
x_{i}^{*},y_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
converge to
x
and
y
, respectively, in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense. If there exists
t_{0}\in(-(1-2\sigma)T,0)
and a constant
D
such that

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{t_{0}}(\nu_{x_{i}^{*};t_{0}},\nu_{y_{i}^{*};t_{0}})\leq D.

Then
\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{t})=\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{t})
for any
t\in[-(1-2\sigma)T,t_{0})
. Moreover, for any
t_{1}\in[-(1-2\sigma)T,t_{0})
such that
\mathcal{X}^{x}
is continuous at
t_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{x}_{t_{1}}}\left(\nu_{x;t_{1}},(\iota^{-1}_{x})_{*}(\nu_{y;t_{1}})\right)=\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{W_{1}}^{t_{1}}(\nu_{x_{i}^{*};t_{1}},\nu_{y_{i}^{*};t_{1}}).

As a corollary of Proposition
5.16
, we have

Corollary 5.18
.

For any
x,y\in\mathcal{R}
with
r=d_{Z}(x,y)
and
\max\{\mathfrak{t}(x),\mathfrak{t}(y)\}-r^{2}>-(1-2\sigma)T
, then for any
-(1-2\sigma)T\leq s<s^{\prime}<\max\{\mathfrak{t}(x),\mathfrak{t}(y)\}-d^{2}_{Z}(x,y)
and any
w\in\mathcal{R}_{s}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert K_{Z}(x;w)-K_{Z}(y;w)\right\rvert\leq C(n,Y,s^{\prime}-s)\epsilon_{0}r.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.16
, the conclusion is clear if
w\notin\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{s})
, since
K_{Z}(x;w)=K_{Z}(y;w)=0
in this case.

Next, we assume
w\in\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{s})=\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{s})
. It follows from Theorem
2.16
and the smooth convergence in Theorem
5.2
(b) that for any
z\in\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{s^{\prime}})
and
w\in\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{s})
,

\displaystyle|\nabla_{z}K_{Z}(z;w)|

\displaystyle\leq C(n)K_{Z}(z;w)(s^{\prime}-s)^{\frac{n}{2}}\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{C(n)\mathrm{exp}(-\mathcal{N}_{z}(s^{\prime}-s))}{(s^{\prime}-s)^{\frac{n}{2}}K_{Z}(z;w)}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,s^{\prime}-s)K_{Z}(z;w)\sqrt{C(n,Y,s^{\prime}-s)-\log K_{Z}(z;w)}\leq C(n,Y,s^{\prime}-s),

where we used Theorem
2.15
(ii) for the last inequality. Thus, by the definition of
d_{W_{1}}
-distance,

\displaystyle|K_{Z}(x;w)-K_{Z}(y;w)|

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\int_{\mathcal{R}_{s^{\prime}}}K_{Z}(\cdot;w)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x;s^{\prime}}-\int_{\mathcal{R}_{s^{\prime}}}K_{Z}(\cdot;w)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{y;s^{\prime}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C(n,Y,s^{\prime}-s)d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{x}_{s^{\prime}}}\left(\nu_{x;s^{\prime}},(\iota^{-1}_{x})_{*}(\nu_{y;s^{\prime}})\right)\leq C(n,Y,s^{\prime}-s)\epsilon_{0}r,

where we used Proposition
5.16
for the last inequality.
∎

We next prove:

Lemma 5.19
.

For any
z\in Z\setminus\mathcal{R}
, the conjugate heat kernel measure
\nu_{z;s}
defined in Definition
5.14
is independent of the associated metric flow
\mathcal{X}^{z}
.

Proof.

We only need to prove that the conjugate heat kernel
K_{Z}(z;\cdot)
is independent of
\mathcal{X}^{z}
for
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
. We claim that

\displaystyle K_{Z}(z;\cdot)=\lim_{i\to\infty}K_{Z}(x_{i};\cdot)

(5.11)

where
x_{i}\in\mathcal{R}
converge to
z
in
d_{Z}
. Indeed, it is clear from Corollary
5.18
that the limit in (
5.11
) exists and is independent of the choice of
x_{i}
.

On the other hand, we consider the associated metric flow
\mathcal{X}^{z}
from which the conjugate heat kernel measure at
z
is defined. We fix
s<\mathfrak{t}^{z}(z)
and
w\in\mathcal{R}^{z}_{s}
. We choose a sequence
\delta_{i}\searrow 0
so that
y_{i}\in\mathcal{R}^{z}_{\mathfrak{t}^{z}(z)-\delta_{i}^{2}}
is an
H_{n}
-center of
z
. By the same argument as in the proof of Corollary
5.18
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\lvert K^{z}(z;w)-K^{z}(y_{i};w)\right\rvert\leq C\delta_{i}

for a constant
C
independent of
i
. Consequently, by Proposition
5.6
(iii),

\displaystyle K_{Z}(z;\cdot)=\lim_{i\to\infty}K_{Z}(\iota_{z}(y_{i});\cdot).

In sum, the proof is complete.
∎

Now, we prove the following convergence result.

Theorem 5.20
.

For any
z\in Z
, if
z_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
converge to
z
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense, then

\displaystyle K^{i}(z_{i}^{*};\phi_{i}(\cdot))\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}}K_{Z}(z;\cdot)\quad\text{on}\quad\mathcal{R}_{(-\infty,\mathfrak{t}(z))},

where
\phi_{i}
is from Theorem
5.2
.

Proof.

We only need to prove that for any open set
U
such that
\bar{U}\subset\mathcal{R}_{(-\infty,\mathfrak{t}(z))}
is a compact set, we have

\displaystyle K^{i}(z_{i}^{*};\phi_{i}(\cdot))\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{C^{\infty}}K_{Z}(z;\cdot)\quad\text{on}\quad\bar{U}.

Suppose that the conclusion fails, there exists
\delta>0
such that there exists a subsequence, still denoted by
\mathcal{X}^{i}
, such that

\displaystyle\|K_{Z}(z;\cdot)-K^{i}(z_{i}^{*};\phi_{i}(\cdot))\|_{C^{[\delta^{-1}]}(\bar{U})}\geq\delta.

(5.12)

By passing to a further subsequence, there exists a correspondence
\mathfrak{C}
such that

(\mathcal{X}^{i},(\nu_{z_{i}^{*};t})_{t\in[-T,\mathfrak{t}(z_{i}^{*})]})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathbb{F},\mathfrak{C},J\hskip 8.19447pt}(\mathcal{X}^{z},(\nu_{z;t})_{t\in[-T,\mathfrak{t}^{z}(z)]}),

where
\mathcal{X}^{z}
is a metric flow associated with
z
. By Proposition
5.6
, Definition
5.14
and Lemma
5.19
, we have

K^{z}(z;\cdot)=K_{Z}(z;\iota_{z}(\cdot)).

On the other hand, by Theorem
4.17
(3), there exists an increasing sequence
U^{z}_{1}\subset U^{z}_{2}\subset\ldots\subset\mathcal{R}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}
of open subsets with
\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}U^{z}_{i}=\mathcal{R}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}
. In addition, for sufficiently large
i
, there exist open subsets
V^{z}_{i}\subset M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
, time-preserving diffeomorphisms
\phi^{z}_{i}:U^{z}_{i}\to V^{z}_{i}
and a sequence
\epsilon_{i}\to 0
such that all statements in Theorem
4.17
(3) hold. In particular, we have

\displaystyle\|K^{z}(z;\cdot)-K^{i}(z_{i}^{*};\phi^{z}_{i}(\cdot))\|_{C^{[\epsilon_{i}^{-1}]}(U^{z}_{i})}\leq\epsilon_{i},

(5.13)

for a sequence
\epsilon_{i}\to 0
. As in the proof of Proposition
5.6
, the map
\psi_{i}:=\phi_{i}^{-1}\circ\phi_{i}^{z}
converges locally and smoothly to
\iota_{z}
. Note that on
\mathcal{R}\setminus\mathcal{R}^{z}
, we have
K_{Z}(z;\cdot)=0
, and the limit also holds. Since
K_{Z}(z;\iota_{z}(\cdot))=K^{z}(z;\cdot)
, (
5.13
) contradicts (
5.12
) for sufficiently large
i
.

In sum, the proof is complete.
∎

Now, we define the isometry between two noncollapsed Ricci flow limit spaces.

Definition 5.21

(Isometry)
.

Suppose
(Z,d_{Z},z,\mathfrak{t})
and
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
are two pointed noncollapsed Ricci flow limit spaces defined over the same time interval
I
, with regular parts given by the Ricci flow spacetimes
(\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{t},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}},g^{Z})
and
(\mathcal{R}^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}},g^{Z^{\prime}})
, respectively.

We say that
(Z,d_{Z},z,\mathfrak{t})
and
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
are
isometric
if there exists a bijective map
\phi:Z\to Z^{\prime}
satisfying the following conditions:

(i)

\phi(z)=z^{\prime}
.

(ii)

\phi
is time-preserving, that is,
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\circ\phi=\mathfrak{t}
.

(iii)

For any
x,y\in Z
,
d_{Z^{\prime}}(\phi(x),\phi(y))=d_{Z}(x,y)
.

(iv)

\phi(\mathcal{R})=\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
, and
\phi
is an isomorphism of Ricci flow spacetimes between
(\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{t},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}},g^{Z})
and
(\mathcal{R}^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}},g^{Z^{\prime}})
. That is, for any
t\in I
, the restriction
\phi:\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
is a diffeomorphism such that
\phi^{*}g^{Z^{\prime}}=g^{Z}
and
\phi^{*}\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}=\partial_{\mathfrak{t}}
.

It follows immediately from Theorem
5.2
and Lemma
5.19
that any noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space obtained as a pointed Gromov–Hausdorff limit of a given sequence in
\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
(see Theorem
3.23
) must be isometric to each other.

Lemma 5.22
.

Let
u\in C^{0}(\mathcal{R}_{[a,0]}\bigcap C^{\infty}(\mathcal{R}_{(a,0]})
be a uniformly bounded function satisfying
\square u=0
on
\mathcal{R}_{(a,0]}
. Then for any
z\in\mathcal{R}_{(a,0]}
,

\displaystyle u(z)=\int_{\mathcal{R}_{a}}u\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z;a}.

(5.14)

Proof.

Fix
s\in(a,0)
and
z\in\mathcal{R}_{s}
. By the proof of Lemma
5.3
, there exists a point
y
with
\mathfrak{t}(y)\in(s,0)
such that
z\in\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{s})
. Hence,
\nu_{z;{a}}(\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{a}))=1
. It then follows from
[
Bam20b
, Theorem 15.28(d)]
that

\displaystyle u(z)=\int_{\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{a})}u\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z;a}=\int_{\mathcal{R}_{a}}u\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z;a},

(5.15)

which establishes (
5.14
) for all
z\in\mathcal{R}_{(a,0)}
.

Now consider
z\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
. By Corollary
5.7
and its proof, we may find a sequence
z_{i}\in\mathcal{R}_{t_{i}}
with
t_{i}\nearrow 0
such that
z_{i}\to z
with respect to
d_{Z}
. Then (
5.14
) follows from the convergence of the heat kernel measures in (
5.11
) together with (
5.15
). This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.23
.

In the setting of Lemma
5.22
, we may extend the definition of
u
to
Z\setminus\mathcal{R}
via the integral formula (
5.14
). By (
5.11
), this defines a continuous function on
Z
that solves
\square u=0
on
\mathcal{R}
. Furthermore, combining Lemma
5.22
, (
5.11
), and the argument of
[
Bam20b
, Theorem 15.29]
, we conclude that the family of conjugate heat kernel measures
(\nu_{z;t})_{z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}},t<\mathfrak{t}(z)}
are uniquely determined by the Ricci flow spacetime
(\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{t},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}},g^{Z})
.

Therefore, for any isometry
\phi
as in Definition
5.21
, we also have the following property: for every
x\in Z
and every
s\leq\mathfrak{t}(x)
, the pushforward measure satisfies
\phi_{*}\nu_{x;s}=\nu_{\phi(x);s}
.

Next, we define
\mathrm{Var}_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}
and
d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{R}_{t}}
to be the variance and
d_{W_{1}}
-Wasserstein distance, respectively, with respect to the metric space
(\mathcal{R}_{t},g^{Z}_{t})
. Here, if
x
and
y
lie in different connected components of
\mathcal{R}_{t}
, we set the distance
d_{g^{Z}_{t}}(x,y)=+\infty
.

The following conclusion then follows directly from the fact that any associated metric flow is
H_{n}
-concentrated.

Proposition 5.24
.

For any
z\in Z
, the conjugate heat kernel measure
\nu_{z;s}
is
H_{n}
-concentrated, i.e., for any
s<\mathfrak{t}(z)
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}_{\mathcal{R}_{s}}(\nu_{z;s})\leq H_{n}(\mathfrak{t}(z)-s).

Definition 5.25

(Regular
H
-center)
.

For any
z\in Z
, a point
z_{1}\in\mathcal{R}_{s}
with
s<\mathfrak{t}(z)
is called a
regular
H
-center
of
z
for a constant
H>0
if

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}_{\mathcal{R}_{s}}(\delta_{z_{1}},\nu_{z;s})\leq H(\mathfrak{t}(z)-s).

Note that by Proposition
5.24
, for any
s<\mathfrak{t}(z)
, we can always find an
H_{n}
-center of
z
in
\mathcal{R}_{s}
.

By the definition of a regular
H
-center, the following conclusion is immediate.

Lemma 5.26
.

Given
x\in Z
, if
z\in\mathcal{R}_{s}
is a regular
H
-center of
x
with
s<\mathfrak{t}(x)
, then

\displaystyle\nu_{x;s}\left(B_{g^{Z}_{s}}\left(z,\sqrt{LH(\mathfrak{t}(x)-s)}\right)\right)\geq 1-\frac{1}{L}.

Lemma 5.27
.

For any
x\in Z
, let
z\in\mathcal{R}_{s}
be a regular
H
-center of
x
at
s<\mathfrak{t}(x)
, then

\displaystyle d_{Z}(x,z)\leq\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\sqrt{H(\mathfrak{t}(x)-s)}.

Proof.

This is straightforward by the generalization of Lemma
3.16
on
\mathcal{X}^{x}
.
∎

In general, the Ricci flow spacetime
\mathcal{R}
may not be connected. As a corollary of Proposition
5.16
, we prove

Corollary 5.28
.

For any
x,y\in\mathcal{R}
, if
d_{Z}(x,y)<\sqrt{\max\{\mathfrak{t}(x),\mathfrak{t}(y)\}+(1-2\sigma)T}
, then
x
and
y
lie in the same connected component of
\mathcal{R}_{[-(1-2\sigma)T,\max\{\mathfrak{t}(x),\mathfrak{t}(y)\}]}
. In particular, if
T=+\infty
, then
\mathcal{R}
is connected.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.16
, there exists a time
t\in(-(1-2\sigma)T,\max\{\mathfrak{t}(x),\mathfrak{t}(y)\}-d^{2}_{Z}(x,y))
such that
\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{t})=\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{t})
. We fix a point
z\in\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{t})=\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{t})
. Since
x\in\mathcal{R}
, we can choose a time
s
close to
\mathfrak{t}(x)
and find a regular
H_{n}
-center
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{s}
such that
x
and
x^{\prime}
can be connected by a curve in
\mathcal{R}
. On the other hand, since
\mathcal{R}^{x}
is connected,
z
and
x^{\prime}
can be connected by a curve in
\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x})
. Therefore,
z
can be connected to
x
by a curve in
\mathcal{R}
. Similarly,
z
can be connected to
y
by a curve in
\mathcal{R}
. It follows that
x
and
y
lie in the same connected component of
\mathcal{R}
.
∎

Next, we prove the monotonicity:

Lemma 5.29
.

For any
x,y\in Z
and
s<\min\{\mathfrak{t}(x),\mathfrak{t}(y)\}
, the function

\displaystyle s\mapsto d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{R}_{s}}(\nu_{x;s},\nu_{y;s})

is nondecreasing.

Proof.

Given
s_{1}<s_{2}\leq\min\{\mathfrak{t}(x),\mathfrak{t}(y))\}
, since
\nu_{x;s}
(respectively,
\nu_{y;s}
) has full measure on
\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{s})
(respectively,
\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{s})
), we may assume by Lemma
5.15
that
\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{s})=\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{s})
for any
s\in[s_{1},s_{2}]
. Note that for any two probability measures
\mu,\nu
on
\mathcal{R}^{x}_{s}
, we have
d_{w_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{x}_{s}}(\mu,\nu)=d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{R}_{s}}((\iota_{x})_{*}(\mu),(\iota_{x})_{*}(\nu))
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{t}(x)\leq\mathfrak{t}(y)
. Then, we can regard
\nu_{y;s}
as a conjugate heat flow (see Definition
4.2
) on
\mathcal{X}^{x}
. The desired monotonicity follows from
[
Bam23
, Proposition 3.16(b)]
.
∎

Next, we have the following heat kernel estimate, which follows directly from Theorem
2.15
(ii) and the same argument as in
[
Bam20b
, Lemma 15.9 (a)]
.

Theorem 5.30
.

For any
x\in Z
and
s<\mathfrak{t}(x)
, we have

\displaystyle K_{Z}(x;y)\leq\frac{C(n,Y,\epsilon)}{(\mathfrak{t}(x)-s)^{n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{d_{g^{Z}_{t}}^{2}(z,y)}{(4+\epsilon)(\mathfrak{t}(x)-s)}\right)

for any
y\in\mathcal{R}_{s}
, where
z\in\mathcal{R}_{s}
is any regular
H_{n}
-center of
x
.

Using Theorem
5.30
, we can prove, as in Proposition
2.21
(i), the following lemma. Here, for any
x\in\mathcal{R}
, we denote by
x_{t}\in\mathcal{R}_{t}
the flow of
x
with respect to
\partial_{\mathfrak{t}}
.

Lemma 5.31
.

For any
x\in\mathcal{R}_{t}
, if
x_{s}\in\mathcal{R}_{s}
and
|\mathrm{R}_{g^{Z}}(x_{s})|\leq R_{0}r^{-2}
for any
s\in[t-r^{2},t]
, then

\displaystyle d_{g^{Z}_{t-r^{2}}}(x_{t-r^{2}},z)\leq C(n,Y,R_{0})r,

where
z\in\mathcal{R}_{t-r^{2}}
is any regular
H_{n}
-center of
x
. In particular,
x_{t-r^{2}}
is a regular
H
-center (see Definition
5.25
) of
x
for a constant
H=H(n,Y,R_{0})>0
.

Next, we show that there are at most countable connected components for
\mathcal{R}_{t}
.

Proposition 5.32
.

For any
t\in\mathbb{I}
, the number of connected components of
\mathcal{R}_{t}
is at most countable.

Proof.

We consider a time
t_{0}\in\mathbb{I}
. Suppose
\mathcal{R}_{t_{0}}
has connected components
\{U_{\alpha}\}
for
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
. For each
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
, we choose
x_{\alpha}\in U_{\alpha}
and a small constant
r_{\alpha}>0
such that

\displaystyle P_{\alpha}:=\{x_{t}\mid x\in B_{g^{Z}_{t_{0}}}(x_{\alpha},r_{\alpha}),\,t\in[t_{0}-r_{\alpha}^{2},t_{0}+r_{\alpha}^{2}]\cap\mathbb{I}\}\subset\mathcal{R},

and
|\mathrm{Rm}_{g^{Z}}|\leq r_{\alpha}^{-2}
on
P_{\alpha}
. By the standard distance comparison, there exists
r_{\alpha}^{\prime}<r_{\alpha}
such that

\displaystyle P^{\prime}_{\alpha}:=\{x\mid x\in B_{g^{Z}_{t}}(x_{\alpha,t},r^{\prime}_{\alpha}),\,t\in[t_{0}-r_{\alpha}^{2},t_{0}+r_{\alpha}^{2}]\cap\mathbb{I}\}\subset P_{\alpha}.

It follows from Proposition
3.10
and the smooth convergence in Theorem
5.2
that there exists
r_{\alpha}^{\prime\prime}<r_{\alpha}^{\prime}
such that

\displaystyle B_{Z}^{*}(x_{\alpha},r_{\alpha}^{\prime\prime})\subset P^{\prime}_{\alpha}.

It is clear from the definition that
\{P_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}
are mutually disjoint. Since
(Z,d_{Z})
is separable, we conclude that the cardinality of
\mathcal{A}
is at most countable.
∎

Definition 5.33

(Volume)
.

For any set
Q\subset Z
, we define its volume by

\displaystyle|Q|:=\left\lvert Q\cap\mathcal{R}\right\rvert_{g^{Z}},

where
|\cdot|_{g^{Z}}
denotes the spacetime volume given by the Ricci flow spacetime
(\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{t},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}},g^{Z})
. Moreover, for any
Q\subset Z_{t}
, we set

\displaystyle|Q|_{t}:=\left\lvert Q\cap\mathcal{R}_{t}\right\rvert_{g_{t}^{Z}}.

First, we prove the upper volume bound.

Proposition 5.34
.

If
T<\infty
, then for any
x\in Z
and
L>0
, we have

\displaystyle|B^{*}_{Z}(x,L\sqrt{T})|\leq C(n,\sigma,L)T^{\frac{n}{2}+1}.

If
T=+\infty
, we also have for any
L>0
,

\displaystyle|B^{*}_{Z}(x,L)|\leq C(n)L^{n+2}.

Proof.

We only prove the case
T<\infty
, and the case
T=+\infty
can be proved similarly.

Without loss of generality, we may assume
T=1
and
x\in\mathcal{R}
. Indeed, if
x\notin\mathcal{R}
, we can choose a nearby point
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}\cap B^{*}_{Z}(x,L)
. Then the conclusion follows since
B^{*}_{Z}(x,L)\subset B^{*}_{Z}(x^{\prime},2L)
.

For any spacetime compact set
K\subset B^{*}_{Z}(x,L)\cap\mathcal{R}
containing
x
, it follows from Theorem
5.2
that for sufficiently large
i
,
K\subset U_{i}
. Moreover, if we set
x_{i}^{*}=\phi_{i}(x)\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
, then it follows from Theorem
5.2
(c) that

\displaystyle\phi_{i}(K)\subset B^{*}_{i}(x_{i}^{*},2L),

where
B^{*}_{i}(x_{i}^{*},2L)
denotes the ball with respect to
d_{i}^{*}
on
M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
. By Proposition
3.18
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\phi_{i}(K)\right\rvert_{g_{i}}\leq\left\lvert B^{*}_{i}(x_{i}^{*},2L)\right\rvert_{g_{i}}\leq C(n,\sigma,L).

Thus, from the smooth convergence, we have

\displaystyle|K|\leq C(n,\sigma,L).

By approximation, we conclude

\displaystyle\left\lvert B^{*}_{Z}(x,L)\right\rvert\leq C(n,\sigma,L).

∎

Next, we prove local volume bounds.

Proposition 5.35
.

For any
x\in Z
and
r>0
with
\mathfrak{t}(x)-r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, we have

\displaystyle 0<c(n,Y,\sigma)r^{n+2}\leq|B^{*}_{Z}(x,r)|\leq C(n,\sigma)r^{n+2}.

Proof.

Given
x\in Z
, we consider the associated metric flow
\mathcal{X}^{x}
. If
z\in\mathcal{X}^{x}_{\mathfrak{t}(x)-s^{2}}
is an
H_{n}
-center of
x
, then by Lemma
5.26
and Theorem
5.30
, we have

\displaystyle|B_{g^{x}_{\mathfrak{t}(x)-s^{2}}}(z,\sqrt{2H_{n}}s)|\geq c(n,Y,\sigma)s^{n}>0.

Then one can prove, as in Proposition
3.17
, that

\displaystyle|B^{*}_{\mathcal{X}^{x}}(x,r)\cap\mathcal{R}^{x}_{[\mathfrak{t}(x)-c_{0}r^{2},\mathfrak{t}(x)-c_{1}r^{2}]}|\geq c(n,Y,\sigma)r^{n+2}>0.

for positive constants
c_{0}=c_{0}(n,Y,\sigma)
and
c_{1}=c_{1}(n,Y,\sigma)
. Thus, through
\iota_{x}
, we obtain

\displaystyle|B^{*}_{Z}(x,r)\cap\mathcal{R}_{[\mathfrak{t}(x)-c_{0}r^{2},\mathfrak{t}(x)-c_{1}r^{2}]}|\geq c(n,Y,\sigma)r^{n+2},

which implies the lower bound.

The upper bound can be proved similarly to Proposition
5.34
by using the upper bound in Proposition
3.17
.
∎

We also have the following volume upper bound, which follows directly from the same argument as in the proof of Proposition
5.34
by using Proposition
3.17
(i).

Proposition 5.36
.

For any
x\in Z
and
r>0
with
\mathfrak{t}(x)-r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
and any
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\left\lvert B_{Z}^{*}(x,r)\bigcap Z_{t}\right\rvert_{t}\leq C(n,\sigma)r^{n}.

Next, we define the closeness of two noncollapsed Ricci flow limit spaces, which is an approximate version of Definition
5.21
.

Definition 5.37

(
\epsilon
-close)
.

Suppose
(Z,d_{Z},z,\mathfrak{t})
and
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
are two pointed noncollapsed Ricci flow limit spaces, with regular parts given by the Ricci flow spacetimes
(\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{t},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}},g^{Z})
and
(\mathcal{R}^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}},g^{Z^{\prime}})
, respectively, such that
J
is a time interval.

We say that
(Z,d_{Z},z,\mathfrak{t})
is
\epsilon
-close
to
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})

over
J
if there exists an open set
U\subset\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{J}
and a smooth embedding
\phi:U\to\mathcal{R}_{J}
satisfying the following properties.

(a)

\phi
is time-preserving.

(b)

U\subset B^{*}_{Z^{\prime}}(z^{\prime},\epsilon^{-1})\bigcap\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{J}
and
U
is an
\epsilon
-net of
B^{*}_{Z^{\prime}}(z^{\prime},\epsilon^{-1})\bigcap Z^{\prime}_{J}
with respect to
d_{Z^{\prime}}
.

(c)

For any
x,y\in U
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert d_{Z}(\phi(x),\phi(y))-d_{Z^{\prime}}(x,y)\right\rvert\leq\epsilon.

(d)

The
\epsilon
-neighborhood of
\phi(U)
with respect to
d_{Z}
contains
B^{*}_{Z}(z,\epsilon^{-1}-\epsilon)\bigcap Z_{J}
.

(e)

There exists
x_{0}\in U
such that
d_{Z^{\prime}}(x_{0},z^{\prime})\leq\epsilon
and
d_{Z}(\phi(x_{0}),z)\leq\epsilon
.

(f)

On
U
, the following estimates hold:

\displaystyle\rVert\phi^{*}g^{Z}-g^{Z^{\prime}}\rVert_{C^{[\epsilon^{-1}]}(U)}+\rVert\phi^{*}\partial_{\mathfrak{t}}-\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}\rVert_{C^{[\epsilon^{-1}]}(U)}\leq\epsilon.

It is clear from the above definition that if
(Z,d_{Z},z,\mathfrak{t})
is
\epsilon
-close to
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
over
J
, then
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is
\Psi(\epsilon)
-close to
(Z,d_{Z},z,\mathfrak{t})
over
J
, where
\Psi(\epsilon)\to 0
as
\epsilon\to 0
.

Next, we introduce the following notation.

Notation 5.38
.

For a sequence of noncollapsed Ricci limit spaces

\displaystyle(Z_{i},d_{Z_{i}},z_{i},\mathfrak{t}_{i})\in\overline{\mathcal{M}(n,Y)},\quad i\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\},

we write

(Z_{i},d_{Z_{i}},z_{i},\mathfrak{t}_{i})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\hat{C}^{\infty}\hskip 8.19447pt}(Z_{\infty},d_{Z_{\infty}},z_{\infty},\mathfrak{t}_{\infty}),

if there exists a sequence
\epsilon_{i}\to 0
such that
(Z_{i},d_{Z_{i}},z_{i},\mathfrak{t}_{i})
is
\epsilon_{i}
-close to
(Z_{\infty},d_{Z_{\infty}},z_{\infty},\mathfrak{t}_{\infty})
over
[-\epsilon_{i}^{-1},\epsilon_{i}^{-1}]
.

In particular, it is clear by Theorem
5.2
that the convergence (
5.1
) can be improved to be

(M_{i}\times\mathbb{I},d^{*}_{i},p_{i}^{*},\mathfrak{t}_{i})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\hat{C}^{\infty}\hskip 8.19447pt}(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t}).

6
Extended metric flows

In this section, we consider a Ricci flow limit space
(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t})
obtained from

(M_{i}\times\mathbb{I},d^{*}_{i},p_{i}^{*},\mathfrak{t}_{i})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathrm{pGH}\hskip 8.19447pt}(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t}),

(6.1)

where
\mathcal{X}^{i}=\{M_{i}^{n},(g_{i}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
with base point
p_{i}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}^{i}_{\mathbb{I}}
.

First, we define a distance function
d_{t}^{Z}
on
Z_{t}
.

Definition 6.1
.

For each
t\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, we define the distance at the time-slice
Z_{t}
by

\displaystyle d^{Z}_{t}(x,y):=\lim_{s\nearrow t}d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{R}_{s}}(\nu_{x;s},\nu_{y;s})\in[0,\infty]

for any
x,y\in Z_{t}
, where the limit exists by Lemma
5.29
. Note that if
d^{Z}_{t}(x,y)<\infty
, then for any
s<t
,

\displaystyle\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{s})=\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{s}).

(6.2)

Remark 6.2
.

Note that the definition of
d^{Z}_{t}
is independent of the choice of spacetime distance
d_{Z}
. In other words, if the spacetime distance
d^{*}
in Definition
3.5
is constructed using a different constant
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0}]
, then the resulting limit space
Z
in (
6.1
) is equipped with a different (albeit bi-Lipschitz equivalent) spacetime distance. However, the distance
d^{Z}_{t}
remains unchanged.

Lemma 6.3
.

For any
t\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
,
(Z_{t},d^{Z}_{t})
is an extended metric space.

Proof.

We first prove the triangle inequality. Given
x,y,z\in Z_{t}
, for any
s<t
, we have

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{R}_{s}}(\nu_{x;s},\nu_{z;s})\leq d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{R}_{s}}(\nu_{x;s},\nu_{y;s})+d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{R}_{s}}(\nu_{y;s},\nu_{z;s})\leq d^{Z}_{t}(x,y)+d^{Z}_{t}(y,z).

Letting
s\nearrow t
, we obtain
d^{Z}_{t}(x,z)\leq d^{Z}_{t}(x,y)+d^{Z}_{t}(y,z)
.

In addition, if
d^{Z}_{t}(x,y)=0
, then by Lemma
5.29
,
d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{R}_{s}}(\nu_{x;s},\nu_{y;s})=0
for any
s<t
. Since we can regard
\nu_{y;s}
as a conjugate heat flow on
\mathcal{X}^{x}
, we conclude that
\nu_{x;s}=\nu_{y;s}
. Then we take
w_{i}\in\mathcal{R}_{s_{i}}
for a sequence
s_{i}\nearrow t
so that
w_{i}
is the common
H_{n}
-center of
\nu_{x;s}
and
\nu_{y;s}
. By Lemma
5.27
,
x
and
y
are both limits of
w_{i}
in
d_{Z}
. Thus, we conclude that
x=y
.
∎

Lemma 6.4
.

For any
x,y\in Z_{t}
,

\displaystyle d_{Z}(x,y)\leq\epsilon_{0}^{-1}d^{Z}_{t}(x,y).

Proof.

We assume
d^{Z}_{t}(x,y)<\infty
. Then, (
6.2
) holds for any
s<t
, and we can regard
(\iota_{x}^{-1})_{*}\nu_{y;s}
as a conjugate heat flow on
\mathcal{X}^{x}
.

For any
s_{i}\nearrow t
, we choose
x_{i},y_{i}\in\mathcal{R}^{x}_{s_{i}}
to be regular
H_{n}
-centers of
x
and
y
, respectively. Then, it follows from the generalization of Proposition
3.12
(1) to
\mathcal{X}^{x}
that

\displaystyle d^{*}_{x}(x_{i},y_{i})\leq\epsilon_{0}^{-1}d_{g^{Z}_{s_{i}}}(\iota_{x}(x_{i}),\iota_{x}(y_{i})).

(6.3)

On the other hand, by the definition of the regular
H_{n}
-center, we have

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{R}_{s_{i}}}(\nu_{x;s_{i}},\nu_{y;s_{i}})\geq d_{g^{Z}_{s_{i}}}(\iota_{x}(x_{i}),\iota_{x}(y_{i}))-2\sqrt{H_{n}(t-s_{i})}.

(6.4)

Moreover, it follows from Lemma
5.27
that

\displaystyle d^{*}_{x}(x_{i},y_{i})=d_{Z}(\iota_{x}(x_{i}),\iota_{x}(y_{i}))\geq d_{Z}(x,y)-2\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\sqrt{H_{n}(t-s_{i})}.

(6.5)

Combining (
6.3
), (
6.4
), (
6.5
), and letting
i\to\infty
, the conclusion follows.
∎

Lemma 6.5
.

Given
x,y\in Z_{t}
, there exists a sequence
t_{i}\nearrow t
such that if
x_{i},y_{i}\in\mathcal{R}_{t_{i}}
are regular
H_{n}
-centers of
x
and
y
, respectively, then
d^{Z}_{t_{i}}(x_{i},y_{i})=d_{g^{Z}_{t_{i}}}(x_{i},y_{i})
and

\displaystyle d^{Z}_{t}(x,y)=\lim_{i\to\infty}d^{Z}_{t_{i}}(x_{i},y_{i}).

Proof.

We only prove the case
d_{t}^{Z}(x,y)<\infty
, since the case
d_{t}^{Z}(x,y)=\infty
can be proved similarly.

Since
d_{t}^{Z}(x,y)<\infty
, we regard
(\iota_{x}^{-1})_{*}\nu_{y;s}
as a conjugate heat flow on
\mathcal{X}^{x}
. We take a sequence
t_{i}\nearrow t
such that
\mathcal{X}^{x}
is continuous at
t_{i}
. Then it follows from
[
Bam23
, Equation (4.22)]
that

\displaystyle\lim_{s\nearrow t_{i}}d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{x}_{s}}(\nu_{\iota_{x}^{-1}(x_{i});s},\nu_{\iota_{x}^{-1}(y_{i});s})=d_{\mathcal{X}^{x}_{t_{i}}}(\iota_{x}^{-1}(x_{i}),\iota_{x}^{-1}(y_{i}))=d_{g^{Z}_{t_{i}}}(x_{i},y_{i}).

In other words,

\displaystyle d^{Z}_{t_{i}}(x_{i},y_{i})=d_{g^{Z}_{t_{i}}}(x_{i},y_{i}).

(6.6)

On the other hand, by the definition of the
H_{n}
-center, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{R}_{t_{i}}}(\nu_{x;t_{i}},\nu_{y;t_{i}})-d_{g^{Z}_{t_{i}}}(x_{i},y_{i})\right\rvert\leq 2\sqrt{H_{n}(t-t_{i})}.

(6.7)

Combining (
6.6
) with (
6.7
), and letting
i\to\infty
, the conclusion follows.
∎

Proposition 6.6

(Completeness)
.

For any
t\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, the extended metric space
(Z_{t},d_{t}^{Z})
is complete.

Proof.

Suppose
x_{i}\in Z_{t}
is a Cauchy sequence with respect to
d_{t}^{Z}
. By Lemma
6.4
,

\displaystyle d_{Z}(x_{i},x_{j})\leq\epsilon_{0}^{-1}d_{t}^{Z}(x_{i},x_{j}).

In particular,
x_{i}
is also a Cauchy sequence with respect to
d_{Z}
, we assume that
x_{i}\to x_{\infty}
under
d_{Z}
since
(Z,d_{Z})
is complete. Moreover, it is clear that
\mathfrak{t}(x_{\infty})=t
by the continuity of
\mathfrak{t}
.

We set
z=x_{1}
so that
(\iota_{z}^{-1})_{*}\nu_{x_{i};s}
can be regarded as a conjugate heat flow on
\mathcal{X}^{z}
for any
s<t
. In particular,

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{s}}((\iota_{z}^{-1})_{*}\nu_{x_{i};s},(\iota_{z}^{-1})_{*}\nu_{x_{j};s})\leq d_{t}^{Z}(x_{i},x_{j}).

Therefore, we assume
(\iota_{z}^{-1})_{*}\nu_{x_{i};s}\to\mu_{s}
in
d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{s}}
. Note that this convergence is uniform in
s
. By taking the limit of the reproduction formula, it is clear that
\mu_{s}
is a conjugate heat flow on
\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}
. Moreover, we have, by Proposition
2.5
and Proposition
5.24
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}_{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{s}}(\mu_{s})\leq H_{n}(t-s).

Now, we take a sequence
t_{k}\nearrow t
and assume that
y_{k}\in\mathcal{X}^{z}_{t_{k}}
is an
H_{n}
-center of
\mu_{t_{k}}
at
t_{k}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}_{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{t_{k}}}(\mu_{t_{k}},\delta_{y_{k}})\leq H_{n}(t-t_{k}).

We claim that
\iota_{z}(y_{k})\to x_{\infty}
under
d_{Z}
. Indeed, if
z_{i,k}\in\iota_{z}(\mathcal{R}^{z}_{t_{k}})
is a regular
H_{n}
-center of
x_{i}
, then

\displaystyle d_{Z}(x_{i},\iota_{z}(y_{k}))\leq d_{Z}(x_{i},z_{i,k})+d_{Z}(z_{i,k},\iota_{z}(y_{k}))\leq\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\sqrt{H_{n}(t-t_{k})}+\epsilon_{0}^{-1}d^{Z}_{t_{k}}(z_{i,k},\iota_{z}(y_{k})),

where we used Lemma
5.27
and Lemma
6.4
. In addition,

\displaystyle d^{Z}_{t_{k}}(z_{i,k},\iota_{z}(y_{k}))\leq

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{t_{k}}}(\delta_{\iota_{z}^{-1}(z_{i,k})},(\iota_{z}^{-1})_{*}\nu_{x_{i};t_{k}})+d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{t_{k}}}((\iota_{z}^{-1})_{*}\nu_{x_{i};t_{k}},\mu_{t_{k}})+d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{t_{k}}}(\delta_{y_{k}},\mu_{t_{k}})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{t_{k}}}((\iota_{z}^{-1})_{*}\nu_{x_{i};t_{k}},\mu_{t_{k}})+2\sqrt{H_{n}(t-t_{k})}.

Combining the above inequalities, we obtain

\displaystyle d_{Z}(x_{i},\iota_{z}(y_{k}))\leq 3\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\sqrt{H_{n}(t-t_{k})}+\epsilon_{0}^{-1}d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{t_{k}}}((\iota_{z}^{-1})_{*}\nu_{x_{i};t_{k}},\mu_{t_{k}})

and by letting
i\to\infty
,

\displaystyle d_{Z}(x_{\infty},\iota_{z}(y_{k}))\leq 3\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\sqrt{H_{n}(t-t_{k})}.

Thus,
\iota_{z}(y_{k})\to x_{\infty}
in
d_{Z}
. From this, we conclude that for any
s<t_{k}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{R}^{z}_{s}}(\nu_{y_{k};s},(\iota_{z}^{-1})_{*}\nu_{x_{\infty};s})=0.

Now, it follows from the definition of
d_{t}^{Z}
that

\displaystyle d_{t}^{Z}(x_{i},x_{\infty})=

\displaystyle\lim_{s\nearrow t}d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{R}^{z}_{s}}((\iota_{z}^{-1})_{*}\nu_{x_{i};s},(\iota_{z}^{-1})_{*}\nu_{x_{\infty};s})=\lim_{s\nearrow t}\lim_{k\to\infty}d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{s}}((\iota_{z}^{-1})_{*}\nu_{x_{i};s},\nu_{y_{k};s})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lim_{s\nearrow t}d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{s}}((\iota_{z}^{-1})_{*}\nu_{x_{i};s},\mu_{s})+\lim_{s\nearrow t}\lim_{k\to\infty}d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{s}}(\nu_{y_{k};s},\mu_{s})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lim_{s\nearrow t}d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{s}}((\iota_{z}^{-1})_{*}\nu_{x_{i};s},\mu_{s})+\lim_{k\to\infty}d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{t_{k}}}(\delta_{y_{k}},\mu_{t_{k}})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lim_{s\nearrow t}d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{s}}((\iota_{z}^{-1})_{*}\nu_{x_{i};s},\mu_{s})+\lim_{k\to\infty}\sqrt{H_{n}(t-t_{k})}\leq\lim_{s\nearrow t}d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{s}}((\iota_{z}^{-1})_{*}\nu_{x_{i};s},\mu_{s}),

where we used the monotonicity of
d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{s}}(\nu_{y_{k};s},\mu_{s})
; see
[
Bam23
, Proposition 3.16 (b)]
.
Since
(\iota_{z}^{-1})_{*}\nu_{x_{i};s}
converges to
\mu_{s}
uniformly for
s<t
, the conclusion follows.
∎

Proposition 6.7

(
H_{n}
-concentration I)
.

For
x,y\in Z_{t}
and
s<t
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}_{\mathcal{R}_{s}}(\nu_{x;s},\nu_{y;s})\leq d^{Z}_{t}(x,y)+H_{n}(t-s).

Proof.

We assume
d^{Z}_{t}(x,y)<\infty
and consider a sequence
t_{i}\nearrow t
as in Lemma
6.5
and let
x_{i}\in\mathcal{R}_{t_{i}}
and
y_{i}\in\mathcal{R}_{t_{i}}
be regular
H_{n}
-centers of
x
and
y
, respectively. By Lemma
5.27
,
x_{i}\to x,y_{i}\to y
in
d_{Z}
. Then we have for any
s<t
,

\displaystyle\nu_{\iota_{x}^{-1}(x_{i});s}\to\nu_{x;s}\quad\text{and}\quad\nu_{\iota_{x}^{-1}(y_{i});s}\to(\iota_{x}^{-1})_{*}\nu_{y;s}

in
d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{x}_{s}}
. By Proposition
2.5
and the
H_{n}
-concentration of
\mathcal{X}^{x}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}(\nu_{x;s},\nu_{y,s})\leq

\displaystyle\liminf_{i\to\infty}\mathrm{Var}_{\mathcal{X}^{x}_{s}}(\nu_{\iota_{x}^{-1}(x_{i});s},\nu_{\iota_{x}^{-1}(y_{i});s})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\liminf_{i\to\infty}\left(d_{g^{x}_{t_{i}}}(\iota_{x}^{-1}(x_{i}),\iota_{x}^{-1}(y_{i}))+H_{n}(t_{i}-s)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\liminf_{i\to\infty}d_{g^{Z}_{t_{i}}}(x_{i},y_{i})+H_{n}(t-s).

(6.8)

On the other hand, by Lemma
6.5
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{g^{Z}_{t_{i}}}(x_{i},y_{i})=d^{Z}_{t}(x,y).

(6.9)

Therefore, the conclusion follows from (
6
) and (
6.9
).
∎

In general, the distance
d_{g^{Z}_{t}}
induced by
g^{Z}_{t}
, when restricted on
\mathcal{R}_{t}
, may not agree with
d^{Z}_{t}
. For instance, it is possible that
d^{Z}_{t}(x,y)<\infty
, but
x
and
y
lie in different connected components of
\mathcal{R}_{t}
. Next, we prove that locally, those two distance functions match.

Proposition 6.8
.

For any
w\in\mathcal{R}_{t}
, there exists a small constant
r>0
such that for any
x,y\in B_{g^{Z}_{t}}(w,r)
,

\displaystyle d_{g^{Z}_{t}}(x,y)=d^{Z}_{t}(x,y).

Moreover, for any
x,y\in\mathcal{R}_{t}
,
d^{Z}_{t}(x,y)\leq d_{g^{Z}_{t}}(x,y)
.

Proof.

We choose a sufficiently small
r>0
such that there is a product domain
U=B_{g^{Z}_{t}}(w,r)\times[t-r^{2},t]\subset\mathcal{R}
such that
U\cap\mathcal{R}_{s}
is geodesically convex for any
s\in[t-r^{2},t]
. Here, being geodesically convex means that any two points in
U\cap\mathcal{R}_{s}
can be connected by a minimal geodesic with respect to
g^{Z}_{s}
and any such minimal geodesic is contained in
U\cap\mathcal{R}_{s}
.

For any
x,y\in B_{g^{Z}_{t}}(w,r)
, we regard
(\iota_{x}^{-1})_{*}\nu_{y;s}
as a conjugate heat flow on
\mathcal{X}^{x}
. We take a sequence
t_{i}\nearrow t
such that
\mathcal{X}^{x}
is continuous at
t_{i}
. Then, we set
x_{i}\in U\cap\mathcal{R}_{t_{i}}
and
y_{i}\in U\cap\mathcal{R}_{t_{i}}
to be the flows of
x
and
y
with respect to
\partial_{\mathfrak{t}}
, respectively. By Lemma
5.31
,
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
are regular
H
-centers of
x
and
y
, respectively, where
H
is a positive constant. As in the proof of Lemma
6.5
, we conclude that

\displaystyle d^{Z}_{t}(x,y)=\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{g_{t_{i}}^{Z}}(x_{i},y_{i}).

On the other hand, it is clear from our construction that
\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{g_{t_{i}}^{Z}}(x_{i},y_{i})=d_{g^{Z}_{t}}(x,y)
. Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle d^{Z}_{t}(x,y)=d_{g^{Z}_{t}}(x,y).

Now, since
d_{g^{Z}_{t}}
is a length metric on any connected component of
\mathcal{R}_{t}
, we conclude immediately that
d^{Z}_{t}(x,y)\leq d_{g^{Z}_{t}}(x,y)
for any
x,y\in\mathcal{R}_{t}
, by the local isometry.
∎

Proposition
6.8
implies, in particular, that
d_{g^{Z}_{t}}
and
d_{t}^{Z}
induce the same topology on
\mathcal{R}_{t}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{R}_{t}
is an open set of
Z_{t}
with respect to
d^{Z}_{t}
. Therefore, we can regard any conjugate heat kernel measure
\nu_{x;t}
as a probability measure on
Z_{t}
.

Definition 6.9

(Variance and
W_{1}
-Wasserstein distance)
.

For any
t\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, the variance between two probability measures
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(Z_{t})
is defined by

\mathrm{Var}_{Z_{t}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\int_{Z_{t}}\int_{Z_{t}}d^{Z}_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x_{1})\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}(x_{2}).

Moreover, the
W_{1}
-Wasserstein distance between
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(Z_{t})
is defined by

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{Z_{t}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\sup\int_{Z_{t}}f\,\mathrm{d}(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}),

where the supremum is taken over all bounded
1
-Lipschitz function
f:Z_{t}\to\mathbb{R}
.

Next, we prove

Lemma 6.10
.

For any
x,y\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
, if
d_{W_{1}}^{Z_{t_{0}}}(\nu_{x;t_{0}},\nu_{y;t_{0}})<\infty
for some
t_{0}\in(-(1-2\sigma)T,\min\{\mathfrak{t}(x),\mathfrak{t}(y)\})
, then
\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{t})=\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{t})
for any
t\in[-(1-2\sigma)T,t_{0})
.

Proof.

If
\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{t_{0}})=\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{t_{0}})
, we are done. Otherwise, by the definition of
d_{W_{1}}^{Z_{t_{0}}}
, there exist
x^{\prime}\in\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{t_{0}})
and
y^{\prime}\in\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{t_{0}})
such that
d_{t_{0}}^{Z}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})<\infty
. By (
6.2
) and the reproduction formula, we conclude that
\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{t})=\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{t})
for any
t\in[-(1-2\sigma)T,t_{0})
.
∎

The following result is immediate from Proposition
6.7
and Proposition
6.8
.

Proposition 6.11

(
H_{n}
-concentration II)
.

For
x,y\in Z_{t}
and
-(1-2\sigma)T<s<t
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}_{Z_{s}}(\nu_{x;s},\nu_{y;s})\leq d^{Z}_{t}(x,y)+H_{n}(t-s).

Next, we define

Definition 6.12

(
H
-center)
.

For any
z\in Z
, a point
z_{1}\in Z_{s}
with
s\in(-(1-2\sigma)T,\mathfrak{t}(z))
is called an
H
-center
of
z
for a constant
H>0
if

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}_{Z_{s}}(\delta_{z_{1}},\nu_{z;s})\leq H(\mathfrak{t}(z)-s).

Note that by Proposition
6.11
, for any
s\in(-(1-2\sigma)T,\mathfrak{t}(z))
, we can always find an
H_{n}
-center of
z
in
Z_{s}
. Moreover, by Proposition
6.8
, any regular
H
-center
z_{2}\in\mathcal{R}_{s}
of
z
is also an
H
-center of
z
.

By the definition of an
H
-center, the following conclusion is immediate.

Lemma 6.13
.

Given
x\in Z
, if
z\in Z_{s}
is a regular
H
-center of
x
with
s\in(-(1-2\sigma)T,\mathfrak{t}(x))
, then

\displaystyle\nu_{x;s}\left(B_{Z_{s}}\left(z,\sqrt{LH(\mathfrak{t}(x)-s)}\right)\right)\geq 1-\frac{1}{L},

where
B_{Z_{s}}(z,r):=\{w\in Z_{s}\mid d^{Z}_{s}(z,w)<r\}
denotes the metric with respect to
d^{Z}_{s}
.

We also have the following estimate, which should be compared to Lemma
3.16
and Lemma
5.27
.

Lemma 6.14
.

For any
x\in Z
, let
z\in Z_{s}
be an
H
-center of
x
at
s\in(-(1-2\sigma)T,\mathfrak{t}(x))
, then

\displaystyle d_{Z}(x,z)\leq 3\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\sqrt{(H_{n}+H)(\mathfrak{t}(x)-s)}.

Proof.

We set
z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{s}
to be a regular
H_{n}
-center of
x
. Then it follows from Lemma
5.26
, Proposition
6.8
and Lemma
6.13
that

\displaystyle d^{Z}_{s}(z,z^{\prime})\leq\sqrt{2H_{n}(t-s)}+\sqrt{2H(t-s)}.

Thus, by Lemma
5.27
and Lemma
6.4
, we conclude that

\displaystyle d_{Z}(x,z)\leq d_{Z}(x,z^{\prime})+d_{Z}(z,z^{\prime})\leq\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\sqrt{H_{n}(t-s)}+\epsilon_{0}^{-1}d^{Z}_{s}(z,z^{\prime})\leq 3\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\sqrt{(H_{n}+H)(\mathfrak{t}(x)-s)}.

∎

In general, it is unclear whether
(Z_{t},d_{t})
is separable. For this reason, we have the following definition:

Definition 6.15

(Extended metric flow)
.

An
extended metric flow
over a subset
I
of
\mathbb{R}
is a tuple of the form

\displaystyle\left(Z,\mathfrak{t},(d_{t})_{t\in I},(\nu_{x;s})_{x\in Z,s\in I,s\leq\mathfrak{t}(x)}\right)

satisfying all conditions (1)-(7) in Definition
4.1
, except that condition (3) is replaced by the following:

•

(Z_{t},d_{t})
is a complete extended metric space for any
t\in I
.

Theorem 6.16

(Extended metric flow)
.

\left(Z,\mathfrak{t},(d^{Z}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{-}},(\nu_{z;s})_{s\in\mathbb{I}^{-},s\leq\mathfrak{t}(z)}\right)
is an
H_{n}
-concentrated extended metric flow over
\mathbb{I}^{-}
.

Proof.

All items in Definition
4.1
except item (6) follow from (
5.5
) and Proposition
6.6
.

For item (6) we consider a function
u_{t_{0}}=\Phi\circ f_{t_{0}}
for some
L^{-\frac{1}{2}}
-Lipschitz function
f_{t_{0}}:Z_{t_{0}}\to\mathbb{R}
(if
L=0
, then there is no additional assumption on
u_{t_{0}}
). We define
u_{t}:Z_{t}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle u_{t}(z)=\int_{Z_{t_{0}}}u_{t_{0}}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z;{t_{0}}}.

For any
x,y\in Z_{t}
with
d^{Z}_{t}(x,y)<\infty
, we may regard
(\iota_{x}^{-1})_{*}\nu_{y;s}
as a conjugate heat flow on
\mathcal{X}^{x}_{s<t}
. We take a sequence
t_{i}\nearrow t
such that
\mathcal{X}^{x}
is continuous at
t_{i}
and choose
x_{i},y_{i}\in\mathcal{R}_{t_{i}}
to be regular
H_{n}
-centers of
x
and
y
, respectively. As in the proof of Lemma
6.5
, we have

d_{\mathcal{X}^{x}_{t_{i}}}(\iota_{x}^{-1}(x_{i}),\iota_{x}^{-1}(y_{i}))=d_{g^{Z}_{t_{i}}}(x_{i},y_{i})

(6.10)

and

\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{g^{Z}_{t_{i}}}(x_{i},y_{i})=d^{Z}_{t}(x,y).

(6.11)

Since
\mathcal{X}^{x}
is a metric flow, we obtain from (
6.10
)

\left\lvert f_{t_{i}}(x_{i})-f_{t_{i}}(y_{i})\right\rvert\leq(t_{i}-t_{0}+L)^{-\frac{1}{2}}d_{g^{Z}_{t_{i}}}(x_{i},y_{i}),

(6.12)

where
u_{s}=\Phi\circ f_{s}
for any
s\in[t_{0},t]
. On the other hand, by Lemma
5.27
on
\mathcal{X}^{x}
, we have for any
s<t

\displaystyle\nu_{\iota_{x}^{-1}(x_{i});s}\xrightarrow{i\rightarrow\infty}\nu_{x;s}

in
d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{x}_{s}}
. Since

\displaystyle u_{t_{i}}(x_{i})=\int_{Z_{t_{0}}}u_{t_{0}}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{i};t_{0}},

this implies
u_{t}(x)=\lim_{i\to\infty}u_{t_{i}}(x_{i})
. Similarly, we have
u_{t}(y)=\lim_{i\to\infty}u_{t_{i}}(y_{i})
.

Thus, by passing to the limit and using (
6.11
) and (
6.12
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\lvert f_{t}(x)-f_{t}(y)\right\rvert\leq(t-t_{0}+L)^{-\frac{1}{2}}d^{Z}_{t}(x,y).

This proves item (6). Finally, Proposition
6.11
implies that the extended metric flow
\left(Z,\mathfrak{t},(d^{Z}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{-}},(\nu_{z;s})_{s\in\mathbb{I}^{-},s\leq\mathfrak{t}(z)}\right)
is
H_{n}
-concentrated.
∎

Remark 6.17
.

If two noncollapsed Ricci flow limit spaces
(Z,d_{Z},z,\mathfrak{t})
and
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
are isometric (see Definition
5.21
), then they are also isometric as extended metric flows (see
[
Bam23
, Definition 3.7]
), by Remark
5.23
and Definition
6.1
.

By the same proof of
[
Bam23
, Proposition 3.16]
, we have the following monotonicity.

Lemma 6.18
.

For any
x,y\in Z
and
s\in(-(1-2\sigma)T,\min\{\mathfrak{t}(x),\mathfrak{t}(y)\}]
, the function

\displaystyle s\mapsto d_{W_{1}}^{Z_{s}}(\nu_{x;s},\nu_{y;s})\quad\text{is nondecreasing.}

Now, we prove the past-continuity.

Proposition 6.19
.

For any
x,y\in Z_{t}
with
t\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{s\nearrow t}d_{W_{1}}^{Z_{s}}(\nu_{x;s},\nu_{y;s})=d^{Z}_{t}(x,y).

Proof.

By Lemma
6.5
, there exists a sequence
t_{i}\nearrow t
such that if
x_{i},y_{i}\in\mathcal{R}_{t_{i}}
are regular
H_{n}
-centers of
x
and
y
, respectively, then

\displaystyle d^{Z}_{t}(x,y)=\lim_{i\to\infty}d^{Z}_{t_{i}}(x_{i},y_{i}).

Since

\displaystyle\left\lvert d_{W_{1}}^{Z_{t_{i}}}(\nu_{x;t_{i}},\nu_{y;t_{i}})-d^{Z}_{t_{i}}(x_{i},y_{i})\right\rvert\leq 2\sqrt{H_{n}(t-t_{i})},

we conclude that

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{W_{1}}^{Z_{t_{i}}}(\nu_{x;t_{i}},\nu_{y;t_{i}})=d^{Z}_{t}(x,y),

which, when combined with Lemma
6.18
, yields the conclusion. ∎

Next, we have the following characterization of
d_{Z}
.

Proposition 6.20
.

For any
x,y\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
with
t_{0}=\mathfrak{t}(x)\geq\mathfrak{t}(y)
, if
r=d_{Z}(x,y)
satisfies
t_{0}-r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{t\nearrow t_{0}-r^{2}}d_{W_{1}}^{Z_{t}}(\nu_{x;t},\nu_{y;t})\leq\epsilon_{0}r\leq\lim_{t\searrow t_{0}-r^{2}}d_{W_{1}}^{Z_{t}}(\nu_{x;t},\nu_{y;t}).

Proof.

By Proposition
5.16
, we conclude that
\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{t})=\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{t})
for any
t\in[-(1-2\sigma)T,t_{0}-r^{2})
. Moreover, it follows from Proposition
6.8
and (
5.6
) that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\nearrow t_{0}-r^{2}}d_{W_{1}}^{Z_{t}}(\nu_{x;t},\nu_{y;t})\leq\lim_{t\nearrow t_{0}-r^{2}}d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{x}_{t}}\left(\nu_{x;t},(\iota^{-1}_{x})_{*}(\nu_{y;t})\right)\leq\epsilon_{0}r.

Suppose that the other inequality fails. Then we can find
\delta>0
and
t_{1}>t_{0}-r^{2}
such that

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{Z_{t_{1}}}(\nu_{x;t_{1}},\nu_{y;t_{1}})\leq\epsilon_{0}r-\delta.

By Lemma
6.10
, we conclude that
\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{t})=\iota_{y}(\mathcal{R}^{y}_{t})
for any
t\in[-(1-2\sigma)T,t_{1})
. Then we choose
x^{*}_{i},y^{*}_{i}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
so that
x^{*}_{i}\to x
and
y^{*}_{i}\to y
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense and fix a
t_{2}\in(t_{0}-r^{2},t_{1})
. Moreover, we choose
t_{3}\in(t_{0}-r^{2},t_{2})
such that
\mathcal{X}^{x}
is continuous at
t_{3}
. In particular, the distance
d^{Z}_{t_{3}}
on
\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{t_{3}})
agrees with
d_{g^{Z}_{t_{3}}}
.

We claim that

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{t_{2}}(\nu_{x_{i}^{*};t_{2}},\nu_{y_{i}^{*};t_{2}})\leq D

(6.13)

for a constant
D
. To see this, let
z\in\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{t_{2}})
be a regular
H_{n}
-center of
x
, and suppose that
z_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\{t_{2}\}
converge to
z
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense. Then, by Theorem
2.15
(ii) and Theorem
5.20
,
z_{i}^{*}
is an
H
-center of
x_{i}^{*}
for some constant
H>0
independent of
i
. Similarly, let
w\in\iota_{x}(\mathcal{R}^{x}_{t_{2}})
be a regular
H_{n}
-center of
y
, and suppose that
w_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\{t_{2}\}
converge to
w
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense. Then
w_{i}^{*}
is also an
H
-center of
y_{i}^{*}
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{t_{2}}(\nu_{x_{i}^{*};t_{2}},\nu_{y_{i}^{*};t_{2}})\leq 2\sqrt{H(t_{0}-t_{2})}+d_{g_{i}(t_{2})}(z_{i}^{*},w_{i}^{*}).

By smooth convergence (Theorem
5.2
), the distance
d_{g_{i}(t_{2})}(z_{i}^{*},w_{i}^{*})
remains uniformly bounded. This establishes (
6.13
).

Therefore, it follows from Lemma
5.17
that

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{W_{1}}^{t_{3}}(\nu_{x_{i}^{*};t_{3}},\nu_{y_{i}^{*};t_{3}})=d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{x}_{t_{3}}}(\nu_{x;t_{3}},(\iota^{-1}_{x})_{*}(\nu_{y;t}))=d_{W_{1}}^{Z_{t_{3}}}(\nu_{x;t_{3}},\nu_{y;t_{3}})\leq\epsilon_{0}r-\delta,

where we used Lemma
6.18
. On the other hand, we have for sufficiently large
i
,

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{t_{3}}(\nu_{x_{i}^{*};t_{3}},\nu_{y_{i}^{*};t_{3}})\geq d_{W_{1}}^{t_{0}-r_{i}^{2}}(\nu_{x_{i}^{*};t_{0}-r_{i}^{2}},\nu_{y_{i}^{*};t_{0}-r_{i}^{2}})=\epsilon_{0}r_{i},

where
r_{i}:=d_{i}^{*}(x_{i}^{*},y_{i}^{*})
. Since
r_{i}\to r
, we obtain a contradiction.
∎

Combining Lemma
6.18
and Proposition
6.20
, the following corollary is immediate.

Corollary 6.21
.

If
x_{i}\to x
in
d_{Z}
, then for any
s<\mathfrak{t}(x)
,

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{W_{1}}^{Z_{s}}(\nu_{x_{i};s},\nu_{x;s})=0.

We also have the following result.

Lemma 6.22
.

If
x_{i},y_{i}\in Z_{t_{i}}
and
x,y\in Z_{t}
satisfy
x_{i}\to x,y_{i}\to y
in
d_{Z}
, then

\displaystyle d^{Z}_{t}(x,y)\leq\liminf_{i\to\infty}d^{Z}_{t_{i}}(x_{i},y_{i}).

Proof.

For any
s<t
, since
x_{i}\to x,y_{i}\to y
in
d_{Z}
, we have, by Corollary
6.21
,
\nu_{x_{i};s}\to\nu_{x;s},\nu_{y_{i};s}\to\nu_{y;s}
in
d_{W_{1}}^{Z_{s}}
, and hence
\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{W_{1}}^{Z_{s}}(\nu_{x_{i};s},\nu_{y_{i};s})=d_{W_{1}}^{Z_{s}}(\nu_{x;s},\nu_{y;s})
. By Lemma
6.18
and Proposition
6.19
,
d_{W_{1}}^{Z_{s}}(\nu_{x_{i};s},\nu_{y_{i};s})\leq d^{Z}_{t_{i}}(x_{i},y_{i})
for large
i
, and therefore

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{Z_{s}}(\nu_{x;s},\nu_{y;s})\leq\liminf_{i\to\infty}d^{Z}_{t_{i}}(x_{i},y_{i}),

which, by using Proposition
6.19
, implies

\displaystyle d^{Z}_{t}(x,y)\leq\liminf_{i\to\infty}d^{Z}_{t_{i}}(z_{i},w_{i}).

∎

We end this section by proving the following result.

Proposition 6.23
.

For all but countably many times
t\in\mathbb{I}
, we have

\displaystyle d^{Z}_{t}=d_{g^{Z}_{t}}

on each connected component of
\mathcal{R}_{t}
.

Proof.

We choose
\{t_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}=(-(1-2\sigma)T,0)\cap\mathbb{Q}
. For each
t_{k}
, it follows from Proposition
5.32
that each
\mathcal{R}_{t_{k}}
has at most countable connected components, denoted by
U_{k,j}
. By Corollary
5.12
, there exist
z_{k,j}\in\mathcal{R}
such that

\displaystyle U_{k,j}=\iota_{z_{k,j}}(\mathcal{R}^{z_{k,j}}_{t_{k}}).

For the associated metric flow
\mathcal{X}^{z_{k,j}}
, it follows from
[
Bam23
, Corollary 4.11]
that there exists a countable set
J_{k,j}\subset\mathbb{I}
such that
\mathcal{X}^{z_{k,j}}
is continuous at time
t\notin J_{k,j}
. Thus, it follows from
[
Bam23
, Equation (4.22)]
and Definition
6.1
that

\displaystyle d^{Z}_{t}=d_{g^{Z}_{t}}

(6.14)

on
\iota_{z_{k,j}}(\mathcal{R}^{z_{k,j}}_{t})
, for any
t\in[-(1-2\sigma)T,\mathfrak{t}(z_{k,j}))\setminus J_{k,j}
.

We set
J=\bigcup_{k,j}J_{k,j}\bigcup\{0\}\bigcup\{-(1-2\sigma)T\}
, which is a countable set. For any
t\in\mathbb{I}\setminus J
and a connected component
U
of
\mathcal{R}_{t}
, there exists
z\in Z
such that
\iota_{z}(\mathcal{R}^{z}_{t})=U
. We choose
t_{k}\in(t,\mathfrak{t}(z))
and
j
so that

\displaystyle\iota_{z}(\mathcal{R}^{z}_{t_{k}})=U_{k,j}=\iota_{z_{k,j}}(\mathcal{R}^{z_{k,j}}_{t_{k}}).

Thus, it follows from Lemma
5.11
that

\displaystyle\iota_{z}(\mathcal{R}^{z}_{t})=\iota_{z_{k,j}}(\mathcal{R}^{z_{k,j}}_{t}).

Consequently, it follows from (
6.14
) that on
U
,

\displaystyle d^{Z}_{t}=d_{g^{Z}_{t}}.

∎

7
Ricci shrinker spaces and tangent flows

As the last section, we consider a Ricci flow limit space
(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t})
obtained from

(M_{i}\times\mathbb{I},d^{*}_{i},p_{i}^{*},\mathfrak{t}_{i})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathrm{pGH}\hskip 8.19447pt}(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t}),

(7.1)

where
\mathcal{X}^{i}=\{M_{i}^{n},(g_{i}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
with base point
p_{i}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}^{i}_{\mathbb{I}}
.

First, we define the Nash entropy
\mathcal{N}_{z}(\tau)
at a point
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
, which is a direct generalization of Definition
2.7
.

Definition 7.1

(Nash entropy)
.

For
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
, we write
K(z;\cdot)=(4\pi(\mathfrak{t}(z)-\mathfrak{t}(\cdot)))^{-n/2}e^{-f_{z}(\cdot)}
, where
f_{z}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{R}_{(-(1-2\sigma)T,\mathfrak{t}(z))})
. Then the Nash entropy at
z
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{z}(\tau):=\int_{\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{t}(z)-\tau}}f_{z}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z;\mathfrak{t}(z)-\tau}-\frac{n}{2}

for any
\tau\in(0,\mathfrak{t}(z)+(1-2\sigma)T)
.

Lemma 7.2
.

Suppose
z_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
converges to
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense. Then for any
\tau\in(0,\mathfrak{t}(z)+(1-2\sigma)T)
,

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}\mathcal{N}_{z_{i}^{*}}(\tau)=\mathcal{N}_{z}(\tau).

Proof.

Suppose otherwise. There exist
\delta>0
and a subsequence
\{i_{j}\}
such that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{N}_{z_{i_{j}}^{*}}(\tau)-\mathcal{N}_{z}(\tau)\right\rvert\geq\delta.

(7.2)

By taking a further subsequence if necessary, there exists a correspondence
\mathfrak{C}
such that

(\mathcal{X}^{i_{j}},(\nu_{z_{i_{j}}^{*};t})_{t\in[-T,\mathfrak{t}(z_{i_{j}}^{*})]})\xrightarrow[j\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathbb{F},\mathfrak{C},J\hskip 8.19447pt}(\mathcal{X}^{z},(\nu_{z;t})_{t\in[-T,\mathfrak{t}(z)]}).

In particular, we have

\displaystyle z_{i_{j}}^{*}\xrightarrow[j\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathfrak{C},J\hskip 8.19447pt}z.

For any
\tau\in(0,\mathfrak{t}(z)+(1-2\sigma)T)
, since
\nu_{z;\mathfrak{t}(z)-\tau}
has a full measure on
\iota_{z}(\mathcal{R}^{z}_{\mathfrak{t}(z)-\tau})
, we conclude from Lemma
5.19
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{z}(\tau):=\int_{\iota_{z}(\mathcal{R}^{z}_{\mathfrak{t}(z)-\tau})}f_{z}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z;\mathfrak{t}(z)-\tau}-\frac{n}{2}.

However, it follows from
[
Bam20b
, Theorem 2.10]
that

\displaystyle\lim_{j\to\infty}\mathcal{N}_{z_{i_{j}}^{*}}(\tau)=\mathcal{N}_{z}(\tau)

which contradicts (
7.2
).
∎

Proposition 7.3
.

For any
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
,
\mathcal{N}_{z}(\tau)
is nonincreasing for
\tau>0
. If
\mathcal{N}_{z}(\tau)
is a constant for any
\tau>0
, then
\mathcal{X}^{z}_{(-(1-2\sigma)T,\mathfrak{t}(z))}
is a metric soliton. Moreover, we have on
\iota_{z}\left(\mathcal{R}^{z}_{(-(1-2\sigma)T,\mathfrak{t}(z))}\right)
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}(g^{Z})+\nabla^{2}f_{z}=\frac{g^{Z}}{2\tau_{z}},

where
\tau_{z}=\mathfrak{t}(z)-\mathfrak{t}(\cdot)
.

Proof.

From the convergence (
7.1
), there exists
z_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
so that
z_{i}^{*}\to z
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense. Then, the fact that
\mathcal{N}_{z}(\tau)
is increasing follows immediately from Lemma
7.2
.

If
\mathcal{N}_{z}(\tau)
is constant, then it follows from Proposition
2.8
and Lemma
7.2
that there exists a sequence
\delta_{i}\to 0
such that

\displaystyle\int_{-T+\delta_{i}}^{\mathfrak{t}_{i}(z_{i}^{*})-\delta_{i}}\int_{M_{i}}\left\lvert\mathrm{Ric}(g_{i})+\nabla^{2}f_{z_{i}^{*}}-\frac{g_{i}}{2(\mathfrak{t}_{i}(z_{i}^{*})-\mathfrak{t}_{i})}\right\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{i}^{*};t}\mathrm{d}t\leq\delta_{i}.

By the smooth convergence in Theorem
5.2
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}(g^{Z})+\nabla^{2}f_{z}=\frac{g^{Z}}{2\tau_{z}}

holds on
\iota_{z}\left(\mathcal{R}^{z}_{(-(1-2\sigma)T,\mathfrak{t}(z))}\right)
. By the high codimension of the singular set of
\mathcal{X}^{z}
, it can be proved (see
[
Bam20b
, Theorem 15.69]
) that
\mathcal{X}^{z}_{(-(1-2\sigma)T,\mathfrak{t}(z))}
is a metric soliton.
∎

Next, we define

Definition 7.4

(Curvature radius)
.

For any
z\in\mathcal{R}
, the curvature radius
r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(z)
is defined to be the supremum of all
r>0
such that
B_{g^{Z}_{t}}(z,r)
is relatively compact in
\mathcal{R}_{t}
, and the product domain

\displaystyle B_{g^{Z}_{t}}(z,r)\times[\mathfrak{t}(z)-r^{2},\mathfrak{t}(z)+r^{2}]\cap\mathbb{I}

is defined on
\mathcal{R}
with the curvature bound
|\mathrm{Rm}|\leq r^{-2}
.

The following lemma is immediate from Definition
5.1
and Theorem
5.2
.

Lemma 7.5
.

Suppose that
z_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
converge to
z\in Z
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense and
\mathfrak{t}(z)-r^{2}_{\mathrm{Rm}}(z)\in\mathbb{I}
. Then

\displaystyle r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(z)=\lim_{i\to\infty}r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(z_{i}^{*}).

By taking the limit of Proposition
3.21
and using Lemma
7.5
, we immediately obtain the following result.

Proposition 7.6
.

For any
x,y\in Z
with
\mathfrak{t}(x)-r^{2}_{\mathrm{Rm}}(x)\in\mathbb{I}
and
\mathfrak{t}(y)-r^{2}_{\mathrm{Rm}}(y)\in\mathbb{I}
, we have

\displaystyle|r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(x)-r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(y)|\leq C(n,Y)d_{Z}(x,y).

Next, we prove

Proposition 7.7
.

There exists a constant
\epsilon=\epsilon(n)>0
such that if
\mathcal{N}_{z}(r^{2})\geq-\epsilon
, then

\displaystyle r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(z)\geq\epsilon r.

Proof.

We choose
\epsilon=\epsilon_{n}/2
, where
\epsilon_{n}
is the same constant as in Theorem
2.11
. From the convergence (
7.1
), there exists
z_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
so that
z_{i}^{*}\to z
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense. Then it follows from Lemma
7.2
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{z_{i}^{*}}(r^{2})\geq\epsilon_{n}

for large
i
. By Theorem
2.11
, we conclude that
r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(z_{i}^{*})\geq\epsilon_{n}r
, which yields the conclusion by Lemma
7.5
.
∎

Definition 7.8

(Tangent flow)
.

For any
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
, a
tangent flow
at
z
is a pointed parabolic metric space
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
, which is a pointed Gromov–Hausdorff limit of
(Z,r_{j}^{-1}d_{Z},z,r_{j}^{-2}(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}(z)))
for a sequence
r_{j}\searrow 0
.

Suppose
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is a tangent flow at
z
, which is obtained from the pointed Gromov–Hausdorff limit of
(Z,r_{j}^{-1}d_{Z},z,r_{j}^{-2}(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}(z)))
. Then
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is a Ricci flow limit space over
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{R}_{-}
. Indeed, by the convergence (
7.1
), there exists
z_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
so that
z_{i}^{*}\to z
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense. For each
j
, we can find
i_{j}
so that if we set

\displaystyle g^{\prime}_{j}(t)=r_{j}^{-2}g_{i_{j}}\left(r_{j}^{2}t+\mathfrak{t}_{i_{j}}(z_{i_{j}}^{*})\right),\quad\mathfrak{t}_{j}^{\prime}:=r_{j}^{-2}(\mathfrak{t}_{i_{j}}-\mathfrak{t}_{i_{j}}(z^{*}_{i_{j}})),

\displaystyle T_{j}=r_{j}^{-2}(T+\mathfrak{t}_{i_{j}}(z^{*}_{i_{j}})),\quad T_{j}^{\prime}=-r_{j}^{-2}\mathfrak{t}_{i_{j}}(z_{i_{j}}^{*}),\quad\mathbb{I}^{++}_{j}=[-T_{j},T_{j}^{\prime}],

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}_{j}^{+}=[-T_{j}+\sigma(T_{j}^{\prime}+T_{j}),T_{j}^{\prime}],\quad\mathbb{I}_{j}=[-T_{j}+2\sigma(T_{j}^{\prime}+T_{j}),T_{j}^{\prime}],

then, after a time translation,
\{M_{i_{j}},(g_{j}^{\prime}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}_{j}^{++}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T_{j}^{\prime}+T_{j})
. Thus, by taking a subsequence, we have the following convergence (see Remark
3.25
and Notation
5.38
)

\displaystyle(M_{i_{j}}\times\mathbb{I}_{j},d^{\prime,*}_{j},z_{i_{j}}^{*},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}_{j})\xrightarrow[j\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\hat{C}^{\infty}\hskip 8.19447pt}(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}),

(7.3)

where
d^{\prime,*}_{j}
is the induced
d^{*}
-distance by
g_{j}^{\prime}(t)
. Consequently, we conclude that
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space over
\mathbb{R}
if
\mathfrak{t}(z)\in(-(1-2\sigma)T,0)
or over
\mathbb{R}_{-}
if
\mathfrak{t}(z)=0
.

As in the Definition
5.1
, we denote by
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
the set of points at which (
7.3
) is smooth. Then
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
is realized as a Ricci flow spacetime
(\mathcal{R}^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}},g^{Z^{\prime}}_{t})
.

Proposition 7.9
.

For any tangent flow
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
at
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
, we have on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}(g^{Z^{\prime}})+\nabla^{2}f_{z^{\prime}}=\frac{g^{Z^{\prime}}}{2\tau},

(7.4)

where
\tau(\cdot)=-\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(\cdot)
. Moreover,
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}
is connected for any
t\in(-\infty,0)
.

Proof.

We assume that the convergence (
7.3
) holds. For any
\tau>0
, it follows from Lemma
7.2
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{z^{\prime}}(\tau)=\lim_{j\to\infty}\mathcal{N}_{z}(\tau r_{j}^{2}).

Since the last limit is independent of
\tau
, we conclude that
\mathcal{N}_{z^{\prime}}(\tau)
is constant for
\tau>0
. Thus, it follows from Proposition
7.3
that on
\iota_{z^{\prime}}(\mathcal{R}^{z^{\prime}}_{(-\infty,0)})
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}(g^{Z^{\prime}})+\nabla^{2}f_{z^{\prime}}=\frac{g^{Z^{\prime}}}{2\tau}.

(7.5)

To finish the proof, we only need to prove that
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}
is connected for any
t\in(-\infty,0)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t_{0}}
is disconnected for some
t_{0}\in(-\infty,0)
. We fix
x_{0}\in\iota_{z^{\prime}}(\mathcal{R}^{z^{\prime}}_{t_{0}})
and
y_{0}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t_{0}}
so that
y_{0}
lies in a different connected component than
x_{0}
. By Corollary
5.28
, there exists a curve
\gamma(s)
,
s\in[0,L]
, contained in
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
such that
\gamma(0)=z_{0}\in\iota_{z^{\prime}}(\mathcal{R}^{z^{\prime}}_{t_{1}})
for some
t_{1}<t_{0}
, and
\gamma(L)=y_{0}
. Then we set

\displaystyle s_{0}=\sup\{s^{\prime}\in[0,L]\mid\gamma(s)\in\iota_{z^{\prime}}(\mathcal{R}^{z^{\prime}}_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(\gamma(s))})\text{ for any }s\in[0,s^{\prime}]\}

and
t_{2}:=\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(\gamma(s_{0}))
. It is clear that
t_{2}\in(t_{1},t_{0}]
and

\displaystyle\lim_{s\nearrow s_{0}}f_{z^{\prime}}(\gamma(s))=+\infty.

(7.6)

On the other hand, it follows from (
7.5
) that

\displaystyle\partial_{t^{\prime}}f_{z^{\prime}}=|\nabla f_{z^{\prime}}|^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad f_{z^{\prime}}-\tau(|\nabla f_{z^{\prime}}|^{2}+\mathrm{R}_{g^{Z^{\prime}}})=\mathcal{N}_{z^{\prime}}(1).

(7.7)

Since
\mathrm{R}_{g^{Z^{\prime}}}\geq 0
, we conclude from (
7.7
) that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathchoice{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mus}}{\displaystyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mus}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mus}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mus}}}f_{z^{\prime}}(\gamma(s))\right\rvert\leq C_{0}(f_{z^{\prime}}(\gamma(s))+1)

for some constant
C_{0}>0
and any
s\in[0,s_{0})
.
By integration, we conclude that
\lim_{s\nearrow s_{0}}f_{z^{\prime}}(\gamma(s))
must be finite, which contradicts (
7.6
).

Consequently, we have proved that
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}
is connected for any
t\in(-\infty,0)
, and hence
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}=\iota_{z^{\prime}}(\mathcal{R}^{z^{\prime}}_{t})
for any
t\in(-\infty,0)
.
∎

By Proposition
7.3
and Proposition
7.9
, the metric flow
\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}
associated with
z^{\prime}
is a metric soliton so that
\iota_{z^{\prime}}(\mathcal{R}^{z^{\prime}}_{t})=\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}
for any
t<0
. Moreover, since any metric soliton is continuous, by Remark
4.24
, the map
\iota_{z^{\prime}}
is injective on
\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}
. Thus, by Theorem
4.27
,
\iota_{z^{\prime}}
is an isometric embedding from
\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}
to
Z^{\prime}
. Moreover, since
\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}
is continuous, it follows from Definition
6.1
that the following result holds.

Corollary 7.10
.

For any
t<0
, the metric
d^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}
, when restricted on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}
, agrees with
g^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}
.

Definition 7.11

(Tangent metric soliton)
.

The metric soliton
\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}
is called a
tangent metric soliton
at
z
.

We have the following fundamental estimates for
f_{z^{\prime}}
, which are well-known for smooth Ricci shrinkers. The estimates can be proved in the same way as
[
CMZ24
, Theorem 1.1]
. Notice that the lower bound is improved due to Theorem
2.15
(ii).

Lemma 7.12
.

For any tangent flow
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
at
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
, we have for any
x\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
,

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}_{g^{Z^{\prime}}_{\mathfrak{t}(x)}}(x,p_{\mathfrak{t}(x)})}{(4+\epsilon)\tau(x)}-C(n,\epsilon)\leq f_{z^{\prime}}(x)-\mathcal{N}_{z^{\prime}}(1)\leq\frac{1}{4\tau(x)}\left(d_{g^{Z^{\prime}}_{\mathfrak{t}(x)}}(x,p_{\mathfrak{t}(x)})+C(n)\sqrt{\tau(x)}\right)^{2},
