where
p_{-1}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}
is a regular
H_{n}
-center of
z^{\prime}
and
p_{t}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}
is the flow of
\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}-\nabla f_{z^{\prime}}
from
p_{-1}
.

We also need the following no-local-collapsing theorem in
[
CMZ24
, Lemma 8.1]
, which was originally proved in
[
LW20
, Theorem 22]
for smooth Ricci shrinkers.

Lemma 7.13
.

Given a tangent flow
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
at
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
. For any
x\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}
with
t<0
, if
\mathrm{R}_{g^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}}\leq r^{-2}
on
B_{g^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}}(x,r)
, then

\displaystyle\left\lvert B_{g^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}}(x,r)\right\rvert_{t}\geq c(n,Y)r^{n}>0.

Definition 7.14

(Regular and singular sets)
.

For the Ricci flow limit space
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
, a point
z\in Z_{(-T,0)}
is
regular
if there exists a tangent flow at
z
that is isometric (see Definition
5.21
) to
(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R},d^{*}_{E,\epsilon_{0}},(\vec{0},0),\mathfrak{t})
, where
d^{*}_{E,\epsilon_{0}}
denotes the induced
d^{*}
-distance on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
defined with respect to the same spacetime distance constant
\epsilon_{0}
(see Example
3.9
). Similarly, a point
z\in Z_{0}
is
regular
if there exists a tangent flow at
z
that is isometric to
(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}_{-},d^{*}_{E,\epsilon_{0}},(\vec{0},0),\mathfrak{t})
. Any point in
Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
that is not regular is called
singular
.

Theorem 7.15
.

Let
\mathcal{R}^{*}\subset Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
denote the set of regular points. Then
\mathcal{R}^{*}=\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
.

Proof.

For any
z\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
, suppose that
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is a tangent flow at
z
. By Lemma
7.5
, we conclude that
z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
and
r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(z^{\prime})=+\infty
. By Definition
7.4
, this implies that
(\mathcal{R}^{\prime},g^{Z^{\prime}}_{t})
is given by the conventional Ricci flow
(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R},g_{E})
or
(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}_{-},g_{E})
so that
z^{\prime}
corresponds to
(\vec{0},0)
. By Proposition
3.10
, both
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}_{-}
, when equipped with
d^{*}_{E,\epsilon_{0}}
, are complete. Thus, we conclude that
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is isometric to
(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R},d^{*}_{E,\epsilon_{0}},(\vec{0},0),\mathfrak{t})
or
(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}_{-},d^{*}_{E,\epsilon_{0}},(\vec{0},0),\mathfrak{t})
.

Conversely, if
z\in\mathcal{R}^{*}
, then, by Lemma
7.2
, we can find a small
r>0
such that
\mathcal{N}_{z}(r^{2})\geq-\epsilon
, where
\epsilon
is the same constant in Proposition
7.7
. Then, from Lemma
7.5
and Proposition
7.7
we obtain
z\in\mathcal{R}
.
∎

Definition
7.14
and Theorem
7.15
give rise to the following regular-singular decomposition:

\displaystyle Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}=\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}\sqcup\mathcal{S},

(7.8)

where
\mathcal{S}
denotes the set of singular points.

Next, we introduce a class that contains all tangent flows.

Definition 7.16

(Ricci shrinker space)
.

A pointed parabolic metric space
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
with
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(z^{\prime})=0
is called an
n
-dimensional
Ricci shrinker space
with entropy bounded below by
-Y
if it satisfies
\mathbb{R}_{-}\subset\mathrm{image}(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
and arises as the pointed Gromov–Hausdorff limit of a sequence of Ricci flows in
\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T_{i})
with
T_{i}\to+\infty
(see Remark
3.25
). Moreover,
\mathcal{N}_{z^{\prime}}(\tau)
remains constant for all
\tau>0
.

As above, we denote by
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
the regular set, which is realized as a Ricci flow spacetime
(\mathcal{R}^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}},g^{Z^{\prime}}_{t})
. With identical proofs, one can show Proposition
7.9
, Corollary
7.10
, Lemma
7.12
and Lemma
7.13
also hold for Ricci shrinker spaces.

We make the following definitions:

Definition 7.17

(Static/quasi-static cone)
.

Let
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
be a Ricci shrinker space.

•

It is called a
static cone
if the Ricci curvature vanishes on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}
and the
arrival time

\displaystyle t_{a}:=\sup\{\mathfrak{t}(x)\mid x\in\mathrm{spine}(Z^{\prime})\}=+\infty.

(7.9)

•

It is called a
quasi-static cone
if the Ricci curvature vanishes on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}
and
t_{a}<+\infty
.

The definition of the spine and its properties are provided in Appendix
D
.

Note that since
(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)},g^{Z^{\prime}}_{t})
is self-similar, the Ricci curvature vanishes on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
for a static cone.

Definition 7.18

(Noncollapsed and collapsed Ricci shrinker space)
.

A Ricci shrinker space
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is called
noncollapsed
if for some base point
p\in Z^{\prime}_{-1}
,

\displaystyle\liminf_{r\to\infty}\frac{\left\lvert\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}\bigcap B_{g^{Z^{\prime}}_{-1}}(p,r)\right\rvert_{-1}}{r^{n}}>0.

(7.10)

Otherwise,
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is called
collapsed
.

It is clear from Proposition
2.17
(i) and Lemma
7.13
that any static cone is noncollapsed whose asymptotic volume ratio is contained in
[C(n,Y)^{-1},C(n,Y)]
for a constant
C(n,Y)>1
.

Theorem 7.19
.

Suppose a Ricci shrinker space
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
satisfies that the scalar curvature is uniformly bounded on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}
. Then

\displaystyle\iota_{z^{\prime}}\left(\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}_{(-\infty,0)}\right)=Z^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}.

Proof.

By the self-similarity of
(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)},g^{Z^{\prime}}_{t})
, we conclude that the scalar curvature is uniformly bounded on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{J}
for any compact interval
J\subset(-\infty,0)
.

To finish the proof, we only need to show that
\iota_{z^{\prime}}(\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}_{J})
is complete with respect to
d_{Z^{\prime}}
, for any compact interval
J\subset(-\infty,0)
. In other words, we need to prove that
\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}_{J}
is complete with respect to
d^{*}_{z^{\prime}}
(see Definition
4.22
). In the following, we use
d_{t}
to denote the distance function at time
t
on
\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}_{t}
.

Consider a Cauchy sequence
x_{i}\in\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}_{J}
. Since
\mathcal{R}^{z^{\prime}}_{J}
is dense in
\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}_{J}
, we may assume that all
x_{i}\in\mathcal{R}^{z^{\prime}}_{J}
. Since
\sqrt{|\mathfrak{t}^{z^{\prime}}(x)-\mathfrak{t}^{z^{\prime}}(y)|}\leq d_{z^{\prime}}^{*}(x,y)
, we assume, by taking a subsequence, that
\mathfrak{t}^{z^{\prime}}(x_{i})\to t_{0}\in J
. Since
x_{i}
is a Cauchy sequence, then for any
t<t_{0}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{i,j\to\infty}d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}_{t}}(\nu_{x_{i};t},\nu_{x_{j};t})=0.

(7.11)

We fix a time
t_{1}<t_{0}
to be determined later and set
z_{i}\in\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}_{t_{1}}
to be an
H_{n}
-center of
x_{i}
. By (
7.11
), we have for large
i,j
,

\displaystyle\lim_{i,j\to\infty}d_{t_{1}}(z_{i},z_{j})\leq\lim_{i,j\to\infty}\left(d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}_{t_{1}}}(\nu_{x_{i};t_{1}},z_{i})+d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}_{t_{1}}}(\nu_{x_{j};t_{1}},z_{j})+d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}_{t_{1}}}(\nu_{x_{i};t_{1}},\nu_{x_{j};t_{1}})\right)\leq 2\sqrt{H_{n}(t_{0}-t_{1})}.

In particular,
\{z_{i}\}
is uniformly bounded with respect to
d_{t_{1}}
.

Since the scalar curvature is assumed to be uniformly bounded on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{J}
for any compact interval
J\subset(-\infty,0)
, we conclude from Lemma
7.13
that

\displaystyle|B_{\mathfrak{t}^{z^{\prime}}(x_{i})}(x_{i},1)|_{\mathfrak{t}^{z^{\prime}}(x_{i})}\geq c_{0}>0

(7.12)

for a constant
c_{0}
. Then, it follows from (
7.12
) and
[
Bam20b
, Theorem 2.31, Lemma 15.27 (a)]
(see also Corollary
8.16
) that we can find
y_{i}\in B_{\mathfrak{t}^{z^{\prime}}(x_{i})}(x_{i},1)\cap\mathcal{R}^{z^{\prime}}
such that

\displaystyle r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(y_{i})\geq c_{1}>0

(7.13)

for a constant
c_{1}
.

Next, we fix
t_{1}
so that
t_{0}-t_{1}\leq c_{1}^{2}/10
. Then it follows from (
7.13
) and Lemma
5.31
that the
H_{n}
-center of
y_{i}
in
\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}_{t_{1}}
, denoted by
w_{i}
, satisfies

\displaystyle d_{t_{1}}(w_{i},y_{i,t_{1}})\leq C_{2}

for a constant
C_{2}>0
, where
y_{i,t}\in\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}_{t}
denotes the flow line of
\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{z^{\prime}}}
from
y_{i}
. Moreover, it follows from the monotonicity that

\displaystyle d_{t_{1}}(w_{i},z_{i})\leq d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}_{t_{1}}}(\nu_{x_{i};t_{1}},\nu_{y_{i};t_{1}})+2\sqrt{H_{n}(\mathfrak{t}^{z^{\prime}}(x_{i})-t_{1})}\leq 1+2\sqrt{H_{n}(\mathfrak{t}^{z^{\prime}}(x_{i})-t_{1})}.

Thus, we conclude that
\{w_{i}\}
and hence
\{y_{i,t_{1}}\}
are uniformly bounded with respect to
d_{t_{1}}
. In particular, it follows from Lemma
7.12
that
f_{z^{\prime}}(y_{i,t_{1}})
are uniformly bounded.

On the other hand, since
\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}f_{z^{\prime}}=|\nabla f_{z^{\prime}}|^{2}\leq\tau^{-1}(f_{z^{\prime}}+C(n,Y))
, we obtain that
f_{z^{\prime}}(y_{i})
are uniformly bounded. Now, we set
x_{i;s}\in\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}_{s}
to be the flow line of
\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{z^{\prime}}}-\nabla f_{z^{\prime}}
from
x_{i}
and define
x_{i}^{\prime}=x_{i;t_{0}}
. By the definition of a metric soliton, we conclude that
x_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}^{z^{\prime}}_{t_{0}}
, and
\{x_{i}^{\prime}\}
are uniformly bounded with respect to
d_{t_{0}}
. Since
(\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}_{t_{0}},d_{t_{0}})
is complete, we assume that
x_{i}^{\prime}
converge to
x_{\infty}
in
d_{t_{0}}
.

We first assume
\mathfrak{t}^{z^{\prime}}(x_{i})\geq t_{0}
. It is clear from Lemma
7.12
that for any
s\in[t_{0},\mathfrak{t}^{z^{\prime}}(x_{i})]
,

\displaystyle|\nabla f_{z^{\prime}}|^{2}(x_{i;s})+\mathrm{R}_{g^{z^{\prime}}}(x_{i;s})\leq C_{3}

(7.14)

for a constant
C_{3}
. On the other hand, the heat kernel satisfies:

\displaystyle K_{Z^{\prime}}(x_{i};x_{i}^{\prime})\geq\frac{1}{\left(4\pi|t_{0}-\mathfrak{t}^{z^{\prime}}(x_{i})|\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\mathfrak{t}^{z^{\prime}}(x_{i})-t_{0}}}\int_{t_{0}}^{\mathfrak{t}^{z^{\prime}}(x_{i})}\sqrt{\mathfrak{t}^{z^{\prime}}(x_{i})-s}\left(|\nabla f_{z^{\prime}}|^{2}(x_{i;s})+\mathrm{R}_{g^{z^{\prime}}}(x_{i;s})\right)\,\mathrm{d}s\right).

(7.15)

Indeed, this estimate follows from the corresponding estimate for the closed Ricci flow (see
[
Per02
, Corollary 9.4]
) and the smooth convergence.

Therefore, if we denote an
H_{n}
-center in
\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}_{t_{0}}
of
x_{i}
by
z^{\prime}_{i}
, then it follows from Theorem
5.30
, (
7.14
) and (
7.15
) that

\displaystyle d_{t_{0}}(x_{i}^{\prime},z_{i}^{\prime})\leq C_{4}(\mathfrak{t}^{z^{\prime}}(x_{i})-t_{0}),

and hence by Proposition
3.12
(1) and Lemma
5.27
that

\displaystyle d_{z^{\prime}}^{*}(x_{i},x_{i}^{\prime})\leq C_{5}\sqrt{\mathfrak{t}^{z^{\prime}}(x_{i})-t_{0}}.

(7.16)

If
\mathfrak{t}^{z^{\prime}}(x_{i})<t_{0}
, we can also obtain (
7.16
) in a similar way.

Now, it follows from Proposition
3.12
(1) and (
7.16
) that
x_{i}\to x_{\infty}
in
d_{z^{\prime}}^{*}
. Therefore, we have completed the proof.
∎

Remark 7.20
.

By the same argument as in the proof of Theorem
7.19
, one can show that for any Ricci shrinker space
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
,

\displaystyle d^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}(x,y)=+\infty

for any
t<0
, whenever
x\in\iota_{z^{\prime}}\left(\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}_{t}\right)
and
y\in Z^{\prime}_{t}\setminus\iota_{z^{\prime}}\left(\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}_{t}\right)
. In general, we conjecture that the conclusion of Theorem
7.19
remains valid even without the assumption on the scalar curvature.

Theorem
7.19
applies, in particular, to static or quasi-static cones. In fact, we have the following characterization.

Theorem 7.21
.

Let
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
be a Ricci shrinker space that is a static or quasi-static cone. Then
(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,t_{a}]},g^{Z^{\prime}})
is isometric to
(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}\times(-\infty,t_{a}])
, where
t_{a}
is defined in (
7.9
).

Proof.

By Lemma
D.5
, we know that
\mathrm{Ric}\equiv 0
on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,t_{a}]}
. Now, it follows from
[
Bam20b
, Theorem 2.16, Theorem 15.60, Claim 22.7]
that

\displaystyle\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},x}K_{Z^{\prime}}(x;y)+\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},y}K_{Z^{\prime}}(x;y)=0

for
x,y\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,t_{a}]}
. In other words, if we denote the flow induced by
\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}
by
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}
. Then
K_{Z^{\prime}}(x;y)=K_{Z^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(x);\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(y))
for any
x,y,\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(x),\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(y)\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,t_{a}]}
. Thus, one can follow the same argument as in the proof of
[
Bam20b
, Theorem 15.60]
to show that the Nash entropy
\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(x)}(\tau)
is constant as long as
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(x)\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
. This, by Proposition
7.7
, implies that
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(x)\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,t_{a}]}
for any
t\in(-\infty,t_{a}-\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(x)]
as long as
x\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,t_{a}]}
.

Consequently, we conclude that
(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,t_{a}]},g^{Z^{\prime}})
is isometric to
(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}\times(-\infty,t_{a}],g^{Z^{\prime}}_{-1})
.
∎

Combining Corollary
7.10
, Theorem
7.19
and Theorem
7.21
, we have

Corollary 7.22
.

With the above assumptions, for any
t\in(-\infty,t_{a}]
,
g^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}
on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}
agrees with
d^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}
. Moreover,
(Z^{\prime}_{t},d^{Z^{\prime}}_{t})
is the completion of
(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t},g^{Z^{\prime}}_{t})
.

In the setting of Theorem
7.21
, there exists a flow induced by
\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}
on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
. More precisely, for any
x\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,t_{a}]}
, we define
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(x)\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{t}(x)+t}
to be the flow line of
\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}
from
x
, where
t\in(-\infty,t_{a}-\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(x)]
.

Proposition 7.23
.

With the above assumptions,
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}
can be defined on
Z^{\prime}
so that the following statements hold.

(i)

For any
x,y\in Z^{\prime}_{(-\infty,t_{a}]}
,
d_{Z^{\prime}}(x,y)=d_{Z^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(x),\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(y))
for all
t\in(-\infty,t_{a}-\max\{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(x),\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(y)\}]
.

(ii)

For any
x,y\in Z^{\prime}_{s}
,
d^{Z^{\prime}}_{s}(x,y)=d^{Z^{\prime}}_{s+t}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(x),\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(y))
for all
s\leq t_{a}
and
t\in(-\infty,t_{a}-s]
.

(iii)

For any
x\in Z^{\prime}_{(-\infty,t_{a}]}
and
\tau>0
,
\mathcal{N}_{x}(\tau)=\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(x)}(\tau)
for all
t\in(-\infty,t_{a}-\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(x)]
.

Proof.

As in the proof of Theorem
7.21
, we have

\displaystyle K_{Z^{\prime}}(x;y)=K_{Z^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(x);\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(y))

(7.17)

for any
x,y\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,t_{a}]}
and
t\in(-\infty,t_{a}-\max\{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(x),\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(y)\}]
. Thus, by Definition
6.1
, we conclude that

\displaystyle d^{Z^{\prime}}_{s}(x,y)=d^{Z^{\prime}}_{s+t}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(x),\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(y))

(7.18)

for any
x,y\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{s}
with
s\leq t_{a}
and
t\leq t_{a}-s
. By Proposition
6.20
, this implies

\displaystyle d_{Z^{\prime}}(x,y)=d_{Z^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(x),\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(y))

(7.19)

for any
x,y\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,t_{a}]}
and
t\in(-\infty,t_{a}-\max\{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(x),\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(y)\}]
.

Next, for any
w\in Z^{\prime}_{(-\infty,t_{a}]}
, we choose a sequence
w_{i}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,\mathfrak{t}(w)]}
such that
w_{i}\to w
in
d_{Z^{\prime}}
. Then, for any
t\in(-\infty,t_{a}-\mathfrak{t}(w)]
,
\{\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(w_{i})\}
is a Cauchy sequence by (
7.19
) with respect to
d_{Z^{\prime}}
. We define

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(w)=\lim_{i\to\infty}\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(w_{i}).

It is clear that the definition of
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(w)
is independent of the choice of
\{w_{i}\}
. Moreover, it follows from (
5.11
) and (
7.17
) that

\displaystyle K_{Z^{\prime}}(x;y)=K_{Z^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(x);\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(y))

(7.20)

for any
x\in Z^{\prime}_{(-\infty,t_{a}]}
,
y\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(x))}
and
t\in(-\infty,t_{a}-\mathfrak{t}(x)]
.

(i): This follows from (
7.19
) by taking the limit.

(ii): For any
x,y\in Z^{\prime}_{s}
with
s\leq t_{a}
, since
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}
is connected for any
s^{\prime}<s
, it can be proved as Lemma
6.5
that there exists a sequence
s_{i}\nearrow s
such that if
x_{i},y_{i}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{s_{i}}
are regular
H_{n}
-centers of
x
and
y
, respectively, then

\displaystyle d^{Z^{\prime}}_{s}(x,y)=\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{s_{i}}^{Z^{\prime}}(x_{i},y_{i}).

(7.21)

Since
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(x_{i})
and
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(y_{i})
converge to
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(x)
and
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(y)
, respectively, it follows from Lemma
6.22
that

\displaystyle\liminf_{i\to\infty}d_{s_{i}+t}^{Z^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(x_{i}),\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(y_{i}))\geq d^{Z^{\prime}}_{s+t}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(x),\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(y)).

Combining this with (
7.18
) and (
7.21
), we obtain

\displaystyle d^{Z^{\prime}}_{s}(x,y)\geq d^{Z^{\prime}}_{s+t}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(x),\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(y)).

The reverse inequality also holds since
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}
is the inverse map of
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{-t}
.

(iii): This is immediate from (
7.20
) and Theorem
7.21
.
∎

Next, we prove the following bi-Lipschitz estimate.

Lemma 7.24
.

With the above assumptions, for any
x\in Z^{\prime}_{(-\infty,t_{a}]}
and
t\in(-\infty,t_{a}-\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(x)]
,

\displaystyle|t|^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq d_{Z^{\prime}}(x,\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(x))\leq C(n,Y)|t|^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Proof.

The first inequality is immediate, so we focus on proving the second. Without loss of generality, assume
t<0
and
x\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,t_{a}]}
. The general case follows by approximation.

Since
\mathrm{Ric}(g^{Z^{\prime}})=0
, it follows from Lemma
5.31
that
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(x)
is a regular
H
-center of
x
for some
H=H(n,Y)>0
. Therefore, by Lemma
5.27
, we obtain

\displaystyle d_{Z^{\prime}}(x,\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}(x))\leq C(n,Y)\sqrt{|t|},

which completes the proof.
∎

For general Ricci shrinker spaces, we have the following result.

Theorem 7.25
.

Let
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
be a collapsed Ricci shrinker space. Then
\mathrm{image}(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})=\mathbb{R}_{-}
.

Proof.

Suppose
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is collapsed and
Z^{\prime}_{(0,\infty)}
is nonempty. We fix a point
q\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{0}
with
r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(q)\geq\delta>0
. In particular, there exists a product domain
B_{g^{Z^{\prime}}_{0}}(q,\delta)\times[-\delta^{2},0]\subset\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
on which the curvature is bounded by
\delta^{-2}
.

We choose a sequence
t_{i}\nearrow 0
and define
q_{i}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t_{i}}
as the flow of
\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}
from
q
. By distance comparison, we obtain
B_{g^{Z^{\prime}}_{t_{i}}}(q_{i},\delta/2)\subset B_{g^{Z^{\prime}}_{0}}(q,\delta)
for sufficiently large
i
.

Next, we set
q^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}
to be the flow of
\tau(\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}-\nabla f_{z^{\prime}})
from
q_{i}
. It is clear from the Ricci shrinker equation (
7.4
) that the flow of
\tau(\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}-\nabla f_{z^{\prime}})
from
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t_{i}}
to
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}
is an isometry with respect to metrics
|t_{i}|^{-1}g^{Z^{\prime}}_{t_{i}}
and
g^{Z^{\prime}}_{-1}
. Thus, we conclude that

\displaystyle|\mathrm{Rm}(g^{Z^{\prime}})|\leq|t_{i}|\delta^{-2}

on
B_{g^{Z^{\prime}}_{-1}}(q^{\prime}_{i},|t_{i}|^{-1/2}\delta/2)
. Combined with Lemma
7.13
, we conclude that (
7.10
) holds. However, this contradicts our assumption.

In sum, the proof is complete.
∎

In the special case of tangent flows, we have

Theorem 7.26
.

Let
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
be a tangent flow at a point
z\in Z_{(-(1-2\sigma)T,0)}
, where
(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t})
is the noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space from (
7.1
). Then
\mathrm{image}(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})=\mathbb{R}
if
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is noncollapsed, and
\mathrm{image}(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})=\mathbb{R}_{-}
if collapsed.

Proof.

The collapsed case follows from Theorem
7.25
, so we focus on the noncollapsed case.

Suppose
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is noncollapsed. It follows from Theorem
8.22
(iii) (see also
[
LW24a
, Corollary 6.24]
) that

\displaystyle\int_{B_{Z^{\prime}_{-1}}(p,r)}r^{-4+\epsilon}_{\mathrm{Rm}}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z^{\prime}}_{-1}}\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon)r^{n-2+\epsilon}

(7.22)

for any small
\epsilon>0
and any
r\geq 1
, where
p\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}
is a regular
H_{n}
-center of
z^{\prime}
. By (
7.10
) and (
7.22
), there exists a sequence
r_{i}\to\infty
such that

\displaystyle C_{0}r_{i}^{n}\delta_{i}^{-4+\epsilon}\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon)r_{i}^{n-2+\epsilon},

for a constant
C_{0}>0
, where
\delta_{i}=\sup_{B_{Z^{\prime}_{-1}}(p,r_{i})}r_{\mathrm{Rm}}
. Thus, we conclude that there exist
x_{i}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}
such that
r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(x_{i})\to+\infty
.

Suppose that
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is obtained as in (
7.3
). We can find
q_{k,i_{j}}^{*}\in M_{i_{j}}\times\mathbb{I}_{j}
so that
q_{k,i_{j}}^{*}
converge to
x_{k}
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense, as
j\to\infty
. By Lemma
7.5
, we conclude that

\displaystyle r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(q_{k,i_{j}}^{*})\geq\frac{1}{2}r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(x_{k}),

for sufficiently large
j
. Since
T_{j}^{\prime}\to\infty
as
j\to\infty
and
r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(x_{k})
can be arbitrarily large, we conclude that
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}
is nonempty for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
. In particular,
\mathrm{image}(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})=\mathbb{R}
.
∎

Remark 7.27
.

By the same proof, the conclusion of Theorem
7.26
also holds for Ricci shrinker spaces obtained from the convergence (
3.21
) in Remark
3.25
.

Let
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
be a Ricci shrinker space. We define the flow
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}
on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
generated by
X=\tau(\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}-\nabla f_{z^{\prime}})
with
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{0}=\mathrm{id}
. It is proved in
[
Bam20b
, Theorem 15.69]
that
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(x)\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
if
x\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
.

We first prove:

Lemma 7.28
.

For any
x,y\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle d_{Z^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(x),\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(y))=e^{-\frac{s}{2}}d_{Z^{\prime}}(x,y).

(7.23)

Proof.

First, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{X}g^{Z^{\prime}}=\tau\left(\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}}g^{Z^{\prime}}-\nabla^{2}f_{z^{\prime}}\right)=-g^{Z^{\prime}}.

(7.24)

Moreover, it is clear that for any
x\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(x))=e^{-s}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(x).

(7.25)

On the other hand, it follows from
[
Bam20b
, Theorem 15.69]
that

\displaystyle X_{x}K_{Z^{\prime}}(x;y)+X_{y}K_{Z^{\prime}}(x;y)=\frac{n}{2}K_{Z^{\prime}}(x;y)

for any
x,y\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
. Therefore, we obtain

\displaystyle K_{Z^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(x);\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(y))=e^{\frac{n}{2}s}K_{Z^{\prime}}(x;y)

(7.26)

for any
x,y\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
. Combining (
7.24
), (
7.25
) and (
7.26
), we have

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}}(\nu_{x;t},\nu_{y;t})=d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}}}\left(\nu_{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(x);t^{\prime}},\nu_{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(y);t^{\prime}}\right),

for any
t\leq\min\{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(x),\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(y)\},
where
t^{\prime}=e^{-s}t
. Since
\iota_{z^{\prime}}(\mathcal{R}^{z^{\prime}})=\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
, it follows from Definition
4.22
that

\displaystyle d^{*}_{z^{\prime}}(\iota_{z^{\prime}}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(x)),\iota_{z^{\prime}}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(y)))=e^{-\frac{s}{2}}d^{*}_{z^{\prime}}(\iota_{z^{\prime}}^{-1}(x),\iota_{z^{\prime}}^{-1}(y)).

By Theorem
4.27
, this implies (
7.23
).
∎

Next, we prove

Lemma 7.29
.

For any
x,y\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}
with
t<0
and any
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle d^{Z^{\prime}}_{e^{-s}t}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(x),\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(y))=e^{-\frac{s}{2}}d^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}(x,y).

(7.27)

Proof.

It follows from (
7.24
) and (
7.25
) that

\displaystyle d_{g^{Z^{\prime}}_{e^{-s}t}}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(x),\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(y))=e^{-\frac{s}{2}}d_{g^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}}(x,y).

Consequently, the conclusion (
7.27
) follows from Corollary
7.10
.
∎

Now, we can extend
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}
to all
Z^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
.

Proposition 7.30
.

\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}
can be defined on
Z^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
so that the following statements hold.

(i)

For any
x,y\in Z^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
,
d_{Z^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(x),\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(y))=e^{-\frac{s}{2}}d_{Z^{\prime}}(x,y)
for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

(ii)

For any
x,y\in Z^{\prime}_{t}
with
t<0
,
d^{Z^{\prime}}_{e^{-s}t}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(x),\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(y))=e^{-\frac{s}{2}}d^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}(x,y)
for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

(iii)

For any
x\in Z^{\prime}_{t}
with
t<0
and
\tau>0
,
\mathcal{N}_{x}(\tau)=\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(x)}(e^{-s}\tau)
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

For any
w\in Z^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
, we choose a sequence
w_{i}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
such that
w_{i}\to w
in
d_{Z^{\prime}}
. Then, for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
\{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(w_{i})\}
is a Cauchy sequence by Lemma
7.28
with respect to
d_{Z^{\prime}}
. We define

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(w)=\lim_{i\to\infty}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(w_{i}).

It is clear that the definition of
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(w)
is independent of the choice of
\{w_{i}\}
.

(i): This follows from (
7.23
) by taking the limit.

(ii): For any
x,y\in Z^{\prime}_{t}
with
t<0
, it follows from Lemma
6.5
that there exists a sequence
t_{i}\nearrow t
such that if
x_{i},y_{i}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t_{i}}
are regular
H_{n}
-centers of
x
and
y
, respectively, then

\displaystyle d^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}(x,y)=\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{t_{i}}^{Z^{\prime}}(x_{i},y_{i}).

(7.28)

Since
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(x_{i})
and
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(y_{i})
converge to
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(x)
and
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(y)
, respectively, it follows from Lemma
6.22
that

\displaystyle\liminf_{i\to\infty}d_{e^{-s}t_{i}}^{Z^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(x_{i}),\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(y_{i}))\geq d^{Z^{\prime}}_{e^{-s}t}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(x),\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(y)).

Combining this with Lemma
7.29
and (
7.28
), we obtain

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{s}{2}}d^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}(x,y)\geq d^{Z^{\prime}}_{e^{-s}t}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(x),\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(y)).

The reverse inequality also holds, since
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}
is the inverse map of
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-s}
.

(iii) This is immediate from (
5.11
), (
7.26
), the definition of
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}
and
[
Bam20b
, Theorem 15.69]
.
∎

8
Stratification and dimension of the singular set

First, we introduce the following definition for Ricci shrinker spaces.

Definition 8.1

(
k
-splitting)
.

A Ricci shrinker space
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is called
k
-splitting
if
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}
splits off an
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-factor isometrically.

We first prove:

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
be a Ricci shrinker space. If
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is
k
-splitting, then
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}=\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
is isometrically decomposed as the product of Ricci flow spacetimes, where
\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}
is another Ricci flow spacetime of dimension
n-k
over
(-\infty,0)
.

Proof.

By assumption, there exist
k
smooth maps
\{y_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq k}
on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}
satisfying

\displaystyle\langle\nabla y_{i},\nabla y_{j}\rangle=\delta_{ij}\quad\text{and}\quad\nabla^{2}y_{i}=0\quad\mathrm{on}\quad\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}.

Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}}y_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z^{\prime},-1}=0.

By the self-similarity of
(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)},g^{Z^{\prime}})
, these functions extend smoothly to
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
such that

\displaystyle\langle\nabla y_{i},\nabla y_{j}\rangle=\delta_{ij},\quad\nabla^{2}y_{i}=0,\quad\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}y_{i}=0,\quad\mathrm{on}\quad\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}.

(8.1)

Indeed, let
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}
be the map in Proposition
7.30
. Define

\displaystyle y_{i}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(x))=e^{-\frac{s}{2}}y_{i}(x),\quad\text{for}\quad x\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}.

(8.2)

A direct computation confirms that (
8.1
) holds.

By assumption, the flow of
\nabla y_{i}
at
t=-1
preserves the regular part
\mathcal{R}_{-1}^{\prime}
. Hence, by (
8.2
) and Proposition
7.30
, the flow of
\nabla y_{i}
preserves the regular part
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}
for all
t<0
. Let
\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}
denote the flow generated by
{\nabla y_{1},\ldots,\nabla y_{k}}
for
s\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
. Then, for every
s\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
x\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
, we have
\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(x)\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
.

Consequently, we conclude that
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}=\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
, where
\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}
is another Ricci flow spacetime of dimension
n-k
over
(-\infty,0)
, and the splitting is isometric.

∎

Remark 8.3
.

To reach the same conclusion as in Proposition
8.2
, it is enough to assume the existence of smooth functions
\{y_{i}\}

(1\leq i\leq k)
on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}
satisfying

\displaystyle\langle\nabla y_{i},\nabla y_{j}\rangle=\delta_{ij},\quad\nabla^{2}y_{i}=0,

(8.3)

rather than assuming an
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-splitting a priori. Indeed, it is proved in Lemma
D.1
that
(\iota_{z^{\prime}}(\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}_{-1}),d^{Z^{\prime}}_{-1},\nu_{z^{\prime};-1})
is an
\mathrm{RCD}(1/2,\infty)
-space. The existence of the functions
y_{i}
in (
8.3
) ensures that the eigenspace of the weighted Laplacian
\Delta_{f_{z^{\prime}}(-1)}
corresponding to the eigenvalue
1/2
has dimension at least
k
. Consequently, by
[
GKKO20
]
, the vector fields
\nabla y_{i}
generate splitting directions that preserve
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}
. Alternatively, the same conclusion follows from Theorem
4.19
.

For a
k
-splitting Ricci shrinker space
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
, we define the flow
\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}
for
s\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
induced by the splitting in Proposition
8.2
with
\boldsymbol{\phi}^{0}=\mathrm{id}
. More precisely, for any
x\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
, we write
x=(x^{\prime},s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
, then
\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(x)
is defined as
(x^{\prime},s^{\prime}+s)
.

Next, we prove

Proposition 8.4
.

With the above assumptions,
\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}
can be defined on
Z^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0]}
so that the following statements hold.

(i)

For any
x,y\in Z^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0]}
,
d_{Z^{\prime}}(x,y)=d_{Z^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(x),\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(y))
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

(ii)

For any
x,y\in Z^{\prime}_{t}
with
t\leq 0
,
d^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}(x,y)=d^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(x),\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(y))
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

(iii)

For any
x\in Z^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0]}
and
\tau>0
,
\mathcal{N}_{x}(\tau)=\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(x)}(\tau)
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

Proof.

It is clear that for any
x\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}
with
t<0
,
\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(x)\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle K_{Z^{\prime}}(x;y)=K_{Z^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(x);\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(y))

(8.4)

for any
x,y\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Thus, by Definition
6.1
, we conclude that

\displaystyle d^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}(x,y)=d^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(x),\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(y))

(8.5)

for any
x,y\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}
with
t<0
and
s\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. By Proposition
6.20
, this implies

\displaystyle d_{Z^{\prime}}(x,y)=d_{Z^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(x),\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(y))

(8.6)

for any
x,y\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

For any
w\in Z^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0]}
, we choose a sequence
w_{i}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
such that
w_{i}\to w
in
d_{Z^{\prime}}
. Then, for any
s\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\{\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(w_{i})\}
is a Cauchy sequence by (
8.6
). We define

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(w)=\lim_{i\to\infty}\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(w_{i}).

It is clear that the definition of
\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(w)
is independent of the choice of
\{w_{i}\}
. Moreover, it follows from (
5.11
) and (
8.4
) that

\displaystyle K_{Z^{\prime}}(x;y)=K_{Z^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(x);\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(y))

(8.7)

for any
x\in Z^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0]}
,
y\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

(i): This follows from (
8.6
) by taking the limit.

(ii): For any
x,y\in Z^{\prime}_{t}
with
t\leq 0
, it follows from Lemma
6.5
that there exists a sequence
t_{i}\nearrow t
such that if
x_{i},y_{i}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t_{i}}
are regular
H_{n}
-centers of
x
and
y
, respectively, then

\displaystyle d^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}(x,y)=\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{t_{i}}^{Z^{\prime}}(x_{i},y_{i}).

(8.8)

Since
\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(x_{i})
and
\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(y_{i})
converge to
\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(x)
and
\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(y)
, respectively, it follows from Lemma
6.22
that

\displaystyle\liminf_{i\to\infty}d_{t_{i}}^{Z^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(x_{i}),\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(y_{i}))\geq d^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(x),\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(y)).

Combining this with (
8.5
) and (
8.8
), we obtain

\displaystyle d^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}(x,y)\geq d^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(x),\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(y)).

The reverse inequality also holds since
\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}
is the inverse map of
\boldsymbol{\phi}^{-s}
.

(iii): This is immediate from (
8.7
) and Proposition
8.2
.
∎

Next, we prove the following bi-Lipschitz estimate.

Lemma 8.5
.

For any
x\in Z^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0]}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,

\displaystyle 0\leq c(n)|s|\leq d_{Z^{\prime}}(x,\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(x))\leq\epsilon_{0}^{-1}|s|.

Proof.

The second inequality follows immediately from Lemma
6.4
and Proposition
6.8
, so we only prove the first one.

We set
r=d_{Z^{\prime}}(x,\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(x))
, then it follows from Proposition
6.20
that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\nearrow\mathfrak{t}(x)-r^{2}}d^{Z^{\prime}_{t}}_{W_{1}}(\nu_{x;t},\nu_{\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(x);t})\leq\epsilon_{0}r.

For any
t<\mathfrak{t}(x)-r^{2}
, if we set
w\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}
to be an
H_{n}
-center of
x
, it is clear that
\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(w)
is an
H_{n}
-center of
\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(x)
. Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle d^{Z^{\prime}_{t}}_{W_{1}}(\nu_{x;t},\nu_{\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(x);t})\geq d^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}(w,\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(w))-2\sqrt{H_{n}|\mathfrak{t}(x)-t|}=|s|-2\sqrt{H_{n}|\mathfrak{t}(x)-t|}.

Consequently, we obtain

\displaystyle|s|\leq C(n)r+\epsilon_{0}r\leq C(n)r,

which completes the proof.
∎

For later applications, we also need the following characterization of the potential function.

Proposition 8.6
.

With the above assumptions, for any
s\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, we have

\displaystyle f_{z^{\prime}}(x)-f_{\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(z^{\prime})}(x)=\frac{1}{2\tau}\langle\vec{x},s\rangle+\frac{c}{\tau}

for any
x\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
, where
\tau=-\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
,
\vec{x}
is the component of
x
in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
with respect to the decomposition
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}=\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and
c
is a constant independent of
x
and
\tau
.

Proof.

Since
K_{Z^{\prime}}(z^{\prime};x)=K_{Z^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(z^{\prime});\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(x))
for any
x\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
, we conclude that
f_{z^{\prime}}\left(\boldsymbol{\phi}^{-s}(x)\right)=f_{\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(z^{\prime})}(x)
.

By the Ricci shrinker equation

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}(g^{Z^{\prime}})+\nabla^{2}f_{z^{\prime}}=\frac{g^{Z^{\prime}}}{2\tau}

and the fact that
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}=\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
, we know that

\displaystyle f_{z^{\prime}}(x)=h(x^{\prime\prime})+\frac{|\vec{x}-v|^{2}}{4\tau},

for some
v\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, where
x=(x^{\prime\prime},\vec{x})\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
for
x\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
, and
h(x^{\prime\prime})
is a function on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}
. Thus, we conclude that

\displaystyle f_{z^{\prime}}(x)-f_{\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(z^{\prime})}(x)=f_{z^{\prime}}(x)-f_{z^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\phi}^{-s}(x))=\frac{|\vec{x}-v|^{2}}{4\tau}-\frac{|\vec{x}-s-v|^{2}}{4\tau}=\frac{1}{2\tau}\langle\vec{x},s\rangle+\frac{c}{\tau}.

∎

Definition 8.7

(
k
-symmetric)
.

A Ricci shrinker space
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is called
k
-symmetric
if one of the following holds:

(1)

(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is
k
-splitting and is not a static cone.

(2)

(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is a static cone that is
(k-2)
-splitting.

The number
k
in Definition
8.7
represents the number of directions in which the tangent flow is invariant, as established by Proposition
7.23
and Proposition
8.4
. Notably, in item (2), the tangent flow is invariant along the time direction, and since we view the time direction as two dimensions in the parabolic setting, it contributes two to the count. In addition, if
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is a
(k-2)
-splitting static cone, the map
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}
(for
t\in\mathbb{R}
) defined in Proposition
7.23
and
\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}
(for
s\in\mathbb{R}^{k-2}
) defined in Proposition
8.4
commute, since they do so on the regular part.

As in the last section, we consider a Ricci flow limit space
(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t})
obtained from

(M_{i}\times\mathbb{I},d^{*}_{i},p_{i}^{*},\mathfrak{t}_{i})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathrm{pGH}\hskip 8.19447pt}(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t}),

(8.9)

where
\mathcal{X}^{i}=\{M_{i}^{n},(g_{i}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
with base point
p_{i}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}^{i}_{\mathbb{I}}
.

Recall that we have the following regular-singular decomposition from (
7.8
):

\displaystyle Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}=\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}\sqcup\mathcal{S}.

In particular, any tangent flow at
z\in\mathcal{R}_{(-(1-2\sigma)T,0)}
is
(n+2)
-symmetric and any tangent flow at
z\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
is
n
-symmetric.

Thus, we have the following natural stratification of
\mathcal{S}
:

\mathcal{S}^{0}\subset\mathcal{S}^{1}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{S}^{n+1}=\mathcal{S},

(8.10)

where
z\in\mathcal{S}^{k}
if and only if no tangent flow at
z
is
(k+1)
-symmetric.

The next result shows that
\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{n-2}
is in fact empty.

Theorem 8.8
.

In the same setting as above, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}^{n-2}.

Moreover, no tangent flow at any singular point is a static or quasi-static cone that is
(n-2)
- or
(n-3)
-splitting.

Proof.

Given a tangent flow
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
at a point
z\in\mathcal{S}
, we consider its tangent metric soliton
\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}
(see Definition
7.11
), which can be regarded as a tangent metric flow of
\mathcal{X}^{z}
. Then it follows from Proposition
7.9
that
\iota_{z^{\prime}}(\mathcal{R}^{z^{\prime}})=\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
.

It follows from
[
Bam20b
, Theorem 2.8]
that either the Ricci curvature vanishes on
\mathcal{R}^{z^{\prime}}_{-1}
, in which case
\mathcal{R}^{z^{\prime}}_{-1}
splits off an
\mathbb{R}^{k}
for some
k\leq n-4
, or the scalar curvature is positive on
\mathcal{R}^{z^{\prime}}_{-1}
, in which case
\mathcal{R}^{z^{\prime}}_{-1}
splits off an
\mathbb{R}^{k}
for some
k\leq n-2
.

Consequently, this implies
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}^{n-2}
. The last conclusion also follows.
∎

By Theorem
8.8
, we can refine the stratification (
8.10
) as follows:

\mathcal{S}^{0}\subset\mathcal{S}^{1}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{S}^{n-2}=\mathcal{S}.

To control the size of each stratum
\mathcal{S}^{k}
, we next recall the following definition of the Minkowski content and dimension.

Definition 8.9
.

For a subset
Z_{1}\subset Z
, set
B_{Z}^{*}(Z_{1},r)
the
r
-neighborhood of
Z_{1}
with respect to
d_{Z}
. For any
s>0
, we define the
s
-dimensional (upper)
Minkowski content
of
Z_{1}
as

\displaystyle\mathscr{M}^{s}(Z_{1}):=\sup_{L>0}\,\limsup_{r\to 0}\frac{|B_{Z}^{*}(Z_{1},r)\cap B_{Z}^{*}(x_{0},L)|}{r^{n+2-s}},

where
x_{0}
is a fixed point in
Z
. The common value of
\inf\{s\geq 0\mid\mathscr{M}^{s}(Z_{1})=0\}=\sup\{s\geq 0\mid\mathscr{M}^{s}(Z_{1})=+\infty\}
is called the (upper)
Minkowski dimension
of
Z_{1}
, denoted by
\dim_{\mathscr{M}}Z_{1}
.

Next, we define the quantitative singular sets. The concept of
\epsilon
-closeness can be found in Definition
5.37
.

Definition 8.10
.

A point
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
is called
(k,\epsilon,r)
-symmetric
if
\mathfrak{t}(z)-\epsilon^{-1}r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, and there exist a
k
-symmetric Ricci shrinker space
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
such that

\displaystyle(Z,r^{-1}d_{Z},z,r^{-2}(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}(z)))\quad\text{is $\epsilon$-close to}\quad(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})\quad\text{over}\quad[-\epsilon^{-1},\epsilon^{-1}].

Furthermore, if
k\in\{n-3,n-2\}
, then the model space
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
cannot be a quasi-static cone. If
k\geq n-1
, then the model space
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is isometric to
(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,t_{a}],d^{*}_{E,\epsilon_{0}},(\vec{0},0),\mathfrak{t})
for some constant
t_{a}\in[0,+\infty]
.

Definition 8.11
.

For
\epsilon>0
and
0<r_{1}<r_{2}<\infty
, the quantitative singular strata

\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,0}_{r_{1},r_{2}}\subset\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,1}_{r_{1},r_{2}}\subset\ldots\subset\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,n-2}_{r_{1},r_{2}}\subset Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}

are defined as follows:

z\in\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,k}_{r_{1},r_{2}}
if and only if
\mathfrak{t}(z)-\epsilon^{-1}r_{2}^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
and for all
r\in[r_{1},r_{2}]
,
z
is not
(k+1,\epsilon,r)
-symmetric.

The following identity is clear from the above definitions and Theorem
8.8
: for any
L>1
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{k}=\bigcup_{\epsilon\in(0,L^{-1})}\bigcap_{0<r<\epsilon L}\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,k}_{r,\epsilon L}.

(8.11)

Notice that the quantitative singular set
\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,k}_{r_{1},r_{2}}
can be defined in
M\times\mathbb{I}^{-}
for any
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
, even though
\mathcal{X}
contains no singular set.

Next, we compare the quantitative singular sets in Definition
4.20
and
\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,k}_{r_{1},r_{2}}
for the top stratum.

Lemma 8.12
.

Given
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
, for any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, if
\epsilon^{\prime}\leq\epsilon^{\prime}(n,Y,\sigma,\epsilon)
and
r_{1}<r_{2}\epsilon^{\prime}
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,n-2}_{r_{1},r_{2}\epsilon}\subset\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon^{\prime},n-2,\mathbb{F}}_{r_{1},r_{2}\epsilon^{\prime}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,n-2,\mathbb{F}}_{r_{1},r_{2}\epsilon}\subset\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon^{\prime},n-2}_{r_{1},r_{2}\epsilon^{\prime}}.

(8.12)

Proof.

We only prove the first inclusion as the second can be proved similarly.

Suppose that the first inclusion in (
8.12
) fails. Then, for a fixed
\epsilon>0
, we can find a sequence
\mathcal{X}^{i}=\{M_{i}^{n},(g(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T_{i})
such that there exists
z^{*}_{i}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}^{-}
such that
z^{*}_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,n-2}_{r_{1}^{i},r_{2}^{i}\epsilon}
, but
z^{*}_{i}\notin\mathcal{S}^{i^{-2},n-2,\mathbb{F}}_{r_{1}^{i},r_{2}^{i}i^{-2}}
, where
r_{1}^{i}<r_{2}^{i}i^{-2}
.

From Definition
4.20
, there exists
s_{i}\in[r_{1}^{i},r_{2}^{i}i^{-2}]
such that, by taking a subsequence, we have

\displaystyle(M_{i}\times\mathbb{I},s_{i}^{-1}d^{*}_{i},z^{*}_{i},s_{i}^{-2}(\mathfrak{t}_{i}-\mathfrak{t}_{i}(z_{i}^{*})))\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\hat{C}^{\infty}\hskip 8.19447pt}(Z,d_{Z},z,\mathfrak{t}).

Moreover, the Ricci flow limit space
(Z,d_{Z},z,\mathfrak{t})
satisfies
\mathbb{R}_{-}\subset\mathrm{image}(\mathfrak{t})
and Proposition
7.9
on
\mathcal{R}_{(-\infty,0)}
. By considering the associated metric flow
\mathcal{X}^{z}
, we conclude that either
\mathcal{R}_{-1}
splits off an
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
, or the Ricci curvature vanishes on
\mathcal{R}_{-1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{-1}
splits off an
\mathbb{R}^{n-3}
. For both cases, it is clear that
(Z,d_{Z},z,\mathfrak{t})
is isometric to
(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,t_{a}],d^{*}_{E,\epsilon_{0}},(\vec{0},0),\mathfrak{t})
or
(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}_{-},d^{*}_{E,\epsilon_{0}},(\vec{0},0),\mathfrak{t})
. Thus,
z_{i}^{*}
is
(n-1,\epsilon,s_{i})
-symmetric, for sufficiently large
i
. However, this implies
z_{i}^{*}\notin\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,n-2}_{r_{1}^{i},r_{2}^{i}\epsilon}
, which is a contradiction.
∎

By Proposition
3.15
, Theorem
4.21
and Lemma
8.12
, the following theorem is immediate.

Theorem 8.13
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
with
x_{0}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
. Given
\epsilon>0
and
r>0
with
\mathfrak{t}(x_{0}^{*})-2r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, for any
\delta\in(0,\epsilon)
, there exist
x_{1}^{*},x_{2}^{*},\ldots,x_{N}^{*}\in B^{*}(x^{*}_{0},r)
with
N\leq C(n,Y,\sigma,\epsilon)\delta^{-n+2-\epsilon}
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,n-2}_{\delta r,\epsilon r}\cap B^{*}(x_{0}^{*},r)\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}B^{*}(x_{i}^{*},\delta r).

Moreover, if
\epsilon\leq\epsilon(n,Y,\sigma)
, then

\displaystyle r_{\mathrm{Rm}}\geq\delta r,\quad on\quad B^{*}(x_{0}^{*},r)\setminus\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,n-2}_{\delta r,\epsilon r}.

Next, we prove

Theorem 8.14
.

Let
(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t})
be the Ricci flow limit space from (
8.9
) with
x_{0}\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
. Given
\epsilon>0
and
r>0
with
\mathfrak{t}(x_{0})-2r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, for any
\delta\in(0,\epsilon)
, there exist
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{N}\in B_{Z}^{*}(x_{0},1.1r)
with
N\leq C(n,Y,\sigma,\epsilon)\delta^{-n+2-\epsilon}
and

\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,n-2}_{\delta r,\epsilon r}\cap B_{Z}^{*}(x_{0},r)\subset\bigcup_{j=1}^{N}B_{Z}^{*}(x_{j},\delta r).

(8.13)

Moreover, if
\epsilon\leq\epsilon(n,Y,\sigma)
, then

\displaystyle r_{\mathrm{Rm}}\geq\delta r,\quad on\quad B_{Z}^{*}(x_{0},r)\setminus\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,n-2}_{\delta r,\epsilon r}.

(8.14)

Proof.

We set
\mathcal{S}^{\cdot,\cdot,i}_{\cdot,\cdot}
to be the corresponding quantitative singular set in
M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}^{-}
, which is from (
8.9
). Then, we choose a sequence
x_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}^{-}
so that
x_{i}^{*}
converge to
x_{0}
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense.

We may further assume
\delta\in(0,\epsilon/2)
, since otherwise (
8.13
) holds from a standard covering argument by Proposition
5.35
. By Theorem
8.13
, for each
i
, there exist
x_{i,1}^{*},x_{i,2}^{*},\ldots,x_{i,N_{i}}^{*}\in B_{i}^{*}(x_{i}^{*},1.01r)
with

\displaystyle N_{i}\leq C(n,Y,\sigma,\epsilon)\delta^{-n+2-\epsilon}

and

\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon/2,n-2,i}_{\delta r,\epsilon r/2}\cap B_{i}^{*}(x_{i}^{*},1.01r)\subset\bigcup_{j=1}^{N_{i}}B_{i}^{*}(x_{i,j}^{*},\delta r/2).

(8.15)

By taking a subsequence, we may assume
N_{i}=N
to be a constant. By taking a further diagonal sequence, we assume
x_{i,j}^{*}
converge to
x_{j}\in B_{Z}^{*}(x_{0},1.1r)
as
i\to\infty
, for any
1\leq j\leq N
.

We claim that

\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,n-2}_{\delta r,\epsilon r}\cap B_{Z}^{*}(x_{0},r)\subset\bigcup_{j=1}^{N}B_{Z}^{*}(x_{j},\delta r).

(8.16)

Indeed, suppose (
8.16
) fails, one can find
y\in\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,n-2}_{\delta r,\epsilon r}\cap B_{Z}^{*}(x_{0},r)
so that
d_{Z}(y,x_{j})\geq\delta r
for any
1\leq j\leq N
. We choose a sequence
y_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}^{-}
converging to
y
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense. For sufficiently large
i
, it is clear that
y_{i}^{*}\in\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon/2,n-2,i}_{\delta r,\epsilon r/2}\cap B_{i}^{*}(x_{i}^{*},1.01r)
. Then, it follows from (
8.15
) that we can find
x_{i,j_{i}}^{*}
with
d_{i}^{*}(y_{i}^{*},x_{i,j_{i}}^{*})<\delta r/2
. By taking a subsequence, we conclude that

\displaystyle d_{Z}(y,x_{j})\leq\delta r/2

for some
1\leq j\leq N
, which is a contradiction.

For the last conclusion, we consider
z\in B_{Z}^{*}(x_{0},r)\cap Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,n-2}_{\delta r,\epsilon r}
so that
\epsilon
is sufficiently small. Then we choose a sequence
z_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}^{-}
converging to
z
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense. Then, for sufficiently large
i
,
z_{i}^{*}\in B_{i}^{*}(x_{i}^{*},1.1r)\cap\mathcal{X}^{i}_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{2\epsilon,n-2,i}_{\delta r,2\epsilon r}
. Thus, by Theorem
8.13
, we have

\displaystyle r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(z_{i}^{*})\geq\delta r.

Consequently, the conclusion follows from Lemma
7.5
.
∎

We obtain the following volume estimates:

Corollary 8.15
.

Given
x_{0}\in Z
,
\epsilon>0
and
r>0
with
\mathfrak{t}(x_{0})-2r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, the for any
\delta\in(0,\epsilon)
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert B^{*}_{Z}\left(\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,n-2}_{\delta r,\epsilon r},\delta r\right)\cap B_{Z}^{*}(x_{0},r)\right\rvert\leq C(n,Y,\sigma,\epsilon)\delta^{4-\epsilon}r^{n+2},

where
B^{*}_{Z}\left(\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,k}_{\delta r,\epsilon r},\delta r\right)
denotes the
\delta r
-neighborhood of
\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,k}_{\delta r,\epsilon r}
with respect to
d_{Z}
, and
|\cdot|
denotes the volume (see Definition
5.33
).

Proof.

Given
x_{0}\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
and a constant
r>0
with
\mathfrak{t}(x_{0})-2r^{2}>-(1-2\sigma)T
. It follows from (
8.13
) that for any
\delta\in(0,\epsilon)
, there exist
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{N}\in B_{Z}^{*}(x_{0},1.1r)
with
N\leq C(n,Y,\sigma,\epsilon)\delta^{-n+2-\epsilon}
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,n-2}_{\delta r,\epsilon r}\cap B_{Z}^{*}(x_{0},r)\subset\bigcup_{j=1}^{N}B_{Z}^{*}(x_{j},\delta r).

In particular, we have

\displaystyle B^{*}_{Z}\left(\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,n-2}_{\delta r,\epsilon r},\delta r\right)\cap B_{Z}^{*}(x_{0},r)\subset\bigcup_{j=1}^{N}B_{Z}^{*}(x_{j},2\delta r).

By Proposition
5.35
, this implies

\displaystyle\left\lvert B^{*}_{Z}\left(\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,n-2}_{\delta r,\epsilon r},\delta r\right)\cap B_{Z}^{*}(x_{0},r)\right\rvert\leq\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left\lvert B_{Z}^{*}(x_{j},2\delta r)\right\rvert\leq C(n,Y,\sigma)N\delta^{n+2}r^{n+2}\leq C(n,Y,\sigma,\epsilon)\delta^{4-\epsilon}r^{n+2}.

∎

By the same argument as in the proof of Corollary
8.15
, we obtain the following result using Proposition
5.36
.

Corollary 8.16
.

Given
x_{0}\in Z
,
\epsilon>0
and
r>0
with
\mathfrak{t}(x_{0})-2r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, then for any
\delta\in(0,\epsilon)
and any
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert B^{*}_{Z}\left(\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,n-2}_{\delta r,\epsilon r},\delta r\right)\cap B_{Z}^{*}(x_{0},r)\cap Z_{t}\right\rvert_{t}\leq C(n,Y,\sigma,\epsilon)\delta^{2-\epsilon}r^{n}.

By Theorem
8.8
, Definition
8.9
, (
8.11
) and Corollary
8.15
, the following result is immediate.

Corollary 8.17
.

We have

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathscr{M}}\mathcal{S}\leq n-2.

Next, we prove the following integral estimates.

Theorem 8.18
.

Let
(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t})
be the Ricci flow limit space from (
8.9
). Given
x_{0}\in Z
and
r>0
with
\mathfrak{t}(x_{0})-2r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, then for any
\epsilon>0
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{B^{*}_{Z}(x_{0},r)\cap\mathcal{R}}|\mathrm{Rm}|^{2-\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z}_{t}}\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{B^{*}_{Z}(x_{0},r)\cap\mathcal{R}}r_{\mathrm{Rm}}^{-4+2\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z}_{t}}\mathrm{d}t\leq C(n,Y,\sigma,\epsilon)r^{n-2+2\epsilon}.

(8.17)

Moreover, for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\int_{B^{*}_{Z}(x_{0},r)\cap\mathcal{R}_{t}}|\mathrm{Rm}|^{1-\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z}_{t}}\leq\int_{B^{*}_{Z}(x_{0},r)\cap\mathcal{R}_{t}}r_{\mathrm{Rm}}^{-2+2\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z}_{t}}\leq C(n,Y,\sigma,\epsilon)r^{n-2+2\epsilon}.

(8.18)

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
r=1
. It follows from (
8.14
) and Corollary
8.15
that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\{r_{\mathrm{Rm}}<\delta\}\cap B_{Z}^{*}(x_{0},1)\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,n-2}_{\delta,\epsilon}\cap B_{Z}^{*}(x_{0},1)\right\rvert\leq C(n,Y,\sigma,\epsilon)\delta^{4-\epsilon}.

(8.19)

Thus, it follows from (
8.19
) and Proposition
5.35
that

\displaystyle\int_{B^{*}_{Z}(x_{0},1)\cap\mathcal{R}}r_{\mathrm{Rm}}^{-4+2\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z}_{t}}\mathrm{d}t=

\displaystyle\int_{B^{*}_{Z}(x_{0},1)\cap\{r_{\mathrm{Rm}}\geq 1\}}1\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z}_{t}}\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k\geq 1}\int_{B^{*}_{Z}(x_{0},1)\cap\{2^{-k}\leq r_{\mathrm{Rm}}<2^{-k+1}\}}r_{\mathrm{Rm}}^{-4+2\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z}_{t}}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(n,Y)+C(n,Y,\sigma,\epsilon)\sum_{k\geq 1}2^{k(4-2\epsilon)}2^{(1-k)(4-\epsilon)}\leq C(n,Y,\sigma,\epsilon).

Consequently, the proof of (
8.17
) is complete. The proof of (
8.18
) is similar by using

\displaystyle\left\lvert\{r_{\mathrm{Rm}}<\delta\}\cap B_{Z}^{*}(x_{0},1)\cap Z_{t}\right\rvert_{t}\leq\left\lvert\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,n-2}_{\delta,\epsilon}\cap B_{Z}^{*}(x_{0},1)\cap Z_{t}\right\rvert_{t}\leq C(n,Y,\sigma,\epsilon)\delta^{2-\epsilon},

(8.20)

where the last inequality is from Corollary
8.16
.
∎

For applications, we have the following construction of cutoff functions, which can be proved in a similar way as
[
Bam20b
, Lemma 15.27 (b)]
by smoothing
\eta(r_{\mathrm{Rm}}/r)
, where
\eta
is a fixed cutoff function with
\eta=0
on
[0,1.1]
and
\eta=1
on
[1.9,\infty]
. Notice that the last item (6) below follows from (
8.19
) and (
8.20
).

Proposition 8.19
.

Let
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
be the Ricci flow limit space from (
8.9
). There is a family of smooth functions
\{\eta_{r}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{R})\}_{r>0}
taking values in
[0,1]
such that the following holds:

(1)

r_{\mathrm{Rm}}\geq r
on
\{\eta_{r}>0\}
.

(2)

\eta_{r}=1
on
\{r_{\mathrm{Rm}}\geq 2r\}
.

(3)

r|\nabla\eta_{r}|+r^{2}|\partial_{\mathfrak{t}}\eta_{r}|+r^{2}|\nabla^{2}\eta_{r}|\leq C_{0}
for some dimensional constant
C_{0}
.

(4)

For any
z\in Z
with
t=\mathfrak{t}(z)
,
L<\infty
and
r>0
, the set
\{\eta_{r}>0\}\cap B_{g^{Z}_{t}}(z,L)
is relatively compact in
\mathcal{R}_{t}
.

(5)

For any
L<\infty
,
z\in Z
and
t\in\mathbb{I}
, the set
\{\eta_{r}>0\}\cap B_{Z}^{*}(z,L)\cap\mathcal{R}_{t}
is relatively compact in
\mathcal{R}_{t}
.

(6)

Given any
A>1
,
z\in Z
and
L>0
with
\mathfrak{t}(z)-2L^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, and for any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, there exist a constant
C=C(n,Y,\sigma,A,L,\epsilon)
such that the following holds:

\displaystyle\iint_{(\mathrm{supp}\,\eta_{r})^{c}\cap\mathcal{R}_{[\mathfrak{t}(z)-L^{2},\mathfrak{t}(z)+L^{2}]}\cap B^{*}_{Z}(z,A)}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z}_{t}}\mathrm{d}t\leq Cr^{4-\epsilon}.

Moreover, for any
t\in[\mathfrak{t}(z)-L^{2},\mathfrak{t}(z)+L^{2}]
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{(\mathrm{supp}\,\eta_{r})^{c}\cap\mathcal{R}_{t}\cap B^{*}_{Z}(z,A)}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z}_{t}}\leq Cr^{2-\epsilon}.

In practice, we can slightly modify the cutoff functions above so that the resulting functions have compact support. We fix a point
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
, and let
\eta_{r}
be the cutoff functions in Proposition
8.19
. For any
A\gg 1
and
r\ll 1
, we set
\kappa_{r,A}
to be a smoothing of the characteristic function
\chi_{r,A}
of
B^{*}_{Z}(z,1.1A)\cap\{r_{\mathrm{Rm}}>2r\}
. Indeed, we only need to mollify
\chi_{r,A}
on
B_{g^{Z}_{\mathfrak{t}(x)}}(x,r)\times[\mathfrak{t}(x)-r^{2},\mathfrak{t}(x)+r^{2}]
for any
x\in\partial\left(B^{*}_{Z}(z,1.1A)\cap\{r_{\mathrm{Rm}}>2r\}\right)
. Since
r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(x)\geq 2r
, this can be done by the standard convolution process. We define

\displaystyle\eta_{r,A}:=\kappa_{r,A}\eta_{r}.

(8.21)

Then, the following proposition follows easily from Proposition
8.19
and our construction.

Proposition 8.20
.

For any
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
, the family of smooth cutoff functions
\{\eta_{r,A}\}
defined in (
8.21
) satisfy the following properties for
r\leq r(n,Y,\sigma)
.

(1)

r_{\mathrm{Rm}}\geq r
and
d_{Z}(z,\cdot)\leq 2A
on
\{\eta_{r,A}>0\}
.

(2)

\eta_{r,A}=1
on
\{r_{\mathrm{Rm}}\geq 2r\}\cap B_{Z}^{*}(z,A)
.

(3)

\displaystyle r|\nabla\eta_{r,A}|+r^{2}|\partial_{\mathfrak{t}}\eta_{r,A}|+r^{2}|\nabla^{2}\eta_{r,A}|\leq C(n)
.

(4)

For any
L
with
\mathfrak{t}(z)-2L^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
and any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, there exists a constant
C=C(n,Y,\sigma,L,A,\epsilon)>0
such that

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathcal{R}_{[\mathfrak{t}(z)-L^{2},\mathfrak{t}(z)+L^{2}]}\cap\{0<\eta_{r,A}<1\}}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z}_{t}}\mathrm{d}t\leq Cr^{4-\epsilon}.

Moreover, for any
t\in[\mathfrak{t}(z)-L^{2},\mathfrak{t}(z)+L^{2}]
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}\cap\{0<\eta_{r,A}<1\}}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z}_{t}}\leq Cr^{2-\epsilon}.

Next, we consider a Ricci shrinker space
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
such that
p\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}
is a regular
H_{n}
-center of
z^{\prime}
.

Lemma 8.21
.

There exists a constant
C=C(n)>0
such that for any
w\in B_{Z^{\prime}_{-1}}(p,r)
with
r\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(w)\in B_{Z^{\prime}}^{*}(z^{\prime},C\epsilon_{0}^{-1}r)

for any
s\in[0,1]
, where
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}
is the map from Proposition
7.30
.

Proof.

For any
s\in[0,1]
, it follows from the self-similarity (see Proposition
7.30
) that
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(p)
is an
H
-center of
z^{\prime}
for a constant
H=H(n)>0
. Thus, it follows from Lemma
6.14
that

\displaystyle d_{Z^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(p),z^{\prime})\leq C(n)\epsilon_{0}^{-1}.

(8.22)

On the other hand, it follows from Proposition
7.30
that for any
w\in B_{Z^{\prime}_{-1}}(p,r)
and
s\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle d^{Z^{\prime}}_{-e^{-s}}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(p),\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(w))=e^{-s/2}d^{Z^{\prime}}_{-1}(p,w)\leq r,

which, when combined with Lemma
6.4
and (
8.22
), implies

\displaystyle d_{Z^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(w),z^{\prime})\leq\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\left(C(n)+r\right).

Thus, the proof is complete.
∎

Combining Corollary
8.15
, Theorem
8.18
and Lemma
8.21
, the following result is immediate from Proposition
7.30
.

Theorem 8.22
.

With the above assumptions, the following statements are true.

(i)

For any
t<0
, the Minkowski dimension of
\mathcal{S}\cap Z^{\prime}_{t}
with respect to
d^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}
is at most
n-4
.

(ii)

For any
\epsilon>0
and
r\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert\{r_{\mathrm{Rm}}<\delta r\}\cap B_{Z^{\prime}_{-1}}(p,r)\right\rvert_{-1}\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon)\delta^{4-\epsilon}r^{n+2}.

(iii)

For any
\epsilon>0
and
r\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{B_{Z^{\prime}_{-1}}(p,r)\cap\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}}|\mathrm{Rm}|^{2-\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z^{\prime}}_{-1}}\leq\int_{B_{Z^{\prime}_{-1}}(p,r)\cap\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}}r_{\mathrm{Rm}}^{-4+2\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z^{\prime}}_{-1}}\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon)r^{n-2+2\epsilon}.

Proof.

(i): Without loss of generality, we assume
t=-1
. For any
w\in\mathcal{S}\cap B_{Z^{\prime}_{-1}}(p,r)
with
r\geq 1
, it follows from Proposition
7.30
that
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(w)\in\mathcal{S}
for any
s\in[0,1]
. Moreover, if
x\in B_{Z^{\prime}_{-1}}(w,\delta r)
, then
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(x)\in B_{Z^{\prime}_{-e^{-s}}}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(w),e^{-s/2}\delta r)
. By Lemma
6.4
, this implies that for any
s\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle d_{Z^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(x),\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}(w))\leq\epsilon_{0}^{-1}e^{-s/2}\delta r\leq\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\delta r.

Thus, by Proposition
7.30
and Lemma
8.21
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert B_{Z^{\prime}_{-1}}\left(\mathcal{S}\cap B_{Z^{\prime}_{-1}}(p,r),\delta r\right)\right\rvert_{-1}\leq\left\lvert B_{Z^{\prime}}^{*}\left(B_{Z^{\prime}}^{*}(z^{\prime},C(n)\epsilon_{0}^{-1}r)\cap\mathcal{S},\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\delta r\right)\right\rvert,

where
B_{Z^{\prime}_{-1}}\left(\mathcal{S}\cap B_{Z^{\prime}_{-1}}(p,r),\delta r\right)
denotes the
\delta r
-neighborhood of
\mathcal{S}\cap B_{Z^{\prime}_{-1}}(p,r)
in
(Z^{\prime}_{-1},d^{Z^{\prime}}_{-1})
. Therefore, it follows from Corollary
8.15
that with respect to
d^{Z^{\prime}}_{-1}
,

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathscr{M}}\left(\mathcal{S}\cap Z^{\prime}_{-1}\right)\leq n-4.

(ii): By a similar argument as in the proof of (i), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\lvert\{r_{\mathrm{Rm}}<\delta r\}\cap B_{Z^{\prime}_{-1}}(p,r)\right\rvert_{-1}\leq\left\lvert\{r_{\mathrm{Rm}}<2\delta r\}\cap B^{*}_{Z^{\prime}}(z^{\prime},C(n)\epsilon_{0}^{-1}r)\right\rvert.

By using (
8.19
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\lvert\{r_{\mathrm{Rm}}<\delta r\}\cap B_{Z^{\prime}_{-1}}(p,r)\right\rvert_{-1}\leq C(n,Y,\sigma,\epsilon)\delta^{4-\epsilon}r^{n+2}.

(8.23)

We now claim that the constant
C
can be chosen independently of
\sigma
. Indeed, by Remark
6.2
, the left-hand side of (
8.23
) does not depend on the choice of
d_{Z^{\prime}}
. Hence, if the Ricci shrinker space
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
arises as the pointed Gromov–Hausdorff limit of a sequence
\mathcal{X}^{i}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T_{i})
as in Remark
3.25
, we may assume without loss of generality that all
d^{*}
-distances are defined using a fixed parameter, say
\sigma=1/100
.

(iii): This follows from (ii) and integration, as in the proof of Theorem
8.18
.
∎

Using Theorem
8.22
, one can construct a family of cutoff functions on each negative time-slice of
Z^{\prime}
, similar to Proposition
8.19
and Proposition
8.20
.

Proposition 8.23
.

There exists a family of smooth cutoff functions
\{\eta_{r,A}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1})\}
taking values in
[0,1]
such that the following holds:

(1)

r_{\mathrm{Rm}}\geq r
and
d^{Z^{\prime}}_{-1}(p,\cdot)\leq 2A
on
\{\eta_{r,A}>0\}
.

(2)

\eta_{r,A}=1
on
\{r_{\mathrm{Rm}}\geq 2r\}\cap B_{Z^{\prime}_{-1}}(p,A)
.

(3)

\displaystyle r|\nabla\eta_{r,A}|+r^{2}|\nabla^{2}\eta_{r,A}|\leq C(n)
.

(4)

For any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, there exists a constant
C=C(n,Y,A,\epsilon)>0
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}\cap\{0<\eta_{r,A}<1\}}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z}_{-1}}\leq Cr^{4-\epsilon}.

9
Application: the first singular time of the Ricci flow

In this section, we present some applications of the results established above in a specific setting.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in[-T,0)}\}
be a closed Ricci flow such that
0
is the first singular time. We assume
T<\infty
and that
\mathcal{X}
has entropy bounded below by
-Y
.

We consider the
d^{*}
-distance on
\mathcal{X}_{[-0.99T,0)}
, defined as in Definition
3.5
, using the spacetime distance constant
\epsilon_{0}=\epsilon_{0}(n,Y)>0
. For simplicity, we set
\sigma=1/100
throughout this discussion.

We then define

\displaystyle(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})

to be the metric completion of
\mathcal{X}_{[-0.98T,0)}
with respect to
d^{*}
. By construction, we have
(Z_{[-0.98T,0)},d_{Z})=(\mathcal{X}_{[-0.98T,0)},d^{*})
; that is, the completion adds only the points in
Z_{0}
.

It is clear that
Z
has bounded diameter with respect to
d_{Z}
. Indeed, for any
x^{*},y^{*}\in\mathcal{X}_{[-0.99T,0)}
, it follows from Definition
2.1
that

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{-0.99T}(\nu_{x^{*};-0.99T},\nu_{y^{*};-0.99T})\leq\mathrm{diam}_{g(-0.99T)}(M).

Thus, it follows from (
3.6
) and (
3.7
) that

\displaystyle\mathrm{diam}_{d_{Z}}(Z)\leq\max\{\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\mathrm{diam}_{g(-0.99T)}(M),\sqrt{T}\}.

(9.1)

(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
is a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space over
I=[-0.98T,0]
. Indeed, we consider a sequence
t_{i}\nearrow 0
and set

\displaystyle g_{i}(t)=g(t_{i}+t),\quad\mathfrak{t}_{i}=\mathfrak{t}+t_{i}.

Fix a base point
p^{*}\in[-0.98T,0)
. Then, by taking a subsequence, we have the pointed Gromov–Hausdorff convergence

\displaystyle(M\times[-0.98T,0],d^{*},p^{*},\mathfrak{t}_{i})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\hat{C}^{\infty}\hskip 8.19447pt}(Z_{1},d_{Z_{1}},z_{1},\mathfrak{t}).

It is not difficult to show that
(Z_{1},d_{Z_{1}},\mathfrak{t})
is isometric to
(Z_{I},d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
. In particular, the limit is independent of the choices of
\{t_{i}\}
and
p^{*}
.

First, we prove

Theorem 9.1
.

For any
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\int_{-T}^{0}\int_{M}|\mathrm{Rm}|^{2-\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{-T}^{0}\int_{M}r_{\mathrm{Rm}}^{-4+2\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}\mathrm{d}t\leq C_{\epsilon}.

(9.2)

Moreover, for any
t\in[-T,0)
,

\displaystyle\int_{M}|\mathrm{Rm}|^{1-\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}\leq\int_{M}r_{\mathrm{Rm}}^{-2+2\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}\leq C_{\epsilon}.

(9.3)

Here, the constant
C_{\epsilon}
depends on
\epsilon
and the Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}
.

Proof.

We set
r_{0}=\sqrt{T}/20
and assume that
\{B_{Z}^{*}(x_{i},r_{0})\}_{1\leq i\leq N}
is the maximal set of mutually disjoint balls contained in
Z_{[-T/4,0]}
. It is clear from (
9.1
), Proposition
5.34
and Proposition
5.35
that
N
is finite. Moreover,
\{B_{Z}^{*}(x_{i},2r_{0})\}_{1\leq i\leq N}
cover
Z_{[-T/4,0]}
.

By Theorem
8.18
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{-T/4}^{0}\int_{M}r_{\mathrm{Rm}}^{-4+2\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}\mathrm{d}t\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{B^{*}_{Z}(x_{i},2r_{0})\cap\mathcal{R}}r_{\mathrm{Rm}}^{-4+2\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z}_{t}}\mathrm{d}t\leq C_{\epsilon},

which completes the proof of (
9.2
). The proof of (
9.3
) is similar by using (
8.18
) in Theorem
8.18
.
∎

By considering the Ricci flow on the standard
S^{2}
, it is clear that the constant
\epsilon
in (
9.2
) cannot be
0
.

Next, we show that the volume of
M
at time
t
has a limit as
t
approaches
0
.

Proposition 9.2
.

With the above assumptions, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{t\nearrow 0}|M|_{t}=V_{0}\in[0,+\infty).

(9.4)

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}{\displaystyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}|M|_{t}=-\int_{M}\mathrm{R}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}\leq C(n,T)|M|_{t}

for
t\in[-T/2,0)
, which implies that

\displaystyle|M|_{t}\leq C(n,T)|M|_{-T/2}\quad\text{for all }t\in[-T/2,0).

(9.5)

For any
-T/2<t_{1}<t_{2}<0
, it follows from Theorem
9.1
and (
9.5
) that

\displaystyle\left\lvert|M|_{t_{2}}-|M|_{t_{1}}\right\rvert\leq\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{M}|\mathrm{R}|\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}\mathrm{d}t\leq C(n)\left(\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{M}|\mathrm{Rm}|^{\frac{3}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{2}{3}}\left\lvert M\times[t_{1},t_{2}]\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{3}}\leq C(t_{2}-t_{1})^{\frac{1}{3}},

(9.6)

where
C
depends on the Ricci flow. From this it is clear that
\lim_{t\nearrow 0}|M|_{t}
exists, which, by (
9.5
) again, must be finite.
∎

Using Theorem
9.1
and the same argument as above (see (
9.6
)), we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 9.3
.

For any
\epsilon>0
, there exists a constant
C_{\epsilon}
depending on
\epsilon
and the Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}
such that for any
t\in[-T,0)
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert|M|_{t}-V_{0}\right\rvert\leq C_{\epsilon}|t|^{\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon}.

Notice that if the regular part
\mathcal{R}_{0}
of
Z
at time
0
is nonempty, then the limit
V_{0}
in (
9.4
) is positive by smooth convergence. On the other hand, we prove the following volume estimate if
\mathcal{R}_{0}=\emptyset
, which improves Corollary
9.3
and is analogous to
[
LW24a
, Corollary 6.25]
.

Proposition 9.4
.

Suppose
\mathcal{R}_{0}=\emptyset
, then for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists a constant
C_{\epsilon}
depending on
\epsilon
and the Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}
such that for any
t\in[-T,0)
,

\displaystyle|M|_{t}\leq C_{\epsilon}|t|^{1-\epsilon}.

Proof.

We only need to prove the conclusion for
t
close to
0
.

It follows from the definition of the curvature radius that
r_{\mathrm{Rm}}<2\sqrt{|t|}
on
M\times\{t\}
, since otherwise
\mathcal{R}_{0}
is not empty. Then by (
8.20
), we have

\displaystyle|M|_{t}\leq\left\lvert\left\{r_{\mathrm{Rm}}<2\sqrt{|t|}\right\}\right\rvert_{t}\leq C(n,Y,T,\epsilon)|t|^{1-\epsilon/2},

which completes the proof.
∎

As a corollary of Proposition
9.4
, we obtain the following dichotomy, depending on whether
\mathcal{R}_{0}
is empty or not.

Corollary 9.5
.

For the limit
V_{0}
in (
9.4
),
V_{0}>0
if and only if
\mathcal{R}_{0}\neq\emptyset
.

Thus, we have the following definition.

Definition 9.6
.

With the above assumptions,
\mathcal{X}
is called
noncollapsed
at the first singular time if
V_{0}>0
, and
collapsed
if
V_{0}=0
.

The term “collapsed” is justified by the following lemma.

Proposition 9.7
.

\mathcal{X}
is collapsed at
0
if and only if any tangent flow at
z\in Z_{0}
is collapsed (see Definition
7.18
).

Proof.

If every tangent flow at every point of
Z_{0}
is collapsed, then in particular
\mathcal{R}_{0}=\emptyset
. Hence,
\mathcal{X}
is collapsed at
0
.

Conversely, suppose for contradiction that
\mathcal{X}
is collapsed at
0
, but there exists a tangent flow
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
at some point
z\in Z_{0}
that is noncollapsed. We may assume that this tangent flow is obtained as the pointed Gromov–Hausdorff limit of
(Z,r_{j}^{-1}d_{Z},z,r_{j}^{-2}\mathfrak{t})
for a sequence
r_{j}\searrow 0
. Then, by the same argument as in the proof of Theorem
7.26
, there exists a sequence
x_{i}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}
such that
r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(x_{i})\to+\infty
.

By smooth convergence on the regular part and Theorem
2.23
, we can find points
y_{j}^{*}=(y_{j},-r_{j}^{2})\in M\times[-T,0)
such that
r_{j}^{-1}r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(y_{j}^{*})\to+\infty
. This implies that
\mathcal{R}_{0}
is nonempty, contradicting the assumption that
\mathcal{X}
is collapsed at time
0
.
∎

Next, we prove

Proposition 9.8
.

We have

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{R}_{0}\right\rvert_{0}=V_{0},

(9.7)

where
\left\lvert\mathcal{R}_{0}\right\rvert_{0}
denotes the volume of
\mathcal{R}_{0}
with respect to
g^{Z}_{0}
.

Proof.

By (
8.20
), we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert\{r_{\mathrm{Rm}}<\delta\}\right\rvert_{t}\leq C(n,Y,T,\epsilon)\delta^{2-\epsilon}

(9.8)

for all
t\in[-T/100,0]
and small
\delta>0
. From this and the smooth convergence from
\mathcal{R}_{t}
to
\mathcal{R}_{0}
along
\partial_{\mathfrak{t}}
, we obtain (
9.7
).
∎

We end this section by proving the following result.

Theorem 9.9
.

For any small
\delta>0
and
\epsilon>0
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\{y\in Z_{0}\mid d_{0}^{Z}(y,\mathcal{S})<\delta\right\}\right\rvert_{0}\leq C_{\epsilon}\delta^{2-\epsilon},

where
C_{\epsilon}
depends on
\epsilon
and the Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}
.

Proof.

It follows from Lemma
6.4
that

\displaystyle\left\{y\in Z_{0}\mid d_{0}^{Z}(y,\mathcal{S})<\delta\right\}\subset S^{\prime}:=\left\{y\in Z_{0}\mid d_{Z}(y,\mathcal{S})<\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\delta\right\}.

Since
r_{\mathrm{Rm}}=0
on
\mathcal{S}
, we obtain from Proposition
7.6
that any
y\in S^{\prime}
satisfies

\displaystyle r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(y)<C(n,Y)\delta,

which implies

\displaystyle S^{\prime}\subset\left\{y\in Z_{0}\mid r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(y)<C(n,Y)\delta\right\}.

Thus, the proof is complete by (
9.8
).
∎

10
Almost splitting maps

In this section, we consider a closed Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in I}\}
with a fixed spacetime point
x_{0}^{*}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
. Moreover, we set

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}=\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*}_{0};t}=(4\pi\tau)^{-n/2}e^{-f}\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)},

where
\tau=t_{0}-t
.

Now, we have the following definition of almost splitting maps, which is similar to
[
Bam20b
, Definition 5.7]
.

Definition 10.1

(
(k,\epsilon,r)
-splitting map)
.

A map
\vec{u}=(u_{1},\cdots,u_{k})
is called a
(k,\epsilon,r)
-splitting map at
x_{0}^{*}
if
t_{0}-10r^{2}\in I
, and for all
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
, the following properties hold:

(i)

u_{i}(x^{*}_{0})=0
.

(ii)

\square u_{i}=0
on
M\times[t_{0}-10r^{2},t_{0}]
.

(iii)

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}-10r^{2}}^{t_{0}-r^{2}/10}\int_{M}|\nabla^{2}u_{i}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t\leq\epsilon.

(iv)

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}-10r^{2}}^{t_{0}-r^{2}/10}\int_{M}\langle\nabla u_{i},\nabla u_{j}\rangle-\delta_{ij}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t=0.

In the following, we always assume that
\epsilon
is a small number, say
\epsilon\leq 10^{-3}
.

Proposition 10.2
.

Let
\vec{u}=(u_{1},\cdots,u_{k})
be a
(k,\epsilon,r)
-splitting map at
x_{0}^{*}
. Then for any
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}

and for all
t\in[t_{0}-10r^{2},t_{0}-r^{2}/10]
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert\int_{M}\langle\nabla u_{i},\nabla u_{j}\rangle-\delta_{ij}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\right\rvert\leq 2\epsilon\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{M}\big|\langle\nabla u_{i},\nabla u_{j}\rangle-\delta_{ij}\big|\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\leq 50\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Moreover, for all
t\in[t_{0}-\frac{10}{p-1}r^{2},t_{0})
and
p\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\left(\int_{M}|\nabla u_{i}|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\right)^{1/p}\leq 1+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(10.1)

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
t_{0}=0
and
r=1
. We set

\displaystyle I_{ij}(t)=\int_{M}\langle\nabla u_{i},\nabla u_{j}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}-\delta_{ij}.

Since

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}{\displaystyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}I_{ij}(t)=-2\int_{M}\langle\nabla^{2}u_{i},\nabla^{2}u_{j}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t},

we obtain for any
-10\leq s,t\leq-1/10
,

\displaystyle|I_{ij}(t)-I_{ij}(s)|\leq

\displaystyle 2\int_{-10}^{-1/10}\int_{M}|\nabla^{2}u_{i}||\nabla^{2}u_{j}|\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\left(\int_{-10}^{-1/10}\int_{M}|\nabla^{2}u_{i}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{-10}^{-1/10}\int_{M}|\nabla^{2}u_{j}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq 2\epsilon.

(10.2)

Then, it follows from (iv) in Definition
10.1
that for all
t\in[-10,-1/10]
,

\displaystyle|I_{ij}(t)|\leq 2\epsilon.

(10.3)

Applying Theorem
2.18
to
\langle\nabla u_{i},\nabla u_{j}\rangle-\delta_{ij}
, we have for all
t\in[-10,-1/10]
,

\displaystyle\int_{M}\left|\langle\nabla u_{i},\nabla u_{j}\rangle-\delta_{ij}-I_{ij}(t)\right|\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\pi|t|}\int_{M}\left|\nabla(\langle\nabla u_{i},\nabla u_{j}\rangle-\delta_{ij})\right|\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{10\pi}\int_{M}|\nabla^{2}u_{i}||\nabla u_{j}|+|\nabla^{2}u_{j}||\nabla u_{i}|\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}.

Integrating in time, we get

\displaystyle\int_{-10}^{-1/10}\int_{M}\left|\langle\nabla u_{i},\nabla u_{j}\rangle-\delta_{ij}-I_{ij}(t)\right|\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{10\pi}\left(\int_{-10}^{-1/10}\int_{M}|\nabla^{2}u_{i}|^{2}+|\nabla^{2}u_{j}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{-10}^{-1/10}\int_{M}|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}+|\nabla u_{j}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\leq 20\sqrt{\pi}\epsilon^{1/2},

where we used Definition
10.1
(iv) to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{-10}^{-1/10}\int_{M}|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}+|\nabla u_{j}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t\leq 20.

Combining with (
10.3
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{-10}^{-1/10}\int_{M}\big|\langle\nabla u_{i},\nabla u_{j}\rangle-\delta_{ij}\big|\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t\leq 20\epsilon+20\sqrt{\pi}\epsilon^{1/2}\leq 40\epsilon^{1/2}.

(10.4)

Since

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}{\displaystyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}\int_{M}\big|\langle\nabla u_{i},\nabla u_{j}\rangle-\delta_{ij}\big|\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{M}\left|\square(\langle\nabla u_{i},\nabla u_{j}\rangle-\delta_{ij})\right|\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{M}|\nabla^{2}u_{i}||\nabla^{2}u_{i}|\mathrm{d}\nu_{t},

we obtain as (
10.2
) that for any
s,t\in[-10,-1/10]
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\int_{M}\big|\langle\nabla u_{i},\nabla u_{j}\rangle-\delta_{ij}\big|\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}-\int_{M}\big|\langle\nabla u_{i},\nabla u_{j}\rangle-\delta_{ij}\big|\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{s}\right\rvert\leq 2\epsilon.

Combining this with (
10.4
), we conclude that for all
t\in[-10,-1/10]
,

\displaystyle\int_{M}\big|\langle\nabla u_{i},\nabla u_{j}\rangle-\delta_{ij}\big|\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\leq 40\epsilon^{1/2}+2\epsilon\leq 50\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}.

For the last statement (
10.1
), we apply Theorem
2.19
to
|\nabla u_{i}|
with
0<\tau_{1}\leq\frac{10}{p-1}
and
\tau_{2}=10
so that

\displaystyle\left(\int_{M}|\nabla u_{i}|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{-\tau_{1}}\right)^{1/p}\leq\left(\int_{M}|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{-10}\right)^{1/2}\leq 1+\epsilon^{1/2},

for any
p\geq 2
, where we used Proposition
10.2
for the last inequality.
∎

Proposition 10.3

(Gradient estimate)
.

Let
\vec{u}=(u_{1},\cdots,u_{k})
be a
(k,\epsilon,r)
-splitting map at
x_{0}^{*}
and
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
. Then there exists a constant
\hat{C}=\hat{C}(n)>0
such that on
M\times[t_{0}-r^{2},t_{0})
,

\displaystyle|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}(x,t)\leq 1+\hat{C}\epsilon^{1/8}\exp\left(\frac{\hat{C}d^{2}_{t}(x,z)}{r^{2}}\right),

(10.5)

where
(z,t)
is an
H_{n}
-center of
x_{0}^{*}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
t_{0}=0
and
r=1
.

We consider
x^{*}=(x,t)
with
t\in[-1,0)
. By the reproduction formula and
\square(|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}-1)=-2|\nabla^{2}u_{i}|^{2}
, we have,

\displaystyle(|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}-1)(x^{*})

\displaystyle=\int_{M}(|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}-1)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};-2}+\int_{-2}^{t}\int_{M}\square(|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}-1)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};s}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\int_{M}\big||\nabla u_{i}|^{2}-1\big|\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};-2}.

(10.6)

Take an
H_{n}
-center
(z,t)
of
x_{0}^{*}
. Then
d^{t}_{W_{1}}(\nu_{t},\delta_{z})\leq\sqrt{H_{n}|t|}\leq C(n)
. By Proposition
A.2
, for any small constant
\alpha\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};-2}\leq e^{C(n,\alpha)\big(d_{W_{1}}^{t}(\nu_{t},\delta_{x})\big)^{2}}e^{\alpha f}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{-2}\leq C(n,\alpha)e^{C(n,\alpha)d_{t}^{2}(x,z)}e^{\alpha f}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{-2}.

(10.7)

Combining (
10.7
) with (
10
), we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert(|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}-1)(x^{*})\right\rvert\leq

\displaystyle C(n,\alpha)e^{C(n,\alpha)d_{t}^{2}(x,z)}\int_{M}\big||\nabla u_{i}|^{2}-1\big|e^{\alpha f}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{-2}.

(10.8)

On the other hand, we have
