\displaystyle\int_{M}\big||\nabla u_{i}|^{2}-1\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{-2}\leq\left(\int_{M}\big||\nabla u_{i}|^{2}-1\big|\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{-2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{M}\big||\nabla u_{i}|^{2}-1\big|^{3}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{-2}\right)^{1/2}\leq C\epsilon^{1/4},

for a universal constant
C>0
, where we used (
10.1
) with
p=6
. Combining this with (
10.8
) and using Proposition
2.22
(with
\alpha=\alpha(n)
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\lvert(|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}-1)(x^{*})\right\rvert\leq C(n)e^{C(n)d_{t}^{2}(x,z)}\epsilon^{1/8}.

This completes the proof of (
10.5
).
∎

We next prove that the almost splitting map is locally Lipschitz with respect to the spacetime distance.

Proposition 10.4
.

Let
\vec{u}=(u_{1},\cdots,u_{k})
be a
(k,\epsilon,r)
-splitting map at
x_{0}^{*}
. Then there exists a small constant
\hat{c}=\hat{c}(n)>0
such that for any
z^{*}
with
d^{*}(x_{0}^{*},z^{*})\leq\hat{c}r
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert\vec{u}(z^{*})\right\rvert\leq C(n)d^{*}(x_{0}^{*},z^{*}).

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
t_{0}=0
and
r=1
. For simplicity, we also assume
\mathfrak{t}(z^{*})\leq 0
as the other case can be proved similarly.

Define
s:=100d^{*}(x_{0}^{*},z^{*})
, and let
(z_{0},-s^{2})
and
(z_{1},-s^{2})
be
H_{n}
-centers of
x_{0}^{*}
and
z^{*}
, respectively.

By Definition
3.5
, we have

\displaystyle\epsilon_{0}s\geq d_{W_{1}}^{-s^{2}}(\nu_{x_{0}^{*};-s^{2}},\nu_{z^{*};-s^{2}})\geq d_{-s^{2}}(z_{0},z_{1})-2s\sqrt{H_{n}}

and hence

\displaystyle d_{-s^{2}}(z_{0},z_{1})\leq C(n)s.

(10.9)

We choose
\hat{c}
to be small enough so that
\frac{1}{10s^{2}}\geq\max\{1,\hat{C}(n)\}
, where
\hat{C}(n)
is the same constant as in Proposition
10.3
. Since
\vec{u}
solves heat equation, we have
\vec{u}(x^{*}_{0})=\int_{M}\vec{u}(y,t)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}
. Thus, by Proposition
10.3
, we obtain that

\displaystyle|\vec{u}(x^{*}_{0})-\vec{u}(z_{0},-s^{2})|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{M}|\vec{u}(y,-s^{2})-\vec{u}(z_{0},-s^{2})|\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{-s^{2}}(y)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{M}\left(1+C(n)\exp\left(\frac{d_{-s^{2}}^{2}(y,z_{0})}{6s^{2}}\right)\right)d_{-s^{2}}(y,z_{0})\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{-s^{2}}(y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\int_{\{ks\leq d_{-s^{2}}(y,z_{0})\leq(k+1)s\}}\left(1+C(n)\exp\left(\frac{d_{-s^{2}}^{2}(y,z_{0})}{6s^{2}}\right)\right)d_{-s^{2}}(y,z_{0})\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{-s^{2}}(y)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(1+C(n)e^{(k+1)^{2}/6}\right)(k+1)se^{-k^{2}/5}\leq C(n)s,

(10.10)

where we used Theorem
2.15
(i) for the third inequality. Since
\vec{u}(x^{*}_{0})=0
, we conclude

\displaystyle|\vec{u}(z_{0},-s^{2})|\leq C(n)s.

(10.11)

By using (
10.9
) and the same argument as (
10.10
), we obtain

\displaystyle|\vec{u}(z^{*})-\vec{u}(z_{1},-s^{2})|\leq C(n)s.

(10.12)

Combining (
10.11
) and (
10.12
), we conclude from Proposition
10.3
(ii) that

\displaystyle|\vec{u}(z^{*})|\leq|\vec{u}(z^{*})-\vec{u}(z_{1},-s^{2})|+|\vec{u}(z_{0},-s^{2})-\vec{u}(z_{1},-s^{2})|+|\vec{u}(z_{0},-s^{2})|\leq C(n)s.

In sum, the proof is complete.
∎

For the rest of the section, we consider a Ricci flow limit space
(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t})\in\overline{\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)}
; see Notation
3.26
.

Next, we introduce the following quantitative concept of splitting on
Z
.

Definition 10.5
.

A point
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
is called
(k,\epsilon,r)
-splitting
if
\mathfrak{t}(z)-10r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
and there exists a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space such that its regular part
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{[-10,0]}
splits off an
\mathbb{R}^{k}
as a Ricci flow spacetime. Moreover,

\displaystyle(Z,r^{-1}d_{Z},z,r^{-2}(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}(z)))\quad\text{is $\epsilon$-close to}\quad(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})\quad\text{over}\quad[-10,0].

Moreover, we generalize Definition
10.1
on
Z
.

Definition 10.6
.

A map
\vec{u}=(u_{1},\cdots,u_{k})
is called a
(k,\epsilon,r)
-splitting map at
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
if
\mathfrak{t}(z)-10r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, and
\vec{u}
is obtained as the limit of a sequence of
(k,\epsilon,r)
-splitting maps
\vec{u}^{i}=(u^{i}_{1},\ldots,u^{i}_{k})
at
z_{i}^{*}
with
z_{i}^{*}\to z
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense. Note that
\vec{u}
is defined on
Z_{(\mathfrak{t}(z)-10r^{2},0]}
by reproduction formula and Theorem
5.20
.

Note that by taking the limit, all the above propositions and corollaries hold for almost splitting maps on
Z
.

We end this section by proving the following result.

Proposition 10.7
.

Let
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})\in\overline{\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)}
. For any
\epsilon>0
, if
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\epsilon)
and
z
is a
(k,\delta,r)
-splitting point, then there exists a map
\vec{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{k})
defined on
Z_{(\mathfrak{t}(z)-9r^{2},0]}
such that for any
x\in B^{*}_{Z}(z,\epsilon^{-1}r)\cap Z_{[\mathfrak{t}(z)-\delta r^{2},\mathfrak{t}(z)+\delta r^{2}]}
and
s\in[\epsilon r,r/2]
, there exists a matrix
T_{x,s}
satisfying
\|T_{x,s}-\mathrm{Id}\|\leq\epsilon
, for which the rescaled map
\vec{u}_{x,s}:=T_{x,s}\big(\vec{u}-\vec{u}(x)\big)
is a
(k,\epsilon,s)
-splitting map at
x
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
r=1
and
\mathfrak{t}(z)=0
.

Assume that the conclusion is false. Then we can find Ricci flow limit spaces
(Z^{l},d_{Z^{l}},\mathfrak{t}_{l})\in\overline{\mathcal{M}(n,Y)}
such that there exist
z_{l}\in Z^{l}
that is
(k,l^{-2},1)
-splitting.

By taking a subsequence, we assume

\displaystyle(Z^{l},d_{Z^{l}},\mathfrak{t}_{l},z_{l})\xrightarrow[l\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathrm{\hat{C}^{\infty}}\hskip 8.19447pt}(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t},z).

After taking a diagonal subsequence, we may assume that
(Z^{l},d_{Z^{l}},\mathfrak{t}_{l},z_{l})=(M_{l}\times\mathbb{I}_{l},d_{l}^{*},\mathfrak{t}_{l},z_{l}^{*})\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T_{l})
.

By Definition
10.5
, we conclude that the regular part
\mathcal{R}_{[-10,0]}=\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Moreover, it follows from Lemma
5.22
that for any
w=(w^{\prime},\vec{b})\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
and any
s\in[-10,\mathfrak{t}(w))
, we have

\displaystyle\nu_{w;s}=\nu^{\prime}_{w^{\prime};s}\otimes\nu^{\mathbb{R}^{k}}_{\vec{b};s},

(10.13)

where
\nu^{\mathbb{R}^{k}}_{\vec{b};s}
is the standard Gaussian measure on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
defined by

\displaystyle\nu^{\mathbb{R}^{k}}_{\vec{b};s}=(4\pi(\mathfrak{t}(w)-s))^{-\frac{k}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|\vec{b}-\vec{x}|^{2}}{4(\mathfrak{t}(w)-s)}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\vec{x}.

Let
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
denote the corresponding coordinate functions satisfying
\int_{\mathcal{R}_{-10}}y_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z;-10}=0
. By solving the corresponding heat equation, we assume that
y_{i}
satisfies
\square y_{i}=0
on
\mathcal{R}_{[-10,0]}
and is defined on
Z_{(-10,0]}
.

By (
10.13
), we conclude that for any
w\in\mathcal{R}_{(-10,0]}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{R}_{-9}}\left(y_{i}-y_{i}(w)\right)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{w;-9}=2(\mathfrak{t}(w)+9),\quad\int_{\mathcal{R}_{-9}}\langle\nabla y_{i},\nabla y_{j}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{w;-9}=\delta_{ij}.

(10.14)

By taking the limit (see (
5.11
)), we conclude that (
10.14
) also holds for
w\in Z_{(-10,0]}
.

Using the cutoff functions in Proposition
8.20
and the smooth convergence, we can find smooth functions
\vec{u}^{l}=(u^{l}_{1},\ldots,u^{l}_{k})
on
M_{l}\times[-9,0]
with
\square\vec{u}^{l}=0
so that
\vec{u}^{l}
converge smoothly to
\vec{y}
on
\mathcal{R}_{(-9,0)}
.

According to our assumption, there exist
x_{l}^{*}\in B^{*}(z_{l}^{*},\epsilon^{-1})\cap M_{l}\times[-l^{-2},l^{-2}]
and
s_{l}\in[\epsilon,1/2]
such that
\vec{u}^{l}
does not satisfy the conclusion. By taking a subsequence, we assume that
s_{l}\to s_{\infty}\in[\epsilon,1/2]
, and
x_{l}^{*}
converge to
x_{\infty}\in\overline{B^{*}_{Z}(z,\epsilon^{-1})}\cap Z_{0}
.

Applying (
10.14
) to
x_{\infty}
, we obtain by smooth convergence that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\int_{M_{l}}(u_{i}^{l}-a_{i}^{l}(x_{l}^{*}))^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{l}^{*};-9}-18\right\rvert\xrightarrow[l\to\infty]{}0,

where

\displaystyle a_{i}^{l}(x_{l}^{*}):=\int_{M_{l}}u_{i}^{l}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{l}^{*};-9}.

Moreover, the following estimate holds:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\int_{M_{l}}\langle\nabla u^{l}_{i},\nabla u^{l}_{j}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{l}^{*};-9}-\delta_{ij}\right\rvert\xrightarrow[l\to\infty]{}0

for any
1\leq i,j\leq k
. Thus, the corresponding frequency function
F_{u_{i}^{l}-a_{i}^{l}(x_{l}^{*})}
with respect to
x_{l}^{*}
(see Definition
C.1
) satisfies

\displaystyle\left\lvert F_{u_{i}^{l}-a_{i}^{l}(x_{l}^{*})}(-9)-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert\xrightarrow[l\to\infty]{}0.

Thus, by the same argument as in the proof of Proposition
C.3
(see also Corollary
C.4
), we conclude that for sufficiently large
l
, there exists a matrix
T_{x_{l}^{*},s_{l}}
satisfying
\|T_{x_{l}^{*},s_{l}}-\mathrm{Id}\|\to 0
, such that the map
T_{x_{l}^{*},s_{l}}\left(\vec{u}^{l}-\vec{u}^{l}(x_{l}^{*})\right)
is a
(k,\epsilon,s_{l})
-splitting map at
x_{l}^{*}
.

This, however, contradicts our assumption, thereby completing the proof.
∎

11
Further discussions

In this section, we discuss how the results in this paper can be generalized to Ricci flows with bounded curvature. We also examine certain special properties that arise in the Kähler setting.

Complete Ricci flows with bounded curvature

For a fixed constant
T\in(0,+\infty]
and a small parameter
\sigma\in(0,1/100]
, we define as before

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}^{-}=(-(1-2\sigma)T,0],\quad\mathbb{I}=[-(1-2\sigma)T,0],\quad\mathbb{I}^{+}=[-(1-\sigma)T,0],\quad\mathbb{I}^{++}=[-T,0].

Definition 11.1
.

For fixed constants
T\in(0,+\infty]
and
Y>0
, the moduli space
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(n,Y,T)
consists of all
n
-dimensional complete Ricci flows
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}
with bounded curvature on every compact time subinterval of
\mathbb{I}^{++}
and entropy bounded below by
-Y
.

It is clear that
\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(n,Y,T)
. As shown in
[
Bam21
, Appendix A]
, the results of
[
Bam20a
]
,
[
Bam23
]
, and
[
Bam20b
]
remain valid for Ricci flows in
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(n,Y,T)
. For related heat kernel estimates on complete noncompact Ricci flows, we refer to
[
Li25
]
, where full details are provided. For example, the upper bound of the heat kernel in Theorem
2.15
appears as
[
Li25
, Theorem 11.4]
; Theorem
2.19
on hypercontractivity corresponds to
[
Li25
, Theorem 12.1]
; and the integral estimates in Proposition
2.22
are established in
[
Li25
, Section 13]
.

Consequently, all results in Sections
2
-
4
also hold for
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(n,Y,T)
. In particular, we have the following weak-compactness theorem as Theorem
3.23
.

Theorem 11.2
.

Consider a sequence

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}^{i}=\{M_{i}^{n},(g_{i}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(n,Y,T)

with base point
p_{i}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}^{i}
. When
T=+\infty
, we additionally assume
\limsup_{i\to\infty}\mathfrak{t}_{i}(p_{i}^{*})>-\infty
.

By taking a subsequence if necessary, we obtain the pointed Gromov–Hausdorff convergence

(M_{i}\times\mathbb{I},d^{*}_{i},p_{i}^{*},\mathfrak{t}_{i})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathrm{pGH}\hskip 8.19447pt}(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t}),

(11.1)

where
d^{*}_{i}
denotes the
d^{*}
-distance associated with
\mathcal{X}^{i}
, and
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
is a complete, separable, locally compact parabolic metric space over
\mathbb{I}
.

In addition, all results in Section
5
-
8
hold for the Ricci flow limit space
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
. We summarize some key properties in the following theorem.

Theorem 11.3
.

Suppose
(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t})
is a Gromov–Hausdorff limit obtained in Theorem
11.2
. Then the following properties hold.

(1)

There exists a decomposition

\displaystyle Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}=\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}\sqcup\mathcal{S}

such that
\mathcal{R}
is given by an
n
-dimensional Ricci flow spacetime
(\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{t},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}},g^{Z})
and
\dim_{\mathscr{M}}\mathcal{S}\leq n-2
, where
\dim_{\mathscr{M}}
denotes the Minkowski dimension in Definition
8.9
. Moreover,
\mathcal{R}
is connected and open.

(2)

For each
t\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, there exists an extended distance
d^{Z}_{t}
on
Z_{t}
such that
d^{Z}_{t}
, when restricted on
\mathcal{R}_{t}
, agrees with
d_{g^{Z}_{t}}
locally. In addition, each point
z\in Z
is assigned with a conjugate heat kernel measure
\nu_{z;s}
such that
(Z,\mathfrak{t},(d^{Z}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{-}},(\nu_{z;s})_{z\in Z,\,-(1-2\sigma)T\leq s\leq\mathfrak{t}(z)})
is an extended metric flow in the sense of Definition
6.15
. Moreover, for any
x,y\in Z
with
\mathfrak{t}(x)\geq\mathfrak{t}(y)
and
r=d_{Z}(x,y)
with
\mathfrak{t}(x)-r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{t\nearrow\mathfrak{t}(x)-r^{2}}d_{W_{1}}^{Z_{t}}(\nu_{x;t},\nu_{y;t})\leq\epsilon_{0}r\leq\lim_{t\searrow\mathfrak{t}(x)-r^{2}}d_{W_{1}}^{Z_{t}}(\nu_{x;t},\nu_{y;t}).

(3)

Every tangent flow
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},z^{\prime})
at a point
z\in Z
, when restricted on the regular part
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
, satisfies the equation

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}(g^{Z^{\prime}})+\nabla^{2}f_{z^{\prime}}=\frac{g^{Z^{\prime}}}{2\tau}.

Moreover, each
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}
is connected for any
t\in(-\infty,0)
.

(4)

The convergence (
11.1
) is smooth on
\mathcal{R}
in the following sense. There exists an increasing sequence
U_{1}\subset U_{2}\subset\ldots\subset\mathcal{R}
of open subsets with
\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}U_{i}=\mathcal{R}
, open subsets
V_{i}\subset M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
, time-preserving diffeomorphisms
\phi_{i}:U_{i}\to V_{i}
and a sequence
\epsilon_{i}\to 0
such that the following holds:

(a)

We have

\displaystyle\|\phi_{i}^{*}g^{i}-g^{Z}\|_{C^{[\epsilon_{i}^{-1}]}(U_{i})}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon_{i},

\displaystyle\|\phi_{i}^{*}\partial_{\mathfrak{t}_{i}}-\partial_{\mathfrak{t}}\|_{C^{[\epsilon_{i}^{-1}]}(U_{i})}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon_{i},

where
g^{i}
is the spacetime metric induced by
g_{i}(t)
, and
\partial_{\mathfrak{t}_{i}}
is the standard time vector field.

(b)

For
U_{i}^{(2)}=\{(x,y)\in U_{i}\times U_{i}\mid\mathfrak{t}(x)>\mathfrak{t}(y)+\epsilon_{i}\}
,
V_{i}^{(2)}=\{(x^{*},y^{*})\in V_{i}\times V_{i}\mid\mathfrak{t}_{i}(x^{*})>\mathfrak{t}_{i}(y^{*})+\epsilon_{i}\}
and
\phi_{i}^{(2)}:=(\phi_{i},\phi_{i}):U_{i}^{(2)}\to V_{i}^{(2)}
, we have

\displaystyle\|(\phi_{i}^{(2)})^{*}K^{i}-K_{Z}\|_{C^{[\epsilon_{i}^{-1}]}(U_{i}^{(2)})}\leq\epsilon_{i},

where
K^{i}
and
K_{Z}
denote the heat kernels on
(M_{i}\times\mathbb{I},g_{i}(t))
and
(\mathcal{R},g^{Z})
, respectively.

(c)

Let
y\in\mathcal{R}
and
y_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
. Then
y_{i}^{*}\to y
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense if and only if
y_{i}^{*}\in V_{i}
for large
i
and
\phi_{i}^{-1}(y_{i}^{*})\to y
in
\mathcal{R}
.

(d)

If
y_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
converge to
y\in Z
in Gromov–Hausdorff sense, then

\displaystyle K^{i}(y_{i}^{*};\phi_{i}(\cdot))\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}}K_{Z}(y;\cdot)\quad\text{on}\quad\mathcal{R}_{(-\infty,\mathfrak{t}(y))}.

(e)

For each
t\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, there are at most countable connected components of the time-slice
\mathcal{R}_{t}
.

(f)

For all but countably many times
t\in\mathbb{I}
, we have

\displaystyle d^{Z}_{t}=d_{g^{Z}_{t}}

on each connected component of
\mathcal{R}_{t}
.

(5)

Given
x_{0}\in Z
and
r>0
with
\mathfrak{t}(x_{0})-2r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, then for any
\epsilon>0
, we can find a constant
C=C(n,Y,\sigma,\epsilon)>0
such that

\displaystyle\int_{B^{*}_{Z}(x_{0},r)\cap\mathcal{R}}r_{\mathrm{Rm}}^{-4+2\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z}_{t}}\mathrm{d}t\leq Cr^{n-2+2\epsilon}.

Moreover, for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\int_{B^{*}_{Z}(x_{0},r)\cap\mathcal{R}_{t}}r_{\mathrm{Rm}}^{-2+2\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z}_{t}}\leq Cr^{n-2+2\epsilon}.

All results in Section
10
also hold for
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(n,Y,T)
, except that we need to modify the definition of the
(k,\epsilon,r)
-splitting map slightly as follows.

Definition 11.4

(
(k,\epsilon,r)
-splitting map)
.

Given
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(n,Y,T)
with
x_{0}^{*}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
, a map
\vec{u}=(u_{1},\cdots,u_{k})
is called a
(k,\epsilon,r)
-splitting map at
x_{0}^{*}
if
t_{0}-10r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, and for all
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
, the following properties hold:

(i)

u_{i}(x^{*}_{0})=0
.

(ii)

\square u_{i}=0
on
M\times[t_{0}-10r^{2},t_{0}]
.

(iii)

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}-10r^{2}}^{t_{0}-r^{2}/10}\int_{M}|\nabla^{2}u_{i}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t\leq\epsilon.

(iv)

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}-10r^{2}}^{t_{0}-r^{2}/10}\int_{M}\langle\nabla u_{i},\nabla u_{j}\rangle-\delta_{ij}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t=0.

(v)

For any compact interval
J\subset[t_{0}-10r^{2},t_{0})
, there exist a constant
m>0
and
z\in M
such that on
M\times J
,

\displaystyle|\nabla u_{i}(x,t)|\leq m\left(d^{m}_{t}(z,x)+1\right).

Note that (v) above obviously holds for any closed Ricci flow. Thus, Definition
11.4
matches with Definition
10.1
.

Example 11.5

(Tangent flow at infinity)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in(-\infty,0]}\}
be a complete Ricci flow with bounded curvature on any compact time interval of
(-\infty,0]
. Moreover, we assume that
\mathcal{X}
has entropy bounded below by
-Y
at a spacetime point
p^{*}
. Note that it follows from
[
CMZ23
]
that this assumption implies that
\mathcal{X}
has entropy bounded below by
-Y
at any spacetime point.

We consider the
d^{*}
-distance on
\mathcal{X}
, defined as Definition
3.5
with respect to a constant
\epsilon_{0}=\epsilon_{0}(n,Y)>0
. For a sequence
r_{i}\to+\infty
and set

\displaystyle g_{i}(t)=r_{i}^{-2}g(r_{i}^{2}t),\quad\mathfrak{t}_{i}=r_{i}^{-2}\mathfrak{t}\quad d^{*}_{i}=r_{i}^{-1}d^{*}.

Then, by taking a subsequence, we have the pointed Gromov–Hausdorff convergence

\displaystyle(M\times(-\infty,0],d^{*}_{i},p^{*},\mathfrak{t}_{i})\xrightarrow[j\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathrm{pGH}\hskip 8.19447pt}(Z,d_{Z},z,\mathfrak{t}).

where
(Z,d_{Z},z,\mathfrak{t})
is a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space over
(-\infty,0]
, which is called a
tangent flow at infinity
. Note that
(Z,d_{Z},z,\mathfrak{t})
depends on the sequence
\{r_{i}\}
, but is independent of
p^{*}
.

By Lemma
7.2
, it is not hard to see
\mathcal{N}_{z}(\tau)
is constant. Thus, as Proposition
7.9
and Corollary
7.10
, we conclude that on the regular part
\mathcal{R}_{(-\infty,0)}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}(g^{Z})+\nabla^{2}f_{z}=\frac{g^{Z}}{2\tau},

where
\tau(\cdot)=-\mathfrak{t}(\cdot)
. Moreover,
\mathcal{R}_{t}
is connected for any
t\in(-\infty,0)
, and the metric
d^{Z}_{t}
on
\mathcal{R}_{t}
agrees with
g^{Z}_{t}
. In addition, there exists a flow
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{s}
on
Z_{(-\infty,0)}
so that statements as in Proposition
7.30
hold.

Kähler Ricci flows

Now we consider the subspace
\widetilde{\mathcal{KM}}(n,Y,T)
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(n,Y,T)
which consists of all Kähler Ricci flows. In particular,
n=2m
is even.

We have the following definition similar to Definition
7.16
.

Definition 11.6

(Kähler Ricci shrinker space)
.

A pointed parabolic metric space
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
with
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(z^{\prime})=0
is called an
m
-dimensional
Kähler Ricci shrinker space
with entropy bounded below by
-Y
if it satisfies
\mathbb{R}_{-}\subset\mathrm{image}(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
and arises as the pointed Gromov–Hausdorff limit of a sequence of Kähler Ricci flows in
\widetilde{\mathcal{KM}}(2m,Y,T_{i})
with
T_{i}\to+\infty
. Moreover,
\mathcal{N}_{z^{\prime}}(\tau)
remains constant for all
\tau>0
.

It is clear from the smooth convergence that any Kähler Ricci shrinker space
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
has a complex structure
J
on the regular part
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
.

Lemma 11.7
.

Any
m
-dimensional Kähler Ricci shrinker space
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is
2k
-symmetric for some
k\in\{0,\ldots,m+1\}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
8.2
(see also Remark
8.3
), we only need to prove that on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}
, if
\nabla y
for some smooth function
y
induces a splitting direction, then so does
J\nabla y
.

Indeed, define
y^{\prime}=2g^{Z^{\prime}}(\nabla f_{z^{\prime}},J\nabla y)
. A direct computation yields:

\displaystyle\nabla y^{\prime}=2g^{Z^{\prime}}(\nabla^{2}f_{z^{\prime}},J\nabla y)+2g^{Z^{\prime}}(\nabla f_{z^{\prime}},J\nabla^{2}y)=2g^{Z^{\prime}}(g^{Z^{\prime}}/2-\mathrm{Ric}(g^{Z^{\prime}}),J\nabla y)=J\nabla y,

since
\mathrm{Ric}(g^{Z^{\prime}})(J\nabla y)=0
. Since
J\nabla y
is a nonvanishing parallel vector field, it is clear that
\nabla y^{\prime}
induces another splitting direction.
∎

By using Lemma
11.7
, we have the following stratification of singular points (see also
[
HJ23
]
).

Let
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
be a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space, which is the pointed Gromov–Hausdorff limit of a sequence of Kähler Ricci flows in
\widetilde{\mathcal{KM}}(2m,Y,T)
. Then the singular set satisfies the following refined stratification:

\mathcal{S}^{0}\subset\mathcal{S}^{2}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{S}^{2(m-1)}=\mathcal{S}.

Appendix A
Change of basis for conjugate heat kernel measures

In this appendix, we derive two versions of estimates for the conjugate heat kernel measures. The proofs follow from slight modifications of
[
Bam20b
, Proposition 8.1]
.

Proposition A.1
.

Let
\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in I}\}
be a closed Ricci flow. Suppose
s,t_{0},t_{1}\in I
satisfy for some constants
D
and
Y
:

•

s<t_{1}\leq t_{0}
, and
\mathrm{R}(\cdot,s)\geq-D(t_{1}-s)^{-1}
;

•

t_{0}-s\leq D(t_{1}-s)
;

•

d_{W_{1}}^{t_{1}}(\nu_{x_{0},t_{0};t_{1}},\delta_{x_{1}})\leq D\sqrt{t_{1}-s}
;

•

\mathcal{N}_{x_{0},t_{0}}(t_{0}-s)\geq-Y
or
\mathcal{N}_{x_{1},t_{1}}(t_{1}-s)\geq-Y
.

Define
\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{i},t_{i};t}=(4\pi\tau_{i})^{-n/2}e^{-f_{i}}dV_{g(t)}
for
i=0
or
1
, where
\tau_{i}=t_{i}-t
. Then,

\displaystyle\nu_{x_{1},t_{1};s}\leq C(n,Y,D)e^{C(n,Y,D)\sqrt{f_{0}(\cdot,s)+C(n,Y,D)}}\nu_{x_{0},t_{0};s}.

Proof.

In the proof, all constants
C_{i}
are positive and depend on
n,Y
and
D
. Without loss of generality, we assume
s=0
and
t_{1}=1\leq t_{0}
.

By Proposition
2.9
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}(x_{0},t_{0})-\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}(x_{1},1)|

\displaystyle\leq C(n)d^{1}_{W_{1}}(\nu_{x_{0},t_{0};1},\delta_{x_{1}})+\frac{n}{2}\log(t_{0})\leq C_{1}.

(A.1)

Moreover, we set
(z_{1},1)
to be an
H_{n}
-center of
(x_{0},t_{0})
. Then,

\displaystyle d_{1}(x_{1},z_{1})\leq d_{W_{1}}^{1}(\nu_{x_{0},t_{0};1},\delta_{x_{1}})+d_{W_{1}}^{1}(\nu_{x_{0},t_{0};1},\delta_{z_{1}})\leq C_{2}.

(A.2)

Fix
y_{0}\in M
and let
u:=K(\cdot,1;y_{0},0)
. By the reproduction formula, it suffices to prove

u(x_{1})\leq C(n,Y,D)e^{C(n,Y,D)\sqrt{f_{0}(y_{0},0)+C(n,Y,D)}}\int_{M}u\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{0},t_{0};1}.

(A.3)

By Theorem
2.15
(i) and Proposition
2.16
, we have

\displaystyle u\leq C_{3}\exp\left(-\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}(\cdot,1)\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{|\nabla u|}{u}\leq C_{3}\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{C_{3}\exp(-\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}(\cdot,1))}{u}\right)}.

We set
v:=C_{3}^{-1}u\exp(\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}(\cdot,1))/2
and
w:=\sqrt{-\log v}
. Then we obtain

\displaystyle|\nabla w|\leq C_{4}.

(A.4)

Thus, to prove (
A.3
), we only need to show

\displaystyle v(x_{1})\exp\left(-\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}(x_{1},1)\right)\leq C(n,Y,D)e^{C(n,Y,D)\sqrt{f_{0}(y_{0},0)+C(n,Y,D)}}\int_{M}v\exp\left(-\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}(\cdot,1)\right)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{0},t_{0};1}.

(A.5)

By (
A.1
) and our assumption, we have

\displaystyle-\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}(x_{1},1)\leq C_{5}.

(A.6)

Moreover, by (
A.2
) and (
A.4
), we obtain

\displaystyle w(x_{1})\geq\left(w(z_{1})-C_{4}d_{1}(x_{1},z_{1})\right)_{+}\geq\left(w(z_{1})-C_{6}\right)_{+},

which implies

\displaystyle v(x_{1})=\exp(-w(x_{1})^{2})\leq\exp\left(-(w(z_{1})-C_{6})_{+}^{2}\right).

(A.7)

Similarly, for any
y\in B:=B_{1}(z_{1},\sqrt{2H_{n}(t_{0}-1)})
, we have

\displaystyle v(x_{1})\leq\exp\left(-(w(y)-C_{4}d_{1}(x_{1},y))_{+}^{2}\right)\leq C_{7}\exp\left(C_{7}w(z_{1})\right)\exp\left(-w(y)^{2}\right).

(A.8)

We define
L\geq 0
such that

\displaystyle w(z_{1})=L\sqrt{f_{0}(y_{0},0)+L}.

If
L\leq C(n,Y,D)
, then it follows from (
A.6
) and (
A.8
) that (
A.5
) holds. Indeed, since
\nu_{x_{0},t_{0};1}(B)\geq 1/2
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{M}v\exp\left(-\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}(\cdot,1)\right)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{0},t_{0};1}\geq\int_{M}v\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{0},t_{0};1}\geq\frac{1}{2}\min_{y\in B}\exp\left(-w(y)^{2}\right),

which implies (
A.5
).

Otherwise, it follows from (
A.7
) that for some constant
C_{8}
,

\displaystyle v(x_{1})\exp\left(-\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}(x_{1},1)\right)\leq C_{8}\exp\left(-(L\sqrt{f_{0}(y_{0},0)+L}-C_{6})^{2}\right).

(A.9)

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\int_{M}v\exp\left(-\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}(\cdot,1)\right)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{0},t_{0};1}=C_{3}^{-1}\int_{M}u\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{0},t_{0};1}=C_{3}^{-1}(4\pi t_{0})^{-\frac{n}{2}}e^{-f_{0}(y_{0},0)}\geq C_{9}e^{-f_{0}(y_{0},0)}

(A.10)

for some constant
C_{9}
. Combining (
A.9
) and (
A.10
), we conclude that (
A.5
) also holds if
L\gg C(n,Y,D)
.

In sum, the proof is complete.
∎

Proposition A.2
.

Let
\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in I}\}
be a closed Ricci flow. Suppose
s,t^{*},t_{0},t_{1}\in I
satisfy:

•

s<t^{*}\leq\min\{t_{0},t_{1}\}
, and
\mathrm{R}(\cdot,s)\geq-A(t^{*}-s)^{-1}
;

•

for any constants
-\infty<\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{0}<1
and some
\theta=\theta(n,A)>0
, we have

\displaystyle t_{0}-t^{*}\leq A(t^{*}-s),\ t_{1}-t^{*}\leq\theta\frac{\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{1}}{1-\alpha_{0}}(t^{*}-s).

Denote
\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{i},t_{i};t}=(4\pi\tau_{i})^{-n/2}e^{-f_{i}}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}
for
i=0
or
1
, where
\tau_{i}=t_{i}-t
and assume

d_{W_{1}}^{t^{*}}(\nu_{x_{0},t_{0};t^{*}},\nu_{x_{1},t_{1};t^{*}})=D\sqrt{t^{*}-s}
,
then

\displaystyle e^{\alpha_{1}f_{1}}\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{1},t_{1};s}\leq e^{(\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{1})\mathcal{N}_{x_{0},t_{0}}(t_{0}-s)}C(n,A,\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})e^{C(n,A,\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})D^{2}}e^{\alpha_{0}f_{0}}\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{0},t_{0};s}.

Proof.

In the proof, all constants
C_{i}
are positive and depend on
n
and
A
, and
L_{i}
are positive constants depending on
n
,
A
,
\alpha_{0}
and
\alpha_{1}
.

Without loss of generality, we assume
s=0,t^{*}=1
and hence

\displaystyle d^{1}_{W_{1}}(\nu_{x_{0},t_{0};1},\nu_{x_{1},t_{1};1})=D,\quad\mathrm{R}\geq-A,\quad t_{0}\leq A+1.

By Proposition
2.9
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}(x_{0},t_{0})-\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}(x_{1},t_{1})|\leq C(n,A)D+\frac{n}{2}\log\max\{t_{0},t_{1}\}\leq L_{1}(D+1).

Let
(z_{i},1)
be an
H_{n}
-center of
(x_{i},t_{i})
for
i=1,2
. Then by Proposition
2.9
again, we know that

\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}(z_{1},1)\geq\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}(x_{1},t_{1})-C(n,A)\sqrt{H_{n}(t_{1}-1)}\geq\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}(x_{0},t_{0})-L_{2}(D+1).

(A.11)

Moreover, we have

\displaystyle d_{1}(z_{1},z_{0})\leq D+2\sqrt{H_{n}(t_{1}-1)}\leq D+L_{2}.

We set
\lambda:=\frac{1-\alpha_{0}}{1-\alpha_{1}}<1
and
u(\cdot):=K(\cdot,1;y_{0},0)
for a fixed
y_{0}\in M
. By the reproduction formula, we only need to prove

\int_{M}u\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{1},t_{1};1}\leq e^{(1-\lambda)\mathcal{N}_{x_{0},t_{0}}(t_{0}-s)}C(n,A,\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})e^{C(n,A,\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})D^{2}}\left(\int_{M}u\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{0},t_{0};1}\right)^{\lambda}.

(A.12)

As in the proof of Proposition
A.1
, we can find
C_{1}
such that
v:=C_{1}^{-1}u\exp(\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}(\cdot,1))\leq 1/2
, and
w:=\sqrt{-\log v}
satisfies:

\displaystyle|\nabla w|\leq C_{1}.

(A.13)

Thus, (
A.12
) becomes

\displaystyle\int_{M}v\exp(-\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}(\cdot,1))\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{1},t_{1};1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle e^{(1-\lambda)\mathcal{N}_{x_{0},t_{0}}(t_{0}-s)}C(n,A,\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})e^{C(n,A,\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})D^{2}}\left(\int_{M}v\exp(-\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}(\cdot,1))\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{0},t_{0};1}\right)^{\lambda}.

(A.14)

We set
B:=B_{1}\left(z_{0},\sqrt{2H_{n}(t_{0}-1)}\right)
and choose
y_{1}\in\bar{B}
so that
u(y_{1})=\inf_{y\in B}u(y)
. In particular, since
\nu_{x_{0},t_{0};1}(B)\geq 1/2
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\int_{M}v\exp(-\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}(\cdot,1))\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{0},t_{0};1}\right)^{\lambda}\geq

\displaystyle 2^{-\lambda}\exp\left(-\lambda w(y_{1})^{2}-\lambda\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}(y_{1},1)\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 2^{-\lambda}\exp\left(-\lambda w(y_{1})^{2}-\lambda\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}(x_{0},t_{0})-C_{2}\right),

(A.15)

where we used Proposition
2.9
for the last inequality.

Next, it follows from (
A.11
) and Proposition
2.9
that

\displaystyle 0\geq\mathcal{N}^{*}_{0}(\cdot,1)\geq\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}(x_{0},t_{0})-L_{2}(D+1)-C_{3}d_{1}(z_{1},\cdot).

(A.16)

Moreover, for any
q\in M
, we have by (
A.13
)

\displaystyle w(q)\geq

\displaystyle w(y_{1})-C_{1}d_{1}(q,y_{1})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle w(y_{1})-C_{1}\left(d_{1}(q,z_{1})+d_{1}(z_{1},z_{0})+d_{1}(z_{0},y_{1})\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle w(y_{1})-C_{1}d_{1}(q,z_{1})-L_{3}(D+1).

(A.17)

By Theorem
2.15
(i), we see that for any
l>0
,

\nu_{x_{1},t_{1};1}\left(M\setminus B_{1}(z_{1},l)\right)\leq C(n)\exp\left(-\frac{l^{2}}{5(t_{1}-1)}\right)\leq C(n)\exp\left(-\frac{l^{2}\lambda}{5\theta(1-\lambda)}\right).

(A.18)

Consequently, we have

\displaystyle\int_{M}v\exp\left(-\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}(\cdot,1)\right)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{1},t_{1};1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\int_{B_{1}(z_{1},j+1)\setminus B_{1}(z_{1},j)}v\exp\left(-\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}(\cdot,1)\right)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{1},t_{1};1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle e^{-\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}(x_{0},t_{0})+L_{2}(D+1)}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\int_{B_{1}(z_{1},j+1)\setminus B_{1}(z_{1},j)}ve^{C_{3}d_{1}(\cdot,z_{1})}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{1},t_{1};1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(n)e^{-\mathcal{N}_{0}^{*}(x_{0},t_{0})+L_{2}(D+1)}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\left(w(y_{1})-C_{1}(j+1)-L_{3}(D+1)\right)_{+}^{2}+C_{3}(j+1)-\frac{j^{2}\lambda}{5\theta(1-\lambda)}\right),

(A.19)

where we used (
A.16
), (
A.17
) and (
A.18
). If
\theta\leq C_{1}^{-2}/10
, then we have

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\left(w(y_{1})-C_{1}(j+1)-L_{3}(D+1)\right)_{+}^{2}+C_{3}(j+1)-\frac{j^{2}\lambda}{5\theta(1-\lambda)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle e^{L_{4}(D^{2}+1)}\exp\left(-\left(w(y_{1})-C_{1}(j+1)-L_{3}(D+1)\right)_{+}^{2}-\frac{\lambda}{1-\lambda}\left(C_{1}(j+1)+L_{3}(D+1)\right)^{2}-j\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle e^{L_{4}(D^{2}+1)}\exp\left(-\lambda\left(\left(w(y_{1})-C_{1}(j+1)-L_{3}(D+1)\right)_{+}+C_{1}(j+1)+L_{3}(D+1)\right)^{2}-j\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\exp\left(L_{4}(D^{2}+1)-j-\lambda w(y_{1})^{2}\right),

(A.20)

where we used
x^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{1-\lambda}y^{2}\geq\lambda(x+y)^{2}
for the second inequality. Combining (
A
), (
A.19
) and (
A.20
), we obtain (
A.14
).
∎

Appendix B
Comparison of spacetime distances

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
. In Definition
3.5
, we introduced the
d^{*}
-distance on
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
. Note that
d^{*}
is not canonically defined. We now give an alternative definition.

Definition B.1
.

Fix
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0}]
, where
\epsilon_{0}
is the spacetime distance constant (see Definition
3.3
). For any
x^{*}=(x,t),y^{*}=(y,s)\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
with
s\leq t
, we define

\displaystyle d^{*,\epsilon}(x^{*},y^{*}):=\inf_{r\in[\sqrt{t-s},\sqrt{t+(1-\sigma)T})}\left\{r\mid d_{W_{1}}^{t-r^{2}}(\nu_{x^{*};t-r^{2}},\nu_{y^{*};t-r^{2}})\leq\epsilon r\right\}.

If no such
r
exists, we define
d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*}):=\epsilon^{-1}d_{W_{1}}^{-(1-\sigma)T}(\nu_{x^{*};-(1-\sigma)T},\nu_{y^{*};-(1-\sigma)T})
.

The following lemma shows that all these distances are equivalent to
d^{*}=d^{*,\epsilon_{0}}
.

Lemma B.2
.

For any
x^{*},y^{*}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
and
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0}]
,

\displaystyle d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*})\leq d^{*,\epsilon}(x^{*},y^{*})\leq\frac{\epsilon_{0}}{\epsilon}d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*}).

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
t=\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})\geq s=\mathfrak{t}(y^{*})
and set
r=d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*})
. If
t-r^{2}>-(1-\sigma)T
, then by (
3.6
),

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{t-r^{2}}(\nu_{x^{*};t-r^{2}},\nu_{y^{*};t-r^{2}})=\epsilon_{0}r,

which implies

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{\max\{-(1-\sigma)T,t-(\epsilon_{0}\epsilon^{-1}r)^{2}\}}\left(\nu_{x^{*};\max\{-(1-\sigma)T,t-(\epsilon_{0}\epsilon^{-1}r)^{2}\}},\nu_{y^{*};\max\{-(1-\sigma)T,t-(\epsilon_{0}\epsilon^{-1}r)^{2}\}}\right)\leq\epsilon(\epsilon_{0}\epsilon^{-1}r).

Thus by Definition
B.1
,
d^{*,\epsilon}(x^{*},y^{*})\leq\epsilon_{0}\epsilon^{-1}r
. Similarly, if
t-r^{2}\leq-(1-\sigma)T
, we can obtain the same estimate using (
3.7
). This shows
d^{*,\epsilon}(x^{*},y^{*})\leq\epsilon_{0}\epsilon^{-1}d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*})
.

On the other hand, set
r^{\prime}=d^{*,\epsilon}(x^{*},y^{*})
. If
t-(r^{\prime})^{2}>-(1-\sigma)T
, then by Definition
B.1
and Lemma
3.6
,

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{t-(r^{\prime})^{2}}(\nu_{x^{*};t-(r^{\prime})^{2}};\nu_{y^{*};t-(r^{\prime})^{2}})=\epsilon r^{\prime}\leq\epsilon_{0}r^{\prime},

which, by (
3.2
), implies
d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*})\leq r^{\prime}
. Similarly, if
t-(r^{\prime})^{2}\leq-(1-\sigma)T
, we can obtain the same estimate using (
3.7
). Thus we have shown
d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*})\leq d^{*,\epsilon}(x^{*},y^{*})
. This completes the proof.
∎

Recall that it is proved in
[
MT10
, Theorem 2]
that for any
x^{*},y^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
, the function

\displaystyle t\mapsto d_{W_{2}}^{t}(\nu_{x^{*};t},\nu_{y^{*};t})

is non-decreasing. By Definition
3.3
and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, for any
x_{0}^{*}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
with
[t_{0}-r^{2},t_{0}]\subset\mathbb{I}^{+}
, we have

\displaystyle d_{W_{2}}^{t_{0}-r^{2}}(\nu_{x_{0}^{*};t_{0}-r^{2}},\delta_{x})\geq\epsilon_{0}r,

for any
x\in M
. Thus, similar to Definition
3.5
, we also have the following definition.

Definition B.3
.

For any
x^{*}=(x,t),y^{*}=(y,s)\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
with
s\leq t
, we define

\displaystyle d_{2}^{*}(x^{*},y^{*}):=\inf_{r\in[\sqrt{t-s},\sqrt{t+(1-\sigma)T})}\left\{r\mid d_{W_{2}}^{t-r^{2}}(\nu_{x,t;t-r^{2}},\nu_{y,s;t-r^{2}})\leq\epsilon_{0}r\right\}.

If no such
r
exists, we define
d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*}):=\epsilon_{0}^{-1}d_{W_{2}}^{-(1-\sigma)T}(\nu_{x^{*};-(1-\sigma)T},\nu_{y^{*};-(1-\sigma)T})
.

The following proposition shows that
d_{2}^{*}
is equivalent to
d^{*}
.

Proposition B.4
.

For any
x^{*}=(x,t),y^{*}=(y,s)\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
,

\displaystyle(1+\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\sqrt{2H_{n}})^{-1}d_{2}^{*}(x^{*},y^{*})\leq d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*})\leq d_{2}^{*}(x^{*},y^{*}).

(B.1)

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
s\leq t
. Let
r=d_{2}^{*}(x^{*},y^{*})
and
r_{1}=d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*})
. If
t-r^{2}>-(1-\sigma)T
, then by monotonicity,

\displaystyle\lim_{l\nearrow t-r^{2}}d^{l}_{W_{2}}(\nu_{x^{*};l},\nu_{y^{*};l})\leq\epsilon_{0}r\leq\lim_{l\searrow t-r^{2}}d_{W_{2}}^{l}(\nu_{x^{*};l},\nu_{y^{*};l}),

which, by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, implies for any
l<t-r^{2}
,

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{l}(\nu_{x^{*};l},\nu_{y^{*};l})\leq d_{W_{2}}^{l}(\nu_{x^{*};l},\nu_{y^{*};l})\leq\epsilon_{0}r.

Thus,
r_{1}\leq r
. If
t-r^{2}\leq-(1-\sigma)T
, then

\displaystyle d_{W_{2}}^{-(1-\sigma)T}(\nu_{x^{*};-(1-\sigma)T},\nu_{y^{*};-(1-\sigma)T})=\epsilon_{0}r,

which implies

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{-(1-\sigma)T}(\nu_{x^{*};-(1-\sigma)T},\nu_{y^{*};-(1-\sigma)T})\leq d_{W_{2}}^{-(1-\sigma)T}(\nu_{x^{*};-(1-\sigma)T},\nu_{y^{*};-(1-\sigma)T})=\epsilon_{0}r.

Therefore, we also have
r_{1}\leq r
. In either case, we have proved the second inequality in (
B.1
).

By Lemma
2.4
, we see that for any
l\leq s
,

\displaystyle d_{W_{2}}^{l}(\nu_{x^{*};l},\nu_{y^{*};l})

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(\nu_{x^{*};l},\nu_{y^{*};l})}

\displaystyle\leq d_{W_{1}}^{l}(\nu_{x^{*};l},\nu_{y^{*};l})+\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(\nu_{x^{*};l})}+\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(\nu_{y^{*};l})}.

(B.2)

If
t-r_{1}^{2}>-(1-\sigma)T
, then

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{t-r_{1}^{2}}(\nu_{x^{*};t-r_{1}^{2}},\nu_{y^{*};t-r_{1}^{2}})=\epsilon_{0}r_{1},

which, when combined with (
B
) and Proposition
2.12
, implies

\displaystyle d_{W_{2}}^{t-r_{1}^{2}}(\nu_{x^{*};t-r_{1}^{2}},\nu_{y^{*};t-r_{1}^{2}})\leq\epsilon_{0}r_{1}+\sqrt{H_{n}r_{1}^{2}}+\sqrt{H_{n}(s-t+r_{1}^{2})}\leq(\epsilon_{0}+\sqrt{2H_{n}})r_{1}.

By definition, we have
r\leq(1+\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\sqrt{2H_{n}})r_{1}
. If
t-r_{1}^{2}\leq-(1-\sigma)T
, then

\displaystyle d_{W_{1}}^{-(1-\sigma)T}(\nu_{x^{*};-(1-\sigma)T},\nu_{y^{*};-(1-\sigma)T})=\epsilon_{0}r_{1}\geq\epsilon_{0}\sqrt{t+(1-\sigma)T},

which, when combined with (
B
) and Proposition
2.12
, implies

\displaystyle d_{W_{2}}^{-(1-\sigma)T}(\nu_{x^{*};-(1-\sigma)T},\nu_{y^{*};-(1-\sigma)T})\leq\epsilon_{0}r_{1}+\sqrt{H_{n}(t+(1-\sigma)T)}+\sqrt{H_{n}(s+(1-\sigma)T)}\leq(\epsilon_{0}+\sqrt{2H_{n}})r_{1}.

Again, by definition, we have
r\leq(1+\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\sqrt{2H_{n}})r_{1}
, which gives the first inequality in (
B.1
).
∎

Appendix C
Eigenvalues and almost splitting

In this section, we consider a closed Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in I}\}
. All time subintervals considered below are assumed to be contained in
I
.

We fix a spacetime point
x_{0}^{*}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
and set

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}=\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*}_{0};t}=(4\pi\tau)^{-n/2}e^{-f}\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)},

where
\tau=t_{0}-t
. We consider the weighted Laplacian
\Delta_{f}=\Delta-\langle\nabla\cdot,\nabla f\rangle
. It is clear that
\Delta_{f}
is self-adjoint with respect to
\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}
.

Definition C.1
.

Given a subinterval
J\subset I
containing
t_{0}
and a smooth function
u
on
M\times J
, we define

\displaystyle I_{u}(t):=\int_{M}u^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t},\quad\ D_{u}(t):=\int_{M}|\nabla u|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t},\quad F_{u}(t):=\frac{\tau\int_{M}|\nabla u|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}}{\int_{M}u^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}}=\frac{\tau D_{u}(t)}{I_{u}(t)}

for
t\in J
.
F_{u}
is called the
frequency
of
u
.

The following frequency estimate essentially follows from
[
CMI24
]
.

Lemma C.2

(Frequency estimate)
.

Suppose
\square u=0
on
M\times J
. Then the following evolution of the frequency holds for
t\in J
:

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}{\displaystyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}F_{u}(t)=-\frac{F_{u}}{\tau}+\frac{2}{\tau}F_{u}^{2}-\frac{2\tau\int_{M}|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}}{I_{u}(t)}.

(C.1)

In particular, we have

\mathchoice{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}{\displaystyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}F_{u}(t)\leq\frac{F_{u}(t)}{\tau}(2F_{u}(t)-1).

(C.2)

If
F_{u}(t_{0}-r^{2})\leq\dfrac{1}{2}+\epsilon
for some
r>0
, then for any
t\in[t_{0}-r^{2},t_{0}-4\epsilon r^{2}]
, we have

\displaystyle F_{u}(t)\leq\frac{1}{2}+2\tau^{-1}\epsilon.

Proof.

Since

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}{\displaystyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}I_{u}(t)=\int_{M}\square u^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}=\int_{M}2u\square u-2|\nabla u|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}=-2D_{u}(t)

and

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}{\displaystyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}D_{u}(t)=-2\int_{M}|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t},

we have

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}{\displaystyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}F_{u}(t)=\mathchoice{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}{\displaystyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}\left(\frac{\tau D_{u}(t)}{I_{u}(t)}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{D_{u}(t)}{I_{u}(t)}-\frac{2\tau\int_{M}|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}}{I_{u}(t)}+\frac{2\tau\left(\int_{M}|\nabla u|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\right)^{2}}{\left(\int_{M}u^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{F_{u}}{\tau}+\frac{2}{\tau}F_{u}^{2}-\frac{2\tau\int_{M}|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}}{I_{u}(t)},

which gives (
C.1
). Moreover, (
C.2
) follows immediately.

Without loss of generality, we assume
t_{0}=0
and
r=1
. If
F_{u}(-1)\leq\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon
, by integrating (
C.2
), we obtain

\displaystyle F_{u}(t)\leq\frac{1}{2-\tau^{-1}\left(\dfrac{2F_{u}(-1)-1}{F_{u}(-1)}\right)}\leq\frac{1}{2-\dfrac{4\epsilon}{\tau}}.

Thus, if
4\epsilon\leq\tau\leq 1
, then

\displaystyle F_{u}(t)\leq\frac{1}{2}+2\tau^{-1}\epsilon.

∎

Now we discuss the relations between eigenvalues of
\Delta_{f}
and splitting maps. Denote by
0<\lambda_{1}(t)\leq\lambda_{2}(t)\leq\ldots
the eigenvalues of
\Delta_{f}
at time
t
, counted with multiplicities. Recall that by Theorem
2.18
,
\tau\lambda_{1}(t)\geq 1/2
for any
t<t_{0}
.

The next proposition gives the propagation of the eigenvalues:

Proposition C.3

(Propagation of eigenvalues)
.

If
(r^{2}\lambda_{k})(t_{0}-r^{2})\leq\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon
, then for any
t\in[t_{0}-r^{2},t_{0}-4\epsilon r^{2}]
,

\displaystyle(\tau\lambda_{k})(t)\leq\frac{1}{2}+2\tau^{-1}\epsilon.

(C.3)

Moreover, we can find
\vec{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}):M\times[t_{0}-r^{2},t_{0}]\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
such that the following holds.

For any
\delta\in[4\epsilon,1]
and
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
,

(i)

On
M\times[t_{0}-r^{2},t_{0}]
,
\square u_{i}=0
and
u_{i}(x_{0}^{*})=0
.

(ii)

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}-r^{2}}^{t_{0}-\delta r^{2}}\int_{M}|\nabla^{2}u_{i}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t\leq 3\delta^{-1}\epsilon
.

(iii)

For any
t\in[t_{0}-r^{2},t_{0}-\delta r^{2}]
, we have
\displaystyle\left\lvert\int_{M}\langle\nabla u_{i},\nabla u_{j}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}-\delta_{ij}\lambda_{i}\right\rvert\leq 6\delta^{-1}\epsilon
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
r=1
and
t_{0}=0
. Choose
\phi_{i}(-1)
,
i=1,\cdots,k
, as an
L^{2}
-orthonormal (with respect to
\mathrm{d}\nu_{-1}
) eigenfunctions corresponding to
\lambda_{i}(-1)
.

Next, we solve

\displaystyle\square u_{i}=0,\quad u_{i}=\phi_{i}(-1)\quad\mathrm{at}\quad t=-1.

Denote
I_{i}(t):=I_{u_{i}}(t),D_{i}(t):=D_{u_{i}}(t)
and
F_{i}(t)=F_{u_{i}}(t)
. By Lemma
C.2
,

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}{\displaystyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}F_{i}(t)=-\frac{F_{i}}{\tau}+\frac{2}{\tau}F_{i}^{2}-\frac{2\tau\int|\nabla^{2}u_{i}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}}{I_{i}(t)},

(C.4)

and

\mathchoice{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}{\displaystyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}F_{i}(t)\leq\frac{F_{i}(t)}{\tau}(2F_{i}(t)-1).

(C.5)

By the Gram-Schmidt process, it follows from (
C.5
) by using the same argument as in
[
CMI24
, Equation (3.34)]
that

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}{\displaystyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}(\tau\lambda_{k})(-1)\leq\frac{(\tau\lambda_{k})(-1)}{\tau}\big(2(\tau\lambda_{k})(-1)-1\big).

Since the argument works at any time, we can conclude that for any
t\in[-1,0)
,

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}{\displaystyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}(\tau\lambda_{k})(t)\leq\frac{(\tau\lambda_{k})(t)}{\tau}\big(2(\tau\lambda_{k})(t)-1\big).

By integration, we obtain (
C.3
).

By Lemma
C.2
,
F_{i}(t)\in[1/2,1/2+2\tau^{-1}\epsilon]
for any
\tau\in[4\epsilon,1]
. Thus, we can integrate (
C.4
) to get

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{-\delta}\frac{2\tau\int_{M}|\nabla^{2}u_{i}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}}{I_{i}(t)}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=F_{i}(-\delta)-F_{i}(-1)+\int_{-1}^{-\delta}\left(\frac{F_{i}(t)}{\tau}(2F_{i}(t)-1)\right)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq 2\delta^{-1}\epsilon+4\epsilon\int_{-1}^{-\delta}\tau^{-2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq 6\delta^{-1}\epsilon.

(C.6)

Since
\mathchoice{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}{\displaystyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}\log I_{i}(t)=-2\tau^{-1}F_{i}(t)\leq-\tau^{-1}
, we obtain that for
t\in[-1,-\delta]

\displaystyle I_{i}(t)\leq I_{i}(-1)e^{-\int_{-1}^{t}|s|^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}s}=\tau

Combining this with (
C
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{-\delta}\int_{M}|\nabla^{2}u_{i}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t\leq 3\delta^{-1}\epsilon.

(C.7)

Since
\mathchoice{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}{\displaystyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}\int_{M}u_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}=\int_{M}\square u_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}=0
and
\int\phi_{i}(-1)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{-1}=0
, we see that for all
t\in[-1,0]
,

\displaystyle\int_{M}u_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}=0.

Moreover, for any
1\leq i,j\leq k
, since
\int_{M}\langle\nabla u_{i},\nabla u_{j}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{-1}=\delta_{ij}\lambda_{i}
and

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}{\displaystyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}{\scriptstyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mu}{\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.0mut}}}\int_{M}\langle\nabla u_{i},\nabla u_{j}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}=-2\int_{M}\langle\nabla^{2}u_{i},\nabla^{2}u_{j}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t},

we have for any
t\in[-1,-\delta]
,

\displaystyle\left|\int_{M}\langle\nabla u_{i},\nabla u_{j}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}-\delta_{ij}\lambda_{i}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\int_{-1}^{t}\int_{M}|\nabla^{2}u_{i}||\nabla^{2}u_{j}|\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{s}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\left(\int_{-1}^{t}\int_{M}|\nabla^{2}u_{i}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{s}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{-1}^{t}\int_{M}|\nabla^{2}u_{j}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{s}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq 6\delta^{-1}\epsilon,

where we used (
C.7
) for the last inequality.

In sum, the proof is complete.
∎

For the map
\vec{u}
, we can modify it by a positive definite matrix so that the following holds (see Definition
10.1
).

Corollary C.4
.

If
(r^{2}\lambda_{k})(t_{0}-r^{2})\leq\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon
, then there exists a
(k,C\epsilon,r/\sqrt{10})
-splitting map at
x_{0}^{*}
, where
C
is a universal constant.

The following proposition shows that, under the assumption of almost self-similarity, the existence of a
(k,\epsilon,r)
-splitting map is equivalent to the smallness of
r^{2}\lambda_{k}(-r^{2})-\frac{1}{2}
.

Proposition C.5
.

Suppose that
\vec{u}=(u_{1},\cdots,u_{k})
is a
(k,\epsilon,r)
-splitting map at
x_{0}^{*}
with
\epsilon\leq\epsilon(n)
, and

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(r^{2}/10)-\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(10r^{2})\leq\delta.

Then there exists a constant
C=C(n)>0
such that

\displaystyle(\tau\lambda_{k})(t_{0}-r^{2})\leq\frac{1}{2}+C(\epsilon+\delta^{\frac{1}{2}}).

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
r=1
and
t_{0}=0
. In the proof, the constant
C
denotes a universal constant, which can be different line by line.

By our assumption, we know that

\displaystyle 2\int_{-10}^{-1/10}\int_{M}\tau\left\lvert\mathrm{Ric}+\nabla^{2}f-\frac{g}{2\tau}\right\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t\leq\delta.

Let
\{\phi_{i}(t)\}
be a sequence
L^{2}
-orthonormal eigenfunctions corresponding to eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}(t)
. For a smooth function
u
with decomposition
u=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}a_{l}\phi_{l}
at
t
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{M}\frac{1}{2\tau}|\nabla u|^{2}-(\Delta_{f}u)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{l}(\frac{1}{2\tau}-\lambda_{l})a_{l}^{2}\leq 0.

(C.8)

By Bochner’s formula, we have

\displaystyle\int_{M}\left(\frac{g}{2\tau}-\nabla^{2}f-\mathrm{Ric}\right)(\nabla u,\nabla u)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}=\int_{M}|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}+\frac{1}{2\tau}|\nabla u|^{2}-(\Delta_{f}u)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}.

Applying this to
u_{i}(t)=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}a_{l}^{i}(t)\phi_{l}(t)
, we get

\displaystyle\int_{M}\left(\frac{g}{2\tau}-\nabla^{2}f-\mathrm{Ric}\right)(\nabla u_{i},\nabla u_{i})\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}=\int_{M}|\nabla^{2}u_{i}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2\tau}|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}-(\Delta_{f}u_{i})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}.

By Proposition
10.2
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\int_{-3}^{-1/10}\int_{M}\left(\frac{g}{2\tau}-\nabla^{2}f-\mathrm{Ric}\right)(\nabla u_{i},\nabla u_{i})\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\int_{-3}^{-1/10}\int\left\lvert\frac{g}{2\tau}-\nabla^{2}f-\mathrm{Ric}\right\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{-3}^{-1/10}|\nabla u_{i}|^{4}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\leq C\delta^{1/2}.

Combining this with (
C.8
) and Definition
10.1
(iii), we obtain
that for all
1\leq i\leq k
,

\displaystyle\int_{-3}^{-1/10}\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{l}(\lambda_{l}-\frac{1}{2\tau})(a_{l}^{i})^{2}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C(\epsilon+\delta^{1/2}).

In particular, we have

\displaystyle\int_{-3}^{-1/10}(\lambda_{k}-\frac{1}{2\tau})\sum_{l=k}^{\infty}\lambda_{l}(a_{l}^{i})^{2}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{-3}^{-1/10}\sum_{l=k}^{\infty}\lambda_{l}(\lambda_{l}-\frac{1}{2\tau})(a_{l}^{i})^{2}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C(\epsilon+\delta^{1/2}).

Thus, we can find
s_{1}\in[-3,-2]
such that for all
1\leq i\leq k
,

\big(\lambda_{k}(s_{1})-\frac{1}{2\tau(s_{1})}\big)\sum_{l=k}^{\infty}\lambda_{l}(s_{1})(a_{l}^{i})^{2}(s_{1})\leq C(\epsilon+\delta^{1/2}).

(C.9)

Since
\big|\int_{M}\langle\nabla u_{i},\nabla u_{j}\rangle\mathrm{d}\nu_{s_{1}}-\delta_{ij}\big|\leq 2\epsilon
by Proposition
10.2
, we have at time
s_{1}
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\delta_{ij}-\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{l}a_{l}^{i}a_{l}^{j}\right\rvert\leq 2\epsilon.

(C.10)

If there exists
1\leq i_{0}\leq k
such that the following holds: for some small dimensional constant
c_{0}>0
to be determined later,

\displaystyle\sum_{l=k}^{\infty}\lambda_{l}(s_{1})(a_{l}^{i_{0}})^{2}(s_{1})\geq c_{0},

then by (
C.9
), we obtain
\lambda_{k}(s_{1})-\dfrac{1}{2\tau(s_{1})}\leq Cc_{0}^{-1}(\epsilon+\delta^{1/2})
. Therefore, the conclusion follows from Proposition
C.3
. Now we assume that for all
1\leq i\leq k
,

\displaystyle\sum_{l=k}^{\infty}\lambda_{l}(s_{1})(a_{l}^{i})^{2}(s_{1})\leq c_{0}\quad\text{and hence}\quad\sum_{l=1}^{k-1}\lambda_{l}(s_{1})(a_{l}^{i})^{2}(s_{1})\geq 1-c_{0}-2\epsilon.

(C.11)

At time
s_{1}
, by (
C.10
), we have for
i\neq j
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\sum_{l=1}^{k-1}\lambda_{l}a_{l}^{i}a_{l}^{j}\right\rvert\leq 2\epsilon+\big|\sum_{l=k}^{\infty}\lambda_{l}a_{l}^{i}a_{l}^{j}\big|\leq 2\epsilon+\left(\sum_{l=k}^{\infty}\lambda_{l}(a_{l}^{i})^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\sum_{l=k}^{\infty}\lambda_{l}(a_{l}^{j})^{2}\right)^{1/2}\leq 2\epsilon+c_{0}.

(C.12)

We define an inner product for
(k-1)
-tuples as follows: for
\vec{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k-1}),\vec{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{k-1})
, set

\displaystyle\langle\vec{a},\vec{b}\rangle=\sum_{l=1}^{k-1}\lambda_{l}(s_{1})a_{l}b_{l}.

Thus, for
a^{i}=(a_{1}^{i},\ldots,a_{k-1}^{i})
, we see that for all
1\leq i\neq j\leq k
,

\displaystyle 1-c_{0}-2\epsilon\leq\langle a^{i},a^{i}\rangle\leq 1+2\epsilon,\quad\langle a^{i},a^{j}\rangle\leq 2\epsilon+c_{0}.

Thus, if
c_{0}
and
\epsilon
are smaller than some constant depending on
n
, the number of such
\{a^{i}\}
can be at most
k-1
. This contradicts (
C.11
) and (
C.12
).

In sum, the proof is complete.
∎

Appendix D
Spines of Ricci shrinker spaces

Let
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
be an
n
-dimensional Ricci shrinker space with entropy bounded below by
-Y
(see Definition
7.16
). We denote by
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
the regular set, which is realized as a Ricci flow spacetime
(\mathcal{R}^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}},g^{Z^{\prime}}_{t})
. For simplicity, we set
f=f_{z^{\prime}}
and
\nu_{t}=\nu_{z^{\prime};t}
.

First, we prove the following lemma. Here,
\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}
is the associated metric flow at
z^{\prime}
.

Lemma D.1
.

\left(\iota_{z^{\prime}}(\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}_{-1}),d^{Z^{\prime}}_{-1},\nu_{-1}\right)
is an
\mathrm{RCD}(1/2,\infty)
-space.

Proof.

By Proposition
7.9
, the following Ricci shrinker equation holds on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}(g^{Z^{\prime}})+\nabla^{2}f=\frac{g^{Z^{\prime}}}{2\tau},

(D.1)

where
\tau:=-\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
. In addition, thanks to Corollary
7.10
,
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}
is connected for any
t<0
, and the distance
d^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}
on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}
agrees with the distance induced by
g^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}
.

Furthermore, the Minkowski dimension of the singular set
\iota_{z^{\prime}}(\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}_{-1})\setminus\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}
is at most
n-4
(see Theorem
8.22
(i)). By combining the Ricci shrinker equation (
D.1
) with the high codimension of the singular set, one can then derive the desired conclusion using the same argument in
[
LW24b
, Proposition A.16]
.

For the reader’s convenience, we sketch the proof below. For simplicity, we set
(X,d,\mu)=\left(\iota_{z^{\prime}}(\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}_{-1}),d^{Z^{\prime}}_{-1},\nu_{-1}\right)
. We define the Sobolev space
W^{1,2}(X,\mu)
to be the subspace of
L^{2}(X,\mu)
consisting of functions
u
for which

\displaystyle\|u\|^{2}_{W^{1,2}}=\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\inf_{u_{i}}\liminf_{i\to\infty}\|h_{i}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}<\infty,

where the infimum is taken over all upper gradients
h_{i}
of the function
u_{i}
with
\|u_{i}-u\|_{L^{2}}\to 0
. Then, it can be proved as
[
CW17
, Corollary 2.12]
that
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1})
is dense in
W^{1,2}
, which holds due to the singular set having codimension greater than
2
. We then consider the standard nonnegative symmetric bilinear form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(u,v):=\int_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}}\langle\nabla u,\nabla v\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu

for
u,v\in W^{1,2}
. It can be proved (see
[
CW17
, Corollary 2.15]
) that
\mathcal{D}
is an irreducible, strongly local and regular Dirichlet form. Moreover, if we denote by
\Delta_{f}
the unique generator concerning
\mathcal{D}
, then the following Bakry-Émery condition holds:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int|\nabla u|^{2}\Delta_{f}v\,\mathrm{d}\mu\geq\frac{1}{2}\int v|\nabla u|^{2}\Delta_{f}v\,\mathrm{d}\mu+\int v\langle\nabla u,\nabla\Delta_{f}v\rangle\Delta_{f}v\,\mathrm{d}\mu

for any
u\in D(\Delta_{f})
with
\Delta_{f}u\in W^{1,2}
and
v\in L^{\infty}\cap D(\Delta_{f})
with
v\geq 0
and
\Delta_{f}v\in L^{\infty}
. Here,
D(\Delta_{f})
denotes the domain of
\Delta_{f}
.

With these facts established, we see that conditions (i), (iii), and (iv) in
[
Gig18
, Definition 2.1]
are satisfied. Condition (ii) in
[
Gig18
, Definition 2.1]
is trivially satisfied since
\mu
is a probability measure.
∎

Next, we prove

Lemma D.2
.

Suppose
u
is a smooth function on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}
for
t<0
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}}u^{2}+|\nabla u|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}<\infty

and
\Delta_{f}u+\frac{u}{2|t|}=0
, where
\Delta_{f}=\Delta_{g^{Z^{\prime}}}-\langle\nabla f,\nabla\rangle
at
t
. Then
\nabla u
induces a splitting factor
\mathbb{R}
on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
t=-1
.

Using the notation from the proof of Lemma
D.1
, our assumptions imply that
u\in W^{1,2}
. The conclusion then follows directly from
[
GKKO20
, Proposition 3.2]
, since
\left(\iota_{z^{\prime}}(\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}_{-1}),d^{Z^{\prime}}_{-1},\nu_{z;-1}\right)
is an
\mathrm{RCD}(1/2,\infty)
-space.
∎

We call

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}:=\mathcal{N}_{z^{\prime}}(1)

the
entropy
of the Ricci shrinker space.

Next, we show

Lemma D.3
.

For any
x\in Z^{\prime}
and
\tau>0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to+\infty}\mathcal{N}_{x}(\tau)=\boldsymbol{\mu}.

(D.2)

In particular, for any
\tau>0
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{x}(\tau)\geq\boldsymbol{\mu}.

(D.3)

Proof.

We only prove (
D.2
), from which (
D.3
) follows by monotonicity.

After taking the limit for Proposition
3.20
, we obtain for any
x\in Z^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{N}_{x}(\tau)-\mathcal{N}_{z^{\prime}}(\tau)\right\rvert\leq\frac{C(n)}{\sqrt{\tau}}d_{Z^{\prime}}(x,z^{\prime}).

Letting
\tau\to+\infty
, we obtain (
D.2
) and hence complete the proof.
∎

We have the following definition.

Definition D.4
.

The
spine
of a Ricci shrinker space
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is a set defined as

\displaystyle\mathrm{spine}(Z^{\prime}):=\{x\in Z^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{N}_{x}(\tau)=\boldsymbol{\mu},\,\forall\tau>0\}.

Moreover, we define the
arrival time
:

\displaystyle t_{a}:=\sup\{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(x)\mid x\in\mathrm{spine}(Z^{\prime})\}\in[0,\infty].

The
dimension
of
\mathrm{spine}(Z^{\prime})
is defined to be the unique integer
k\in[0,n+2]
such that
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is
k
-symmetric, but not
(k+1)
-symmetric (see Definition
8.7
).

We next prove the static principle.

Lemma D.5
.

Suppose
y\in\mathrm{spine}(Z^{\prime})
with
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(y)\neq 0
. Then
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is a static or quasi-static cone (see Definition
7.17
). In this case, the Ricci curvature vanishes on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,t_{a}]}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(y)>-1
.

We set
f^{\prime}=f_{y}
. Then it follows from Proposition
7.9
that on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(y))}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}(g^{Z^{\prime}})+\nabla^{2}f^{\prime}=\frac{g^{Z^{\prime}}}{2(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(y)-\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})},

which, when combined with
\mathrm{Ric}(g^{Z^{\prime}})+\nabla^{2}f=\frac{g^{Z^{\prime}}}{2|\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}|}
, implies

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}+\nabla^{2}u=0

(D.4)

on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}
, where

\displaystyle u:=\frac{(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(y)+1)f^{\prime}-f}{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(y)}.

Using
\mathrm{div}_{f}
on (
D.4
), we obtain on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}_{f}(\nabla^{2}u)=\nabla\left(\Delta_{f}u+\frac{1}{2}u\right)=0.

Thus, it follows that

\displaystyle\Delta_{f}u+\frac{1}{2}u\equiv c

for a constant
c
, since
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}
is connected. Define
u^{\prime}:=u-2c
. Then we have

\displaystyle\Delta_{f}u^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2}u^{\prime}=0.

(D.5)

On the other hand, since all
f
,
f^{\prime}
,
|\nabla f|^{2}
and
|\nabla f^{\prime}|^{2}
increase at most quadratically (see (
7.7
) and Lemma
7.12
), we conclude that
|u^{\prime}|+|\nabla u^{\prime}|
belongs to
L^{2}(Z^{\prime}_{-1},\nu_{-1})
. Consequently, it follows from (
D.5
) and Lemma
D.2
that
\nabla^{2}u^{\prime}\equiv 0
on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}
. Combined with (
D.4
), it follows that
\mathrm{Ric}\equiv 0
on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}
. Thus,
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is a static or quasi-static cone.

By the same argument, one concludes that
\mathrm{Ric}\equiv 0
on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,t_{a})}
. By taking the limit, we also obtain
\mathrm{Ric}\equiv 0
on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,t_{a}]}
.
∎

Suppose that
\mathrm{spine}(Z^{\prime})
has dimension
k
and
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is a static cone. Then it follows from Proposition
7.23
and Proposition
8.4
that there exist maps
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}
for
s\in\mathbb{R}^{k-2}
. Next, we prove

Proposition D.6
.

With the above assumptions, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{spine}(Z^{\prime})=\{\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}\circ\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(z^{\prime})\mid t\in\mathbb{R},\,s\in\mathbb{R}^{k-2}\}.

Proof.

We set
S:=\{\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}\circ\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(z^{\prime})\mid t\in\mathbb{R},\,s\in\mathbb{R}^{k-2}\}
. For any
y\in S
, it follows from Proposition
7.23
(iii) and Proposition
8.4
(iii) that
y\in\mathrm{spine}(Z^{\prime})
.

Conversely, suppose
x\in\mathrm{spine}(Z^{\prime})
. We define
x^{\prime}:=\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{-\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(x)}(x)\in Z^{\prime}_{0}
. It is clear that
x^{\prime}\in\mathrm{spine}(Z^{\prime})
and we only need to prove that
x^{\prime}\in S
.

We set
f^{\prime}:=f_{x^{\prime}}
, then by the equation
\mathrm{Ric}(g^{Z^{\prime}})+\nabla^{2}f^{\prime}=g^{Z^{\prime}}/2
on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}u\equiv 0

on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}
, where
u=f-f^{\prime}
. If
u
is a constant
c
, then we have

\displaystyle(4\pi)^{\frac{n}{2}}=\int_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}}e^{-f}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z^{\prime}}_{-1}}=\int_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}}e^{-f^{\prime}-c}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z^{\prime}}_{-1}}=e^{-c}(4\pi)^{\frac{n}{2}},

(D.6)

which implies that
c=0
. Then we have
f=f^{\prime}
, meaning that
x^{\prime}=z^{\prime}\in S
.

If
u
is not a constant, then
\nabla u
induces a splitting factor
\mathbb{R}
in
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}
. On the other hand, by our assumption, we have a decomposition
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}=\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}_{-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{k-2}
. For any
w\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}
, we denote its components in the above decomposition by
(w_{1},w_{2})
. By the Ricci shrinker equation, we have

\displaystyle f(w)=h_{1}(w_{1})+\frac{|w_{2}-v_{1}|^{2}}{4}

and

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(w)=h_{2}(w_{1})+\frac{|w_{2}-v_{2}|^{2}}{4}

for any
w\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1}
, where
v_{1},v_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{k-2}
are constant vectors. Since
\nabla u
must be parallel to
\mathbb{R}^{k-2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle u(w)=\langle w_{2},v\rangle+c^{\prime}

for some
v\in\mathbb{R}^{k-2}
and
c^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
. Thus, by Proposition
8.6
, there exists
s_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{k-2}
such that

\displaystyle f^{\prime}=f_{\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s_{0}}(z^{\prime})}+c^{\prime\prime}

for some constant
c^{\prime\prime}
. By the same argument as in (
D.6
), we conclude that
c^{\prime\prime}=0
and hence
x^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s_{0}}(z^{\prime})
. Thus,
x^{\prime}\in S
and the proof is complete.
∎

Similarly to Proposition
D.6
, we also obtain the following results by the same proof.

Proposition D.7
.

Suppose that
\mathrm{spine}(Z^{\prime})
has dimension
k
and
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is a quasi-static cone. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{spine}(Z^{\prime})=\{\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{t}\circ\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(z^{\prime})\mid t\in(-\infty,t_{a}],\,s\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\}.

Proposition D.8
.

Suppose
\mathrm{spine}(Z^{\prime})
has dimension
k
, and
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is neither a static cone nor a quasi-static cone. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{spine}(Z^{\prime})=\{\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}(z^{\prime})\mid s\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\}.

In particular,
\mathrm{spine}(Z^{\prime})\subset Z^{\prime}_{0}
.
